- **Fix:** After resolving `resolvedPath`, assert that it starts with `workflowBasePath` using `fs::canonical` (which resolves symlinks) and compare the real paths. Reject and log any path that escapes the workflow base directory.

---

### CRITICAL — Path traversal in `InjectUpstreamOutputs` / `InjectUpstreamAiStructuredOutput` JSON loading

- **Location:** `InjectUpstreamOutputs` and `InjectUpstreamAiStructuredOutput`, anonymous namespace, ~lines 150–250 in the .cpp
- **Issue:** `responseJsonPath` is built by joining `taskDir` (derived from `upstreamDef.m_WorkingDirectory`) with a filename that includes a user-controlled suffix parsed from `upstreamInstanceId` after the `#` character (`responseFilename = "response_" + suffix + ".json"`). The suffix undergoes no validation. Similarly, in `InjectUpstreamAiStructuredOutput`, `jsonFilePath` is taken directly from `upstreamState.m_OutputValues` (attacker-influenced) and passed to `simdjson::dom::parser::load` with only an existence check. No containment assertion is made.
- **Impact:** An attacker who can control instance IDs, working-directory values, or output-slot values can cause the runtime to load and flatten arbitrary JSON files from the filesystem into the run context, leaking their contents into task inputs that may subsequently be logged or returned via the callback mechanism.
- **Fix:** Validate the `#` suffix is a pure decimal integer before formatting the filename. For `jsonFilePath`, verify the canonical path is within the expected workflow output directory before loading it.

---

### HIGH — Sensitive file contents exfiltrated via completion callback

- **Location:** `FireCompletionCallback`, ~line 360–380 in the .cpp
- **Issue:** For every succeeded task with output values, the code reads up to 64 KiB of the output file (`kMaxOutputBytes = 65536`) and embeds the raw bytes in the JSON callback payload sent to the (attacker-controllable) `callbackUrl`. Output files may contain secrets, private keys, tokens, or other sensitive data written by AI tasks or shell tasks.
- **Impact:** Combined with the SSRF finding above, an attacker can exfiltrate up to 64 KiB of any file the process can read, delivered to an endpoint of their choosing. Even with a fixed `callbackUrl`, this pushes potentially sensitive file contents to an external server.
- **Fix:** Do not include file contents in the callback payload by default. If a summary is required, include only declared metadata (slot name, file size, hash). Gate file-content inclusion behind an explicit opt-in flag that is set server-side, not via the run context.

---

### HIGH — TLS certificate verification not enabled on callback curl handle

- **Location:** `FireCompletionCallback`, anonymous namespace, ~line 390–420 in the .cpp
- **Issue:** The `curl_easy_init` handle used for the completion callback sets `CURLOPT_URL`, `CURLOPT_POSTFIELDS`, `CURLOPT_HTTPHEADER`, `CURLOPT_TIMEOUT_MS`, and `CURLOPT_NOSIGNAL`, but never sets `CURLOPT_SSL_VERIFYPEER` or `CURLOPT_SSL_VERIFYHOST`. libcurl defaults for these options are `1L` only when a CA bundle is found; on some platforms (particularly Windows with the bundled curl, or embedded deployments without a system CA store) the default may be `0L` or verification may silently fail. There is no explicit enforcement.
- **Impact:** A network attacker positioned between the server and the callback endpoint can perform a TLS man-in-the-middle interception of the POST payload, which includes task output file contents and run metadata.
- **Fix:** Explicitly set `CURLOPT_SSL_VERIFYPEER, 1L` and `CURLOPT_SSL_VERIFYHOST, 2L`. Optionally pin a CA bundle path via `CURLOPT_CAINFO`.

---

### HIGH — `callbackUrl` logged without sanitisation — log injection

- **Location:** `FireCompletionCallback`, ~line 338 and ~line 410 in the .cpp
- **Issue:** `callbackUrl` (attacker-controlled) and `runId` (derived partly from `workflowId`, also potentially attacker-influenced) are passed directly into `LOG_APP_INFO` and `LOG_APP_WARN` format strings. If the logging backend writes to a terminal or a log viewer that interprets ANSI escape sequences or newlines, an attacker can inject false log entries or terminal commands.
- **Impact:** Log forgery, potential terminal escape injection in developer/ops consoles, confusion of log-parsing security monitoring tools.
- **Fix:** Sanitise logged URLs and IDs: strip or escape newlines (`\n`, `\r`), ANSI escape sequences (`\x1b[`), and non-printable characters before passing them to the logger.

---

### HIGH — Unbounded memory growth in `m_DeferredAiCompletions`; cap is racy / insufficient

- **Location:** `WorkflowRuntimeManager::DrainAiRequestCompletions`, ~line 610–640 in the .cpp
- **Issue:** Completions that cannot be matched to an active run are pushed onto `m_DeferredAiCompletions`. The cap is 256 entries, but each `AiRequestCompletion` may hold arbitrarily large `m_ResponseText` and `m_OutputValues` strings (AI responses, file paths). At 256 entries with 1 MB responses each, that is 256 MB of live heap. Furthermore `TryApplyAiCompletion` contains a redundant second `m_Mutex` lock inside a function that is only called from `Update()`, which already holds no lock but accesses `m_ActiveRuns` and `m_LastRuns` without the mutex — this is a data-race on those members when `Update()` is not called from a single thread.
- **Impact:** A source that floods the AI completion queue with large unmatched responses causes unbounded memory growth leading to OOM/crash (DoS). The data race can produce undefined behaviour if `Update()` is ever called from multiple threads.
- **Fix:** Cap individual response size before storing (apply `TruncateUtf8Safe` at ingestion). Evict the oldest deferred completion instead of silently dropping. Remove the inner `m_Mutex` lock inside `TryApplyAiCompletion` and ensure all callers document their locking contract.

---

### HIGH — Run ID generated from millisecond wall-clock timestamp — predictable / collision-prone

- **Location:** `WorkflowRuntimeManager::GenerateRunId`, ~line 900 in the .cpp; fallback in `EnqueueWorkflowRunAndGetRunId`, ~line 540 in the .cpp
- **Issue:** `GenerateRunId` appends `std::to_string(static_cast<long long>(nowTimeT))` (1-second resolution) to the workflow ID. The fallback in `EnqueueWorkflowRunAndGetRunId` uses millisecond resolution. Both are deterministic and trivially predictable by an observer who knows the approximate time of submission.
- **Impact:** An attacker who knows a run was started can predict its run ID and call `RequestCancelRun`, `RequestPauseRun`, `TryGetActiveRun`, or `Heartbeat` for that run without any other authorisation, disrupting legitimate workflow execution.
- **Fix:** Generate run IDs using a cryptographically secure random source (e.g. `RAND_bytes` / `BCryptGenRandom` / `/dev/urandom`) formatted as a hex or base64 string, not from the clock.

---

### MEDIUM — `m_SubWorkflowLinks` / `m_ActiveWatchdogs` accessed without `m_Mutex`

- **Location:** `WorkflowRuntimeManager::RegisterSubWorkflowLink` and `PropagateSubWorkflowCompletions`, ~lines 1380–1440 in the .cpp; `Heartbeat`, `RegisterWatchdog`, `UnregisterWatchdog` use `m_WatchdogMutex` correctly but `RegisterSubWorkflowLink` uses no lock at all
- **Issue:** `RegisterSubWorkflowLink` writes to `m_SubWorkflowLinks` without acquiring any mutex. `PropagateSubWorkflowCompletions` and `CancelChildSubWorkflowRuns` read and write the same map from `Update()` without a lock. If `RegisterSubWorkflowLink` is called from a thread other than the main thread (e.g. from a REST handler), this is an unsynchronised concurrent access.
- **Impact:** Data race → undefined behaviour, potential map corruption, heap corruption, or silent data loss.
- **Fix:** Protect `m_SubWorkflowLinks` with `m_Mutex` in all access paths, consistent with how `m_PendingRuns` is protected.

---

### MEDIUM — `workflowId` used directly as filesystem path component without validation

- **Location:** `CleanWorkflow`, ~line 1010–1020 in the .cpp (`queueWorkflowDir = queueRoot / workflowId`)
- **Issue:** `workflowId` is concatenated directly onto the queue root path. If `workflowId` contains path separators or `..` sequences, `queueRoot / workflowId` resolves outside the queue directory. `fs::remove_all` is then called on the resulting path.
- **Impact:** An attacker who can register a workflow with ID `../../important` can cause recursive deletion of arbitrary directories.
- **Fix:** Validate that `workflowId` matches a strict allowlist pattern (alphanumeric, hyphens, underscores) and assert the canonical form of `queueWorkflowDir` is a child of `queueRoot` before calling `remove_all`.

---

### MEDIUM — `JsonEscape` does not escape control characters below 0x20

- **Location:** `JsonEscape`, anonymous namespace, ~line 285 in the .cpp
- **Issue:** The function escapes `"`, `\`, `\n`, `\r`, `\t` but does not escape other ASCII control characters (0x00–0x1F). JSON RFC 8259 §7 requires all characters U+0000 through U+001F to be escaped. Task IDs, error messages, and output values containing NUL bytes or other control characters will produce malformed JSON in the callback payload and the `m_OutputsJson` / `m_InputsJson` summary strings.
- **Impact:** A malformed JSON payload sent to the callback URL may be misinterpreted by the receiver, enabling injection of additional JSON keys or values (e.g. a task error message containing `\u0000","ok":true,"injected":"` could manipulate the parsed result).
- **Fix:** Add a default case that emits `\uXXXX` for all code points in the range 0x00–0x1F.

---

### MEDIUM — `response_<suffix>.json` filename suffix not validated as a decimal integer

- **Location:** `InjectUpstreamOutputs`, anonymous namespace, ~line 185 in the .cpp
- **Issue:** The suffix after `#` in `upstreamInstanceId` is used verbatim in `responseFilename = "response_" + suffix + ".json"`. No check ensures `suffix` is a pure decimal integer. A suffix containing `/`, `..`, or null bytes produces a path component that could escape the `taskDir` or cause unexpected file access.
- **Impact:** Attacker-controlled instance IDs (e.g. `task#../../../secret`) allow reading arbitrary files from the filesystem as JSON and injecting their flattened contents into the run context.
- **Fix:** After extracting `suffix`, verify `std::all_of(suffix.begin(), suffix.end(), ::isdigit)` before using it. Reject (log and skip) any instance ID where the suffix fails this check.

---

### LOW — `const_cast` used to call mutating methods from a `const` member function

- **Location:** `WorkflowRuntimeManager::ExecuteTaskOnWorker`, ~line 950 and ~line 970 in the .cpp
- **Issue:** `ExecuteTaskOnWorker` is declared `const` but calls `const_cast<WorkflowRuntimeManager*>(this)->RegisterWatchdog(...)` and `UnregisterWatchdog(...)`, which acquire `m_WatchdogMutex` and mutate `m_ActiveWatchdogs`. This circumvents the `const` contract and hides the mutation from callers and static analysis tools.
- **Impact:** Not directly exploitable, but masks a concurrent mutation that could interact with the `Heartbeat` path in an unexpected way; also undermines auditing of thread-safety guarantees.
- **Fix:** Remove the `const` qualifier from `ExecuteTaskOnWorker` and update the call site accordingly.


---

### 101_doc_application_workflow_workflowTriggerBinder_h

<a id="101-doc-application-workflow-workflowtriggerbinder-h"></a>

*Source doc:* `../../../queue/jarvisCppCyberSecAudit/101_doc_application_workflow_workflowTriggerBinder_h/docu.output.md`



### HIGH — Webhook secret stored in-memory and silently degraded to unauthenticated on any parse failure

- **Location:** `ParseWebhookParams`, ~line 230–280; called from `WorkflowTriggerBinder::RegisterAll`, `WorkflowTriggerType::Webhook` branch
- **Issue:** When `paramsJson` is non-empty but JSON parsing fails (bad JSON, wrong root type, missing `"secret"` key, or wrong value type), the function returns `true` with `outSecret` left empty. The caller unconditionally passes whatever `secret` contains to `triggerEngine.AddWebhookTrigger`. An empty secret almost certainly means the webhook engine treats the endpoint as unauthenticated. An attacker who can supply a malformed `m_ParamsJson` for a webhook trigger (e.g., by writing a workflow definition) causes HMAC/secret verification to be silently skipped.
- **Impact:** Authentication bypass on webhook triggers; arbitrary workflow execution without a valid signature.
- **Fix:** Return `false` (or a distinct error state) whenever `paramsJson` is non-empty but a valid `"secret"` string cannot be extracted. The caller should then refuse to register the trigger rather than register it as open. At minimum, log a WARNING that the endpoint is being registered without a secret so operators can detect the degraded state.

---

### HIGH — Unbounded `poll_interval_seconds` truncation / integer narrowing for S3Watch, AzureBlobWatch, GcsWatch

- **Location:** `WorkflowTriggerType::S3Watch` branch ~line 390, `WorkflowTriggerType::AzureBlobWatch` ~line 490, `WorkflowTriggerType::GcsWatch` ~line 540
- **Issue:** `poll_interval_seconds` is read as `int64_t` (S3Watch, AzureBlobWatch, GcsWatch) and then cast directly to `uint32_t` without any range check: `pollIntervalSeconds = static_cast<uint32_t>(interval)`. A negative or very large attacker-controlled value wraps silently. A value of `-1` becomes `4294967295` (~136 years), effectively disabling polling. A value of `4294967296` wraps to `0`, which may cause a spin-loop or divide-by-zero inside `AddS3WatchTrigger`. Compare this with the careful clamping applied to `debounce_ms` in `ParseFileWatchParams` (~line 215).
- **Impact:** DoS via disabled or spin-looping polling triggers; resource exhaustion.
- **Fix:** Apply the same clamp used for `debounce_ms`: reject negative values and cap at `std::numeric_limits<uint32_t>::max()`, and consider enforcing a reasonable maximum (e.g., 86400 seconds).

---

### HIGH — Unbounded `poll_interval_seconds` narrowing for OneDriveWatch and EmailWatch (unsigned path)

- **Location:** `WorkflowTriggerType::OneDriveWatch` ~line 430, `WorkflowTriggerType::EmailWatch` ~line 460
- **Issue:** These two branches read `poll_interval_seconds` with `get_uint64()` into a local `uint64_t interval`, then truncate with `static_cast<uint32_t>(interval)` without checking whether `interval > UINT32_MAX`. A value like `4294967296` wraps to `0`. The `uint64_t` type avoids the negative-value problem but not the overflow.
- **Impact:** Same spin-loop / DoS as above.
- **Fix:** Add `if (interval > std::numeric_limits<uint32_t>::max()) interval = std::numeric_limits<uint32_t>::max();` before the cast, and enforce a practical upper bound.

---

### MEDIUM — Path traversal: no confinement check after `lexically_normal` for FileWatch path

- **Location:** `WorkflowTriggerType::FileWatch` branch, ~line 340–365
- **Issue:** After resolving a relative `watchedPath` against the workflow base directory, the code calls `.lexically_normal().generic_string()` and passes the result directly to `triggerEngine.AddFileWatchTrigger`. `lexically_normal` collapses `..` components purely lexically, but it does not verify that the resulting absolute path is still inside the workflow base directory. A workflow definition containing `path: "../../etc/passwd"` produces a valid absolute path outside the sandbox.
- **Impact:** An attacker who controls a workflow definition can make the engine watch (and react to events on) arbitrary files on the host, including sensitive system files and other workflows' secrets.
- **Fix:** After normalization, verify that the resolved path starts with the canonical workflow base directory path (using `std::filesystem::relative` or a prefix check on the normalized strings). Reject paths that escape the base directory.

---

### MEDIUM — Sensitive workflow/trigger IDs and file paths emitted in INFO logs without redaction

- **Location:** `WorkflowTriggerBinder::RegisterAll`, `WorkflowTriggerType::FileWatch` branch, ~lines 348 and 358–366 (`LOG_APP_INFO` calls tagged `[paths debug]`)
- **Issue:** Both log statements emit `workflowId`, `triggerId`, `watchedPathRelative`, `watchedPathAbsolute`, `workflowBaseDirectoryRelative`, and `workflowBaseDirectoryAbsolute` at INFO level. Absolute host paths in logs can reveal installation layout, home directories, and internal project structure. The `[paths debug]` tag implies these were intended as temporary debugging aids.
- **Impact:** Information disclosure; aids reconnaissance if logs are accessible to lower-privilege users or forwarded to external log aggregators.
- **Fix:** Remove or demote these statements to DEBUG/TRACE level and gate them behind a compile-time or runtime debug flag before shipping.

---

### LOW — `m_ParamsJson` content logged in error messages on parse failure, potentially leaking secrets

- **Location:** `ParseWebhookParams` is lenient and never logs, but `ParseCronParams` (~line 100) and `ParseFileWatchParams` (~line 145) log `simdjson::error_message(errorCode)` without logging the raw JSON. However, callers in `RegisterAll` log `workflowTrigger.m_Id` and `workflowDefinition.m_Id` on failure (~lines 380, 395). If `m_Id` fields are derived from or contain secret material (unlikely but possible depending on the workflow authoring tool), these would be leaked. More concretely, `m_ParamsJson` itself is never sanitised before being handed to simdjson; if a future log statement is added naively during debugging it would expose the raw JSON including the `"secret"` field.
- **Impact:** Low-severity now; latent risk that a future log addition exposes webhook secrets.
- **Fix:** Ensure the raw `m_ParamsJson` is never passed to any logging macro. Document this constraint in comments near `ParseWebhookParams`.


---

### 102_doc_application_workflow_workflowTypes_h

<a id="102-doc-application-workflow-workflowtypes-h"></a>

*Source doc:* `../../../queue/jarvisCppCyberSecAudit/102_doc_application_workflow_workflowTypes_h/docu.output.md`



### NONE
No security issues identified.

This file is a header containing only plain data-structure definitions (structs, enums, a trivial UTF-8 truncation helper, and an atomic-based watchdog). There is no I/O, no cryptography, no network access, no memory allocation beyond standard containers, no shell or SQL construction, no deserialization logic, no authentication decisions, and no secrets. The `TruncateUtf8Safe` helper performs a bounded backward walk with a `cut > 0` guard that prevents underflow. `TaskWatchdog` uses `std::atomic` correctly with appropriate memory orders. No security-relevant behavior is present in this translation unit.


---

### 103_doc_application_workflow_workflowValidator_h

<a id="103-doc-application-workflow-workflowvalidator-h"></a>

*Source doc:* `../../../queue/jarvisCppCyberSecAudit/103_doc_application_workflow_workflowValidator_h/docu.output.md`



### MEDIUM Path traversal check bypassable via encoded or alternate separator sequences

- **Location:** `ContainsPathTraversal`, anonymous namespace, ~line 175; consumed in `ValidateTaskParams` ~line 255 and `ValidateShellScriptContent` ~line 495
- **Issue:** The traversal guard looks only for the two-character literal string `".."`. It does not reject `....//` (four dots, which collapse to `../` on some shells after processing), null-byte injections (`../\x00`), URL-encoded variants if the path string ever passed through a decoder before reaching this function, or Windows-style `..\\` separators. Because the validated `command` value is later used to construct a `fs::path` that is executed on disk, a bypass here could let a crafted workflow escape the `scripts/` root.
- **Impact:** An attacker who can submit a workflow definition (e.g. through the AI generation pipeline or a webhook-sourced workflow) could reference a script outside `scripts/`, potentially executing arbitrary binaries accessible within the working tree.
- **Fix:** Replace the substring search with a proper canonicalization check: resolve `(launchCwd / command).lexically_normal()` and then assert that the result has `(launchCwd / "scripts/")` as a prefix. This is already done for the existence check but the traversal guard runs before that and blocks further checks on failure; the two checks should be unified into one canonical-prefix assertion.

---

### MEDIUM Secrets leaked into validation-issue messages and structured context fields

- **Location:** `ValidateTrigger` ~line 215 (webhook secret check); `ValidateFileInputReachability` ~line 735 (LOG_APP_INFO call); general `AddIssue`/`AddIssueEx` throughout
- **Issue:** (a) The webhook-secret check at ~line 215 reads the `secret` field from `m_ParamsJson` and passes it through `TryGetParamsString`; although the current code only checks for *emptiness* and does not embed the secret value in the issue message, the `m_Context` and `m_SuggestedFix` fields of `WorkflowValidationIssue` are populated with arbitrary workflow data that flows to the AI suggestion layer (see `m_SuggestedFix` used in Stage 5). If any future caller embeds secret values here, they reach the AI back-end. More immediately, (b) `LOG_APP_INFO` at ~line 735 logs `taskId`, `fileInput`, `resolvedRelative`, and `absoluteCheckPath.string()`. All of these are attacker-controlled strings from the workflow definition. If the logging backend writes to a shared log or an external SIEM, file-system layout is disclosed; if log injection is possible (newlines in field values), structured log parsers can be confused.
- **Impact:** Disclosure of internal file-system paths to anyone with log access; potential log-injection if task IDs or file paths contain newline or ANSI escape characters.
- **Fix:** (a) Sanitize all workflow-sourced strings before embedding them in log messages; reject or strip control characters (including `\n`, `\r`, `\x1b`) from `taskId`, `fileInput`, and path strings before the `LOG_APP_INFO` call. (b) Audit every `m_Context` and `m_SuggestedFix` population site to ensure secret-bearing fields (webhook `secret`, API keys from `m_ParamsJson`) are never embedded verbatim.

---

### MEDIUM Uncontrolled allocation via unbounded `issues` vector growth

- **Location:** `WorkflowValidator::Validate` (both overloads), `ValidateSubWorkflowGraph`, and all `ValidateXxx` helpers throughout the file
- **Issue:** Every validation function appends to the caller-supplied `std::vector<WorkflowValidationIssue>` without any cap. A workflow definition with a large number of tasks (the tasks map is an `unordered_map` with no stated size limit), a large `m_Dataflows` list, or a large `m_ControlflowEdges` list can produce one or more issues per element, each issue containing multiple `std::string` members. There is no maximum-issues guard and no maximum-tasks guard before iteration begins.
- **Impact:** A malicious or malformed workflow submitted to the validator (e.g. through the AI generation pipeline) can cause unbounded heap growth, leading to OOM or extreme latency in the hosting process.
- **Fix:** Add a hard cap (e.g. 500–1000 issues) inside `AddIssue` / `AddIssueEx`: if `issues.size() >= kMaxIssues` return immediately without pushing. Similarly add an early-return guard in `Validate` if `workflow.m_Tasks.size() > kMaxTasks`.

---

### MEDIUM TOCTOU race between `fs::exists` check and subsequent file read/execution

- **Location:** `ValidateTaskParams` ~line 265 (`fs::exists(scriptPath)`); `ValidateShellScriptContent` ~line 495 (`fs::exists(scriptPath)`); `ValidatePythonScriptRegistry` ~line 590 (`fs::exists`); `ReadFirstLines` ~line 420 (`std::ifstream`)
- **Issue:** The pattern check-then-use (`fs::exists` followed later by `ifstream::open` or executor launch) is a classic TOCTOU. Between the existence check and the actual open/exec, the file at the resolved path could be replaced by a symlink pointing elsewhere on the filesystem.
- **Impact:** On a multi-user or container system where the `scripts/` directory is writable by a different process, an attacker could swap a checked script for a malicious one between validation and execution. The validator's blessing of the path would apply to the replaced target.
- **Fix:** For the content-reading path in `ReadFirstLines`, open the file with `O_NOFOLLOW` (POSIX) or equivalent to refuse symlink traversal at open time. For the existence-only checks, document clearly that they are advisory only and cannot be used as a security gate; the executor must perform its own canonical-prefix check at execution time with the file descriptor already open.

---

### MEDIUM `SubWorkflowGraph` DFS is unbounded and recursion-based — stack exhaustion

- **Location:** `WorkflowValidator::ValidateSubWorkflowGraph`, lambda `dfs` ~line 920
- **Issue:** The DFS for sub-workflow graph validation is implemented as a recursive `std::function` lambda. `maxDepth` defaults to 10 and is checked, but only *after* the recursive call is already on the stack (`currentDepth > maxDepth` check is post-increment). More critically, the `dependencyGraph` is caller-supplied with no size limit, and the depth guard applies per-path not per-total-stack-frame. A graph with a very wide fan-out at each level can still drive deep recursion before the depth guard fires. The `DetectCycle` DFS in the anonymous namespace has the same structure.
- **Impact:** A crafted `dependencyGraph` or workflow adjacency structure can cause stack overflow in the validator thread, crashing the host process.
- **Fix:** Convert both DFS implementations to iterative (explicit stack) form. The depth limit should also be enforced as a pre-condition guard (`if (currentDepth >= maxDepth) { emit error; return; }`) rather than post-increment.

---

### LOW `IsInPendingScripts` and `FunctionExistsInPendingScript` perform suffix matching without strict path-separator anchor

- **Location:** `IsInPendingScripts` ~line 540; `FunctionExistsInPendingScript` ~line 560
- **Issue:** The suffix match `gs.path.compare(offset, filePath.size(), filePath) == 0 && (offset == 0 || gs.path[offset - 1] == '/')` is intended to ensure a path-component boundary. However, `filePath` is constructed by `ModuleToFilePath` which converts dots to slashes without validating that the module name is free of path separators or other special characters. A module name like `scripts.../../etc/passwd` would produce `filePath = "scripts/../../etc/passwd.py"`. The suffix match would then anchor against this traversal-containing path in the pending-scripts list.
- **Impact:** An attacker-controlled module name could cause the validator to falsely report a dangerous path as "found in pending scripts", suppressing a warning that would otherwise fire. The actual execution risk is bounded by the `StartsWith(module, "scripts.")` guard that runs before this code.
- **Fix:** Validate `module` with `IsValidId`-equivalent logic on each dot-separated component before calling `ModuleToFilePath`. Reject module paths containing `/`, `\`, or `..` segments.

---

### LOW `ValidateSubWorkflowGraph` emits duplicate cycle/depth errors due to missing early-exit after depth exceeded

- **Location:** `WorkflowValidator::ValidateSubWorkflowGraph` ~line 930, depth-exceeded branch
- **Issue:** When `currentDepth > maxDepth`, the function emits an error and returns, but the caller's loop continues iterating over sibling nodes at the same depth, so multiple depth-exceeded errors are emitted for the same path. More importantly, after emitting the depth error the node is colored `Black`, which means child cycles through that node will not be detected. This is a logic gap rather than a direct security issue, but it means the validator can be silenced: a workflow graph that exceeds max depth will stop cycle-checking for that subtree, potentially missing a cycle error that would otherwise block execution.
- **Impact:** A deeply nested sub-workflow graph that also contains a cycle may receive only a depth warning and no cycle error, potentially allowing a cycle to reach the executor.
- **Fix:** Do not color the node `Black` and return on depth exceeded; instead continue the DFS (or at minimum still check for back-edges) so cycle detection is not skipped.


---

### 104_doc_engine_auxiliary_file_h

<a id="104-doc-engine-auxiliary-file-h"></a>

*Source doc:* `../../../queue/jarvisCppCyberSecAudit/104_doc_engine_auxiliary_file_h/docu.output.md`



### MEDIUM Path Traversal – No Canonicalization Before Use
- **Location:** All path-accepting functions (`FileExists`, `IsDirectory`, `CreateDirectory`, `CopyFile`, `FileSize`, `GetNewestTimestamp`) — throughout both files
- **Issue:** No caller-visible input validation or canonicalization (`std::filesystem::canonical` / `weakly_canonical`) is performed before passing attacker-controlled strings to filesystem operations. A caller supplying `../../etc/passwd`, `../../sensitive.db`, or null-byte–embedded strings (on implementations that truncate at NUL) can reach arbitrary paths outside any intended root.
- **Impact:** If any of these utilities are invoked with values derived from HTTP request parameters, webhook payloads, JSON config, or other external input (consistent with the described engine), an attacker can read, overwrite, or stat arbitrary files reachable by the process UID.
- **Fix:** Accept a `base_directory` root parameter (or enforce one at the call site) and reject any path that, after `std::filesystem::weakly_canonical`, does not have the expected root as a prefix. Example: `if (canonical(p).string().find(allowed_root) != 0) throw ...;`

### MEDIUM TOCTOU Race in `CreateDirectory` (MSVC branch)
- **Location:** `CreateDirectory`, approximately line 195–200 (MSVC `#ifdef` branch)
- **Issue:** `std::filesystem::create_directories(filename)` is called and then `IsDirectory(filename)` is called as a separate, non-atomic check. Between the two calls another process can remove the directory or replace it with a symlink to an unintended location.
- **Impact:** Logic relying on the `true` return value to make security decisions (e.g., "safe to write secrets here") could be deceived. Combined with path traversal, a symlink swap could redirect subsequent writes to an attacker-chosen location.
- **Fix:** Use the error-code overload: `std::error_code ec; std::filesystem::create_directories(filename, ec); return !ec || std::filesystem::is_directory(filename, ec);` and treat the is_directory result as advisory only, not a security gate.

### MEDIUM TOCTOU Race in `GetNewestTimestamp`
- **Location:** `GetNewestTimestamp`, approximately line 230
- **Issue:** `fs::exists(p)` is checked and then `fs::last_write_time(p)` is called as two separate syscalls. A symlink can be swapped between the two calls to point to a different target.
- **Impact:** In a workflow engine that uses timestamps to decide whether to re-execute or trust cached AI responses/secrets, an attacker with local write access could manipulate apparent modification times, causing stale or attacker-supplied data to be used.
- **Fix:** Call `fs::last_write_time(p, ec)` directly with an `std::error_code` and check `ec` instead of pre-checking `exists`. This collapses two calls into one.

### LOW Undefined Behavior on Empty String in `AddSlash`
- **Location:** `AddSlash`, approximately line 210
- **Issue:** `filename.substr(filename.size() - 1)` when `filename` is empty causes `size() - 1` to wrap to `std::string::npos` (unsigned underflow), and `substr` with that offset throws `std::out_of_range` — or, if the check is somehow bypassed, produces undefined behaviour. No guard for the empty-string case exists.
- **Impact:** If an attacker can supply an empty path string (e.g., via a missing JSON field), this crashes the calling thread, enabling denial of service.
- **Fix:** Add `if (filename.empty()) return filename;` as the first line of `AddSlash`.

### LOW Silent Failure Leaks No Error Context but Also Swallows Errors Silently
- **Location:** `CopyFile` (~line 205), `FileSize` (~line 208), `FileExists` (char* overload, ~line 85)
- **Issue:** Stream open failures are silently swallowed and reported as a boolean `false` or a sentinel value (`-1` from `tellg`). No error is surfaced. While this is partly a reliability issue, the security concern is that calling code may interpret a failed open (e.g., due to a permission error distinguishing two files) as "file does not exist" and make incorrect authorization or caching decisions. `FileSize` returns `-1` cast to `std::ifstream::pos_type` (an unsigned type on many platforms), yielding a very large positive number that could trigger uncontrolled allocation if used as a buffer size.
- **Impact:** Callers using `FileSize` return value to pre-allocate a read buffer without range-checking will allocate up to ~18 EB, causing immediate OOM / denial of service. Callers using `FileExists` to gate access may incorrectly conclude a protected file is absent.
- **Fix:** For `FileSize`, check `in.is_open()` before calling `tellg` and return a sentinel or throw on failure. For `CopyFile` and `FileExists`, consider using `std::filesystem::exists` / `std::filesystem::file_size` with `std::error_code` so callers can distinguish "not found" from "permission denied".


---

### 105_doc_engine_auxiliary_threadPool_h

<a id="105-doc-engine-auxiliary-threadpool-h"></a>

*Source doc:* `../../../queue/jarvisCppCyberSecAudit/105_doc_engine_auxiliary_threadPool_h/docu.output.md`

### 106_doc_engine_core_h

<a id="106-doc-engine-core-h"></a>

*Source doc:* `../../../queue/jarvisCppCyberSecAudit/106_doc_engine_core_h/docu.output.md`



### MEDIUM Hardcoded log file path with fixed name and truncation

- **Location:** `Core::Core()`, line ~`m_LogFile->open(filename, ...)` where `filename = "log/log.txt"`
- **Issue:** The log file path is a hardcoded relative path (`"log/log.txt"`) opened with `std::ios::trunc`. Because the path is relative to the CWD at launch time, an attacker who controls the CWD (e.g., via symlink in a writable directory, or a poorly configured service unit) can redirect log writes to an arbitrary file. `trunc` also means each restart silently destroys prior audit evidence.
- **Impact:** Log tampering or overwrite of an attacker-chosen file (limited to files writable by the process UID). Loss of audit trail on restart.
- **Fix:** Resolve the log path to an absolute path under a fixed, permission-restricted directory (e.g., a directory created during installation). Append (`std::ios::app`) rather than truncate, or rotate with a timestamped filename. Validate the resolved canonical path before opening.

---

### MEDIUM Uncontrolled thread-pool size from config — potential DoS

- **Location:** `Core::Start()`, line `m_ThreadPool.Reset(m_EngineConfig.m_MaxThreads + THREADS_REQUIRED_BY_APP)`
- **Issue:** `m_EngineConfig.m_MaxThreads` is taken directly from the parsed config with no visible upper-bound check in this translation unit. If the config file is attacker-influenced or simply misconfigured, an arbitrarily large thread count is passed to `Reset()`, causing unbounded resource allocation.
- **Impact:** Process or host denial of service through thread exhaustion.
- **Fix:** Enforce a hard upper bound (e.g., `std::min(m_EngineConfig.m_MaxThreads, MAX_ALLOWED_THREADS)`) before calling `Reset()`, where `MAX_ALLOWED_THREADS` is a compile-time or deployment-time constant.

---

### MEDIUM `JarvisRedirect` logs raw external C-string to `std::cout` without sanitization

- **Location:** `JarvisRedirect()`, approximately lines 60–68
- **Issue:** This `extern "C"` function accepts a `const char*` from an external caller (described as forwarding Python output) and writes it verbatim to `std::cout`, which is redirected to a terminal manager and a log file. There is no length limit, no control-character stripping, and no encoding validation. A caller (e.g., embedded Python or a native plugin) can inject terminal escape sequences, ANSI control codes, or arbitrarily large strings.
- **Impact:** Terminal escape-sequence injection (cursor movement, color manipulation, screen clear) that can corrupt the ncurses display or mislead operators reading the log. Log injection that falsifies audit entries. If the downstream log file is consumed by a SIEM, log-forging attacks apply.
- **Fix:** Enforce a maximum message length. Strip or escape non-printable and non-ASCII-printable bytes before writing. If ncurses is in use, use `waddstr`-safe paths rather than raw `std::cout`.

---

### MEDIUM `g_Core` global raw pointer — use-after-free / dangling pointer risk

- **Location:** `Core::Core()` sets `g_Core = this`; `Core::CheckSignalFlags()` dereferences `g_Core`
- **Issue:** `g_Core` is a raw `Core*` set to `this` in the constructor and never cleared in `Core::Shutdown()` or the destructor. `CheckSignalFlags()` dereferences `g_Core->RequestQuit()` and `g_Core->PushEvent(...)` on every main-loop iteration. If a `Core` object is destroyed while the main loop (or a signal handler path) is still running — possible if ownership semantics are violated — this is a use-after-free. The signal handler itself calls `_exit` but the non-async-signal-safe `CheckSignalFlags()` is polled from the run loop and dereferences the global.
- **Impact:** Use-after-free leading to undefined behavior, potential for memory corruption or exploitable state.
- **Fix:** Clear `g_Core = nullptr` at the top of `Core::Shutdown()` (or destructor) under a memory fence. Add a null-check in `CheckSignalFlags()` before dereferencing. Consider an `std::atomic<Core*>` for the global.

---

### LOW Sleep duration taken directly from config without runtime bounds enforcement

- **Location:** `Core::Run()`, `std::this_thread::sleep_for(std::chrono::milliseconds(m_EngineConfig.m_SleepDuration))`
- **Issue:** The sleep duration is guarded only by a `CORE_ASSERT`, which is typically compiled out in release builds. In a release build a config-supplied value of 0 ms or a very large value (INT64_MAX ms) passes unchecked into `sleep_for`, causing either a busy-spin (CPU DoS) or an indefinitely blocked main loop.
- **Impact:** CPU exhaustion or complete main-loop stall, depending on the supplied value.
- **Fix:** Replace the assert with a hard runtime clamp (e.g., `std::clamp(duration, 1ms, 256ms)`) that applies in all build configurations.

---

### LOW `std::cerr` redirected to `TerminalLogStreamBuf` but restored to the original `std::cout` buffer, not the original `std::cerr` buffer

- **Location:** `Core::Core()` ~line `std::cerr.rdbuf(m_TerminalBuf.get())` and `Core::~Core()` / `Core::Shutdown()` restoring with `m_OriginalCoutBuffer`
- **Issue:** `m_OriginalCoutBuffer` is captured from `std::cout.rdbuf()` only. On destruction/shutdown, both `std::cout` and `std::cerr` are restored to `m_OriginalCoutBuffer`. If `std::cerr` originally had a different buffer (which is the default on most implementations), `std::cerr` ends up pointing to the wrong streambuf for the lifetime of the process after shutdown.
- **Impact:** Post-shutdown error output (e.g., from destructors of other objects, or OS-level crash reporting) is silently misdirected or lost, potentially hiding security-relevant crash diagnostics.
- **Fix:** Save `std::cerr.rdbuf()` separately (`m_OriginalCerrBuffer`) and restore it independently from `m_OriginalCoutBuffer`.


---

### 107_doc_engine_curlWrapper_authSigner_h

<a id="107-doc-engine-curlwrapper-authsigner-h"></a>

*Source doc:* `../../../queue/jarvisCppCyberSecAudit/107_doc_engine_curlWrapper_authSigner_h/docu.output.md`



### MEDIUM HTTP header injection via unsanitized API key

- **Location:** `BearerSigner::Apply`, `XGoogApiKeySigner::Apply`, `AnthropicXApiKeySigner::Apply`, `AzureApiKeySigner::Apply` — all `Apply` overrides in `authSigner.cpp`
- **Issue:** `q.m_ApiKey` is concatenated directly into HTTP header strings without any validation or sanitization. If `m_ApiKey` contains CR (`\r`) or LF (`\n`) characters, the resulting string injected into the curl slist will split into additional attacker-controlled headers. For example, a key value of `foo\r\nX-Evil: bar` would produce two headers: the intended auth header and a spurious `X-Evil: bar`.
- **Impact:** An attacker who controls the API key value (e.g., via a config file, environment variable, or a compromised upstream source) can inject arbitrary HTTP headers into outbound requests. This could be used to smuggle headers to the upstream AI provider, override existing headers (e.g., `Content-Type`, `Host`), or poison logs on the receiving end.
- **Fix:** Before building the header string, validate that `m_ApiKey` contains no `\r` or `\n` characters and does not exceed a reasonable maximum length (e.g., 512 bytes). Reject or sanitize the key at the point where `QueryData` is constructed rather than at each signer. Example inline check:

    if (q.m_ApiKey.find_first_of("\r\n") != std::string::npos)
        throw std::invalid_argument("API key contains illegal characters");

---

### LOW Unenumerated `AuthStyle` silently falls back to Bearer authentication

- **Location:** `IAuthSigner::Get`, `authSigner.cpp`, the `switch` statement's implicit default
- **Issue:** The `switch` over `CurlWrapper::AuthStyle` has no `default` case. If a new `AuthStyle` value is added to the enum without a corresponding case, or if memory corruption produces an out-of-range value, the function silently returns `s_Bearer`, sending a Bearer `Authorization` header with whatever key is in `q.m_ApiKey`. This is a silent mis-authentication rather than a hard failure.
- **Impact:** A request intended for a provider that requires a different auth scheme (e.g., SigV4) could instead emit a Bearer token to an unintended endpoint, potentially exposing the key or allowing an unauthorized request to succeed against the wrong signer.
- **Fix:** Add an explicit `default` case that throws or asserts:

    default:
        throw std::invalid_argument("Unknown AuthStyle; cannot sign request");

---

### LOW API key value exposed in header strings held in `outHeaders`

- **Location:** All `Apply` overrides; the `outHeaders` parameter passed by reference
- **Issue:** The complete header string, including the raw secret key, is stored as a plain `std::string` in a `std::vector<std::string>`. If that vector is logged, serialized to an error report, copied into a crash dump, or passed to code that traces curl headers (`CURLOPT_VERBOSE`, `CURLOPT_DEBUGFUNCTION`), the secret is leaked in plaintext.
- **Impact:** Secret exposure in logs, crash dumps, or debug output; facilitates credential theft without directly compromising the key store.
- **Fix:** Ensure no call site enables `CURLOPT_VERBOSE` or a debug callback that prints the slist in production builds. Consider a thin wrapper type for secret-bearing header strings that overrides any implicit-conversion-to-string or stream operator to redact the value portion, and ensure destructors zero the memory (`explicit_bzero` / `SecureZeroMemory` before deallocation).


---

### 108_doc_engine_curlWrapper_awsSigV4_h

<a id="108-doc-engine-curlwrapper-awssigv4-h"></a>

*Source doc:* `../../../queue/jarvisCppCyberSecAudit/108_doc_engine_curlWrapper_awsSigV4_h/docu.output.md`



### HIGH: HTTP header injection via unsanitized host, date, token, and path values

- **Location:** `SigV4Signer::Apply` and `SigV4Signer::Sign`, approximately the `outHeaders.push_back(...)` block near the end of `Apply`, and the `UriEncode`/`ParseUrl` calls in `Sign`
- **Issue:** Every value sourced from `q.m_Url`, `q.m_ApiKey`, `q.m_Params` (region, service, session_token, secret_access_key) is used without stripping CR (`\r`) or LF (`\n`) characters before being concatenated into HTTP header strings and pushed into `outHeaders`. For example, `out.m_Host` is derived directly from `ParseUrl(in.m_Url)` which does simple `substr` operations with no newline filtering. If any of those fields contain `\r\n`, the caller's HTTP library will interpret the injected bytes as additional headers or a body separator.
- **Impact:** An attacker who controls any of the URL, access key, region, service, or session token inputs can inject arbitrary HTTP headers into the outgoing request (e.g., overwrite `Authorization`, add `X-Forwarded-For`, split the response, or poison a shared connection).
- **Fix:** Before any value is concatenated into a header string, strip or percent-encode all CR and LF characters. A minimal inline helper — `for (char& c : s) if (c == '\r' || c == '\n') c = '_';` — applied to `out.m_Host`, `out.m_AmzDate`, `out.m_ContentSha256`, `out.m_Authorization`, and `out.m_SecurityToken` immediately before the `push_back` calls in `Apply` is sufficient. Apply the same sanitisation inside `Sign` before any field is embedded into `canonicalHeaders` or `credentialScope`.

---

### HIGH: Secret key and session token logged in plaintext on configuration error

- **Location:** `SigV4Signer::Apply`, the `LOG_CORE_ERROR` call when credentials are missing, and any future expansion of that branch; also the self-test in `RunSelfTest` which embeds a literal secret key in source.
- **Issue:** The current error log does not print the secret, but `Apply` passes `in.m_SecretKey` and `in.m_SessionToken` into `Sign` after only a presence check. If `LOG_CORE_ERROR` or any debug-build trace macro is ever added around the `Inputs` struct (common during development), the 40-character secret access key and the full STS session token will appear in application logs. More concretely, `RunSelfTest` hard-codes the canonical AWS documentation secret `wJalrXUtnFEMI/K7MDENG+bPxRfiCYEXAMPLEKEY` in a log error message path (`LOG_CORE_ERROR("SigV4 self-test (2) signing-key derivation: got '{}', expected '{}'", got, kExpected)`) alongside variables derived directly from that key. While the test key is public, the pattern establishes a template that production callers can inadvertently replicate with real keys.
- **Impact:** Secret access keys or session tokens written to application logs give an attacker who can read logs full AWS API access under the signed identity.
- **Fix:** Never log any field of `Inputs` that contains credential material. In `Apply`, log only which field is absent (key ID, region, etc.), never the value. In `RunSelfTest`, restrict credential-containing log output to the derived hex, not the input secret. Mark `m_SecretKey` and `m_SessionToken` in `Inputs` with a comment prohibiting logging, and enforce this via a wrapper type (e.g., `SecretString`) whose `operator<<` and format adapters are deleted.

---

### MEDIUM: `HmacSha256` silently produces a zero-length result on OpenSSL error

- **Location:** `HmacSha256`, anonymous namespace, approximately lines 85–93
- **Issue:** The return value of `HMAC(...)` is not checked. If `HMAC` fails (returns `NULL` in OpenSSL 1.x), `len` remains 0, `out.resize(0)` is called, and an empty vector is returned. Downstream, `HmacSha256` is called four times in the key-derivation chain; each call passes the previous result as the key. An empty key to `HMAC` is a valid call that HMAC will process — but it no longer represents the AWS4 signing key. The final signature will be incorrect yet the code proceeds without error, producing a silently wrong `Authorization` header.
- **Impact:** In a fault-injection scenario (or with a misconfigured OpenSSL FIPS provider), every request gets an identically wrong signature derived from a zero-length key, potentially making signatures predictable or trivially forgeable if an attacker can induce the failure condition.
- **Fix:** Check the return value of `HMAC`: `if (!HMAC(...)) { /* throw or return error */ }`. Prefer the EVP HMAC API (`EVP_MAC`) available in OpenSSL 3.x, which has cleaner error propagation. At minimum: `if (HMAC(EVP_sha256(), key.data(), static_cast<int>(key.size()), ..., out.data(), &len) == nullptr) throw std::runtime_error("HMAC failed");`.

---

### MEDIUM: `dateStamp` silently truncated from a potentially short `m_AmzDate`

- **Location:** `SigV4Signer::Sign`, line computing `dateStamp = out.m_AmzDate.substr(0, 8)`
- **Issue:** `std::string::substr` with a `len` argument does not throw if the string is shorter than 8 characters; it returns whatever is available. If `in.m_AmzDate` is supplied by a caller (test seam or `Apply`) as a string shorter than 8 bytes, `dateStamp` will be truncated. This truncated value is used both in the credential scope embedded in the `Authorization` header and as the HMAC-SHA256 input for the signing-key derivation, producing a structurally valid but semantically incorrect signature without any error being raised.
- **Impact:** Requests signed with a malformed date are rejected by AWS (low operational impact), but the silent failure means the engine has no way to distinguish a configuration bug from an attack. A caller that can inject a short `m_AmzDate` can force signing-key derivation to use a predictable short date prefix.
- **Fix:** Validate that `out.m_AmzDate` matches the pattern `[0-9]{8}T[0-9]{6}Z` (15 characters) before proceeding. Throw or return an error state if it does not.

---

### MEDIUM: SSRF — URL passed to `ParseUrl` and then into the `Host` header without scheme or hostname validation

- **Location:** `SigV4Signer::Apply` → `SigV4Signer::Sign` → `ParseUrl`, then `outHeaders.push_back("Host: " + out.m_Host)`
- **Issue:** `ParseUrl` accepts any string as a URL and extracts a host with no validation that the scheme is `https`, that the host is a legitimate AWS endpoint, or that the host does not contain port numbers pointing to internal services. The extracted host is directly placed into the `Host` HTTP header. Because `Apply` receives the URL from `q.m_Url` (external query data), an attacker who controls that field can direct signed requests to any host reachable from the engine's network.
- **Impact:** Internal service enumeration or exfiltration of AWS credentials to an attacker-controlled server via SSRF. The signed `Authorization` header (containing the access key ID and a valid signature) is sent to the attacker's host.
- **Fix:** Enforce an allowlist of permitted host suffixes (e.g., `*.amazonaws.com`) and require the `https` scheme before `Sign` is called. Reject any URL that does not match. Perform this check in `Apply` before constructing `Inputs`.

---

### LOW: `FormatAmzDateNow` uses `std::time` / `gmtime_r` without monotonic guarantees; clock skew not detected

- **Location:** `FormatAmzDateNow`, anonymous namespace
- **Issue:** `std::time(nullptr)` returns wall-clock time. If the system clock is skewed by more than ±5 minutes relative to AWS servers, every request will be rejected with a `SignatureDoesNotMatch` or `RequestExpired` error. No skew detection or warning is issued. This is a reliability gap, but it also means an attacker who can manipulate the system clock (e.g., via NTP poisoning) can force all Bedrock requests to fail silently, creating a denial-of-service condition.
- **Impact:** Availability denial through NTP-based clock manipulation; no operator alerting.
- **Fix:** Compare the generated timestamp against a previously cached value and log a warning if the delta exceeds a threshold (e.g., 60 seconds). Consider fetching the authoritative time from an AWS endpoint (`HEAD` response `Date` header) during initialization and using the offset.


---

<a id="109-doc-engine-curlwrapper-curlmanager-h"></a>
