### 10_doc_application_cloud_azureSharedKeySigner_h

<a id="10-doc-application-cloud-azuresharedkeysigner-h"></a>

*Source doc:* `../../../queue/jarvisCppCyberSecAudit/10_doc_application_cloud_azureSharedKeySigner_h/docu.output.md`



### HIGH HMAC-SHA256 return value not checked — silent signing failures
- **Location:** `AzureSharedKeySigner::HmacSha256`, approximately line 80
- **Issue:** The return value of `HMAC()` is not checked. If `HMAC()` returns `NULL` (e.g., due to an engine or algorithm failure), `resultLen` remains 0 and the function returns an empty string, which then becomes the signature. The caller in `Sign()` has no way to distinguish a zero-length key-decode failure from a real HMAC result.
- **Impact:** A configuration or OpenSSL failure silently produces a blank signature, meaning the Authorization header is well-formed but cryptographically meaningless. Depending on the server-side behaviour this could allow unauthenticated requests to appear to pass client-side validation, or it degrades to a predictable, fixed signature that an attacker who knows the failure condition can replay.
- **Fix:** Check the return value: `if (HMAC(...) == nullptr) throw std::runtime_error("HMAC failed");` and propagate the error through `Sign()` so callers cannot use an invalid `SignedRequest`.

---

### HIGH Caller-controlled header values injected into signed string without sanitisation (header-value injection in StringToSign)
- **Location:** `AzureSharedKeySigner::CanonicalizedHeaders` (~line 180) and `AzureSharedKeySigner::Sign` (~line 270)
- **Issue:** Values from `extraHeaders` are concatenated directly into the canonicalized-headers string as `key + ":" + value + "\n"`. A header value containing a newline (`\n`) allows a caller to inject additional lines into the StringToSign, breaking the canonical structure and potentially forging signatures over attacker-chosen content. The same applies to `accountName` and URL components in `CanonicalizedResource`.
- **Impact:** A caller who can supply crafted header values can cause the signed string to represent a different request than intended, enabling signature confusion or bypassing integrity guarantees that downstream consumers rely on.
- **Fix:** Before including any header value in the canonicalized string, fold embedded newlines per the Azure spec (replace `\r\n` and `\n` with a single space), and reject or strip any remaining control characters. Validate `accountName` against `[a-z0-9]{3,24}`.

---

### HIGH `Base64Decode` silently returns empty string on decode failure; key material lost
- **Location:** `AzureSharedKeySigner::Base64Decode`, approximately line 110
- **Issue:** When `BIO_read` returns a negative value (malformed input, wrong padding, etc.), the function returns an empty `std::string`. The caller in `Sign()` proceeds to use the empty string as the HMAC key with no error check. A zero-length key is valid to OpenSSL's `HMAC()` but is not the intended key, so every signing call with a bad `accountKey` silently uses a null key.
- **Impact:** If `accountKey` is accidentally or maliciously malformed, all signed requests use a known, deterministic null key, enabling an attacker who knows the failure condition to forge signatures.
- **Fix:** Throw or return an error indicator when `decodedLen < 0` or `decodedLen == 0`; never proceed to `HmacSha256` with an empty key.

---

### MEDIUM `BIO_get_mem_ptr` return value not checked in `Base64Encode`
- **Location:** `AzureSharedKeySigner::Base64Encode`, approximately line 95
- **Issue:** `BIO_get_mem_ptr(b64, &bufPtr)` is called without checking its return value, and `bufPtr` is dereferenced immediately. If the call fails (returns 0), `bufPtr` remains `nullptr` and `bufPtr->data` / `bufPtr->length` are undefined-behaviour null-pointer dereferences, crashing the process.
- **Impact:** An attacker who can cause memory pressure or an OpenSSL internal failure triggers a denial-of-service crash.
- **Fix:** Check `BIO_get_mem_ptr` return value and that `bufPtr != nullptr` before dereferencing; throw on failure.

---

### MEDIUM `BIO_write` and `BIO_flush` return values not checked in `Base64Encode`
- **Location:** `AzureSharedKeySigner::Base64Encode`, approximately lines 91–93
- **Issue:** `BIO_write` can return -1 or 0 on failure (e.g., memory allocation failure); the result is ignored with `(void)BIO_flush(b64)` also unchecked. A partial write produces truncated Base64 output that silently forms an incorrect signature.
- **Impact:** Silent truncation of the encoded signature; authenticated requests may fail unpredictably, or a predictable truncated value may be accepted by a lenient server.
- **Fix:** Check return values of both calls and throw on non-positive results.

---

### MEDIUM `ParseUrl` does not validate or reject non-HTTPS schemes
- **Location:** `AzureSharedKeySigner::ParseUrl`, approximately line 135
- **Issue:** The parser accepts any scheme or no scheme at all (falls back to `start = 0`). A caller passing an `http://` URL will have its Authorization header (containing the HMAC signature derived from the account key) transmitted in cleartext.
- **Impact:** Network-adjacent attacker captures the Authorization header and the signed StringToSign, enabling offline brute-force or direct replay of the captured credential against Azure Blob Storage.
- **Fix:** In `Sign()`, validate that the URL begins with `https://` (case-insensitive) and return an error or throw if it does not.

---

### MEDIUM Query parameter values are not URL-decoded before canonicalisation
- **Location:** `AzureSharedKeySigner::CanonicalizedResource`, approximately line 210
- **Issue:** Query values extracted from the URL are stored and appended verbatim (e.g., `restype=container` stays as-is, but percent-encoded values like `%2F` are not decoded). The Azure Shared Key spec requires that query parameter values be URL-decoded before inclusion in the canonicalized resource string. An incorrect StringToSign produces signatures that Azure will reject, but more critically, an attacker supplying a crafted URL with encoded characters could manipulate which resource string is signed.
- **Impact:** Signature mismatch denial-of-service for encoded URLs; potential resource confusion if a caller constructs URLs assuming proper decoding.
- **Fix:** Percent-decode query parameter values before inserting them into `params`.

---

### LOW `contentLength` cast to `int` risk in `BIO_write` and `BIO_read`
- **Location:** `Base64Encode` (~line 91) and `Base64Decode` (~line 113)
- **Issue:** `data.size()` and `encoded.size()` are cast to `int` for `BIO_write` and `BIO_read`. On 64-bit platforms `std::string::size_type` is 64-bit; if the string exceeds `INT_MAX` bytes the cast wraps to a negative value, causing `BIO_write` to fail or write fewer bytes than expected.
- **Impact:** With attacker-controlled large inputs, this is a silent truncation or write failure, leading to incorrect signatures (see above).
- **Fix:** Before casting, assert or check that `data.size() <= INT_MAX`; throw `std::length_error` otherwise.


---

### 110_doc_engine_curlWrapper_curlMultiDispatcher_h

<a id="110-doc-engine-curlwrapper-curlmultidispatcher-h"></a>

*Source doc:* `../../../queue/jarvisCppCyberSecAudit/110_doc_engine_curlWrapper_curlMultiDispatcher_h/docu.output.md`



### HIGH TLS Certificate Verification Not Explicitly Enforced

- **Location:** `SetupEasyHandle`, approximately where `CURLOPT_CAINFO` is set
- **Issue:** The code sets `CURLOPT_CAINFO` only when `CurlWrapper::GetCaBundlePath()` returns a non-empty string. It never explicitly sets `CURLOPT_SSL_VERIFYPEER` or `CURLOPT_SSL_VERIFYHOST`. libcurl's defaults are `CURLOPT_SSL_VERIFYPEER=1` and `CURLOPT_SSL_VERIFYHOST=2`, but those defaults can be overridden globally (e.g., via `CURLOPT_SSL_VERIFYPEER=0` set elsewhere in the codebase or by a libcurl build that ships with different defaults). If `GetCaBundlePath()` returns empty and no system CA store is available, libcurl may still proceed without verification depending on platform and build configuration. The absence of an explicit affirmative opt-in means any future change that sets these options to 0 elsewhere (e.g., in `CurlWrapper::Query` for debugging) will silently affect this path too.
- **Impact:** Man-in-the-middle attacker on the network path to any AI provider can intercept API keys in Authorization headers and response bodies. All AI provider secrets are exposed.
- **Fix:** Unconditionally add the following immediately after `curl_easy_init()` in `SetupEasyHandle`:
  ```
  curl_easy_setopt(easy, CURLOPT_SSL_VERIFYPEER, 1L);
  curl_easy_setopt(easy, CURLOPT_SSL_VERIFYHOST, 2L);
  ```
  Do not make verification conditional on the CA bundle path. If no custom bundle is provided, let libcurl use the system store; peer verification must always be on.

---

### HIGH Uncontrolled Retry Queue Growth / DoS via Inbox Flooding

- **Location:** `DrainInbox` (throttle path, ~line where `m_Inbox.push` is called in the throttle branch); `DrainCompleted` (429 and transient retry insertion)
- **Issue:** When the throttle gate fires, the entire remaining local queue is pushed back into `m_Inbox` with no size cap. `m_Inbox` is a `std::queue<PendingRequest>` with no upper bound. Similarly, `DrainCompleted` pushes `RetryEntry` objects into `m_RetryQueue` (also unbounded) on every 429 or transient error. `Submit()` on the producer side also has no bound check before pushing to `m_Inbox`. With `kDefaultMaxRetries429 = 10` and exponential backoff up to `baseRetryMs * 512`, a large burst of callers can grow `m_RetryQueue` and `m_Inbox` without bound.
- **Impact:** A caller (or a misconfigured workflow generating many parallel AI requests) can exhaust process heap memory, causing OOM termination or degraded availability. This is also reachable if an AI provider returns sustained 429s or 503s, which are attacker-controllable in a supply-chain or provider-compromise scenario.
- **Fix:** Enforce a hard maximum on `m_Inbox.size()` inside `Submit()` before the push (e.g., reject with `QueryResult::Fail` when size exceeds a configurable limit such as 4096). Similarly cap `m_RetryQueue.size()` before pushing in `DrainCompleted`. Log and return `QueryResult::Fail(QueryErrorCode::..., "queue full")` to the callback immediately when the cap is exceeded.

---

### HIGH Double Lock of `m_DebugMutex` in `DrainCompleted` Causes Undefined Behavior

- **Location:** `DrainCompleted`, in the 429-retry branch and the transient-error branch, approximately the nested `std::lock_guard<std::recursive_mutex> lock(m_DebugMutex)` inside the block that already holds `debugLock`
- **Issue:** `DrainCompleted` acquires `m_DebugMutex` at the top of the `while` loop via `debugLock`. Inside the 429 and transient-error retry branches, the code then acquires `m_DebugMutex` again with a second `std::lock_guard<std::recursive_mutex>` named `lock`. While `std::recursive_mutex` permits recursive locking from the same thread and thus does not deadlock, the inner lock acquisition is inside a `continue` path that then erases from `m_Active` while both guards are held. The outer `debugLock` is still alive when `m_Active.erase(it)` is called; after the `continue`, the outer loop iteration ends and `debugLock` is still in scope. This means `it` (an iterator into `m_Active`) has already been erased under the inner lock, and the outer loop's `msg` loop iteration continues with `easy` having been cleaned up. Specifically: after `curl_easy_cleanup(easy)` and `m_Active.erase(it)`, execution hits `continue` and the `while ((msg = curl_multi_info_read(...)))` fetches the next message — safe. However the inner lock is redundant and the pattern is fragile; the real issue is that `curl_easy_cleanup` is called while `debugLock` is already held and then the outer scope accesses the (now-erased) `it` via `req` — `req` is a reference to `*it->second` which has been destroyed. The reference `req` is dangling after `m_Active.erase(it)` within the inner block, but `req.m_Headers` is read before the erase, so it is safe there. The structural issue is that every field of `req` used after the inner lock block (none, due to `continue`) is fine today but the pattern is a latent use-after-free waiting for a refactor.
- **Impact:** Currently latent; a future code change adding any access to `req` after the `continue` will be a use-after-free on the I/O thread, potentially enabling memory corruption exploitable for code execution.
- **Fix:** Remove the inner redundant `std::lock_guard<std::recursive_mutex> lock(m_DebugMutex)` in both retry branches. The outer `debugLock` already covers the entire `while` iteration. This also makes the locking intent clear.

---

### MEDIUM Response Body and Header Buffers Grow Without Bound

- **Location:** `MultiWriteCallback` and `MultiHeaderCallback` (static functions at top of .cpp); `ActiveRequest::m_ReadBuffer` and `m_HeaderBuffer`
- **Issue:** Both write callbacks unconditionally append all data from libcurl into the associated `std::string` with no size check. A malicious or malfunctioning AI provider endpoint can stream an arbitrarily large response body or inject arbitrarily large headers, causing the `std::string` to grow until the process runs out of memory.
- **Impact:** OOM process termination (DoS). If the AI provider endpoint is ever pointed at an attacker-controlled URL (e.g., via SSRF through a user-supplied URL in `QueryData::m_Url`), the attacker can trigger this directly. Even without SSRF, a compromised or rogue provider can cause DoS.
- **Fix:** Add a size guard in both callbacks. Example for `MultiWriteCallback`:
  ```cpp
  constexpr size_t kMaxResponseBytes = 32 * 1024 * 1024; // 32 MB
  auto* buf = static_cast<std::string*>(userp);
  if (buf->size() + totalSize > kMaxResponseBytes) return 0; // causes CURLE_WRITE_ERROR
  buf->append(static_cast<char*>(contents), totalSize);
  return totalSize;
  ```
  Choose a limit appropriate for the largest expected AI response (token budgets make this predictable).

---

### MEDIUM SSRF via Unsanitized `QueryData::m_Url`

- **Location:** `SetupEasyHandle`, `curl_easy_setopt(easy, CURLOPT_URL, req.m_Url.c_str())`; `DrainInbox` where `req->m_Url = pending.m_QueryData.m_Url`
- **Issue:** The URL passed to libcurl is taken directly from `QueryData::m_Url` with no validation that it refers to an expected AI provider host. `ExtractHost` is used only for rate-limit tracking, not for allowlist enforcement. If any code path allows user-influenced data to reach `QueryData::m_Url` (e.g., a workflow definition that specifies an API endpoint, or an admin API that registers a custom interface), an attacker can direct requests to internal services (`http://169.254.169.254/`, `http://localhost:6379/`, etc.).
- **Impact:** Server-Side Request Forgery: access to cloud instance metadata, internal databases, and other services not exposed externally. May also be used to exfiltrate API keys by sending them to an attacker-controlled host (circumventing TLS verification if it is also weakened as noted above).
- **Fix:** In `SetupEasyHandle` or `Submit`, validate `req.m_Url` against an allowlist of known AI provider hostnames (extracted via `ExtractHost`). Reject any URL whose host is not in the allowlist. Also restrict schemes to `https` only.

---

### MEDIUM API Keys / Auth Headers Potentially Leaked in Logs on Curl Error

- **Location:** `DrainCompleted`, `LOG_CORE_ERROR("curl error (code {}): {}", curlCode, errMsg)` and `LOG_CORE_ERROR("HTTP error {} for query {}", httpCode, qnum)`; `SetupEasyHandle` where `authHeaders` are built
- **Issue:** When `curl_easy_strerror` returns an error string, it is logged directly. More critically, if the logging framework elsewhere logs the full request URL or headers on error (dependent on `LOG_CORE_ERROR` implementation and any verbose curl debug callback that might be enabled), the Authorization header containing the API key would appear in log files. While this file does not enable `CURLOPT_VERBOSE` or `CURLOPT_DEBUGFUNCTION`, there is no explicit suppression either, and the auth headers are assembled in `SetupEasyHandle` without scrubbing before any potential diagnostic output.
- **Impact:** API keys logged to disk, log aggregation systems, or error monitoring services become available to anyone with log access, enabling unauthorized use of AI provider accounts.
- **Fix:** Never log `req.m_HeaderBuffer` (which contains raw response headers that may echo request context) or `authHeaders` content. Confirm `CURLOPT_VERBOSE` is never set to 1 in any code path that shares these handles. Add a note/assertion in `SetupEasyHandle` that `CURLOPT_DEBUGFUNCTION` must not be set to a logger.

---

### MEDIUM Exponential Backoff Integer Overflow

- **Location:** `DrainCompleted`, `int delayMs = baseRetryMs * (1 << (retryCount - 1))` in both the 429-retry branch and the transient-retry branch
- **Issue:** `retryCount` can reach `kDefaultMaxRetries429 = 10`. At `retryCount = 10`, the shift is `1 << 9 = 512`. With `kDefaultBaseRetryMs = 1000`, `delayMs = 1000 * 512 = 512000`, which fits in a 32-bit int. However, if `m_QueryData.m_BaseRetryMs` is attacker-influenced (e.g., from a config file or API parameter) and set to a large value, or if `m_MaxRetries429` is set high, the multiplication overflows signed `int`, producing undefined behavior and a potentially negative or zero delay, which could cause immediate retry storms.
- **Impact:** If config values are attacker-influenced, retry storms causing rapid token exhaustion against AI providers, or undefined behavior in the delay calculation.
- **Fix:** Cap `baseRetryMs` and `maxRetries429` to safe ranges on ingestion into `QueryData`. In the delay calculation, use `int64_t` arithmetic and clamp the result: `int64_t delayMs = std::min((int64_t)baseRetryMs * (1LL << (retryCount - 1)), (int64_t)120000);`.

---

### LOW `m_Stopping` Check Not Holding Lock When Accessing `m_Active` in `IoThreadFunc` Shutdown Path

- **Location:** `IoThreadFunc`, the `if (stopping)` branch that iterates `m_RetryQueue` without acquiring `m_DebugMutex`
- **Issue:** When `stopping` is true, `IoThreadFunc` iterates `m_RetryQueue` and calls callbacks without holding `m_DebugMutex`. `GetDebugSnapshot()` on another thread can concurrently read `m_RetryQueue` under `m_DebugMutex`. This is a data race on `m_RetryQueue` between the I/O thread (writing, no lock) and a debug snapshot reader (reading, with lock).
- **Impact:** Data race is undefined behavior; in practice, a snapshot read during shutdown could observe a partially modified vector, causing a crash or corrupted snapshot response on the `/api/debug/signals` endpoint.
- **Fix:** Acquire `m_DebugMutex` before iterating and clearing `m_RetryQueue` in the shutdown branch of `IoThreadFunc`, consistent with all other accesses to that container.

---

### LOW `QueryResult::Fail` Called with Raw `CURLE_ABORTED_BY_CALLBACK` Cast as `int` Leaks Internal Code to Callers

- **Location:** `DrainPendingCancellations`, `IoThreadFunc` shutdown path, multiple call sites with `static_cast<int>(CURLE_ABORTED_BY_CALLBACK)`
- **Issue:** The raw libcurl error code integer is passed directly to `QueryResult::Fail` and propagated to callbacks, which may surface it in API responses or logs visible to end users. This leaks implementation details (the use of libcurl) to external consumers.
- **Impact:** Low direct security impact; facilitates fingerprinting of the server-side HTTP client library and version.
- **Fix:** Map internal error codes to an application-defined `QueryErrorCode` enum before calling `QueryResult::Fail`. Use a dedicated `QueryErrorCode::Cancelled` or `QueryErrorCode::Shutdown` value rather than the raw `CURLcode`.


---

### 111_doc_engine_curlWrapper_curlWrapper_h

<a id="111-doc-engine-curlwrapper-curlwrapper-h"></a>

*Source doc:* `../../../queue/jarvisCppCyberSecAudit/111_doc_engine_curlWrapper_curlWrapper_h/docu.output.md`



### HIGH: Unbounded response buffer growth — uncontrolled allocation / DoS

- **Location:** `CurlWrapper::Query`, write_callback lambda, approximately line 290–295
- **Issue:** The `write_callback` appends every byte libcurl delivers directly into `m_ReadBuffer` with no size check. An adversary-controlled server (or a compromised/SSRF-reachable endpoint) can stream an arbitrarily large response body, causing unbounded heap growth until the process is OOM-killed or memory is exhausted.
- **Impact:** Denial of service; if the process handles multiple tenants, one request can starve or crash the entire engine.
- **Fix:** Enforce a maximum response size. Inside `write_callback`, check `buffer->size() + totalSize > MAX_RESPONSE_BYTES` and return `0` (or a value != `totalSize`) to abort the transfer if the limit is exceeded. Choose a sensible cap (e.g., 32 MiB) configurable per provider.

---

### HIGH: Missing CURLOPT_POSTFIELDSIZE — truncation on embedded NUL bytes and potential read past intended boundary

- **Location:** `CurlWrapper::Query`, ~line 285
- **Issue:** `CURLOPT_POSTFIELDS` is set with `data.c_str()`, but `CURLOPT_POSTFIELDSIZE` (or `CURLOPT_POSTFIELDSIZE_LARGE`) is never set. libcurl therefore calls `strlen()` on the pointer to determine the body length. If `queryData.m_Data` contains embedded NUL bytes (possible with binary-encoded JSON or crafted payloads), the body is silently truncated at the first NUL. Additionally, if the `std::string` data happens to be constructed from a source that places data after the logical end, the miscount could expose unintended bytes.
- **Impact:** Request body manipulation; silent data truncation that bypasses content-level validation; minor information leakage of adjacent buffer content in edge cases.
- **Fix:** Always set `curl_easy_setopt(m_Curl, CURLOPT_POSTFIELDSIZE_LARGE, (curl_off_t)data.size())` immediately after `CURLOPT_POSTFIELDS`.

---

### HIGH: No timeout enforced by default — DoS via resource exhaustion

- **Location:** `CurlWrapper::Query`, ~line 300
- **Issue:** When `queryData.m_TimeoutMs` is 0 (the default), the code explicitly sets `CURLOPT_TIMEOUT_MS` to `0L`, meaning libcurl will wait forever. A slow or unresponsive upstream AI endpoint permanently blocks the worker thread. Because `m_TimeoutMs{0}` is the zero-value default in `QueryData`, callers that forget to set it get an indefinite hang.
- **Impact:** A single unresponsive endpoint can permanently consume a worker thread; with enough concurrent requests the thread pool is exhausted, causing a full DoS of the workflow engine.
- **Fix:** Replace the default of `0` with a hard upper-bound (e.g., 120 000 ms). Add a compile-time or runtime assertion that `m_TimeoutMs == 0` is not passed through silently; treat 0 as "use default cap" rather than "no timeout".

---

### HIGH: CA bundle path taken from environment without canonicalization — path traversal / TLS downgrade

- **Location:** `CurlWrapper::GetCaBundlePath`, ~line 185–200
- **Issue:** `CURL_CA_BUNDLE` and `SSL_CERT_FILE` environment variables are accepted and used verbatim as the CA bundle path after only an existence check via `std::filesystem::exists`. A malicious environment (e.g., set before process start, or modified by a compromised subprocess) can point to an attacker-controlled file containing a rogue CA certificate. The existence check is also subject to TOCTOU: the file can be swapped between `exists()` and libcurl's actual `open()`.
- **Impact:** Full TLS certificate validation bypass; all HTTPS connections trust attacker-supplied CAs, enabling MitM attacks that steal API keys and response data.
- **Fix:** Either ignore these environment variables entirely and rely only on the hardcoded candidate list, or validate that the resolved canonical path is under a trusted prefix (e.g., `/etc/ssl/`) using `std::filesystem::canonical()` before accepting it.

---

### MEDIUM: API key presence logged at CRITICAL level on empty-key path — operational secret leakage risk

- **Location:** `CurlWrapper::QueryData::IsValid`, ~line 240; `CurlWrapper::Query`, ~line 265
- **Issue:** The log message "CurlWrapper::QueryData::IsValid(): API key empty" and "CurlWrapper::Query: API key empty" confirm the absence of a key at CRITICAL/ERROR level. While this particular message does not print the key itself, the verbose mode block at ~line 310 logs both `url` and `data` unconditionally: `LOG_CORE_INFO("url: {}, data: {}", url, data)`. The `data` field is the full JSON request body, which in some providers encodes the API key or session credentials inline, and `url` may contain key material in query parameters (e.g., some Gemini endpoints accept `?key=`).
- **Impact:** API keys or session tokens written to log files, syslog, or observability pipelines; exposure to anyone with log read access.
- **Fix:** Never log `data` or `url` in full. Log a truncated, redacted version (e.g., first 80 chars of URL with query string stripped, body length only). Gate even that behind a separate `VerboseSecure` flag distinct from general verbose mode.

---

### MEDIUM: `curl_slist_append` failure leaves `m_List` as `nullptr`, silently drops all subsequent headers

- **Location:** `CurlWrapper::CurlSlist::Append`, ~line 225
- **Issue:** When `curl_slist_append` returns `nullptr` on failure, `m_List` is overwritten with `nullptr`. All previously appended headers are leaked (no `curl_slist_free_all` on the old pointer) and all subsequent `Append` calls start a new list from scratch. The auth headers added by `IAuthSigner::Apply` (including `Authorization: Bearer <key>` or `x-api-key`) may silently be absent from the request.
- **Impact:** Auth headers silently dropped; request sent without credentials, potentially reaching the upstream provider unauthenticated (provider-side behavior varies). Memory leak of previous list nodes.
- **Fix:** Save the old pointer before calling `curl_slist_append`; if the return is `nullptr`, restore the old pointer and propagate an error rather than silently continuing. Log and surface a hard failure.

---

### MEDIUM: `CurlWrapper::Query` dereferences `Core::g_Core` without null check in verbose branch

- **Location:** `CurlWrapper::Query`, ~line 308
- **Issue:** The progress callback (lambda) already guards `Core::g_Core != nullptr`, but the verbose-mode block uses `Core::g_Core->Verbose()` unconditionally. If `g_Core` is `nullptr` at this point (early init, teardown, or unit-test context), this is an unconditional null dereference.
- **Impact:** Crash (null pointer dereference); exploitable as a denial-of-service if an attacker can trigger a `Query` call during shutdown.
- **Fix:** Guard with `if (Core::g_Core != nullptr && Core::g_Core->Verbose())`.

---

### MEDIUM: SSRF — `m_Url` is not validated before use

- **Location:** `CurlWrapper::Query`, ~line 280; `CurlWrapper::QueryData::IsValid`, ~line 235
- **Issue:** `IsValid()` only checks that `m_Url` is non-empty. No scheme validation, no host allowlist, and no rejection of `file://`, `gopher://`, `dict://`, `ftp://`, or other libcurl-supported schemes is performed. Any code path that constructs a `QueryData` from partially-user-controlled input can be redirected to internal services or local files.
- **Impact:** Server-Side Request Forgery; an attacker who influences `m_Url` can probe internal network topology, read local files via `file://`, or interact with internal metadata services (e.g., AWS IMDSv1).
- **Fix:** In `IsValid()`, verify that `m_Url` starts with `https://` (exact scheme check, not a prefix-search that could be bypassed). Optionally maintain an allowlist of permitted hostnames and reject anything not on it. Set `CURLOPT_PROTOCOLS` to `CURLPROTO_HTTPS` only.

---

### LOW: `m_QueryCounter` used for log correlation but accessed with relaxed semantics under contention

- **Location:** `CurlWrapper::Query`, ~line 315 (`++m_QueryCounter`) and subsequent `.load()` calls
- **Issue:** `m_QueryCounter` is `std::atomic<uint32_t>` but the pre-increment and subsequent loads use default (`seq_cst`) ordering, which is correct for atomicity. The security concern is that the counter is shared across all `CurlWrapper` instances and threads: the counter value logged at "sending query N" may differ from the value logged in the error/response lines for the same request if another thread increments it between the two reads. Log entries therefore cannot reliably be correlated to a specific request, undermining audit trails.
- **Impact:** Inability to accurately audit which request produced which error; attacker-triggered errors may be misattributed in post-incident review.
- **Fix:** Capture the counter value once at the start of `Query` into a local `uint32_t queryId = ++m_QueryCounter;` and use `queryId` consistently throughout the function. Consider a per-request UUID instead of a global counter for high-concurrency deployments.


---

<a id="113-doc-engine-curlwrapper-ratelimitobservation-h"></a>

### 114_doc_engine_curlWrapper_rateLimitStrategy_h

<a id="114-doc-engine-curlwrapper-ratelimitstrategy-h"></a>

*Source doc:* `../../../queue/jarvisCppCyberSecAudit/114_doc_engine_curlWrapper_rateLimitStrategy_h/docu.output.md`



### LOW — Uncontrolled integer accumulation in ParseOpenAIDurationMs enables silent overflow

- **Location:** `ParseOpenAIDurationMs`, approximately line 90–120
- **Issue:** The local `num` and accumulator `ms` are plain `int`. A header value such as `"2147484s"` causes `num * 1000` to overflow a 32-bit signed integer silently (undefined behaviour in C++). A crafted or malicious upstream response can produce a large negative or near-zero `ms`, which is then rejected by the `ms > 0` guard and yields a zero reset time, effectively suppressing rate-limit back-off.
- **Impact:** An attacker who can influence response headers (e.g., a compromised or MITM'd upstream API endpoint) could disable the rate-limit controller's back-off, causing the engine to hammer the provider without throttling.
- **Fix:** Use `int64_t` for both `num` and `ms`, add an explicit upper-bound check (e.g., reject any value > 3 600 000 ms), and clamp or return an error rather than accumulating silently.

---

### LOW — `ParseIso8601DeltaSec` uses `timegm` which is non-standard and assumes UTC without validation

- **Location:** `ParseIso8601DeltaSec`, approximately line 130–150
- **Issue:** The fields parsed via `sscanf` (`year`, `mon`, `day`, `hour`, `min`, `sec`) are written into a `std::tm` without range-checking (e.g., `mon` = 99, `sec` = 61). `timegm` behaviour with out-of-range `tm` fields is implementation-defined and on some libc versions performs normalisation that could produce unexpected timestamps. Additionally, `sscanf` with `%d` on a provider-controlled string can accept leading signs or whitespace.
- **Impact:** A crafted reset timestamp from a malicious upstream could produce a very large or negative delta, bypassing the `>= 7200` guard and injecting an arbitrarily long (but < 7200 s) forced wait, causing a soft DoS of the dispatch pipeline, or producing a zero/negative delta that is silently discarded, suppressing back-off.
- **Fix:** After `sscanf`, validate each field against its legal range before constructing the `tm`. Reject the value if any field is out of range instead of passing garbage to `timegm`.

---

### LOW — Header values from untrusted upstream are passed to `std::stoll`/`std::stoi` without length or content pre-checks

- **Location:** `RateLimitStrategyOpenAI::Parse` and `RateLimitStrategyAnthropic::Parse`, approximately lines 180–250 and 270–360
- **Issue:** Each rate-limit header value is fed directly into `std::stoll` or `std::stoi` inside a blanket `catch (...)`. A header value that is extremely long (e.g., several MB injected by a compromised upstream) will cause `stoll` to scan the entire string before throwing `std::invalid_argument` or `std::out_of_range`. While the exception is caught, this wastes CPU and is called inside the header-iteration lambda on every response.
- **Impact:** A malicious or compromised upstream can insert an enormous header value, causing measurable CPU consumption per response and contributing to DoS of the engine's I/O thread.
- **Fix:** Before calling `stoll`/`stoi`, verify the value is non-empty and its length is within a sensible bound (e.g., ≤ 20 characters for a 64-bit integer).

---

### NONE (for cryptography, injection, authentication/authorization, memory safety, SSRF, path traversal, TLS, secrets leakage, and insecure deserialization)

The file contains no hardcoded secrets, no shell/SQL/header injection sinks, no dynamic memory ownership issues, no TLS configuration, no file paths, no authentication logic, and no serialization beyond simple string parsing. The singleton strategy objects are `const` and never mutated after construction.


---

### 115_doc_engine_engine_h

<a id="115-doc-engine-engine-h"></a>

*Source doc:* `../../../queue/jarvisCppCyberSecAudit/115_doc_engine_engine_h/docu.output.md`



### MEDIUM — Security logging silently disabled in distribution builds

- **Location:** Preprocessor block, `#ifdef LOGGING_AND_ASSERTS` / `#else` branch, approximately lines 30–110
- **Issue:** `LOG_SECURITY_INFO` and `LOG_SECURITY_WARN` are no-ops in `DISTRIBUTION_BUILD` builds because they are defined as empty blocks in the `#else` branch. The same is true for all other log macros. Any security-relevant event (authentication attempts, key-unlock calls, unknown option rejection, key-load failures) that is logged via these macros produces no output and leaves no audit trail in production.
- **Impact:** An attacker probing or exploiting the system leaves no forensic evidence in production; security-relevant events such as repeated failed unlock attempts at `POST /api/settings/keys/unlock` are invisible to defenders.
- **Fix:** Define `LOG_SECURITY_INFO` and `LOG_SECURITY_WARN` as real log calls unconditionally, independent of `LOGGING_AND_ASSERTS`. Security events should always be recorded regardless of build configuration. Separate the security-audit log tier from the debug/trace tier.

---

### MEDIUM — Plaintext `keys.json` silently takes precedence over the encrypted store

- **Location:** `engine()`, approximately lines 195–215 (plaintext keys loading block)
- **Issue:** When both `keys.json.enc` (the encrypted store) and `keys.json` (plaintext) exist on disk simultaneously, the code loads the plaintext file and sets `KeyLoadStatus::Ok`, bypassing the encrypted store entirely. The operator may believe the sealed store is protecting secrets while the plaintext file is quietly being used. There is no warning emitted for the distribution case (where logging is disabled — see finding above), and no check prevents this combination.
- **Impact:** An operator who forgets to delete `keys.json` after migrating to the encrypted store exposes all API keys in plaintext on disk, with no indication that the intended security control is being bypassed.
- **Fix:** When the encrypted keys file exists and `keys.json` also exists, refuse to start (or at minimum emit a hard warning at `stderr` and `LOG_CORE_WARN`) and document that plaintext and encrypted stores are mutually exclusive. Do not silently fall through to plaintext when the encrypted file is present.

---

### MEDIUM — `connections.json` loaded without size limit (uncontrolled allocation / DoS)

- **Location:** `engine()`, approximately lines 225–245 (connections.json loading block)
- **Issue:** The file is read entirely into a `std::string` using `istreambuf_iterator` with no size check. An arbitrarily large file causes unbounded heap allocation before any parsing occurs.
- **Impact:** A local user or a misconfigured deployment with a world-writable working directory can place a multi-gigabyte `connections.json` to exhaust process heap, causing OOM or denial of service at startup.
- **Fix:** Stat the file size before reading and reject files above a reasonable upper bound (e.g., 10 MB). Apply the same guard to `config.json` and `keys.json`.

---

### MEDIUM — Shutdown watchdog thread detached with dangling references to stack variables

- **Location:** `engine()`, approximately lines 265–285 (watchdog thread and `watchdog.detach()`)
- **Issue:** The watchdog lambda captures `watchdogMutex`, `watchdogCV`, and `watchdogDiffused` by reference. After `watchdog.detach()` the thread runs independently. If the main thread somehow reaches the end of `engine()` and returns before the watchdog checks the predicate, those stack variables are destroyed while the detached thread may still be accessing them, producing undefined behavior (use-after-free of stack memory).
- **Impact:** In the normal path the diffuse sequence prevents this, but any exception or early return between `detach()` and the diffuse block leaves the detached thread referencing freed stack frames, which is exploitable as a memory-corruption primitive in adversarial scenarios (e.g., a signal or exception triggered during `OnShutdown`).
- **Fix:** Do not detach the watchdog; join it after the diffuse signal. If a forced exit is needed on timeout, use `std::quick_exit` or `_exit` inside the thread but keep the thread joinable so the captures remain valid for the thread's lifetime. Alternatively, allocate the shared state on the heap (or as `static`) before spawning.

---

### LOW — Unknown CLI option value echoed to `stderr` without sanitisation

- **Location:** `engine()`, approximately lines 155–160 (`unknownOption` error print)
- **Issue:** `unknownOption` is taken verbatim from `argv[i]` and written to `std::cerr` with no filtering. Terminal emulators that interpret ANSI/VT escape sequences can be manipulated by crafting an argument containing escape codes (terminal injection / "terminal escape sequence injection").
- **Impact:** On vulnerable terminal emulators, an attacker who controls the argument list (e.g., via a wrapper script or CI pipeline) could inject escape sequences that overwrite displayed output, forge log lines visible on screen, or trigger terminal macros.
- **Fix:** Strip or percent-encode non-printable and non-ASCII bytes from `unknownOption` before printing, or use a sanitising wrapper that replaces characters outside `[0x20–0x7E]` with `?`.

---

### LOW — `CORE_ASSERT` / `APP_ASSERT` do not halt execution

- **Location:** Macro definitions, approximately lines 50–60
- **Issue:** Both assert macros only log a critical message; they do not call `abort()`, throw, or otherwise stop execution. Code that relies on an assert to enforce a security invariant (e.g., a null-pointer guard before dereferencing) will continue running in a potentially inconsistent state after the assertion fires. `CORE_HARD_STOP` does halt but the assert macros do not use it.
- **Impact:** Security-critical invariant violations are silently logged and execution continues, potentially reaching vulnerable code paths that the assertion was meant to prevent.
- **Fix:** Make `CORE_ASSERT` and `APP_ASSERT` call `std::abort()` (or `CORE_HARD_STOP`) after logging, consistent with the intent of an assertion macro. In distribution builds where logging is stripped, they should still abort on invariant failure.


---
