### 126_doc_engine_json_configChecker_h

<a id="126-doc-engine-json-configchecker-h"></a>

*Source doc:* `../../../queue/jarvisCppCyberSecAudit/126_doc_engine_json_configChecker_h/docu.output.md`



### LOW Path values logged and printed to stderr without sanitisation

- **Location:** `ConfigChecker::Check`, error-handling block (~lines 115–125)
- **Issue:** `queueFolderFilepath` and `workflowsFolder` are user-supplied strings from the config file and are written verbatim into both the structured log (`LOG_CORE_ERROR`) and `fprintf(stderr, ...)`. If either string contains newline characters, ANSI escape sequences, or log-injection payloads (e.g. `\n[ERROR] fake entry`), an attacker who controls the config file can forge log entries or corrupt terminal output.
- **Impact:** Log injection / log forgery. An attacker with write access to the config file can insert arbitrary text into `log/log.txt`, potentially hiding malicious activity or misleading operators. Terminal escape injection could affect operator consoles.
- **Fix:** Strip or escape control characters (at minimum `\r`, `\n`, and non-printable bytes) from any externally-supplied string before it is passed to a logging macro or `fprintf`. A simple helper that replaces characters outside `[0x20–0x7E]` with `?` or a hex representation is sufficient.

---

### LOW Off-by-one in `checkApiInterface` bounds check

- **Location:** `ConfigChecker::Check`, lambda `checkApiInterface`, ~line 91
- **Issue:** The bounds check reads `if (apiInterfaces.size() < apiIndex)`. This should be `<=` (i.e., `apiIndex >= apiInterfaces.size()`). With the current `<`, when `apiIndex == apiInterfaces.size()` the check passes and the subsequent `apiInterfaces[apiIndex]` access is one element past the end of the vector — undefined behaviour / out-of-bounds read.
- **Impact:** If an attacker controls the config file and sets `m_ApiIndex` equal to the exact size of `m_ApiInterfaces`, the engine reads past the end of the vector. Depending on layout this could crash (DoS) or, in adversarial memory-layout scenarios, expose adjacent heap data.
- **Fix:** Change the condition to `if (apiIndex >= apiInterfaces.size())`.

---

### LOW Weak URL validation allows non-HTTPS schemes and SSRF-enabling hosts

- **Location:** `ConfigChecker::Check`, lambda `checkUrl`, ~lines 96–102
- **Issue:** The check only verifies that the string contains the substring `"https://"` anywhere in it and that `url.size() > 8`. A value such as `"http://evil.internal/x?foo=https://legit"` passes both tests. There is no validation of the host portion, path, or that the scheme appears at position 0.
- **Impact:** A malicious config file can point the engine at an internal network address (SSRF) or a non-TLS endpoint, causing credential leakage or requests to unintended infrastructure.
- **Fix:** Validate that the URL starts with `"https://"` (use `url.rfind("https://", 0) == 0`), and consider a minimal host-allowlist or at least a check that the host is not a private/loopback address when the engine is used in environments where config files may come from untrusted sources.

---

### NONE (for the remaining focus areas)

No issues were identified in the areas of: hardcoded secrets/keys, RNG misuse, missing cert verification (not present in this file), memory lifetime/use-after-free, race conditions, insecure deserialization, uncontrolled allocation, or TLS configuration — those concerns would need to be reviewed in the files that are only referenced here (`engine.h`, `configParser.h`, `file.h`, `CurlMultiDispatcher`, etc.).


---

### 127_doc_engine_json_configParser_h

<a id="127-doc-engine-json-configparser-h"></a>

*Source doc:* `../../../queue/jarvisCppCyberSecAudit/127_doc_engine_json_configParser_h/docu.output.md`



### HIGH — Unvalidated file path accepted as config path (path traversal / arbitrary file read)

- **Location:** `ConfigParser::ConfigParser` constructor and `Parse`, header ~line 170, source ~line 55
- **Issue:** `m_ConfigFilepathAndFilename` is stored and later passed verbatim to `EngineCore::FileExists`, `EngineCore::IsDirectory`, and `padded_string::load` with no canonicalization or containment check. Any caller that derives this path from user-controlled input can point the parser at any readable file on the filesystem.
- **Impact:** An attacker who controls the config path argument can read arbitrary files the process has access to. If simdjson misparses a binary file without crashing, structured data from sensitive files (e.g., `/etc/passwd`, private keys) may be partially reflected in logs.
- **Fix:** Resolve the path with `std::filesystem::canonical` and verify it remains under an expected base directory before storing it. Reject paths that escape the base.

---

### HIGH — `m_QueueFolderFilepath` and `m_WorkflowsFolderFilepath` accepted without path validation

- **Location:** `ConfigParser::Parse`, source ~lines 108–122
- **Issue:** Both folder paths are copied directly from JSON into `EngineConfig` without canonicalization, containment checks, or any character filtering. These values are subsequently used (in code not shown here) for file-watching and workflow loading. A malicious or tampered `config.json` can inject `../` sequences or absolute paths.
- **Impact:** Depending on how the engine consumes these paths, an attacker who can write `config.json` may achieve path traversal into arbitrary directories, potentially reading or executing workflows outside the intended workspace.
- **Fix:** After reading, resolve each path with `std::filesystem::canonical` (or `weakly_canonical` for non-existent-yet paths) and assert it is a subdirectory of the deployment root before assigning to `EngineConfig`.

---

### HIGH — `m_KeysFilePath` and `m_McpKeysFilePath` are user-controlled, unsanitized paths to sensitive key files

- **Location:** `ConfigParser::Parse`, source ~lines 191–196 and ~lines 293–297
- **Issue:** The paths to the encrypted key stores (`keys.json.enc`, `mcp_keys.json.enc`) are read from the config JSON and stored without any path validation or containment check. Default values are relative names, but the JSON can override them to any absolute path.
- **Impact:** An attacker who controls `config.json` can redirect key-file loading to an attacker-chosen path, either to exfiltrate parsing behavior, to bypass encryption (by pointing at a pre-crafted file), or to provoke predictable failures that leak information.
- **Fix:** Apply the same canonical-path-under-base-directory validation as for the folder paths above. Consider refusing absolute paths for key file entries.

---

### HIGH — `m_TrustedProxyHeader` and `m_TrustedRoleHeader` stored without format validation

- **Location:** `ConfigParser::Parse`, source ~lines 205–215
- **Issue:** These strings name the HTTP headers the engine trusts for proxy-supplied identity and role. They are copied verbatim from JSON with no check that they contain only valid HTTP header-name characters (`[A-Za-z0-9\-]`). A value containing CR/LF (`\r\n`) is an HTTP response-splitting payload; a value containing null bytes or spaces may confuse comparison logic downstream.
- **Impact:** If the engine later uses either string to construct or compare HTTP headers, a crafted config can cause header injection, response splitting, or silent authorization-header mismatch (effectively disabling the trusted-header auth path).
- **Fix:** After reading, validate each value against the RFC 7230 header-name token grammar. Reject any value that does not match `[!#$%&'*+\-.0-9A-Z^_`a-z|~]+` (the token production). Log and abort parsing on failure.

---

### MEDIUM — Negative or zero `m_MaxInflightAiCalls` / `m_PythonEngines` / `m_MaxThreads` silently cast to large `size_t`

- **Location:** `ConfigParser::Parse`, source ~lines 265–275 (`max inflight ai calls`), ~lines 276–280 (`python engines`), ~lines 90–97 (`max threads`)
- **Issue:** All three fields are read as `int64_t` and then cast to `size_t` (unsigned) without checking for negative values. A JSON entry of `-1` becomes `SIZE_MAX` (e.g., 18446744073709551615 on 64-bit). The same pattern applies to `m_MaxAiCallsPerJcwf` for negative values before the explicit `cap < 0` guard (which only applies there).
- **Impact:** A malicious or erroneous config can cause the engine to allocate enormous thread pools or semaphore limits, leading to immediate resource exhaustion (DoS) or OS-level failures when the engine starts.
- **Fix:** Add `if (value < 0) { LOG_CORE_WARN/ERROR; use safe default; }` before each cast. Enforce a reasonable upper bound (e.g., max 1024 threads, max 10000 inflight calls).

---

### MEDIUM — `m_MaxRequestBodyMB` has no upper-bound enforcement

- **Location:** `ConfigParser::Parse`, source ~lines 218–222
- **Issue:** The value is read via `get_uint64()` so it cannot be negative, but there is no maximum cap. A config entry of `18446744073709551615` would set the request-body limit to an astronomically large value, effectively disabling it.
- **Impact:** Any code that uses `m_MaxRequestBodyMB` as a guard against oversized uploads would be bypassed, enabling uncontrolled memory allocation from attacker-supplied HTTP request bodies (DoS, possible OOM).
- **Fix:** Clamp to a sanity maximum (e.g., 2048 MB or whatever the platform can safely handle). Log a warning and substitute the default when exceeded.

---

### MEDIUM — `m_TlsCert` and `m_TlsKey` paths logged at INFO level

- **Location:** `ConfigParser::Parse`, source ~lines 195–204
- **Issue:** The filesystem paths to the TLS private key and certificate are emitted to the application log at `LOG_CORE_INFO` level. If logs are shipped to a centralized log aggregator (Elasticsearch, Splunk, CloudWatch, etc.) with broad read access, the key file location is disclosed.
- **Impact:** Knowing the exact private-key path accelerates exploitation of a separate file-read vulnerability. In high-security environments this alone may violate data-handling policies.
- **Fix:** Log the TLS cert path at INFO but omit or redact the TLS key path, or log it only at DEBUG level that is disabled in production builds.

---

### MEDIUM — TOCTOU between `FileExists`/`IsDirectory` check and `padded_string::load`

- **Location:** `ConfigParser::Parse`, source ~lines 60–69
- **Issue:** The code checks existence and directory-ness of `m_ConfigFilepathAndFilename` and then opens it with simdjson in a separate call. Between the two operations another process (or a symlink swap) can replace the file. This is a classic TOCTOU.
- **Impact:** On systems where the config file path is writable by a lower-privilege process, an attacker can swap in a malicious config after the existence/type check passes, causing the engine to load attacker-controlled configuration.
- **Fix:** Open the file descriptor first (`open(O_NOFOLLOW)` on Linux, or the platform equivalent), then check the `fstat` result, and pass the already-open fd to the JSON parser. This collapses check-and-use into a single atomic operation.

---

### MEDIUM — Unbounded `m_ApiInterfaces` vector growth (DoS via config)

- **Location:** `ConfigParser::ParseInterfaces`, source ~last lines of the function
- **Issue:** Every element of the `"API interfaces"` JSON array is pushed into `engineConfig.m_ApiInterfaces` without any limit on array length. A crafted config with thousands of interface entries will cause unconstrained memory allocation and potentially long parse times.
- **Impact:** Denial of service: a process restart with a tampered config.json exhaust memory before the engine becomes operational.
- **Fix:** Enforce a maximum interface count (e.g., 64) and return `FileFormatFailure` if the array exceeds it.

---

### MEDIUM — `default_output_tokens` silently truncated on overflow cast to `int32_t`

- **Location:** `ConfigParser::ParseInterfaces`, source around the `"default_output_tokens"` branch
- **Issue:** `value` is an `int64_t` checked only for `> 0`, then cast unconditionally to `int32_t`. Values between `INT32_MAX + 1` and `INT64_MAX` (e.g., `4000000000`) wrap to a negative `int32_t`, which may then bypass downstream `> 0` guards or produce absurd token budgets.
- **Impact:** Crafted config produces a negative or wildly wrong output-token limit, potentially causing the engine to request enormous outputs from providers (cost amplification) or to skip budget enforcement.
- **Fix:** Add `if (value > INT32_MAX) { value = INT32_MAX; }` before the cast, or use a tighter domain maximum (e.g., 65536).

---

### LOW — Unknown JSON fields from config logged verbatim without sanitization

- **Location:** `ConfigParser::Parse`, the `else` branch, source ~lines 230–255
- **Issue:** Unrecognized key names and their string values are passed directly to `LOG_CORE_INFO`/`LOG_CORE_WARN`. If the logging framework uses `{}` as a format string (spdlog/fmtlib style), and if either the key or value contains format specifiers, format-string injection is theoretically possible depending on how `LOG_CORE_*` is ultimately implemented.
- **Impact:** Low-probability format-string exploitation in the logging layer, or at minimum log injection (CRLF in key/value could pollute structured logs).
- **Fix:** Ensure `LOG_CORE_INFO` is always called with a literal format string and the key/value as arguments (current pattern `"{}: {}"` is correct only if those are separate arguments, not concatenated first). Verify no intermediary builds a combined string and passes it as the format argument. Additionally strip or escape CR/LF in key and value before logging.


---

### 128_doc_engine_json_jsonHelper_h

<a id="128-doc-engine-json-jsonhelper-h"></a>

*Source doc:* `../../../queue/jarvisCppCyberSecAudit/128_doc_engine_json_jsonHelper_h/docu.output.md`



### HIGH Incomplete JSON sanitization — control characters pass through unescaped
- **Location:** `JsonHelper::SanitizeForJson`, default branch (~line 80)
- **Issue:** The `switch` statement only handles `\"`, `\\`, `\n`, `\r`, and `\t`. All other ASCII control characters (0x00–0x1F, except the null-byte case that silently drops the character) fall through to the `default` branch and are emitted verbatim into the JSON string value. RFC 8259 §7 requires that characters U+0000–U+001F be escaped as `\uXXXX`. Bytes such as 0x01–0x08, 0x0B, 0x0C, 0x0E–0x1F are therefore written raw into the output.
- **Impact:** If the resulting JSON is parsed by a lenient downstream parser or forwarded to a browser/shell/log aggregator, unescaped control characters can break JSON framing, corrupt log output, or (depending on downstream context) enable injection into structured formats. In a workflow engine that forwards payloads to AI providers or webhooks, a crafted input containing 0x0B or 0x0C could smuggle additional JSON keys if a downstream parser tolerates those bytes as whitespace.
- **Fix:** In the `default` branch, check whether the character is a control character before appending it. If it is, emit the `\uXXXX` escape:

```
default:
{
    if (static_cast<unsigned char>(c) < 0x20)
    {
        char buf[7];
        snprintf(buf, sizeof(buf), "\\u%04x",
                 static_cast<unsigned char>(c));
        output += buf;
    }
    else
    {
        output += c;
    }
    break;
}
```

Also add explicit cases for `\b` (0x08 → `\\b`) and `\f` (0x0C → `\\f`) as required by the JSON spec.

---

### LOW Null-byte silently stripped without caller awareness
- **Location:** `JsonHelper::SanitizeForJson`, `case '\0'` (~line 56)
- **Issue:** The null byte (0x00) is silently discarded from the output. The caller receives a string that is shorter than the input with no indication that data was dropped. This is a lossy, silent transform.
- **Impact:** If the string is used in a context where the null byte is semantically meaningful (e.g., binary data embedded in a field, or a crafted payload relying on the drop to bypass a length or content check elsewhere in the pipeline), the silent truncation can mask malicious input or produce unexpected downstream behaviour. It is also inconsistent with the RFC 8259 requirement to emit `\u0000` rather than drop the character.
- **Fix:** Replace the silent drop with the proper JSON escape: emit `\\u0000` so the data round-trips faithfully and the caller is not silently given a different string than it provided.


---

### 129_doc_engine_keys_credential_h

<a id="129-doc-engine-keys-credential-h"></a>

*Source doc:* `../../../queue/jarvisCppCyberSecAudit/129_doc_engine_keys_credential_h/docu.output.md`



### LOW Credential fields stored as plain in-memory strings with no secure-erase on destruction
- **Location:** `ApiKeyCredential::m_ApiKey`, `OAuthCredential::m_AccessToken`, `OAuthCredential::m_RefreshToken`, `KeyPairCredential::m_PrivateKeyPem`, `BasicAuthCredential::m_Password` — approximately lines 56, 65–67, 76, 84–85
- **Issue:** All sensitive credential values are held in `std::string` members. `std::string` does not zero its internal buffer on destruction, and the allocator may not either. After the owning object is destroyed, the plaintext key/token/password/PEM data remains readable in process memory until the heap page is reused. A memory-disclosure primitive (heap spray read, core dump, `/proc/<pid>/mem` access by a co-located process, or swap-file analysis) can recover credentials long after the object's lifetime ends.
- **Impact:** An attacker with read access to process memory, a core dump, or swap can recover API keys, OAuth tokens, RSA private keys, and passwords that the application believed it had "released."
- **Fix:** Replace `std::string` for every sensitive field with a custom type (or a thin wrapper around `std::vector<char>`) whose destructor calls `explicit_bzero` / `SecureZeroMemory` / `OPENSSL_cleanse` before freeing the buffer. Mark copy constructors and copy-assignment operators `= delete` on these wrappers (or zero the source after move). If `std::string` must be kept, add a virtual destructor override in each credential subclass that zeroes the field before it goes out of scope:
  ```
  ~ApiKeyCredential() override { OPENSSL_cleanse(m_ApiKey.data(), m_ApiKey.size()); }
  ```

### LOW Credentials placed in a `#pragma once` shared header risk unintentional exposure through transitive inclusion
- **Location:** File-level `#pragma once`, class definitions for all `ICredential` subtypes — approximately lines 22, 44–86
- **Issue:** Defining concrete credential classes (including fields like `m_PrivateKeyPem` and `m_Password`) in a header means any translation unit that needs even the abstract interface pulls in the full layout of every credential type. This widens the blast radius of a future injection of debug-print or serialization code: a developer adding `std::cout << cred.m_Password` in any included file will silently compile and ship a secrets-leaking path. It also makes it harder to apply access controls (e.g., compiler visibility attributes or module partitions) to the sensitive fields.
- **Impact:** Increases the likelihood of accidental secrets leakage in logs or debug output as the codebase grows; not directly exploitable on its own.
- **Fix:** Forward-declare `ICredential` in the shared header. Move the concrete subclass definitions (with their sensitive fields) into a restricted implementation header or a C++20 module partition that is only included by the key-manager translation unit. Expose only opaque handles or the abstract interface to the rest of the codebase.

### LOW No `const`-correctness or access control on sensitive fields
- **Location:** All sensitive `public` member fields — approximately lines 56, 65–67, 76, 84–85
- **Issue:** `m_ApiKey`, `m_AccessToken`, `m_RefreshToken`, `m_RefreshToken`, `m_PrivateKeyPem`, `m_Password` are all `public` non-`const` data members. Any code that obtains a pointer or reference to these objects can read or overwrite credential values without any auditable accessor path. This makes it impossible to add logging-guard assertions, access telemetry, or zeroization hooks in one place.
- **Impact:** Accidental or malicious code anywhere in the same binary can silently read or corrupt credentials; no choke point exists to enforce least-privilege access.
- **Fix:** Make sensitive fields `private`. Expose read access through a method that can be instrumented (e.g., `std::string_view GetSecret() const`), and restrict write access to constructors or a designated `KeyManager` friend class. This does not prevent in-memory disclosure but eliminates the uncontrolled write surface and creates a single point for future access auditing.


---

### 12_doc_application_cloud_cloudConnectionManager_h

<a id="12-doc-application-cloud-cloudconnectionmanager-h"></a>

*Source doc:* `../../../queue/jarvisCppCyberSecAudit/12_doc_application_cloud_cloudConnectionManager_h/docu.output.md`



### HIGH Dangling pointer from GetConnection across lock boundary

- **Location:** `CloudConnectionManager::GetConnection`, approximately line 175
- **Issue:** The function acquires a `shared_lock`, finds an element, releases the lock when the function returns, and then hands the raw pointer `&it->second` to the caller. Any subsequent write operation (`AddConnection`, `UpdateConnection`, `RemoveConnection`, `ParseConnectionsJson`) that holds a `unique_lock` can invalidate that pointer — by erasing the entry, rehashing the map on insert, or calling `m_Connections.clear()` — while the caller still holds and dereferences it. There is no lifetime guarantee on the returned `CloudConnection const*` once the lock is released.
- **Impact:** Use-after-free; an attacker who can race a connection deletion or replacement against a read can cause the consumer of this pointer to read freed/reused memory, potentially leading to information disclosure or memory corruption depending on what is done with the struct fields afterward.
- **Fix:** Return `std::optional<CloudConnection>` (value copy) instead of a raw pointer. The copy is made while the lock is still held; no pointer escape occurs.

---

### HIGH Unbounded allocation via ParseConnectionsJson

- **Location:** `CloudConnectionManager::ParseConnectionsJson`, approximately line 210
- **Issue:** The function accepts an arbitrary JSON string, clears the entire existing connection map, and then iterates over every element of the `"connections"` array without any limit on the number of entries or the size of individual field strings (`m_Name`, `m_Type`, `m_Endpoint`, `m_KeyName`, `m_Params`). An attacker who controls the JSON input (e.g., via the REST API save endpoint mentioned in the comment) can supply a document with millions of connections or multi-megabyte field values, causing unbounded heap allocation.
- **Impact:** Denial of service — the process will consume all available memory and either crash or become unresponsive. Because `m_Connections.clear()` is called before parsing, a failed or slow parse also wipes the existing configuration, providing a secondary disruption.
- **Fix:** (1) Cap the number of parsed connections (e.g., reject input if the array exceeds a reasonable limit such as 1 000). (2) Validate that individual string fields do not exceed a reasonable maximum length before copying them into `conn`. (3) Do not clear `m_Connections` until the entire parse has succeeded; build into a local map first and swap only on success.

---

### HIGH ParseConnectionsJson clears state on partial parse failure

- **Location:** `CloudConnectionManager::ParseConnectionsJson`, approximately line 207
- **Issue:** `m_Connections.clear()` is called unconditionally at the start of the function before any validation of the incoming JSON. If parsing subsequently fails (JSON syntax error, missing `"connections"` key, or an exception), the function returns `false` with the connection store empty. The `m_Dirty` flag is also not set, so the caller may not know the in-memory state is now diverged from disk.
- **Impact:** An attacker who can send a malformed or empty payload to the save/reload endpoint can wipe all in-memory cloud connection configuration, forcing the engine to operate without any connections and possibly causing downstream authentication or routing failures.
- **Fix:** Parse into a local `std::unordered_map<std::string, CloudConnection> newConnections`; only call `m_Connections = std::move(newConnections)` and set `m_Dirty = true` after the entire parse completes successfully.

---

### MEDIUM JsonEscape does not encode all JSON control characters

- **Location:** `JsonEscape`, approximately line 75
- **Issue:** The function handles `"`, `\\`, `\n`, `\r`, and `\t`, but omits the remaining C0 control characters (U+0000–U+001F) required by RFC 8259 §7 to be escaped in JSON strings. Characters such as `\b` (0x08), `\f` (0x0C), and arbitrary bytes 0x00–0x07 and 0x0E–0x1F are passed through as raw bytes into the serialized JSON output.
- **Impact:** If any field value (connection name, endpoint URL, parameter key or value) contains raw control characters — either injected by a user or present in an imported configuration — the resulting JSON written to `config.json` will be syntactically invalid or will be parsed differently by other consumers. In the worst case an attacker can smuggle bytes that cause a downstream JSON parser to misinterpret field boundaries, constituting a JSON injection.
- **Fix:** Add a `default` branch that checks `(unsigned char)c < 0x20` and emits `\\u00XX` using `snprintf` or `std::format`. Also handle `\b` (0x08) and `\f` (0x0C) with their named escapes.

---

### MEDIUM Connection name used as map key without length or character validation

- **Location:** `CloudConnectionManager::AddConnection` and `ParseConnectionsJson`, approximately lines 115 and 261
- **Issue:** `connection.m_Name` is accepted as a map key and written directly to log messages and serialized JSON with no restriction on length, character set, or structure. There is no check for excessively long names, embedded newlines, or other special characters.
- **Impact:** (1) A very long name causes unbounded string allocation and large log entries. (2) A name containing `\n` or `\r` can produce log injection, splitting one log line into multiple lines that look like separate log events, potentially hiding or forging audit records. (3) Names containing JSON-significant characters that slip through `JsonEscape` (see above finding) can corrupt the serialized config file.
- **Fix:** Enforce a maximum name length (e.g., 256 bytes), and restrict the character set to printable ASCII or a defined safe subset before accepting the connection.

---

### MEDIUM Endpoint URL stored and serialized without SSRF validation

- **Location:** `CloudConnectionManager::AddConnection`, `UpdateConnection`, and `ParseConnectionsJson`
- **Issue:** `conn.m_Endpoint` is accepted from JSON or via the API and stored with no validation of scheme, host, or port. The comment in the header indicates these configs drive cloud connector HTTP requests. There is no allowlist or blocklist preventing endpoints such as `http://169.254.169.254/` (AWS IMDSv1), `http://localhost:6379/` (Redis), or `file:///etc/passwd`.
- **Impact:** Server-Side Request Forgery: an authenticated user (or an attacker who has compromised the REST API) can redirect the engine's outbound HTTP calls to internal services, cloud metadata endpoints, or local sockets, potentially exfiltrating credentials or pivoting within the internal network.
- **Fix:** At write time (`AddConnection`/`UpdateConnection`/`ParseConnectionsJson`), validate that `m_Endpoint` parses as a URL with an allowed scheme (`https` only in production), a non-loopback, non-link-local host, and an allowed port. Reject or warn on anything that does not pass.

---

### LOW IsDirty / ClearDirty not protected by the mutex

- **Location:** `CloudConnectionManager::IsDirty` and `CloudConnectionManager::ClearDirty`, approximately lines 55–56 (header)
- **Issue:** Both methods access `m_Dirty` without acquiring any lock. Write methods (`AddConnection`, `UpdateConnection`, `RemoveConnection`, `ParseConnectionsJson`) set `m_Dirty = true` under `unique_lock`, but `IsDirty` and `ClearDirty` read and write it without synchronization.
- **Impact:** Data race on `m_Dirty` under C++ memory model is undefined behaviour. In practice, a persistence thread calling `IsDirty()`/`ClearDirty()` concurrently with a mutation can miss a dirty signal, leaving an updated configuration unpersisted to disk — a correctness and (indirectly) a security concern if the missing config is a credential or endpoint needed for correct auth.
- **Fix:** Acquire at minimum a `shared_lock` in `IsDirty()` and a `unique_lock` in `ClearDirty()` before accessing `m_Dirty`, or make `m_Dirty` a `std::atomic<bool>`.


---

### 130_doc_engine_keys_jwtGenerator_h

<a id="130-doc-engine-keys-jwtgenerator-h"></a>

*Source doc:* `../../../queue/jarvisCppCyberSecAudit/130_doc_engine_keys_jwtGenerator_h/docu.output.md`



### MEDIUM JSON Injection via unescaped account/user in Snowflake JWT payload

- **Location:** `JwtGenerator::GenerateSnowflakeJwt`, payload construction (~lines 230–238)
- **Issue:** `upperAccount`, `upperUser`, and `fingerprint` are embedded directly into the JSON payload string using `std::ostringstream` without any JSON escaping. Although `::toupper` is applied and the account locator is truncated at the first `.`, the raw values originally come from untrusted caller-supplied strings. A value containing `"` or `\` characters will produce malformed or attacker-controlled JSON, allowing claim values to be overwritten (e.g., injecting `","exp":9999999999,"x":"` into the user parameter to extend token lifetime or forge `iss`/`sub`).
- **Impact:** An attacker who controls the `account` or `user` parameter can manipulate JWT claims, potentially forging a token with an arbitrary expiry or subject, leading to privilege escalation or authentication bypass against Snowflake.
- **Fix:** JSON-escape all string values before interpolation. At minimum, reject or strip any character outside `[A-Z0-9_\-]` (which are the only characters valid in Snowflake account identifiers and usernames) before building the payload. Using a proper JSON serialization library (e.g., nlohmann/json) is the preferred fix.

---

### MEDIUM Unvalidated PEM input size enables uncontrolled memory allocation / DoS

- **Location:** `JwtGenerator::Generate` and `JwtGenerator::ComputePublicKeyFingerprint`, `BIO_new_mem_buf` calls (~lines 131, 178)
- **Issue:** `privateKeyPem` is accepted from the caller with no length bound check before being passed to `BIO_new_mem_buf`. The `int` cast `static_cast<int>(privateKeyPem.size())` silently truncates on strings longer than `INT_MAX` (2 GB), which is exploitable on 64-bit systems. Even below that threshold, a caller can pass arbitrarily large input, causing OpenSSL to allocate and parse it, consuming CPU and memory.
- **Impact:** A caller supplying a multi-megabyte or gigabyte PEM string can exhaust memory or CPU, causing denial of service. The truncation of a >2 GB string to a negative `int` may cause undefined behavior in OpenSSL's BIO layer.
- **Fix:** Enforce a reasonable maximum length (e.g., 16 KB) on `privateKeyPem` before calling `BIO_new_mem_buf`, and return an error if exceeded. Replace the `static_cast<int>` with a checked conversion that returns an error on overflow.

---

### MEDIUM `i2d_PUBKEY` called twice without resetting the pointer, risking incorrect DER output

- **Location:** `JwtGenerator::ComputePublicKeyFingerprint`, ~lines 195–200
- **Issue:** `i2d_PUBKEY(pkey, nullptr)` is called first to obtain the required buffer length, then `i2d_PUBKEY(pkey, &derPtr)` writes into `derBuf`. The OpenSSL `i2d_*` functions advance the pointer passed to them; `derPtr` is set to `derBuf.data()` immediately before the second call, which is correct. However, the return value of the second `i2d_PUBKEY` call is discarded without checking. If the second call fails (returns ≤ 0), the code silently SHA-256-hashes whatever happens to be in the zero-initialized `derBuf`, producing a bogus fingerprint rather than returning an error.
- **Impact:** A crafted or corrupt key could cause a silent fingerprint mismatch. If fingerprint validation is relied upon for authorization decisions elsewhere, this produces an incorrect but non-empty fingerprint that passes downstream checks.
- **Fix:** Check the return value of the second `i2d_PUBKEY` call, compare it against `derLen`, and return an error if they differ or if the return is ≤ 0.

---

### LOW Sensitive material (JWT string) registered in `SecretRedactor` but private key PEM is not

- **Location:** `JwtGenerator::Generate`, ~line 170; `JwtGenerator::ComputePublicKeyFingerprint` and `GenerateSnowflakeJwt`, passim
- **Issue:** The generated JWT is registered with `SecretRedactor::Get().AddSecret(jwt)` to prevent it from appearing in logs. The `privateKeyPem` value, which is far more sensitive (a long-lived private key), is never registered. If `privateKeyPem` is logged anywhere in the call stack before or after these functions (e.g., in error paths that log function arguments), it will not be redacted.
- **Impact:** Private key material may appear in application logs, error reports, or telemetry, enabling offline forgery of tokens.
- **Fix:** Register `privateKeyPem` with `SecretRedactor` at the entry of both `Generate` and `ComputePublicKeyFingerprint`, or enforce at the call sites that the PEM is never passed to any logging facility.

---

### LOW `EVP_PKEY_free` not called on error paths that call `EVP_MD_CTX_free`

- **Location:** `JwtGenerator::Generate`, ~lines 148–167
- **Issue:** Within the `if/else if/else` signing chain, `EVP_PKEY_free(pkey)` is called unconditionally after `EVP_MD_CTX_free(mdCtx)` at the bottom of the function. This is correct for the success path. However, the key-size check at ~line 141 correctly frees `pkey` before returning, and the `mdCtx` allocation failure at ~line 147 correctly frees `pkey` before returning. These are fine individually, but the pattern is fragile: if a future maintainer adds an early return inside the signing block (where `pkey` is still live but `EVP_MD_CTX_free` has not yet been called), a leak or double-free could result. More concretely, this is a maintenance-induced memory safety risk rather than a current bug.
- **Impact:** In the current code, no leak or double-free exists, but the manual resource management pattern is one edit away from a real vulnerability.
- **Fix:** Use RAII wrappers (`std::unique_ptr` with custom deleters for `EVP_PKEY` and `EVP_MD_CTX`) to eliminate manual free calls and make resource lifetimes compiler-enforced.


---

### 131_doc_engine_keys_keyEncryption_h

<a id="131-doc-engine-keys-keyencryption-h"></a>

*Source doc:* `../../../queue/jarvisCppCyberSecAudit/131_doc_engine_keys_keyEncryption_h/docu.output.md`



### LOW plaintext string not scrubbed after decryption
- **Location:** `KeyEncryption::Decrypt`, approximately the line `plaintext.resize(outLen + finalLen)` through the `return plaintext` statement
- **Issue:** The decrypted plaintext is returned as a `std::string`. The caller receives a copy (or move), and the local `plaintext` buffer is never cleansed before it goes out of scope. `std::string` memory may linger on the heap after the destructor runs because the standard library does not zero memory on deallocation. If the process memory is later inspected (core dump, swap, memory scraper), decrypted key material is exposed.
- **Impact:** Secrets resident in process memory are recoverable from core dumps, swap files, or a compromised process inspector.
- **Fix:** Call `OPENSSL_cleanse(plaintext.data(), plaintext.size())` immediately before every early-return path and at the end of the function, before the local goes out of scope. Consider using a custom allocator that zeroes on deallocation, or wrap the return value in a type that zeroes in its destructor.

### LOW `masterPassword` not scrubbed from stack/heap on early-return error paths
- **Location:** `KeyEncryption::Encrypt` and `KeyEncryption::Decrypt`, all early-return error paths after PBKDF2 derivation
- **Issue:** `masterPassword` is passed by `const std::string&`, so its storage is owned by the caller and cannot be cleansed here. However, the derived `key` array on the stack is only cleansed (`OPENSSL_cleanse`) on the success path and on some error paths in `Decrypt`, but not consistently on all early-return paths in `Encrypt`. Specifically, in `Encrypt`, if any OpenSSL call after key derivation fails (e.g., `EVP_EncryptInit_ex`, `EVP_CIPHER_CTX_ctrl`, `EVP_EncryptUpdate`, `EVP_EncryptFinal_ex`, or `EVP_CTRL_GCM_GET_TAG`), the function returns `{}` without calling `OPENSSL_cleanse(key, KEY_SIZE)`.
- **Impact:** The derived AES-256 key remains on the stack until overwritten by subsequent calls, leaking key material to a memory inspector or core dump.
- **Fix:** Add `OPENSSL_cleanse(key, KEY_SIZE)` before every `return {}` in `Encrypt` after the key derivation step, mirroring the pattern already used in `Decrypt`. A RAII wrapper or `goto cleanup` pattern would make this less error-prone.

### LOW integer truncation in `EVP_EncryptUpdate` / `EVP_DecryptUpdate` for large inputs
- **Location:** `KeyEncryption::Encrypt` ~line with `static_cast<int>(plaintextSize)`, `KeyEncryption::Decrypt` ~line with `static_cast<int>(ciphertextSize)`
- **Issue:** `plaintextSize` and `ciphertextSize` are `size_t` values cast to `int` without a bounds check. If the blob is larger than `INT_MAX` bytes (2 GiB), the cast wraps to a negative value, and OpenSSL receives an invalid length. This is a realistic concern for a workflow engine that may encrypt large secrets files.
- **Impact:** Passing a negative length to `EVP_EncryptUpdate` or `EVP_DecryptUpdate` results in undefined behavior or an OpenSSL error; an attacker who controls input size could trigger a crash (DoS).
- **Fix:** Add an explicit guard before both casts: `if (plaintextSize > static_cast<size_t>(std::numeric_limits<int>::max())) { /* error */ return {}; }`.

### NONE (remaining focus areas)
The following checked areas have no findings: magic/version checks before key derivation (preventing oracle abuse), RAND_bytes return value is verified, AES-256-GCM with a 12-byte random IV and 16-byte tag is cryptographically sound, PBKDF2 uses SHA-256 with 100 000 iterations (acceptable, though Argon2id would be preferable for new designs), no hardcoded keys or IVs, no weak RNG, no SQL/shell/header injection, no SSRF, no path traversal, no insecure deserialization, no TLS configuration, no authentication bypass, no race conditions in this file. The `EVP_CTRL_GCM_GET_TAG` placement (after `EVP_EncryptFinal_ex`) is correct.


---

### 132_doc_engine_keys_keyManager_h

<a id="132-doc-engine-keys-keymanager-h"></a>

*Source doc:* `../../../queue/jarvisCppCyberSecAudit/132_doc_engine_keys_keyManager_h/docu.output.md`



### CRITICAL: JSON injection in SerializeToJson — plaintext string fields not escaped

- **Location:** `KeyManager::SerializeToJson`, the block that writes `display_name`, `endpoint`, `api_key`, `default_model`, `api_type`, `credential_type`, and their credential-type-specific siblings (`refresh_token`, `scopes`, `token_endpoint`, `client_id`, `client_secret`, `username`, `password`), approximately lines 470–530.
- **Issue:** Every one of those fields is interpolated directly into the JSON output with raw `<<` stream insertion and double-quote delimiters. The only escaping applied is on `private_key_pem` and on the `params` map. Any string field containing `"`, `\`, or control characters can break out of its JSON string context. An attacker who controls a provider name (the map key, line ~474) or any of the unescaped value fields can inject arbitrary JSON structure into the serialized output. When that output is later encrypted and saved, the next `Load`/`ParseProvidersJson` call will parse the attacker-supplied JSON structure instead of the intended one.
- **Impact:** Depending on the receiver, this enables: adding or overwriting arbitrary provider entries (including injecting a provider with an attacker-controlled endpoint to redirect AI requests), corrupting the `default_provider` field, or causing a parse failure that permanently locks the user out of their keys file.
- **Fix:** Apply the same `escape()` lambda that is already used for `params` keys/values to every string field before it is written. The map key (provider name) must also be escaped. Pull `escape()` out of the params block so it is accessible to all serialization code.

---

### HIGH: Master password exposed in plaintext via GetCachedMasterPassword

- **Location:** `KeyManager::GetCachedMasterPassword`, header declaration ~line 106 and implementation ~line 175.
- **Issue:** `GetCachedMasterPassword()` returns the master password as a `std::string` by value. The `SecureString` wrapper is bypassed at the call boundary: the password lands in a heap-allocated `std::string` that the caller must manually zero, but there is no mechanism to enforce that. The comment in the header even documents the intent to keep this password cached "so server-side operations can persist encrypted updates," meaning it is designed to be called repeatedly and held by callers.
- **Impact:** The master password lives in recoverable heap memory for an unbounded duration. It will appear in core dumps, memory snapshots, and swap. Any read-anywhere primitive gives an attacker the master password, enabling offline decryption of all provider credentials.
- **Fix:** Return a scoped wrapper type (e.g. a `SecureString` or a type with a zeroing destructor) instead of `std::string`. Callers that need to pass the password to `Load`/`Save` should do so without materializing a naked `std::string`. If the pattern of passing it to those functions requires a `std::string const&`, change those signatures to accept `SecureString const&` or a `std::string_view` whose backing store is the `SecureString` buffer.

---

### HIGH: No size limit on encrypted blob or plaintext JSON — uncontrolled allocation / DoS

- **Location:** `KeyManager::Load`, ~line 145 (`std::vector<uint8_t> blob(...)`) and `KeyManager::LoadPlaintext`, ~line 200 (`oss << file.rdbuf()`).
- **Issue:** Both paths read the entire file into memory without any size check. An attacker who can place (or replace) the keys file on disk can supply an arbitrarily large file. `Load` will allocate a `vector<uint8_t>` of that size, then `KeyEncryption::Decrypt` will produce another allocation, then `simdjson::padded_string` in `ParseProvidersJson` will produce a third. `LoadPlaintext` goes through `std::ostringstream` with no limit. Additionally, `ParseProvidersJson` iterates every key in the `params` sub-object and inserts entries into `m_Params` without a count limit, enabling a quadratic-time hash-table insertion attack.
- **Impact:** Any process or user that can write to the keys file path can cause the engine to exhaust available memory and crash (denial of service). In environments where the keys file is placed in a shared or world-writable location this is trivially exploitable.
- **Fix:** Add a maximum file size check (e.g. 1 MB) before reading. Add a maximum provider count and maximum `params` entry count inside `ParseProvidersJson`.

---

### HIGH: TOCTOU on keys file path in Unlock

- **Location:** `KeyManager::Unlock`, ~line 260 (`std::filesystem::exists` then `Load`).
- **Issue:** `Unlock` calls `std::filesystem::exists(m_KeysFilePath)` and then, in a separate call, opens the file inside `Load`. Between the existence check and the open, the file can be replaced with a symlink pointing to a different file, or the original file can be swapped for a crafted one. There is no locking of the file or use of `O_NOFOLLOW` semantics.
- **Impact:** An attacker with write access to the directory containing the keys file can redirect the open to a different file, causing `Load` to attempt to decrypt and parse attacker-controlled content. Combined with the injection finding above, this could overwrite provider configurations with attacker-controlled endpoints.
- **Fix:** Remove the separate `exists` check. Open the file directly inside `Load` and treat any `ifstream` failure as the "does not exist" case. On POSIX, open with `O_NOFOLLOW` to reject symlinks.

---

### MEDIUM: Credential-type-specific secrets not registered with SecretRedactor

- **Location:** `KeyManager::ParseProvidersJson`, ~lines 435–450 where `m_ApiKey`, `m_RefreshToken`, `m_ClientSecret`, `m_PrivateKeyPem`, and `m_Password` are assigned.
- **Issue:** The code registers AWS `secret_access_key` and `session_token` with `SecretRedactor`, but does not register any of the other sensitive credential fields: `m_ApiKey` (API keys for every non-AWS provider), `m_RefreshToken`, `m_ClientSecret`, `m_PrivateKeyPem`, and `m_Password`. If any of those values appear in log output (e.g., via a debug log of a curl error that echoes a header, or a future log statement that prints a config struct), they will not be redacted.
- **Impact:** API keys, OAuth client secrets, private key PEM material, and basic-auth passwords leak into log files in plaintext.
- **Fix:** Register each sensitive field with `SecretRedactor::Get().AddSecret(...)` immediately after assigning it, for all credential types.

---

### MEDIUM: Unescaped provider name used as JSON object key in SerializeToJson

- **Location:** `KeyManager::SerializeToJson`, ~line 474: `oss << "        \"" << name << "\": {\n";`
- **Issue:** This is a specific instance of the JSON injection finding above, called out separately because the provider name is the map key, which is sourced from `AddProvider`/`ParseProvidersJson` without any character validation. A provider name containing `"` will produce malformed JSON. A name containing `": {}, "injected_provider": {` will silently inject a new top-level provider object.
- **Impact:** Provider registry corruption and persistent injection of attacker-controlled provider configurations.
- **Fix:** Apply the `escape()` lambda to `name` before writing it as a JSON key.

---

### MEDIUM: `LoadPlaintext` and `SavePlaintext` available in production builds with no guard

- **Location:** `KeyManager::LoadPlaintext` (~line 195) and `KeyManager::SavePlaintext` (~line 215); public API with no compile-time or runtime restriction.
- **Issue:** Both methods are documented "development only" but are compiled into and exposed through the production binary as fully public methods. Any code path that calls `LoadPlaintext` writes all provider credentials — API keys, OAuth secrets, private keys, basic-auth passwords — to disk in cleartext. Any code path that calls `SavePlaintext` writes the same. There is no runtime check, no `#ifdef DEBUG`, and no access control.
- **Impact:** If any caller (including future code) invokes `SavePlaintext` in a production deployment, all credentials are written to a plaintext file. An attacker with read access to the filesystem recovers all secrets.
- **Fix:** Wrap both methods and their implementations in `#ifdef J9T_DEVELOPMENT_BUILD` (or equivalent). If they must remain available at runtime, add an explicit developer-mode flag checked at the top of each function that aborts or returns false in production.

---

### LOW: `std::getenv("OPENAI_API_KEY")` result used after potential environment mutation

- **Location:** `KeyManager::LoadFromEnvironment`, ~line 230: `char const* apiKeyEnv = std::getenv("OPENAI_API_KEY");` followed by use at ~line 240.
- **Issue:** `std::getenv` returns a pointer into the process environment block. If another thread calls `setenv`, `putenv`, or `unsetenv` concurrently, the pointer may become dangling or point to modified memory before it is consumed by `std::string(apiKeyEnv)`. Additionally, `std::strlen(apiKeyEnv) < 8` is not a meaningful security check; a key of exactly 8 characters is not a valid OpenAI key and will be accepted.
- **Impact:** In a multi-threaded server context, a race on the environment block is an extremely narrow window for use-after-free. The length check gives false confidence that a garbage or truncated key is rejected.
- **Fix:** Copy the value into a `std::string` immediately: `std::string apiKey = apiKeyEnv ? apiKeyEnv : "";`. Validate against a more meaningful minimum length (OpenAI keys are 51 characters). The race is only exploitable if `setenv` is called concurrently, which is uncommon in practice but worth noting for threat model completeness.


---

### 133_doc_engine_keys_oauthTokenManager_h

<a id="133-doc-engine-keys-oauthtokenmanager-h"></a>

*Source doc:* `../../../queue/jarvisCppCyberSecAudit/133_doc_engine_keys_oauthTokenManager_h/docu.output.md`



### CRITICAL — No TLS certificate validation (SSL peer verification disabled by omission)
- **Location:** `OAuthTokenManager::RefreshToken`, around the `curl_easy_setopt(curl, CURLOPT_URL, ...)` block
- **Issue:** `CURLOPT_SSL_VERIFYPEER` and `CURLOPT_SSL_VERIFYHOST` are never set. libcurl's defaults are both enabled, but the CA-bundle path is only set when `CurlWrapper::GetCaBundlePath()` returns a non-empty string. If the bundle path is empty (first-run, mis-deployment, or an attacker who empties the config value), libcurl silently falls back to the system CA store *or* may verify against nothing depending on the build. More critically, there is no explicit enforcement of `CURLOPT_SSL_VERIFYPEER = 1L` and `CURLOPT_SSL_VERIFYHOST = 2L`, so any code path that calls `curl_easy_init` and fails to set those options is one configuration error away from accepting any certificate. Since the token endpoint URL comes from user-controlled provider config (`entry.m_TokenEndpoint`), an attacker who can write that value can point the engine at a malicious server and harvest the `client_secret` and `refresh_token` from the POST body.
- **Impact:** Full MitM of OAuth token refresh: attacker can steal `client_secret`, `refresh_token`, and the newly issued `access_token`.
- **Fix:** Unconditionally set both options before `curl_easy_perform`:
  `curl_easy_setopt(curl, CURLOPT_SSL_VERIFYPEER, 1L);`
  `curl_easy_setopt(curl, CURLOPT_SSL_VERIFYHOST, 2L);`
  If the CA bundle is empty, either abort the refresh with an error or enforce use of the compiled-in CA store. Never allow the bundle path to silently degrade verification.

---

### CRITICAL — SSRF via unvalidated `m_TokenEndpoint`
- **Location:** `OAuthTokenManager::RefreshToken`, `curl_easy_setopt(curl, CURLOPT_URL, entry.m_TokenEndpoint.c_str())`
- **Issue:** `entry.m_TokenEndpoint` flows directly from `KeyManager` provider config into `CURLOPT_URL` with zero validation of scheme, host, or path. An attacker who can write or influence provider configuration (compromised config file, insecure admin API, path traversal into config) can supply `http://169.254.169.254/`, `file:///etc/passwd`, `gopher://`, or `dict://` as the endpoint. libcurl supports all of these by default.
- **Impact:** SSRF to internal metadata services (AWS/GCP/Azure IMDS), access to local files via `file://`, or lateral movement to internal HTTP endpoints. The POST body containing `client_secret` and `refresh_token` is sent to the attacker-controlled endpoint.
- **Fix:** Validate `m_TokenEndpoint` before use: require an `https://` prefix, parse the URL with `CURLU`, reject private/link-local IP ranges and `localhost`, and restrict libcurl protocols with `curl_easy_setopt(curl, CURLOPT_PROTOCOLS_STR, "https")`.

---

### CRITICAL — OAuth credentials sent in unencoded POST body (no URL encoding)
- **Location:** `OAuthTokenManager::RefreshToken`, `postBody` construction (~line building `grant_type=refresh_token&refresh_token=...`)
- **Issue:** `entry.m_RefreshToken`, `entry.m_ClientId`, and `entry.m_ClientSecret` are concatenated raw into an `application/x-www-form-urlencoded` body without `curl_easy_escape`. If any of these values contain `&`, `=`, `+`, or `%`, the POST body is malformed and the server may interpret injected fields. For example a refresh token of `abc&grant_type=authorization_code` would override the `grant_type` field sent to the authorization server.
- **Impact:** Form-parameter injection against the OAuth token endpoint: an attacker who controls a stored credential value can inject arbitrary fields into the token request, potentially downgrading the grant type or injecting a `scope` parameter.
- **Fix:** URL-encode each value with `curl_easy_escape(curl, value.c_str(), value.size())` and `curl_free` the result, or use a dedicated form-encoding helper, before concatenating into `postBody`.

---

### HIGH — Dangling reference to `TokenEntry` across mutex unlock/lock in `GetAccessToken`
- **Location:** `OAuthTokenManager::GetAccessToken`, the block starting with `lock.unlock(); bool const success = RefreshToken(keyName, entry);`
- **Issue:** `entry` is a reference into `m_Tokens[keyName]`. The mutex is unlocked before `RefreshToken` is called and re-locked after. During that window, another thread can insert into, erase from, or rehash `m_Tokens` (e.g. `StoreTokens` acquires the same mutex and writes `m_Tokens[keyName] = std::move(entry)`), invalidating the iterator and the reference. After `lock.lock()` re-acquires the mutex, the code reads and writes through a now-dangling `entry` reference.
- **Impact:** Use-after-free / memory corruption; undefined behaviour that can be exploited for arbitrary code execution or silent data corruption of security-sensitive token fields.
- **Fix:** Re-look up the iterator after re-acquiring the lock: replace `entry.m_Refreshing = false;` with a fresh `auto it2 = m_Tokens.find(keyName);` check before accessing the entry. Consider using a per-entry `std::shared_ptr` or keyed condition variable instead of holding a raw reference across an unlock.

---

### HIGH — Same dangling reference in `RefreshLoop` across unlock/lock
- **Location:** `OAuthTokenManager::RefreshLoop`, `lock.unlock(); bool success = RefreshToken(keyName, entry); lock.lock(); entry.m_Refreshing = false;`
- **Issue:** Identical pattern to the `GetAccessToken` finding. `entry` is a structured-binding reference into the range-for over `m_Tokens`. After `lock.unlock()`, a concurrent `StoreTokens` call can rehash the map or replace the entry, invalidating the reference. On re-lock, writing `entry.m_Refreshing = false` and `m_Cv.notify_all()` operates on freed or reallocated memory.
- **Impact:** Use-after-free / memory corruption in the background refresh thread.
- **Fix:** Same as above: re-acquire the iterator after re-locking, or restructure so the map is not mutated while a refresh is logically in progress for an entry (e.g. collect keys to refresh before unlocking, refresh each one individually with a fresh lookup).

---

### HIGH — Unbounded response body accumulation (DoS via memory exhaustion)
- **Location:** `OAuthTokenManager::RefreshToken`, the `writeCallback` lambda and `responseBody` string
- **Issue:** The `CURLOPT_WRITEFUNCTION` appends every received byte into `responseBody` with no size cap. A malicious or compromised token endpoint can stream an arbitrarily large response, causing unbounded heap growth until the process is OOM-killed.
- **Impact:** Denial of service of the workflow engine process; potential heap pressure affecting other in-flight requests.
- **Fix:** Track accumulated size inside the write callback and return 0 (causing `curl_easy_perform` to abort with `CURLE_WRITE_ERROR`) once a reasonable limit is exceeded (e.g. 64 KB is more than enough for any OAuth token response).

---

### HIGH — HTTP error response body logged without redaction
- **Location:** `OAuthTokenManager::RefreshToken`, `LOG_CORE_ERROR("OAuthTokenManager: refresh for '{}' returned HTTP {} — {}", keyName, httpCode, responseBody...)`
- **Issue:** On a non-200 response, up to 500 bytes of `responseBody` are written to the log verbatim. OAuth error responses from some providers echo back submitted parameters (including `client_secret` or `refresh_token`) in error descriptions or debug fields. Even without reflection, logging raw server responses that may contain token material is a secrets-leakage risk.
- **Impact:** OAuth client secrets or tokens written to log files, which may be shipped to log aggregators, accessible to operators with lower clearance, or captured in crash dumps.
- **Fix:** Pass `responseBody` through `SecretRedactor::Get().Redact(responseBody)` (or equivalent) before logging, consistent with how tokens are handled elsewhere in the codebase.

---

### MEDIUM — Race condition in `Start()` between `m_Running` load and thread launch
- **Location:** `OAuthTokenManager::Start()`, `if (m_Running.load())` check followed by `m_Running.store(true)` and thread construction
- **Issue:** Two threads calling `Start()` concurrently can both pass the `m_Running.load()` check before either executes `m_Running.store(true)`, resulting in two `m_RefreshThread` assignments and two background threads. The second assignment overwrites `m_RefreshThread` without joining the first thread, which is then leaked and continues running detached but using the same `m_Tokens` map and mutex, causing data races.
- **Impact:** Double-thread scenario leads to concurrent unsynchronized writes on `m_RefreshThread` (not protected by `m_Mutex`) and a leaked thread that outlives expected lifetime.
- **Fix:** Use a `std::mutex` or `compare_exchange_strong` to make the load-check-store-launch sequence atomic, or document and enforce that `Start()` is only called from one thread.

---

### MEDIUM — `expiresInSeconds` accepted without bounds check in `StoreTokens`
- **Location:** `OAuthTokenManager::StoreTokens`, `entry.m_ExpiresAt = NowUnixSeconds() + expiresInSeconds`
- **Issue:** `expiresInSeconds` is an unchecked `int64_t`. A caller passing a very large positive value overflows `m_ExpiresAt` into a negative or wrap-around timestamp, causing `HasValidToken` and the refresh loop to misclassify a never-expiring token or one that looks perpetually expired. A zero or negative value sets `m_ExpiresAt` in the past, forcing a refresh on every `GetAccessToken` call.
- **Impact:** Tokens treated as perpetually valid (authentication bypass in `HasValidToken`) or forced into an infinite refresh loop (DoS).
- **Fix:** Clamp `expiresInSeconds` to a sane range (e.g. 1–86400 seconds) and reject or log a warning on out-of-range values. Check for signed-integer overflow before the addition.

---

### MEDIUM — `expires_in` from token response trusted without bounds check
- **Location:** `OAuthTokenManager::RefreshToken`, `if (!doc["expires_in"].get_int64().get(parsedExpiry)) { expiresIn = parsedExpiry; }`
- **Issue:** The integer value of `expires_in` from the JSON response is used without validation. A malicious endpoint can return `expires_in: 0` (causing an immediate re-refresh on every access), a negative value (setting `m_ExpiresAt` in the past), or `INT64_MAX` (integer overflow on addition with `NowUnixSeconds()`).
- **Impact:** Same as above: auth bypass or refresh-loop DoS; signed overflow is undefined behaviour in C++.
- **Fix:** Validate `parsedExpiry > 0 && parsedExpiry <= 86400` (or a suitable max) before assigning to `expiresIn`. Guard the addition against overflow.

---

### LOW — `keyName` included verbatim in user-visible error strings
- **Location:** `OAuthTokenManager::GetAccessToken`, error string construction: `"No OAuth tokens stored for '" + keyName + "'"` and similar
- **Issue:** `keyName` comes from caller-supplied input and is reflected into `errorMessage` which may be forwarded to API responses or UI. While not a log-injection issue in the strict sense, it allows an attacker who controls `keyName` to inject arbitrary text (including newlines) into error messages that may be parsed or displayed by downstream consumers.
- **Impact:** Low-severity message-injection; in contexts where `errorMessage` is serialised into JSON or displayed in a UI, could cause confusion or minor information disclosure about internal key naming conventions.
- **Fix:** Sanitise or quote `keyName` in error messages, or limit the characters accepted in key names at the point where they are registered in `KeyManager`.


---

### 134_doc_engine_keys_secureString_h

<a id="134-doc-engine-keys-securestring-h"></a>

*Source doc:* `../../../queue/jarvisCppCyberSecAudit/134_doc_engine_keys_secureString_h/docu.output.md`



### LOW — Sensitive allocation size leaked through unauthenticated log channel

- **Location:** `SecureString::Set`, approximately line 155
- **Issue:** `LOG_CORE_ERROR` emits the exact byte count of the secret being stored (`needed`) to whatever logging sink is wired up. Depending on the engine's log configuration this could be written to a file, syslog, or a remote collector that is not subject to the same access controls as the locked memory itself.
- **Impact:** An observer with access to logs but not to process memory can infer the length of master passwords or other secrets stored in `SecureString`, narrowing brute-force search space.
- **Fix:** Remove the `needed` value from the error message, or at minimum gate it behind a debug-only log level that is compiled out in release builds.

---

### LOW — `mlock`/`VirtualLock` failure is silently tolerated and logged in plain text

- **Location:** `SecureString::Set` / `LockMemory`, approximately lines 158–162
- **Issue:** When `LockMemory` fails the code continues with an unlocked buffer and logs a human-readable warning that includes the allocation size. The warning itself is the only signal that swap protection is absent; there is no mechanism to propagate this failure to callers so they can refuse to store the secret or alert an operator through a secured channel.
- **Impact:** On systems where `mlock` fails (unprivileged processes, exhausted `RLIMIT_MEMLOCK`), the secret will silently reside in swappable memory, defeating the stated security goal. A post-compromise forensic read of a swap partition or a hibernation file recovers the plaintext secret with no indication in the application that protection was absent.
- **Fix:** Return a `bool` or error code from `Set` indicating whether memory locking succeeded, and let callers decide the appropriate policy (fail closed, require elevated privileges, etc.). Do not silently degrade.

---

### LOW — `Clear()` does not release the lock or reset `m_Capacity`, leaving locked pages allocated indefinitely

- **Location:** `SecureString::Clear`, approximately lines 185–190
- **Issue:** `Clear` zeroes the buffer and resets `m_Size` to zero but does not call `UnlockMemory` or `std::free`. The locked allocation remains live until `Release`/destruction. If callers use `Clear` as a "done with the secret" signal (a reasonable assumption given the method name) rather than destroying the object, locked memory pages accumulate, exhausting the process's `RLIMIT_MEMLOCK` quota.
- **Impact:** Repeated `Clear` calls without object destruction progressively consume the locked-page limit, eventually causing `LockMemory` to fail for subsequent `SecureString` objects, silently degrading swap protection for all future secrets in the process.
- **Fix:** Either have `Clear` call `Release` in full (matching the semantics of "zero and free"), or clearly document that `Clear` only zeroes contents and that callers must destroy the object to reclaim the locked pages.

---

### NONE (no additional findings)

No injection, cryptographic misuse, memory-safety (use-after-free, OOB), race condition, deserialization, SSRF, path traversal, or TLS issues are present in this specific file. The move semantics correctly null the donor object, `SecureZero` is correctly applied before `free`, and the portable volatile-loop fallback is not optimisable away.


---

### 135_doc_engine_log_log_h

<a id="135-doc-engine-log-log-h"></a>

*Source doc:* `../../../queue/jarvisCppCyberSecAudit/135_doc_engine_log_log_h/docu.output.md`



### LOW Hardcoded relative log directory and filename with no path validation
- **Location:** `Log::Log()`, line where `std::filesystem::create_directories("log")` and `"log/security.txt"` are used
- **Issue:** The log directory and security log file path are hardcoded as relative paths (`"log"` and `"log/security.txt"`). If the process working directory is attacker-influenced (e.g., through environment variables, a symlink to a sensitive directory, or a calling process that sets the CWD), the security log file can be written to an unintended location. Additionally, there is no check on whether `"log"` already exists as a symlink pointing elsewhere before writing to it, creating a symlink-following TOCTOU condition.
- **Impact:** An attacker with control over the process working directory or the ability to plant a symlink at `log/security.txt` (or the `log/` directory itself) before process start could redirect security audit log writes to an arbitrary file they choose, potentially overwriting sensitive files or silently suppressing the security audit trail.
- **Fix:** Resolve the log path to an absolute directory at startup (e.g., derived from the executable path or a validated configuration value). After calling `create_directories`, verify the resulting path is not a symlink (`std::filesystem::is_symlink`) before opening any files beneath it. On POSIX, open the directory with `O_NOFOLLOW` semantics and use `openat`-based file creation to prevent mid-path symlink substitution.

### LOW Security logger level set to `trace` — sensitive security events visible at lowest severity
- **Location:** `Log::Log()`, `m_SecurityLogger->set_level(spdlog::level::trace)` and `m_SecurityLogger->flush_on(spdlog::level::trace)`
- **Issue:** The security logger is configured at `trace` level, the lowest possible severity. Any code in the codebase that calls `SecurityLogger.trace(...)` with sensitive data (authentication tokens, session IDs, keys, secrets passed through security code paths) will write that data both to stdout/TUI and to the rotating `log/security.txt` file in plaintext.
- **Impact:** Sensitive security-relevant data logged at `trace` level will be persisted to disk in plaintext and displayed in the terminal UI, expanding the attack surface for secret leakage via log file exfiltration or shoulder-surfing/screen capture.
- **Fix:** Set the security logger's minimum level to `warn` or `info` for production builds. Gate `trace`/`debug` level on an explicit debug-mode compile flag. Audit all call sites that log to the security logger at `trace` or `debug` to ensure no secrets, tokens, or PII appear in those messages.

### LOW Security log file has no explicit file permission restriction
- **Location:** `Log::Log()`, `rotating_file_sink_mt("log/security.txt", ...)` construction
- **Issue:** The rotating file sink is created with no explicit file mode/permissions argument. On POSIX systems, the resulting file inherits the process umask, which in many deployment environments defaults to `022`, making `security.txt` world-readable (`-rw-r--r--`).
- **Impact:** Any local user on the system can read the security audit log, including any authentication failures, user identifiers, IP addresses, or other security-relevant information written to it.
- **Fix:** Set a restrictive umask (`0177` or tighter) around the file creation call, or — if spdlog's `rotating_file_sink_mt` supports a file-mode argument in your version — pass `0600`. After creation, explicitly call `chmod("log/security.txt", 0600)` as a defense-in-depth measure.


---

### 137_doc_engine_log_terminalLogStreamBuf_h

<a id="137-doc-engine-log-terminallogstreambuf-h"></a>

*Source doc:* `../../../queue/jarvisCppCyberSecAudit/137_doc_engine_log_terminalLogStreamBuf_h/docu.output.md`



### LOW — Incomplete ANSI escape sequence stripping enables log injection / terminal control-sequence smuggling

- **Location:** `TerminalLogStreamBuf::StripAnsi`, approximately lines 93–117
- **Issue:** The ANSI stripper only recognises sequences that terminate with an ASCII letter (A–Z, a–z). It does not handle the full ANSI/VT100 grammar: sequences beginning with `ESC [` (CSI) followed by parameter bytes (0x30–0x3F) and intermediate bytes (0x20–0x2F) before the final byte; sequences beginning with `ESC ]` (OSC) which terminate with `BEL` (0x07) or `ST` (`ESC \`); `ESC P` (DCS); `ESC _` (APC); etc. An attacker who can influence the text flowing through this stream buffer (e.g. via AI model output, webhook payloads, or external log data) can embed unstripped OSC/DCS sequences in the `clean` string. Those sequences pass through to `TerminalManager::EnqueueLogLine`, the file logger, and the broadcast callback. In a terminal context this can overwrite the window title, trigger hyperlink rendering, execute iTerm2/XTerm operating-system commands, or cause log-viewer injection. In the broadcast callback (e.g. a WebSocket feed) the raw escape bytes may confuse downstream parsers or renderers.
- **Impact:** Terminal control-sequence injection leading to UI spoofing, potential remote code execution in terminals that honour `ESC ]` file-write or clipboard-write extensions (e.g. iTerm2 `ESC ] 1337`), and log injection into file and network consumers.
- **Fix:** Replace the hand-rolled stripper with a complete state machine that handles all ANSI/VT escape sequence classes, or use a well-tested library. At minimum: after seeing `ESC`, if the next byte is `[` (CSI), consume bytes until a byte in the range 0x40–0x7E; if the next byte is `]` (OSC), consume until `BEL` (0x07) or `ESC \`; if the next byte is in `P`, `X`, `^`, `_`, consume until `ESC \`. Bytes 0x00–0x1F that are not part of a recognised sequence (other than `\n`, `\r`, `\t`) should also be removed or replaced with a safe substitute before forwarding to any sink.

---

### LOW — Raw pointer `m_TerminalManager` with no lifetime guarantee

- **Location:** `TerminalLogStreamBuf` constructor and `syncLocked`, approximately lines 39–41 and 131
- **Issue:** `m_TerminalManager` is stored as a raw `TerminalManager*` with no ownership or lifetime contract. The null check at line 131 guards only against an explicit `nullptr` argument; it does not protect against the `TerminalManager` object being destroyed while a `TerminalLogStreamBuf` instance (and its associated `std::ostream`) is still live and receiving data. Because `syncLocked` is called from `overflow` and `xsputn`, which may be invoked from any thread that writes to the stream, a use-after-free is possible if the `TerminalManager` is torn down concurrently or in the wrong destruction order.
- **Impact:** Use-after-free, potentially exploitable for arbitrary code execution or denial of service depending on heap layout and attacker-controlled log content.
- **Fix:** Change `m_TerminalManager` to a `std::weak_ptr<TerminalManager>` (requiring `TerminalManager` to be managed by `shared_ptr`) and lock it in `syncLocked` before use. If `shared_ptr` ownership is not feasible, document and enforce a strict destruction ordering guarantee that the `TerminalLogStreamBuf` is destroyed before its `TerminalManager`, and add a `Detach()` method that sets the pointer to `nullptr` under `m_BufferMutex` to be called before `TerminalManager` destruction.


---

### 138_doc_engine_log_terminalManager_h

<a id="138-doc-engine-log-terminalmanager-h"></a>

*Source doc:* `../../../queue/jarvisCppCyberSecAudit/138_doc_engine_log_terminalManager_h/docu.output.md`



### LOW Unbounded growth of the pending-log queue enables memory exhaustion (DoS)

- **Location:** `TerminalManager::EnqueueLogLine`, approximately the last 5 lines of the file; consumed in `Impl::DrainQueuedLogLines`
- **Issue:** `EnqueueLogLine` appends to `m_PendingLines` under the mutex with no upper bound on queue depth. If the rendering thread falls behind (or `Render()` is never called, e.g. during a paused state driven by `RenderPaused`), any producer thread that calls `EnqueueLogLine` at high rate will grow the vector without limit until the process is OOM-killed.
- **Impact:** A sufficiently verbose AI response pipeline or a misbehaving integration that emits log lines faster than the terminal can drain them will exhaust process heap, causing a crash or OOM kill. In a workflow engine context this is a reliable availability vector if any external input influences log verbosity or log line count.
- **Fix:** Cap the queue at a reasonable maximum (e.g. 4 096 or a configurable limit). Inside `EnqueueLogLine`, after acquiring the lock, check `m_PendingLines.size()` and either drop the newest entry, drop the oldest entry (ring-buffer), or block the caller. Example: `if (m_Impl->m_PendingLines.size() >= kMaxPendingLines) { m_Impl->m_PendingLines.erase(m_Impl->m_PendingLines.begin()); }` (or use a `std::deque` for O(1) front removal).

---

### LOW Terminal state not restored on abnormal exit / signal

- **Location:** `TerminalManager::Shutdown`, `Initialize` (non-Windows path, ~tcgetattr call)
- **Issue:** `Shutdown` is only called from `~TerminalManager`. If the process is terminated by a signal (SIGTERM, SIGINT, SIGPIPE) before the destructor runs, `endwin()` and `tcsetattr()` are never called. The terminal is left in `cbreak`/`noecho` mode, which effectively blinds any operator or downstream shell session that inherits the TTY.
- **Impact:** Post-crash or post-signal, an attacker who can cause the process to crash (e.g. via a malformed AI response that triggers an unhandled exception elsewhere) leaves the operator's terminal in a state where typed passwords and secrets are invisible on screen but still transmitted — a social-engineering or credential-leakage assist.
- **Fix:** Register a `sigaction` handler (or `std::atexit` / `std::at_quick_exit`) during `Initialize` that calls `endwin()` and restores `m_OriginalTermios`. Because `m_OriginalTermios` must be accessible from a signal handler, store it in a file-scope `sig_atomic_t`-guarded structure or use `tcsetattr` directly with a statically stored copy.

---

### NONE (remaining focus areas)

No issues identified in the areas of: injection (SQL/shell/HTTP header/log), authentication/authorization bypass, cryptography, use-after-free or out-of-bounds memory safety, TOCTOU/race conditions beyond the queue issue above, secrets leakage, insecure deserialization, SSRF/path traversal, or TLS configuration. This file is a terminal rendering component with no network I/O, cryptographic operations, or secret handling.


---

### 139_doc_engine_auxiliary_TracyClient_cpp

<a id="139-doc-engine-auxiliary-tracyclient-cpp"></a>

*Source doc:* `../../../queue/jarvisCppCyberSecAudit/139_doc_engine_auxiliary_TracyClient_cpp/docu.output.md`



### NONE
No security issues identified.

This file is a unity-build aggregator (a compile-unit that `#include`s other `.cpp` files) for the Tracy profiler library. It contains no application logic, no input handling, no cryptographic operations, no network code of its own, no authentication, and no secrets. All security-relevant behavior lives in the included translation units, which are not present here and therefore cannot be reviewed from this file alone. The only notable point is that `#pragma warning(push, 0)` suppresses all MSVC warnings for the included files, which could hide issues in those files during review — but that is a code-quality/process observation, not a standalone security vulnerability in this file.
