- **Fix:** Extract all required fields in a single forward pass at the start of the function, storing them in local variables, before any array iteration. Do not re-query fields after document iteration has advanced.

---

### [LOW] `curl_slist_free_all` / `curl_easy_cleanup` not called on early-exit paths

- **Location:** `GitHubRequest`, ~line 170
- **Issue:** If `curl_easy_setopt` or internal libcurl calls fail and an exception propagates (e.g., from `std::string::append` during `writeCallback` on allocation failure), `headers` and `curl` are leaked because cleanup is not in a RAII guard.
- **Impact:** Resource leak; not directly exploitable but can exhaust file descriptors or memory under repeated failures.
- **Fix:** Wrap `curl` in a `std::unique_ptr` with a custom deleter calling `curl_easy_cleanup`; wrap `headers` similarly with `curl_slist_free_all`.


---

### 24_doc_application_cloud_gitHubConnector_h

<a id="24-doc-application-cloud-githubconnector-h"></a>

*Source doc:* `../../../queue/jarvisCppCyberSecAudit/24_doc_application_cloud_gitHubConnector_h/docu.output.md`



### HIGH SSRF via unvalidated user-controlled endpoint URL

- **Location:** `GitHubConnector::GetApiBaseUrl` / `GitHubConnector::TestConnection`, approximately lines 75–85 and 115
- **Issue:** `connection.m_Endpoint` is accepted verbatim and concatenated into the request URL without any scheme, host, or allowlist validation. An attacker who can supply or influence a `CloudConnection` record (e.g., through the workflow engine's configuration API, a malicious import, or a compromised admin session) can point the connector at an arbitrary internal or external host, including `http://169.254.169.254/latest/meta-data/` (cloud IMDS), internal services, or `file://` URIs that some libcurl builds honour.
- **Impact:** Full SSRF: exfiltrate cloud-provider instance metadata (IAM credentials, user-data), probe internal network topology, hit internal HTTP APIs that are not exposed externally, potentially retrieve local files.
- **Fix:** Enforce an allowlist of permitted schemes (`https` only) and, if possible, a set of known-good hostname suffixes. At minimum: parse the URL, reject any scheme that is not `https`, and reject hostnames that resolve to RFC-1918 / link-local / loopback ranges. Also set `CURLOPT_PROTOCOLS` and `CURLOPT_REDIR_PROTOCOLS` to `CURLPROTO_HTTPS` only so libcurl enforces this independently of application-level checks.

---

### HIGH Missing TLS peer verification (conditional CA bundle, no explicit verify flags)

- **Location:** `GitHubConnector::TestConnection`, approximately lines 140–144
- **Issue:** `CURLOPT_SSL_VERIFYPEER` and `CURLOPT_SSL_VERIFYHOST` are never explicitly set. The code relies on libcurl's compiled-in defaults and only conditionally sets `CURLOPT_CAINFO` when `CurlWrapper::GetCaBundlePath()` returns a non-empty string. If that function returns an empty string (misconfiguration, missing file, platform without a default CA store), libcurl falls back to its compiled-in CA path or, on some builds/platforms, silently disables verification. The PAT (`credentials.m_Token`) is then sent over a connection that may not be authenticated.
- **Impact:** A network-adjacent attacker performing TLS interception receives the Personal Access Token in the `Authorization: Bearer` header, giving them full API access to the configured GitHub/GitLab account.
- **Fix:** Unconditionally set `curl_easy_setopt(curl, CURLOPT_SSL_VERIFYPEER, 1L)` and `curl_easy_setopt(curl, CURLOPT_SSL_VERIFYHOST, 2L)` before the conditional CA bundle block. If `GetCaBundlePath()` returns empty and no system store is guaranteed, treat that as a fatal configuration error and abort rather than proceeding.

---

### HIGH Redirect following leaks Bearer token across hosts

- **Location:** `GitHubConnector::TestConnection`, approximately line 137
- **Issue:** `CURLOPT_FOLLOWLOCATION` is enabled. libcurl versions before 7.65 (and many configured builds) forward all request headers, including `Authorization: Bearer <PAT>`, to redirect targets regardless of whether the target host differs from the origin. A server (or an SSRF-reachable intermediary) that responds with a `3xx` redirect to an attacker-controlled host will receive the token.
- **Impact:** PAT exfiltration via redirect chain, especially dangerous in combination with the SSRF finding above.
- **Fix:** Either disable `CURLOPT_FOLLOWLOCATION` (the `/user` endpoint does not redirect in normal operation) or set `CURLOPT_MAXREDIRS` to `0`. If redirects must be followed, set `CURLOPT_REDIR_PROTOCOLS` to `CURLPROTO_HTTPS` only, and implement a redirect callback (`CURLOPT_REDIRECT_PROTOCOLS` + manual host comparison) that strips the `Authorization` header when the host changes.

---

### MEDIUM Uncontrolled response-body allocation (DoS)

- **Location:** `GitHubConnector::TestConnection`, write callback and `responseBody`, approximately lines 121–128
- **Issue:** The `writeCallback` lambda appends all received bytes into `responseBody` with no size cap. A malicious or compromised server (enabled by the SSRF issue above) can stream an arbitrarily large body, causing unbounded heap growth until the process exhausts memory or the OS kills it.
- **Impact:** Denial of service of the workflow engine process; if the engine is multi-tenant, this affects all co-hosted workflows.
- **Fix:** Enforce a maximum response size. Inside the callback, check `buf->size() + (size * nmemb)` against a reasonable limit (e.g., 1 MB for a `/user` response) and return 0 (or a value ≠ `size * nmemb`) to abort the transfer when exceeded. Alternatively use `CURLOPT_MAXFILESIZE_LARGE`.

---

### MEDIUM HTTP header injection via PAT value

- **Location:** `GitHubConnector::TestConnection`, approximately line 151
- **Issue:** `credentials.m_Token` (sourced from `provider->m_ApiKey`) is interpolated directly into the `Authorization` header string with no sanitisation: `"Authorization: Bearer " + credentials.m_Token`. If the PAT value contains a CRLF sequence (`\r\n`), libcurl versions below 7.82.0 will split it into additional headers. A credential stored with a crafted value could inject arbitrary HTTP headers.
- **Impact:** Header injection enabling cache-poisoning at an intermediate proxy, Host override, or injection of headers that alter server-side behaviour.
- **Fix:** Validate that `credentials.m_Token` contains only characters legal in a Bearer token (printable ASCII excluding whitespace and control characters: `[A-Za-z0-9\-._~+/]+=*`) before use, and reject or strip invalid values at `ResolveCredentials` time. Also upgrade libcurl to ≥ 7.82.0 which rejects embedded CRLFs.

---

### LOW PAT partially echoed in error messages

- **Location:** `GitHubConnector::TestConnection`, approximately lines 165–171; `GitHubConnector::ResolveCredentials`, approximately lines 102–106
- **Issue:** When an HTTP error occurs and `responseBody` is under 500 bytes, the raw response body is appended to `errorMessage` and returned to the caller. GitHub's API error responses for bad tokens can echo portions of the token or scopes. Additionally, error messages include the credential key name and connection name, which, if logged at a level visible to non-admin users, leak configuration details.
- **Impact:** Partial credential or configuration leakage in logs or API responses surfaced to callers of `TestConnection`.
- **Fix:** Strip or summarise the response body before including it in user-facing error messages. Log the full body only at a debug level accessible to administrators. Avoid including raw API error payloads in messages that may propagate to end users.


---

### 25_doc_application_cloud_googleSheetsCloudTaskExecutor_h

<a id="25-doc-application-cloud-googlesheetscloudtaskexecutor-h"></a>

*Source doc:* `../../../queue/jarvisCppCyberSecAudit/25_doc_application_cloud_googleSheetsCloudTaskExecutor_h/docu.output.md`



### CRITICAL Path Traversal via `outputFile` and `inputFile` Parameters

- **Location:** `GoogleSheetsCloudTaskExecutor::ExecuteCloud`, approximately where `outputPath = workDir / outputFile` and `inputPath = workDir / inputFile` are constructed (read block ~line 290, write block ~line 340)
- **Issue:** `outputFile` and `inputFile` are taken directly from attacker-controlled task params JSON (`getStringParam("output_file")` / `getStringParam("input_file")`) and joined to `workDir` with `std::filesystem::path::operator/`. On all platforms, if the value begins with `/` or contains `..` segments (e.g., `../../etc/cron.d/evil` or `/etc/passwd`), `operator/` discards `workDir` entirely or traverses outside it. There is zero canonicalization or containment check before `std::ofstream` / `std::ifstream` open the resulting path.
- **Impact:** An attacker who can submit a workflow task definition can write arbitrary content to any file writable by the process (enabling code execution via cron, SSH authorized_keys, shared libraries, etc.) or read any file readable by the process.
- **Fix:** After constructing the candidate path, call `std::filesystem::weakly_canonical()` on both the candidate and `workDir`, then assert the candidate path starts with `workDir`. Reject any path that escapes. Additionally, reject filenames containing `/` or `\` before joining.

---

### CRITICAL Path Traversal via `spreadsheetId` and `range` in URL Construction

- **Location:** `GoogleSheetsCloudTaskExecutor::ExecuteCloud`, URL assembly lines for both read (~line 275) and write (~line 355): `apiBase + "/" + spreadsheetId + "/values/" + UrlEncode(range)`
- **Issue:** `spreadsheetId` is taken from task params JSON (attacker-controlled) and interpolated directly into the URL without any encoding or character-set validation. A value such as `../drives` or `../../admin/...` causes path traversal within the Google API URL. More critically, because `spreadsheetId` is not passed through `UrlEncode`, it can inject URL path separators, query string delimiters (`?`, `#`, `&`), or even change the host component if `apiBase` is also attacker-influenced via `connection.m_Params`.
- **Impact:** Attacker can redirect the HTTP request to an unintended Google API endpoint, potentially accessing or modifying resources beyond the intended spreadsheet. If `apiBase` is also attacker-controlled, this becomes full SSRF.
- **Fix:** Apply `UrlEncode` to `spreadsheetId`. Additionally, validate that `spreadsheetId` matches the documented Google Sheets ID format (`^[a-zA-Z0-9_-]{20,60}$`) before use.

---

### CRITICAL SSRF via Attacker-Controlled `apiBase` URL

- **Location:** `GoogleSheetsCloudTaskExecutor::ExecuteCloud`, `apiBase = GoogleSheetsConnector::GetApiBaseUrl(connection)` (~line 260); the result is used unconditionally in `SheetsRequest` which calls `curl_easy_perform`
- **Issue:** `GetApiBaseUrl` derives the base URL from `connection`, which appears to be user/operator-configurable. If the connection's base URL parameter is attacker-controllable, the final URL passed to libcurl is fully attacker-controlled. `CURLOPT_FOLLOWLOCATION` is enabled, amplifying the impact. There is no allowlist or scheme/host validation performed in this file.
- **Impact:** Full SSRF: an attacker can force the workflow engine to make HTTP requests to internal services (cloud metadata endpoints, internal APIs, `file://`, `gopher://`, etc.) and potentially exfiltrate credentials or pivot into the internal network.
- **Fix:** Validate that `apiBase` begins with `https://sheets.googleapis.com` (hardcode the prefix or use a strict allowlist). Disable `CURLOPT_PROTOCOLS` to restrict to HTTPS only: `curl_easy_setopt(curl, CURLOPT_PROTOCOLS, CURLPROTO_HTTPS)`. Disable or limit redirects: set `CURLOPT_MAXREDIRS` to 0 or 1 and validate the redirect target.

---

### HIGH Missing TLS Certificate Verification Fallback

- **Location:** `SheetsRequest` function, CAINFO block (~line 230)
- **Issue:** `CURLOPT_CAINFO` is only set when `CurlWrapper::GetCaBundlePath()` returns a non-empty string. When it returns empty, no CA bundle path is provided. Depending on the build and system configuration, libcurl may fall back to no verification or a system bundle that is absent (common in container/embedded environments). Crucially, `CURLOPT_SSL_VERIFYPEER` and `CURLOPT_SSL_VERIFYHOST` are never explicitly set to `1L` / `2L`, so their defaults must be relied upon.
- **Impact:** In environments where the system CA bundle is absent or misconfigured, TLS connections to Google's API proceed without certificate verification, enabling man-in-the-middle attacks that can steal OAuth tokens and spreadsheet data.
- **Fix:** Explicitly set `curl_easy_setopt(curl, CURLOPT_SSL_VERIFYPEER, 1L)` and `curl_easy_setopt(curl, CURLOPT_SSL_VERIFYHOST, 2L)` unconditionally. If `GetCaBundlePath()` returns empty, either fail the request or document that the system bundle must be present; do not silently proceed.

---

### HIGH `valueInputOption` Injected into URL Without Validation

- **Location:** `ExecuteCloud`, write block URL construction (~line 355): `"?valueInputOption=" + valueInputOption`
- **Issue:** `valueInputOption` is taken from task params JSON and concatenated directly into the query string without encoding or allowlist validation. A value containing `&`, `=`, or `#` injects additional query parameters or fragments. For example, `USER_ENTERED&key=attacker_key` would append an attacker-chosen API key, potentially routing billing or bypassing the intended credential. The valid set is exactly `{"USER_ENTERED", "RAW"}`.
- **Impact:** Query-string injection; in the worst case, credential substitution or unexpected API behavior.
- **Fix:** Enforce an explicit allowlist: `if (valueInputOption != "USER_ENTERED" && valueInputOption != "RAW") { /* fail */ }`. Do not interpolate any unvalidated string into a URL.

---

### HIGH API Key Exposed in URL (Logged or Cached by Intermediaries)

- **Location:** `SheetsRequest`, `finalUrl += "key=" + credentials.m_Token` (~line 215)
- **Issue:** When `useApiKeyParam` is true, the API key is appended to the URL as a query parameter. URLs are routinely recorded in server access logs, proxy logs, browser history, Referer headers on redirects (`CURLOPT_FOLLOWLOCATION` is enabled), and curl's internal error strings. The `credentials.m_Token` value will appear in plaintext in any of those locations.
- **Impact:** API key leakage via log files, proxy infrastructure, or HTTP Referer headers sent on redirects.
- **Fix:** Prefer the `Authorization` header for all auth types. If query-parameter auth is required by the API, disable `CURLOPT_FOLLOWLOCATION` or strip credentials before following redirects, and ensure logging infrastructure redacts query parameters containing `key=`.

---

### HIGH Error Response Body Reflected into `taskState.m_LastErrorMessage` Without Sanitization

- **Location:** `ExecuteCloud`, read error block (~line 300) and write error block (~line 370): `taskState.m_LastErrorMessage += ": " + responseBody`
- **Issue:** Up to 499 bytes of the raw HTTP response body from Google's API (or a spoofed server in the SSRF scenario) are concatenated verbatim into `taskState.m_LastErrorMessage`. If this field is subsequently rendered in a UI, stored in a database, or emitted in a log without escaping, the attacker-influenced response body can inject content. Control characters, JSON-breaking characters, or HTML/script content may propagate.
- **Impact:** Stored XSS or log injection if `m_LastErrorMessage` reaches a rendering context; also exposes internal error detail to downstream consumers.
- **Fix:** Sanitize or truncate the response body before inclusion (strip non-printable characters, encode for the target context). Consider omitting the raw server response from user-visible error messages entirely and logging it separately at DEBUG level.

---

### MEDIUM Uncontrolled Memory Allocation via Unbounded `responseBody`

- **Location:** `SheetsRequest`, `writeCallback` lambda that appends to `responseBody` (~line 205); caller in `ExecuteCloud` passes `responseBody` by reference
- **Issue:** The `writeCallback` appends all response bytes into a `std::string` with no size limit. A malicious or malfunctioning server can stream an arbitrarily large response, causing unbounded heap growth until the process is OOM-killed or the system is destabilized. `CURLOPT_MAXFILESIZE` / `CURLOPT_MAXFILESIZE_LARGE` are not set.
- **Impact:** Denial of service against the workflow engine process.
- **Fix:** Add a size cap inside `writeCallback`: if `buf->size() + size * nmemb > kMaxResponseBytes` (e.g., 10 MB), return 0 to abort the transfer. Also set `curl_easy_setopt(curl, CURLOPT_MAXFILESIZE_LARGE, (curl_off_t)kMaxResponseBytes)`.

---

### MEDIUM Uncontrolled Memory Allocation via Unbounded CSV File Read

- **Location:** `ExecuteCloud`, write block, `while (std::getline(in, line))` loop (~line 345)
- **Issue:** The entire contents of `inputFile` are read into `values` (a `vector<vector<string>>`) with no limit on file size, number of rows, or number of columns. An attacker who can write a large file into `workDir` (or control `inputFile` via path traversal) can force unbounded allocation.
- **Impact:** Denial of service via memory exhaustion.
- **Fix:** Enforce a maximum row count and maximum field length during parsing; fail with a descriptive error if limits are exceeded.

---

### MEDIUM `operation` Field Not Strictly Validated — Logic Default Silently Assumed

- **Location:** `ExecuteCloud`, ~line 255: `if (operation.empty()) operation = "read";`
- **Issue:** If `operation` is absent from the params JSON, the code silently defaults to `"read"`. This means a task definition that omits `operation` is treated as a read, which may violate the principle of explicit authorization — a write-only connection could be used for reads without the workflow author intending it.
- **Impact:** Unexpected data exfiltration if the workflow engine is used in a multi-tenant context where task params are partially controlled by different principals.
- **Fix:** Require `operation` to be explicitly present and validated against the allowlist `{"read", "write"}`; fail if absent or unrecognized.

---

### LOW `range` Value Logged Without Sanitization

- **Location:** `ExecuteCloud`, `LOG_APP_INFO("[sheets] read {} rows from range '{}'", rows.size(), range)` and the write equivalent (~lines 315, 380)
- **Issue:** `range` is attacker-controlled task param content logged verbatim. If `LOG_APP_INFO` expands to a format-string-based logger and the range contains `%` characters or newlines, it may cause log injection (fake log lines) or, depending on the logger implementation, a format-string vulnerability.
- **Impact:** Log injection; potential format-string vulnerability if the logger is not type-safe.
- **Fix:** Since the fmt-style `{}` placeholder is used, format-string injection is avoided in modern loggers, but newline characters in `range` should still be stripped or escaped before logging to prevent log-line spoofing.


---

### 26_doc_application_cloud_googleSheetsConnector_h

<a id="26-doc-application-cloud-googlesheetsconnector-h"></a>

*Source doc:* `../../../queue/jarvisCppCyberSecAudit/26_doc_application_cloud_googleSheetsConnector_h/docu.output.md`



### HIGH SSRF via unvalidated user-controlled endpoint
- **Location:** `GoogleSheetsConnector::GetApiBaseUrl` / `TestConnection`, lines ~85–95 and ~150–155
- **Issue:** `connection.m_Endpoint` is accepted without any validation and used verbatim as the base URL for the outbound HTTP request. An attacker who can write a `CloudConnection` record (via the workflow engine's configuration interface, API, or a compromised upstream component) can set `m_Endpoint` to any arbitrary URL, including `http://169.254.169.254/` (cloud IMDS), `http://localhost:6379/` (Redis), internal Kubernetes service addresses, or any other host reachable from the server.
- **Impact:** Full SSRF: credential exfiltration from cloud IMDS, lateral movement to internal services, potential RCE via internal APIs (e.g., reaching an unauthenticated admin interface).
- **Fix:** Enforce an allowlist of acceptable base URLs (at minimum, require the scheme to be `https` and the host to match `*.googleapis.com`). Reject or refuse to use any endpoint that does not match. Example check before `return base;`: verify `base.rfind("https://sheets.googleapis.com", 0) == 0`.

---

### HIGH API key leaked in URL and potentially in logs/error messages
- **Location:** `GoogleSheetsConnector::TestConnection`, lines ~160–165
- **Issue:** When `useApiKeyParam` is true the raw API key (`credentials.m_Token`) is appended directly to the URL string: `url += "&key=" + credentials.m_Token`. That URL is then passed to `curl_easy_setopt(curl, CURLOPT_URL, ...)`. libcurl logs the full URL in debug output; any HTTP proxy, access log, or libcurl verbose trace will record the key in plaintext. Additionally, if `curl_easy_strerror` or any other error path ever echoes the URL, the key is in the error message.
- **Impact:** API key exposure in server logs, proxy logs, or monitoring systems, enabling unauthorized access to the Google Sheets API under the victim's quota and permissions.
- **Fix:** Transmit the API key as a custom HTTP header (e.g., `X-Goog-Api-Key: <key>`) or, if the query-parameter form is required, construct the URL without the key for logging purposes and set the key only immediately before `curl_easy_perform` without storing it in the reusable `url` string. At minimum, never include the key in any string that is passed to error-message construction.

---

### HIGH Unvalidated `spreadsheet_id` injected into URL path
- **Location:** `GoogleSheetsConnector::TestConnection`, line ~158
- **Issue:** `spreadsheetIdIt->second` (the value of the `spreadsheet_id` parameter) is concatenated directly into the URL path: `apiBase + "/" + spreadsheetIdIt->second + "?fields=..."`. No sanitization is applied. A value such as `../../other-resource` or `id?injected=1&key=attacker_key#` can alter the path or query string of the outgoing request.
- **Impact:** Combined with the SSRF finding above, path traversal in the URL can redirect the request to unintended Google API endpoints or, with a hostile `m_Endpoint`, to unintended internal paths.
- **Fix:** Validate that `spreadsheet_id` matches the expected format (Google Sheets IDs are alphanumeric with hyphens and underscores, typically 44 characters). Reject any value not matching `^[A-Za-z0-9_-]+$` before constructing the URL. URL-encode the value via `curl_easy_escape` as an additional layer.

---

### HIGH Missing TLS certificate verification fallback
- **Location:** `GoogleSheetsConnector::TestConnection`, lines ~170–175
- **Issue:** `CURLOPT_CAINFO` is only set when `CurlWrapper::GetCaBundlePath()` returns a non-empty string. When it returns empty, libcurl uses its compiled-in default CA bundle — or, on some builds/platforms, may have no bundle at all — without any explicit check that peer verification remains enabled. `CURLOPT_SSL_VERIFYPEER` and `CURLOPT_SSL_VERIFYHOST` are never set to `1L` (the safe value), so if libcurl's defaults differ (e.g., a custom build, embedded environment, or older version where defaults changed) TLS verification may be silently disabled.
- **Impact:** Man-in-the-middle attack: an attacker on the network path can intercept the HTTPS connection, read the Bearer token or OAuth2 access token, and modify the response.
- **Fix:** Explicitly set `curl_easy_setopt(curl, CURLOPT_SSL_VERIFYPEER, 1L)` and `curl_easy_setopt(curl, CURLOPT_SSL_VERIFYHOST, 2L)` unconditionally before the `CURLOPT_CAINFO` block. If `GetCaBundlePath()` is empty and you cannot locate a bundle, fail closed rather than proceeding.

---

### MEDIUM Unbounded response body accumulation (DoS)
- **Location:** `GoogleSheetsConnector::TestConnection`, write callback lambda, lines ~140–145
- **Issue:** The `writeCallback` lambda appends all received bytes into `responseBody` with no size limit. A malicious or compromised server (reachable via the SSRF vector) can stream an arbitrarily large response body, causing unbounded heap allocation.
- **Impact:** Memory exhaustion / process crash (denial of service). Elevated risk because the endpoint is attacker-controllable (see SSRF finding).
- **Fix:** Inside `writeCallback`, check `buf->size() + (size * nmemb)` against a reasonable cap (e.g., 1 MB for a metadata endpoint). Return 0 (or a value ≠ `size * nmemb`) to abort the transfer if the limit is exceeded.

---

### MEDIUM `connection.m_Name` and `connection.m_KeyName` reflected unsanitized into error messages
- **Location:** `GoogleSheetsConnector::ResolveCredentials`, lines ~110, ~120, ~130
- **Issue:** `connection.m_Name` and `connection.m_KeyName` are user-supplied strings embedded directly into `errorMessage` without sanitization. If error messages are forwarded to logs or HTTP responses, this enables log injection (newline injection to forge log entries) or, depending on how the UI renders `errorMessage`, reflected content injection.
- **Impact:** Log injection allowing forged audit entries; potential downstream injection if error strings are rendered in a web UI without escaping.
- **Fix:** Strip or escape newline characters (`\n`, `\r`) and other control characters from `m_Name` and `m_KeyName` before embedding them in error strings. Consider logging structured fields separately rather than interpolating untrusted values into message strings.

---

### LOW `CURLOPT_FOLLOWLOCATION` enabled without redirect limit
- **Location:** `GoogleSheetsConnector::TestConnection`, line ~178
- **Issue:** `CURLOPT_FOLLOWLOCATION` is set to `1L` but `CURLOPT_MAXREDIRS` is never set, leaving it at libcurl's default (typically 30). Combined with the SSRF and unvalidated endpoint issues, an attacker can craft redirect chains that hop through internal networks or exhaust resources.
- **Impact:** Amplifies SSRF impact; mild resource consumption.
- **Fix:** Set `curl_easy_setopt(curl, CURLOPT_MAXREDIRS, 3L)` and, once the endpoint is constrained to `*.googleapis.com`, consider disabling redirects entirely (`CURLOPT_MAXREDIRS, 0L`) since Google APIs do not require them for this call.


---

### 27_doc_application_cloud_jiraCloudTaskExecutor_h

<a id="27-doc-application-cloud-jiracloudtaskexecutor-h"></a>

*Source doc:* `../../../queue/jarvisCppCyberSecAudit/27_doc_application_cloud_jiraCloudTaskExecutor_h/docu.output.md`



### CRITICAL — URL injection / SSRF via unvalidated `baseUrl` and `issueKey`

- **Location:** `JiraCloudTaskExecutor::ExecuteCloud`, all five operation branches; `JiraRequest` (~line 110)
- **Issue:** `baseUrl` comes from `JiraConnector::GetBaseUrl(connection)` without any validation. `issueKey` and `transitionId` are user-supplied strings appended directly into the URL with plain string concatenation (e.g., `baseUrl + "/rest/api/3/issue/" + issueKey`). Neither is URL-encoded nor checked against an allowlist or regex. An attacker who controls connection parameters or task params can craft `issueKey` values such as `PROJ-1/../../admin/anything` (path traversal within the Jira URL), or supply a `baseUrl` that points to an internal network address (SSRF). `CURLOPT_FOLLOWLOCATION` is enabled unconditionally, compounding SSRF risk by following redirects to internal endpoints.
- **Impact:** Full SSRF to any host reachable by the engine process; leakage of cloud credentials to an attacker-controlled server; path traversal within the Jira REST hierarchy reaching unintended API endpoints.
- **Fix:** (1) Validate `baseUrl` against an allowlist or at minimum enforce `https://` scheme and a known hostname. (2) Validate `issueKey` against the regex `^[A-Z][A-Z0-9_]+-[0-9]+$` before use. (3) URL-encode all path segments using `curl_easy_escape`. (4) Disable `CURLOPT_FOLLOWLOCATION` or restrict it to the same origin. (5) Set `CURLOPT_PROTOCOLS` to `CURLPROTO_HTTPS` only.

---

### CRITICAL — Path traversal via `description_file` with no canonicalization or sandbox check

- **Location:** `JiraCloudTaskExecutor::ExecuteCloud`, create-operation branch, ~line 230
- **Issue:** `descriptionFile = getStringParam("description_file")` is opened directly with `std::ifstream in(descriptionFile)` after no validation whatsoever. Any path accepted by the task definition — including `../../etc/passwd`, `/proc/self/environ`, or any absolute path — is read and its contents placed into the Jira issue description (and potentially into `taskState.m_LastErrorMessage` on failure, which may be logged).
- **Impact:** An attacker who controls task parameters can exfiltrate any file readable by the engine process, including private keys, credential files, or other secrets, by observing the created Jira issue's description field.
- **Fix:** Resolve `descriptionFile` to a canonical path with `std::filesystem::canonical` (or `weakly_canonical` + existence check), then assert it is a strict child of the workflow's working directory. Reject any path that escapes that boundary before opening the file. Impose a maximum file read size (e.g., 1 MB) to prevent DoS.

---

### HIGH — `fieldsJson` injected verbatim into Jira request body without parsing or sanitization

- **Location:** `JiraCloudTaskExecutor::ExecuteCloud`, update-operation branch, ~line 295
- **Issue:** `fieldsJson = getStringParam("fields")` is read as a plain string (not as a JSON sub-object) and concatenated directly into `requestBody` as `"{\"fields\":" + fieldsJson + "}"`. No structural validation is performed. Because `getStringParam` reads a JSON *string value* (not a raw object), this expects the caller to embed a JSON object serialized as a JSON string — an inherently fragile pattern. If the engine ever sources this from a route that does not enforce the string-value wrapper, the raw param value is injected into the outgoing JSON body.
- **Impact:** JSON injection into the Jira API request; potential for manipulating the request structure to set arbitrary fields (e.g., security level, reporter, assignee) or to trigger Jira-side parsing anomalies.
- **Fix:** Read `fields` as a `simdjson::ondemand::object` directly from the document, serialize it properly, or at minimum parse the string value through a JSON validator before re-embedding. Never concatenate unvalidated text into a JSON structure.

---

### HIGH — Unbounded response body accumulation enables memory exhaustion (DoS)

- **Location:** `JiraRequest`, write callback lambda, ~line 115
- **Issue:** The `writeCallback` appends every byte libcurl delivers into `responseBody` (a `std::string*`) without any size cap. A malicious or compromised Jira server, or an SSRF target, can stream an arbitrarily large response causing the engine process to exhaust heap memory.
- **Impact:** Remote denial-of-service; potential for OOM-kill of the workflow engine process, disrupting all concurrent workflows.
- **Fix:** Enforce a maximum response size inside `writeCallback`: if `buf->size() + size * nmemb` exceeds a limit (e.g., 10 MB), return 0 to abort the transfer. Alternatively set `CURLOPT_MAXFILESIZE_LARGE`.

---

### HIGH — Missing TLS peer and host verification (conditional CA bundle, no explicit verification flags)

- **Location:** `JiraRequest`, ~lines 130–135
- **Issue:** `CURLOPT_SSL_VERIFYPEER` and `CURLOPT_SSL_VERIFYHOST` are never set explicitly. If `CurlWrapper::GetCaBundlePath()` returns an empty string the CA bundle option is silently skipped, leaving libcurl to use whatever system default exists — or in some builds/platforms, none at all. There is no code path that would fail-closed if verification is unavailable.
- **Impact:** Credential interception (Basic auth username/password or Bearer token) via TLS stripping or MITM if the CA bundle is absent or misconfigured.
- **Fix:** Unconditionally set `CURLOPT_SSL_VERIFYPEER` to `1L` and `CURLOPT_SSL_VERIFYHOST` to `2L`. If `caBundle` is empty, fail the request rather than proceeding without a known-good trust anchor.

---

### MEDIUM — Jira error response body echoed into error messages (potential secrets/PII leakage)

- **Location:** `JiraCloudTaskExecutor::ExecuteCloud`, multiple HTTP-error branches, e.g., ~lines 275, 315
- **Issue:** When `httpCode >= 400` and `responseBody.size() < 500`, the raw Jira API response is appended to `taskState.m_LastErrorMessage`. Jira error responses can contain field values, user data, or internal identifiers that are then stored in task state and potentially surfaced in logs or UI.
- **Impact:** Sensitive data from the Jira instance (field contents, user email addresses, internal IDs) may leak into engine logs or be returned to workflow callers.
- **Fix:** Log the raw response body at DEBUG level only, and surface only a sanitized or structured message to `m_LastErrorMessage`. At minimum strip the body before storing it in task state.

---

### MEDIUM — `issueKey` and `transitionId` logged without sanitization (log injection)

- **Location:** `JiraCloudTaskExecutor::ExecuteCloud`, LOG_APP_INFO calls, ~lines 283, 335, 350, 365, 380
- **Issue:** `issueKey`, `transitionId`, `projectKey`, and `operation` are embedded directly into structured log messages. If LOG_APP_INFO writes to a plain-text log sink, these values can contain embedded newlines, ANSI escape codes, or log-forging sequences (e.g., `\nINFO [jira] admin action performed`).
- **Impact:** Log injection enabling falsification of audit trail; potential terminal escape injection if logs are viewed in a terminal.
- **Fix:** Validate `issueKey` against `^[A-Z][A-Z0-9_]+-[0-9]+$` before use (which also fixes the SSRF issue above). For other fields, strip or encode control characters before logging.

---

### MEDIUM — `description_file` path appears in error message stored in task state

- **Location:** `JiraCloudTaskExecutor::ExecuteCloud`, ~line 235
- **Issue:** On failure to open `descriptionFile`, the full filesystem path is embedded in `taskState.m_LastErrorMessage`. This path may reveal internal directory structure, deployment layout, or other sensitive environment details.
- **Impact:** Information disclosure of internal server file paths to workflow callers or log consumers.
- **Fix:** Log the full path at DEBUG level internally; store only a generic message such as `"description_file not readable"` in `m_LastErrorMessage`.

---

### LOW — `CURLOPT_POSTFIELDS` used for non-POST methods (PUT) without `CURLOPT_POSTFIELDSIZE`

- **Location:** `JiraRequest`, ~line 140
- **Issue:** `CURLOPT_POSTFIELDS` is set regardless of the HTTP method. For the `PUT` method (used in update), libcurl will use the null-terminated length of `requestBody.c_str()`. If `requestBody` contains embedded null bytes (possible if a crafted `fieldsJson` contains `\u0000`), the body will be silently truncated at the first null.
- **Impact:** Garbled or truncated Jira update requests; in edge cases, a partial field update that bypasses intended field values.
- **Fix:** Always set `CURLOPT_POSTFIELDSIZE` to `static_cast<long>(requestBody.size())` when setting `CURLOPT_POSTFIELDS`, ensuring the full byte range is transmitted regardless of null bytes.


---

### 28_doc_application_cloud_jiraConnector_h

<a id="28-doc-application-cloud-jiraconnector-h"></a>

*Source doc:* `../../../queue/jarvisCppCyberSecAudit/28_doc_application_cloud_jiraConnector_h/docu.output.md`



### HIGH Missing TLS peer and host verification
- **Location:** `JiraConnector::TestConnection`, curl setup block (~line 130–145)
- **Issue:** `CURLOPT_SSL_VERIFYPEER` and `CURLOPT_SSL_VERIFYHOST` are never set to their non-default values, and the CA bundle is only applied when `CurlWrapper::GetCaBundlePath()` returns a non-empty string. If that function returns an empty string (misconfiguration, missing file, platform without a default bundle), libcurl falls back to its compiled-in default, which may be absent or wrong. More critically, there is no explicit `curl_easy_setopt(curl, CURLOPT_SSL_VERIFYPEER, 1L)` / `CURLOPT_SSL_VERIFYHOST, 2L` to assert that verification must succeed; a build-time or runtime flag that disables verification would silently pass.
- **Impact:** An attacker on the network path can perform a TLS man-in-the-middle attack, intercept Jira credentials (Basic Auth password or Bearer token), and replay them to the real Jira instance.
- **Fix:** Unconditionally set `CURLOPT_SSL_VERIFYPEER` to `1L` and `CURLOPT_SSL_VERIFYHOST` to `2L` immediately after `curl_easy_init()`. If `caBundle` is empty, fail fast with an error rather than proceeding without an explicit CA store. Do not allow the CA bundle path to be silently skipped.

---

### HIGH Uncontrolled response body growth (DoS / memory exhaustion)
- **Location:** `JiraConnector::TestConnection`, `writeCallback` lambda and `responseBody` string (~line 120–125)
- **Issue:** The write callback appends all data returned by the server into `responseBody` with no size cap. `CURLOPT_MAXFILESIZE` and `CURLOPT_MAXFILESIZE_LARGE` are not set. A malicious or compromised Jira endpoint (or an SSRF-redirected server; see below) can stream an arbitrarily large response, causing unbounded heap allocation.
- **Impact:** An attacker who controls the endpoint or can redirect the request can exhaust process memory, causing a crash or denial of service of the workflow engine.
- **Fix:** Add a size guard inside the write callback: if `buf->size() + size * nmemb` exceeds a reasonable limit (e.g. 1 MB for a `/myself` response), return 0 to abort the transfer. Also set `CURLOPT_MAXFILESIZE_LARGE` as a defense-in-depth measure.

---

### HIGH SSRF via unvalidated endpoint URL
- **Location:** `JiraConnector::GetBaseUrl` and `JiraConnector::TestConnection` (~line 80 and ~line 115)
- **Issue:** `connection.m_Endpoint` is used directly to construct the request URL after only stripping a trailing slash. There is no validation that the scheme is `https://`, that the host is not a private/loopback address, or that the URL is otherwise within an expected domain. `CURLOPT_FOLLOWLOCATION` is also enabled, which allows the server to redirect the request to an internal address.
- **Impact:** An operator or attacker who can supply a connection configuration can direct requests to internal services (cloud metadata endpoints, internal APIs, localhost), exfiltrating instance credentials or probing the internal network.
- **Fix:** Validate that the endpoint starts with `https://` and, if a whitelist of allowed hosts is feasible, enforce it. Disable `CURLOPT_FOLLOWLOCATION` or set `CURLOPT_MAXREDIRS` to 0 for credential-carrying requests. Consider using `CURLOPT_PROTOCOLS` / `CURLOPT_REDIR_PROTOCOLS` to restrict to HTTPS only.

---

### MEDIUM Credentials reflected in error messages
- **Location:** `JiraConnector::ResolveCredentials` and `JiraConnector::TestConnection`, error string construction (~line 93–100 and ~165–170)
- **Issue:** `errorMessage` strings embed `connection.m_KeyName` and, in the failure branch at ~line 93, describe which credential fields are populated (`"has no credentials — Jira requires email+API token or a PAT"`). While the secret values themselves are not directly placed in `errorMessage`, `connection.m_Name` and `connection.m_KeyName` are included. If error messages are logged at a level visible to non-privileged users or forwarded to external systems, they reveal the internal key name and connection name, aiding enumeration.
- **Impact:** Low-privilege users or log aggregation systems learn internal key manager identifiers, facilitating targeted credential theft or configuration enumeration.
- **Fix:** Ensure error messages returned to callers are not written verbatim into user-facing logs or API responses without sanitization. Consider using opaque error codes for external output and retaining the descriptive string only in internal/audit logs.

---

### MEDIUM Bearer token exposed in HTTP header string
- **Location:** `JiraConnector::TestConnection`, Bearer header construction (~line 152)
- **Issue:** `std::string authHeader = "Authorization: Bearer " + credentials.m_Token;` creates a heap-allocated string containing the raw token. The string persists until `curl_slist_free_all` but is not zeroed afterward. Additionally, if `credentials.m_Token` is ever involved in an exception unwind or a crash dump, the token is recoverable from memory.
- **Impact:** A memory disclosure vulnerability elsewhere in the process, or a core dump, can expose the Bearer token.
- **Fix:** After `curl_slist_free_all(headers)`, explicitly zero the `authHeader` buffer (`std::fill(authHeader.begin(), authHeader.end(), '\0')`). Apply the same treatment to `credentials.m_Password` and `credentials.m_Token` after use.

---

### LOW curl handle leaked on early return path (resource exhaustion)
- **Location:** `JiraConnector::TestConnection`, between `curl_easy_init()` and `curl_easy_cleanup()` (~line 118–185)
- **Issue:** If any `curl_easy_setopt` call were to be followed by an early return (e.g. a future refactor inserting a guard), `curl` would leak. More concretely, the current code has no RAII guard around the handle; adding one defensive early-return in the future would silently leak the handle. This is a latent resource exhaustion risk under repeated calls.
- **Impact:** File descriptor and memory exhaustion if handles leak under error conditions, potentially enabling denial of service.
- **Fix:** Wrap the `CURL*` handle in an RAII type (a `std::unique_ptr` with a custom deleter calling `curl_easy_cleanup`) immediately after `curl_easy_init()` succeeds. Apply the same pattern to `curl_slist*`.


---

### 29_doc_application_cloud_oneDriveCloudTaskExecutor_h

<a id="29-doc-application-cloud-onedrivecloudtaskexecutor-h"></a>

*Source doc:* `../../../queue/jarvisCppCyberSecAudit/29_doc_application_cloud_oneDriveCloudTaskExecutor_h/docu.output.md`



### CRITICAL Path Traversal via `local_path` Parameter

- **Location:** `OneDriveCloudTaskExecutor::ExecuteCloud`, upload branch (~line 210) and download branch (~line 240)
- **Issue:** `localPath` is taken directly from task params JSON and appended to the working directory with `workDir / localPath` without any validation that the resulting path stays within `workDir`. A value such as `../../etc/passwd` or `../../../tmp/evil` will silently escape the intended working directory after `std::filesystem::path` normalization.
- **Impact:** On upload, an attacker who can submit workflow task params can read any file accessible to the process and exfiltrate it to OneDrive. On download, they can overwrite arbitrary files the process can write, including binaries, configuration, or credentials.
- **Fix:** After constructing `fullLocalPath`, canonicalize both paths and assert containment:
  ```
  auto canonical = std::filesystem::weakly_canonical(fullLocalPath);
  auto base = std::filesystem::weakly_canonical(workDir);
  if (canonical.string().find(base.string()) != 0) { /* reject */ }
  ```
  Additionally reject `localPath` values that are absolute paths before composing.

---

### CRITICAL Path Traversal via `remote_path` in URL Construction (SSRF / Injection)

- **Location:** `OneDriveCloudTaskExecutor::ExecuteCloud`, ~lines 222 and 251; `GraphRequest` and `GraphDownload`
- **Issue:** `remotePath` from task params JSON is concatenated directly into the Microsoft Graph API URL without percent-encoding or any character validation: `graphBase + "/me/drive/root:/" + remotePath + ":/content"`. A value containing `../` sequences, `?`, `#`, or other special URL characters can redirect the request to a different Graph API endpoint or append attacker-controlled query parameters.
- **Impact:** An attacker can call arbitrary Microsoft Graph API endpoints authenticated with the workflow's bearer token—listing directory contents, accessing other drives, modifying permissions, or accessing other user data accessible to the app's OAuth scope.
- **Fix:** Percent-encode `remotePath` with `curl_easy_escape` before embedding it in the URL. Reject values containing `..` path components or that do not match an expected path pattern (`[A-Za-z0-9_./ -]+`).

---

### HIGH Uncontrolled File Read into Memory on Upload (DoS / Memory Exhaustion)

- **Location:** `OneDriveCloudTaskExecutor::ExecuteCloud`, upload branch, ~lines 213–218
- **Issue:** The entire file identified by `fullLocalPath` is read into a `std::string` in memory (`std::string fileData(static_cast<size_t>(fileSize), '\0')`) with no size limit. `kMaxDownloadBytes` (256 MB) is applied to downloads but there is no analogous cap on uploads.
- **Impact:** A task param pointing at `/dev/zero` or a very large local file causes unbounded heap allocation, crashing the process or triggering OOM-kill, constituting a denial-of-service.
- **Fix:** Check `fileSize` against a compile-time maximum (e.g. the same `kMaxDownloadBytes`) before allocating, and fail the task with a clear error if exceeded. For truly large files, use the OneDrive large-file upload session API rather than loading into memory.

---

### HIGH Bearer Token Injected into HTTP Header Without Validation

- **Location:** `GraphRequest` (~line 153) and `GraphDownload` (~line 182)
- **Issue:** `credentials.m_Token` is concatenated verbatim into the `Authorization` header string: `"Authorization: Bearer " + bearerToken`. If the token contains a newline (`\r\n`) followed by an additional header name, libcurl will split and inject an attacker-controlled header. While newer libcurl versions reject embedded newlines in header values, this is not uniformly true across all versions and is not validated in code.
- **Impact:** HTTP header injection; attacker-controlled OAuth token value can smuggle additional headers, potentially overriding security-relevant headers or poisoning request routing.
- **Fix:** Validate `bearerToken` against the expected Bearer token character set (`[A-Za-z0-9\-._~+/]+=*`) before use, and reject tokens containing whitespace or control characters.

---

### HIGH TLS Certificate Verification Conditionally Disabled

- **Location:** `GraphRequest` (~lines 140–144) and `GraphDownload` (~lines 172–176)
- **Issue:** `CURLOPT_SSL_VERIFYPEER` and `CURLOPT_SSL_VERIFYHOST` are never explicitly set. If the `CurlWrapper::GetCaBundlePath()` call returns an empty string (misconfiguration, missing file, or deployment environment without the bundle), no `CURLOPT_CAINFO` is set, and libcurl falls back to whatever system CA store is present—or, depending on build configuration, may skip verification entirely. There is no enforced minimum: the code silently proceeds without TLS peer verification in degraded environments.
- **Impact:** MITM attack on Microsoft Graph API requests; bearer tokens and file contents are exposed in transit.
- **Fix:** Always explicitly set `CURLOPT_SSL_VERIFYPEER, 1L` and `CURLOPT_SSL_VERIFYHOST, 2L`. If the CA bundle path is empty, fail the request rather than continuing without a known trust anchor.

---

### MEDIUM Partial Error Response Body Leaked into Task State

- **Location:** `OneDriveCloudTaskExecutor::ExecuteCloud`, upload branch, ~lines 228–231
- **Issue:** When an HTTP 4xx/5xx is returned by the Graph API, up to 500 bytes of the raw response body are appended to `taskState.m_LastErrorMessage`. Graph API error responses include structured JSON that may contain account identifiers, tenant IDs, internal correlation IDs, or diagnostic information.
- **Impact:** Internal Microsoft tenant or identity metadata is surfaced into workflow task state records, which may be logged, stored in a database, or returned through an API to less-privileged consumers.
- **Fix:** Log the raw response body only at a debug/trace level accessible to administrators, and expose only a sanitized error code (e.g. the HTTP status code and Graph `error.code` field) in `m_LastErrorMessage`.

---

### MEDIUM Manually Constructed JSON in `responseBody` Lacks Escaping

- **Location:** `OneDriveCloudTaskExecutor::ExecuteCloud`, download branch, ~line 257
- **Issue:** The synthetic success JSON is constructed by string concatenation: `"{\"ok\":true,\"operation\":\"download\",\"remote_path\":\"" + remotePath + "\"}"`. `remotePath` is attacker-controlled and is not JSON-escaped. A value containing `"` or `\` breaks the JSON structure.
- **Impact:** Downstream consumers of `taskState.m_CapturedStdout` or the written response file that parse this JSON may misinterpret the structure. Combined with other logic this could facilitate secondary injection attacks.
- **Fix:** Use a proper JSON serialization library (simdjson is already included; alternatively use a builder) rather than concatenating raw strings.

---

### MEDIUM `GraphDownload` Writes Error-Response Body to Output File

- **Location:** `GraphDownload`, ~lines 163–196
- **Issue:** The `FILE* fp` is opened before the HTTP response code is checked. If the server returns a 4xx or 5xx error, the error response body (which may include diagnostic data) is written to `outputPath` before the function returns `false`. The caller does not remove the partially-written file.
- **Impact:** A stale error-body file at `outputPath` may be consumed by subsequent workflow tasks as if it were valid content, or may overwrite an existing important file with garbage.
- **Fix:** Write to a temporary path, check the HTTP response code after transfer, and only rename to `outputPath` on success; delete the temporary file on failure.

---

### LOW Sensitive Paths Logged at INFO Level

- **Location:** `OneDriveCloudTaskExecutor::ExecuteCloud`, ~lines 234 and 261
- **Issue:** `LOG_APP_INFO` records the full absolute `fullLocalPath` and `remotePath`. These values come from user-supplied task params and may contain sensitive directory structures, project names, or file names that should not appear in general-purpose application logs.
- **Impact:** Log aggregation systems or personnel with log access can enumerate workflow data layouts and file naming conventions.
- **Fix:** Log only the filename component (`fullLocalPath.filename()`) and consider whether `remotePath` should be logged at a lower verbosity level.


---

### 30_doc_application_cloud_oneDriveConnector_h

<a id="30-doc-application-cloud-onedriveconnector-h"></a>

*Source doc:* `../../../queue/jarvisCppCyberSecAudit/30_doc_application_cloud_oneDriveConnector_h/docu.output.md`



### HIGH TLS Certificate Verification Not Enforced — CURLOPT_SSL_VERIFYPEER / CURLOPT_SSL_VERIFYHOST Absent

- **Location:** `OneDriveConnector::TestConnection`, approximately lines 155–175
- **Issue:** The `curl_easy_init` block sets `CURLOPT_CAINFO` only when `CurlWrapper::GetCaBundlePath()` returns a non-empty string. However, it never explicitly sets `CURLOPT_SSL_VERIFYPEER` to `1L` or `CURLOPT_SSL_VERIFYHOST` to `2L`. libcurl defaults these to enabled, but the code also never sets them to `0L`, so the concern is the conditional CA-bundle path: if `GetCaBundlePath()` returns empty (e.g., on a misconfigured host, in a Docker image without the bundle, or if `CurlWrapper` is refactored to return empty by default), libcurl will fall back to whatever system trust store it finds — or, more dangerously, if a caller or future maintainer adds `CURLOPT_SSL_VERIFYPEER, 0L` to "fix" the resulting handshake failures, the door is wide open. The correct posture is to unconditionally assert both options to secure values so the security property is explicit and cannot be silently degraded.
- **Impact:** If peer verification is disabled (either now in edge environments or after a future change), an attacker with a network-adjacent position can perform a TLS MITM, intercept the Bearer token sent in the `Authorization` header, and gain full OAuth-delegated access to the victim's OneDrive data.
- **Fix:** Immediately after `curl_easy_init`, unconditionally add:
  `curl_easy_setopt(curl, CURLOPT_SSL_VERIFYPEER, 1L);`
  `curl_easy_setopt(curl, CURLOPT_SSL_VERIFYHOST, 2L);`
  Then apply `CURLOPT_CAINFO` as an additive override when available. If `GetCaBundlePath()` is empty and no system store is reliably present, treat this as a fatal configuration error and return `false` before making any network call.

---

### HIGH SSRF via Unvalidated `connection.m_Endpoint` Used as HTTP Request Target

- **Location:** `OneDriveConnector::GetGraphBaseUrl` (~line 80) consumed in `TestConnection` (~line 143)
- **Issue:** `GetGraphBaseUrl` returns `connection.m_Endpoint` verbatim (after trimming a trailing slash) when it is non-empty. This user-supplied string is then concatenated directly with `/me/drive` and passed to `CURLOPT_URL`. No scheme validation, hostname allowlisting, or URL parsing is performed. Any value stored in `m_Endpoint` — such as `http://169.254.169.254/latest/meta-data` (AWS IMDS), `file:///etc/passwd`, `dict://`, `gopher://`, or an internal service URL — is accepted.
- **Impact:** An attacker who can write a connection record (e.g., via a compromised admin account, an insecure API endpoint, or a misconfigured multi-tenant data store) can redirect the outbound HTTP request to arbitrary internal hosts, cloud metadata services, or `file://` URIs. The response body is captured in `responseBody` and portions of it (up to 500 bytes) are reflected back in `errorMessage` on HTTP 4xx, creating a direct read primitive for SSRF exfiltration.
- **Fix:** Validate that `m_Endpoint` begins with `https://` and matches an allowlisted hostname suffix (e.g., `graph.microsoft.com` or a configurable override list). Reject connections where the parsed host does not match. Use a URL-parsing library rather than string prefix checks to avoid bypass via `https://evil.com@graph.microsoft.com/`.

---

### HIGH URL Injection via Unvalidated `tenantId` in `GetAuthorizeUrl` / `GetTokenUrl`

- **Location:** `OneDriveConnector::GetAuthorizeUrl` (~line 90), `OneDriveConnector::GetTokenUrl` (~line 97), `OneDriveConnector::GetOAuth2ProviderInfo` (~line 105)
- **Issue:** `tenantId` is taken from `connection.m_Params["tenant_id"]` and concatenated without encoding or validation directly into the Microsoft identity platform URL: `"https://login.microsoftonline.com/" + tenant + "/oauth2/v2.0/authorize"`. A value such as `evil.com/oauth2/v2.0/authorize#` or `common/../../../evil.com` manipulates the constructed URL. There is no check that the tenant ID is a valid GUID or one of the known string literals (`common`, `organizations`, `consumers`).
- **Impact:** If the resulting URL is used for a redirect or a token exchange request, an attacker can redirect OAuth flows to an arbitrary authorization server they control, capture authorization codes, and perform token theft or phishing against users of the workflow engine.
- **Fix:** Validate `tenantId` against a strict allowlist: accept only a UUID-formatted GUID (`[0-9a-fA-F-]{36}`) or the string literals `common`, `organizations`, `consumers`. Reject any other value before building the URL. Apply `curl_easy_escape` if the value is used in a query parameter context.

---

### MEDIUM Bearer Token Reflected in Error Message

- **Location:** `OneDriveConnector::TestConnection`, ~line 195 (`errorMessage += ": " + responseBody`)
- **Issue:** When the Graph API returns an HTTP 4xx response and `responseBody` is under 500 bytes, the raw response body is appended to `errorMessage`. Microsoft Graph error bodies frequently echo back request context. More critically, the `authHeader` string (`"Authorization: Bearer " + credentials.m_Token`) is constructed at ~line 165; while it is not directly appended to `errorMessage`, any logging layer that records `errorMessage` alongside request metadata risks capturing token material. Additionally, if a Graph API error response itself echoes back token-related fields (malformed request scenarios), they flow directly into `errorMessage`.
- **Impact:** If `errorMessage` is written to logs, returned via an API response, or stored in a database, it may expose OAuth Bearer tokens or sensitive API response content to parties who should not have access, enabling token replay attacks.
- **Fix:** Do not append raw API response bodies to user-visible or log-visible error messages. Parse the Graph API error JSON and include only the `error.code` and `error.message` fields after confirming they contain no credential material. Add a dedicated security log level that suppresses token values.

---

### MEDIUM Unbounded Response Body Accumulation — Potential DoS

- **Location:** `OneDriveConnector::TestConnection`, `writeCallback` lambda and `responseBody` (~lines 148–160)
- **Issue:** The `writeCallback` appends all received bytes into `responseBody` with no size cap enforced at the libcurl layer (`CURLOPT_MAXFILESIZE` or similar is not set). The 500-byte gate at ~line 195 only affects how much is included in the error message; it does not prevent the allocation itself. A server (including a malicious one reached via the SSRF vector above) can stream an arbitrarily large response.
- **Impact:** A hostile endpoint can cause the process to allocate gigabytes of heap memory, leading to OOM and denial of service for the entire workflow engine process.
- **Fix:** Set `CURLOPT_MAXFILESIZE` (or use `CURLOPT_MAXFILESIZE_LARGE`) to a small limit (e.g., 64 KB) for this diagnostic call, and/or add a size check inside `writeCallback` that returns 0 (triggering `CURLE_WRITE_ERROR`) once a threshold is exceeded.

---

### LOW `CURLOPT_FOLLOWLOCATION` Enabled Without Redirect Validation

- **Location:** `OneDriveConnector::TestConnection`, ~line 162
- **Issue:** `CURLOPT_FOLLOWLOCATION` is set to `1L` with no restriction on the number of redirects (`CURLOPT_MAXREDIRS` defaults to 30) and no restriction on redirect protocols (`CURLOPT_REDIR_PROTOCOLS` is unset, so in older libcurl versions all protocols including `file://` and `dict://` are permitted). Combined with the SSRF issue above, a redirect chain could be used to reach non-HTTP schemes.
- **Impact:** Amplifies the SSRF surface; a redirect from an `https://` URL to `file:///etc/shadow` would be followed automatically on libcurl builds where protocol restrictions are not the default.
- **Fix:** Set `CURLOPT_MAXREDIRS` to a small value (e.g., `3L`) and set `CURLOPT_REDIR_PROTOCOLS` to `CURLPROTO_HTTPS` only, ensuring redirects cannot escape to non-TLS or non-HTTP schemes.


---

### 31_doc_application_cloud_polarionConnector_h

<a id="31-doc-application-cloud-polarionconnector-h"></a>

*Source doc:* `../../../queue/jarvisCppCyberSecAudit/31_doc_application_cloud_polarionConnector_h/docu.output.md`



### HIGH URL Injection / SSRF via Unvalidated Endpoint

- **Location:** `PolarionConnector::TestConnection`, line ~72 (URL assembly block)
- **Issue:** `connection.m_Endpoint` is used directly to build the HTTP request URL with no validation of scheme, host, or port. An attacker who controls the connection configuration (via API, config file, or UI) can supply an arbitrary URL such as `http://169.254.169.254/latest/meta-data/` (cloud metadata), `file:///etc/passwd`, or an internal service address. The only normalization performed is stripping a trailing slash.
- **Impact:** Full SSRF: requests to internal network services, cloud metadata endpoints, or localhost ports. If `HttpGet` follows redirects, the attack surface widens further.
- **Fix:** Validate that `m_Endpoint` (a) has scheme `https://` only, (b) the hostname resolves to a non-private, non-loopback address (or is on an explicit allowlist), and (c) does not contain embedded credentials (`user:pass@host`). Reject or normalize at parse time before the URL is used.

---

### HIGH Credential Token Written Into Error Message (Secret Leakage)

- **Location:** `PolarionConnector::TestConnection`, line ~82
- **Issue:** When `client.HttpGet` fails, `errorMessage` (which was already populated by `HttpGet`) is concatenated with a prefix and re-exposed: `"Polarion connectivity test failed: " + errorMessage`. Depending on what `HttpGet` places in `errorMessage` on failure (e.g. curl error strings, HTTP 401 response bodies, or internal diagnostics that echo headers), the PAT stored in `credentials.m_Token` may be reflected back to the caller. Additionally, `ResolveCredentials` places API key metadata directly in `errorMessage` at lines ~97 and ~103 — these messages include `connection.m_KeyName` and the fact that no PAT is set, which aids enumeration.
- **Impact:** If `errorMessage` is returned to an HTTP client, logged, or stored, the PAT or its absence leaks to unprivileged callers; credential enumeration against the KeyManager is trivial.
- **Fix:** Ensure `HttpGet` never embeds token values in the error string. Sanitize `errorMessage` before propagation. Return generic failure messages to external callers and reserve detail for server-side structured logging with secrets redacted.

---

### HIGH Missing TLS Certificate Verification (Assumed, Needs Confirmation)

- **Location:** `PolarionConnector::TestConnection` → `client.HttpGet`, line ~79
- **Issue:** The `PolarionClient::HttpGet` implementation is not shown, but the connector passes a Polarion HTTPS URL and a Bearer token with no evidence that certificate verification is enforced or configurable. If `HttpGet` uses libcurl without `CURLOPT_SSL_VERIFYPEER`/`CURLOPT_SSL_VERIFYHOST` set to 1, or uses any HTTP library in a default-permissive configuration, the PAT is transmitted over a connection that cannot be authenticated.
- **Impact:** Network MITM attacker intercepts the Bearer token and gains full Polarion API access.
- **Fix:** Audit `PolarionClient::HttpGet` and assert that SSL peer and host verification are unconditionally enabled. Provide no runtime option to disable them. Enforce a minimum TLS version of 1.2.

---

### MEDIUM Unvalidated `project_id` Injected Into URL Path

- **Location:** `PolarionConnector::TestConnection`, line ~76
- **Issue:** `it->second` (the `project_id` parameter) is passed to `PolarionClient::UrlEncode` before insertion, which mitigates query-string injection. However, the encoded value is placed directly into the **URL path** segment (`/projects/<project_id>/workitems`). If `UrlEncode` does not encode `/` (RFC 3986 percent-encoding of `/` is optional and many encoders skip it), a `project_id` value of `foo/../admin` or `foo/../../other_project` traverses to an unintended path.
- **Impact:** Path traversal within the Polarion REST API: accessing project data outside the intended project, or triggering unintended API endpoints on the server.
- **Fix:** Verify that `UrlEncode` encodes `/` (i.e., produces `%2F`). Additionally, validate that `project_id` matches an expected character class (alphanumeric, hyphen, underscore) before encoding.

---

### MEDIUM Uncontrolled Error-Message Content from External HTTP Response

- **Location:** `PolarionConnector::TestConnection`, line ~82
- **Issue:** The `errorMessage` string returned by `HttpGet` on failure likely contains content derived from the HTTP response (status line, body excerpt, or server headers from the Polarion server). This externally-controlled string is concatenated without sanitization and returned to callers as part of j9t's own error message. If the caller logs or displays this value, it becomes a channel for log injection (CRLF or terminal escape sequences embedded by a malicious or compromised server) or for confusing operators.
- **Impact:** Log injection, misleading diagnostics, potential terminal escape injection in log viewers.
- **Fix:** Strip or encode CR, LF, and non-printable characters from any string sourced from an HTTP response before incorporating it into `errorMessage` or log entries.

---

### LOW Credential Key Name Enumeration via Distinct Error Messages

- **Location:** `PolarionConnector::ResolveCredentials`, lines ~97–103
- **Issue:** Two distinct error messages differentiate "key name not found in KeyManager" from "key found but has no API key value." This allows an attacker with the ability to submit connection configurations to enumerate which credential names exist in the KeyManager.
- **Impact:** Assists targeted credential theft or configuration probing.
- **Fix:** Collapse both conditions into a single message such as `"Credential resolution failed for Polarion connection"` without disclosing whether the name exists or the key value is absent.


---

### 32_doc_application_cloud_polarionWriteTaskExecutor_h

<a id="32-doc-application-cloud-polarionwritetaskexecutor-h"></a>

*Source doc:* `../../../queue/jarvisCppCyberSecAudit/32_doc_application_cloud_polarionWriteTaskExecutor_h/docu.output.md`



### CRITICAL Path Traversal via `field_value_file` and `file_path` Parameters

- **Location:** `PolarionWriteTaskExecutor::ExecuteCloud`, `field_value_file` handling (~line 175) and `file_path` handling for upload/download (~lines 220, 260)
- **Issue:** `fieldValueFile`, `filePath` (for upload), and `filePath` (for download) are taken directly from user-controlled task params JSON with zero path validation. No canonicalization, no check against an allowed base directory, no rejection of `..` components or absolute paths outside the workflow sandbox.
- **Impact:** An attacker who can submit workflow definitions can read arbitrary files on the server (e.g., `/etc/shadow`, private keys, other workflows' secrets) via `field_value_file` or `download_attachment`'s `file_path`, and write arbitrary files via `upload_attachment`'s `file_path` or `download_attachment`'s destination path.
- **Fix:** Resolve all file paths through `TaskPathResolver` and then verify the canonical result is a strict child of the resolved task working directory using `std::filesystem::canonical` and a prefix check before any file open or pass-through to `PolarionClient`.

---

### CRITICAL Unvalidated `operation` Reflected Into Error Message / Log Injection

- **Location:** `PolarionWriteTaskExecutor::ExecuteCloud`, unknown operation branch (~line 295)
- **Issue:** The raw `operation` string read from user-supplied JSON is directly embedded into `taskState.m_LastErrorMessage` and logged without sanitization. If `operation` contains newlines, CRLF sequences, or control characters, this enables log injection and may corrupt structured error output consumed downstream.
- **Impact:** Log forgery, injection of fake log lines, potential corruption of log-analysis pipelines, and if error messages are forwarded to an HTTP response, HTTP response-splitting if the message is placed in a header.
- **Fix:** Sanitize `operation` (strip or reject non-printable/control characters) before embedding it in any message. For the whitelist-driven operation dispatch already in place, the fix is simply to reject the value before the if-chain: if the string contains any character outside `[a-z_]`, fail immediately with a static error message that does not echo the value.

---

### HIGH Uncontrolled File Read Size via `field_value_file`

- **Location:** `PolarionWriteTaskExecutor::ExecuteCloud`, `field_value_file` branch (~lines 175–182)
- **Issue:** The file pointed to by `fieldValueFile` is read entirely into `fieldValue` via `istreambuf_iterator` with no size limit. Any file path (subject to the path traversal issue above) of arbitrary size is loaded into memory.
- **Impact:** An attacker can cause unbounded memory allocation by pointing `field_value_file` at `/dev/zero`, a large file, or a named pipe, exhausting process memory and causing a denial of service.
- **Fix:** Enforce a maximum read size (e.g., check `std::filesystem::file_size` first and reject if above a defined limit, then read at most that many bytes). Also close the stream immediately after reading.

---

### HIGH JSON Injection in `download_attachment` Response Construction

- **Location:** `PolarionWriteTaskExecutor::ExecuteCloud`, `download_attachment` branch (~line 275)
- **Issue:** `responseBody` is constructed by raw string concatenation of `filePath` without calling `JsonEscape`: `"{\"ok\":true,\"file_path\":\"" + filePath + "\"}"`. `filePath` is user-supplied and may contain double-quotes, backslashes, or other JSON metacharacters.
- **Impact:** Downstream consumers parsing this JSON may receive malformed or injected JSON, potentially altering control flow in the workflow engine or exfiltrating data if the response is re-parsed and acted upon.
- **Fix:** Pass `filePath` through the existing `JsonEscape` function before embedding it: `"\"" + JsonEscape(filePath) + "\""`.

---

### HIGH Credentials Token Logged / Leaked in Error Paths

- **Location:** `PolarionWriteTaskExecutor::ExecuteCloud`, all operation branches; error paths that set `taskState.m_LastErrorMessage = "... failed: " + errorMessage`
- **Issue:** `errorMessage` is populated by `PolarionClient` methods and may contain the URL with embedded credentials or token fragments, depending on how `PolarionClient` constructs its error strings. Even if not, `credentials.m_Token` is passed to every `PolarionClient` call and any exception or debug path in those calls could surface it. More concretely, `taskState.m_LastErrorMessage` is a workflow-level observable that may be written to logs, returned to API callers, or stored in run state—all without scrubbing token values.
- **Impact:** Credential leakage to log files, workflow run records, or API responses, enabling session hijacking against the Polarion instance.
- **Fix:** Ensure `PolarionClient` error strings never include the token. Audit all paths where `taskState.m_LastErrorMessage` is stored or transmitted and confirm tokens are scrubbed before storage.

---

### MEDIUM Missing Validation of `work_item_id`, `attachment_id`, and `project_id` Before URL Construction

- **Location:** `PolarionWriteTaskExecutor::ExecuteCloud`, all branches that call `PolarionClient` methods (~lines 196, 240, 260, 280)
- **Issue:** `workItemId`, `attachmentId`, and `projectId` (from `connection.m_Params`) are passed directly to `PolarionClient` methods which presumably build REST URLs such as `.../workitems/{projectId}/{workItemId}/attachments/{attachmentId}`. No validation rejects path-separator characters (`/`, `\`, `..`, `%2f`) or other URL metacharacters in these fields.
- **Impact:** An attacker supplying a crafted `work_item_id` such as `../../admin` or `foo/../../users` can alter the URL path sent to the Polarion server, achieving server-side request forgery against different Polarion API endpoints or triggering unintended operations on other resources.
- **Fix:** Validate `workItemId` and `attachmentId` against a strict allowlist (e.g., `[A-Za-z0-9_\-]+`) before use. Percent-encode or reject any value containing `/`, `\`, `?`, `#`, or `%`.

---

### MEDIUM `kMaxCaptureChars` Substring Truncation Without UTF-8 Boundary Check

- **Location:** `PolarionWriteTaskExecutor::ExecuteCloud`, stdout capture (~line 312): `responseBody.substr(0, std::min(responseBody.size(), kMaxCaptureChars))`
- **Issue:** Truncating a UTF-8 string at a fixed byte offset may split a multibyte character, producing an invalid UTF-8 sequence in `taskState.m_CapturedStdout`. If this string is later serialized as JSON or compared by downstream code that assumes valid UTF-8, it can cause parse errors or undefined behavior in string-handling libraries.
- **Impact:** Downstream JSON parsing of task output may fail or misbehave; in a worst case, crafted multibyte sequences could smuggle content past downstream parsers.
- **Fix:** When truncating, walk back from byte 1024 to the last valid UTF-8 character boundary before calling `substr`.

---

### LOW `operation` Value Not Validated for Length Before Use

- **Location:** `PolarionWriteTaskExecutor::ExecuteCloud`, `operation` extraction (~lines 130–137)
- **Issue:** The `operation` string is accepted without any maximum length check before being used in string comparisons and embedded in error messages. An adversary can supply an arbitrarily long string.
- **Impact:** Minor memory waste and large error message strings propagated through the system; negligible in isolation but contributes to DoS if many workflow runs are created concurrently.
- **Fix:** Reject `operation` values longer than a reasonable bound (e.g., 64 bytes) immediately after extraction.


---

### 33_doc_application_cloud_postgresConnector_h

<a id="33-doc-application-cloud-postgresconnector-h"></a>

*Source doc:* `../../../queue/jarvisCppCyberSecAudit/33_doc_application_cloud_postgresConnector_h/docu.output.md`



### HIGH — libpq connection string injection via unvalidated `sslmode`

- **Location:** `PostgresConnector::BuildConnectionString`, `sslmode` assignment (~line 115)
- **Issue:** `sslmode` is taken from `connection.m_Params["sslmode"]` and passed through the `escape` lambda, but the `escape` function only escapes backslash and single-quote within a single-quoted value. libpq keyword=value format does not treat single-quoted values as a unit the same way a shell does; the actual libpq parser accepts unquoted tokens and spaces delimit the next keyword. The connection string is built by string concatenation, so a caller-supplied `sslmode` of `disable host=attacker.example.com` (no quotes needed if the value is inserted before quoting is applied) can inject additional keywords. More critically, `sslmode=disable` is an accepted value that silently downgrades TLS to plaintext. There is no allowlist enforcing that `sslmode` is one of the documented safe values (`require`, `verify-ca`, `verify-full`).
- **Impact:** An attacker who can influence `m_Params` (e.g., via a crafted workflow configuration, API call, or deserialized config) can force `sslmode=disable`, stripping TLS from the database connection and enabling credential interception. Depending on libpq version and quoting edge cases, additional keyword injection may be possible.
- **Fix:** Validate `sslmode` against an explicit allowlist before use:
  ```
  static const std::set<std::string> kAllowedSSLModes = {"require","verify-ca","verify-full"};
  if (kAllowedSSLModes.find(sslmode) == kAllowedSSLModes.end()) {
      // reject or default to verify-full
      sslmode = "verify-full";
  }
  ```
  Prefer `verify-full` as the hardcoded default rather than `prefer`.

---

### HIGH — Default `sslmode=prefer` allows unencrypted connections

- **Location:** `PostgresConnector::BuildConnectionString`, ~line 112
- **Issue:** The default value for `sslmode` is `"prefer"`, which means libpq will attempt TLS but silently fall back to an unencrypted connection if the server does not support it. This is a soft default that provides no meaningful confidentiality guarantee.
- **Impact:** A network-level attacker (MitM or a rogue PostgreSQL server) can cause the client to connect in plaintext, exposing credentials and all database traffic.
- **Fix:** Change the default to `"verify-full"` and require callers to explicitly opt down (with logging and admin acknowledgment). Never default to `"prefer"` for a connector handling production credentials.

---

### HIGH — Password exposed in `errorMessage` via `PQerrorMessage`

- **Location:** `PostgresConnector::TestConnection`, ~line 158
- **Issue:** On connection failure, `PQerrorMessage(conn)` is copied verbatim into `errorMessage`, which is then returned to the caller. Some libpq builds and some PostgreSQL server error messages echo back connection parameters including the password or parts of the connection string in diagnostic output. The `errorMessage` string is controlled by libpq and may include the full connection URI or keyword=value string it attempted.
- **Impact:** Database credentials leak into error responses, logs, or API responses that surface `errorMessage` to less-privileged callers or external clients.
- **Fix:** Log the raw `PQerrorMessage` output only to a privileged internal log at DEBUG level. Return a sanitized, generic error string (e.g., `"PostgreSQL connection failed; see server logs"`) in `errorMessage` to the caller. Apply the same pattern to the `PQresultErrorMessage` path at ~line 165.

---

### MEDIUM — `escape` lambda does not defend against libpq keyword=value injection for all field types

- **Location:** `PostgresConnector::BuildConnectionString`, `escape` lambda, ~line 118
- **Issue:** The `escape` function wraps values in single quotes and escapes `\` and `'`. However, libpq's keyword=value connection string parser treats a single-quoted token as ending at the matching unescaped `'`. If a value contains a null byte (`\0`), libpq's C-string-based parser will truncate reading at that point, potentially ending the quoted section early and allowing text after the null to be interpreted as new keywords. Additionally, the port value (which is validated to be all-digits) is still passed through `escape` and wrapped in quotes, which is unnecessary and inconsistent — if the digit validation were ever relaxed, this becomes an injection point.
- **Impact:** Values containing embedded null bytes could truncate the quoted value and inject additional connection string keywords, potentially overriding security-relevant settings such as `sslmode` or `host`.
- **Fix:** Reject any field value containing a null byte before building the connection string. For the port specifically, keep the all-digit validation and do not quote it (or keep quoting but add null-byte rejection). Consider using `PQconnectdbParams` (which accepts separate arrays of keywords and values) instead of building a keyword=value string at all; this eliminates the entire class of connection string injection.

---

### MEDIUM — Unvalidated `host` and `database` values enable SSRF

- **Location:** `PostgresConnector::BuildConnectionString`, host parsing and `database` assignment, ~lines 91–112
- **Issue:** The `host` field is taken from `connection.m_Endpoint` with only a colon-based split for port detection. No validation prevents values such as `127.0.0.1`, `169.254.169.254` (cloud metadata endpoint), internal hostnames, or Unix socket paths (a host beginning with `/` is treated as a Unix socket path by libpq). Similarly, `database` is taken from `m_Params` with no validation.
- **Impact:** A user who can control the workflow's `m_Endpoint` or `m_Params["database"]` can direct the connector to probe internal network services, reach cloud metadata endpoints, or connect to unintended databases.
- **Fix:** Validate `host` against an allowlist of permitted hostnames/IP ranges configured by an administrator. Reject hosts that are loopback, link-local, or begin with `/` unless explicitly permitted. Apply similar restrictions to `database` (alphanumeric and underscore only, length-bounded).

---

### LOW — No upper-bound on credential or parameter string lengths

- **Location:** `PostgresConnector::BuildConnectionString`, all string fields, ~lines 91–130
- **Issue:** No length checks are applied to `m_Endpoint`, `m_Params` values, `credentials.m_Username`, or `credentials.m_Password` before they are assembled into the connection string. An adversary who can supply a very large value can cause an unbounded heap allocation when `connStr` is constructed.
- **Impact:** Uncontrolled memory allocation leading to OOM or process crash (DoS).
- **Fix:** Enforce reasonable maximum lengths (e.g., 253 characters for hostnames, 63 for database names per PostgreSQL limits, 128 for credentials) and return an error if exceeded.


---

### 34_doc_application_cloud_providerRateLimitPolicy_h

<a id="34-doc-application-cloud-providerratelimitpolicy-h"></a>
