### 35_doc_application_cloud_redmineCloudTaskExecutor_h

<a id="35-doc-application-cloud-redminecloudtaskexecutor-h"></a>

*Source doc:* `../../../queue/jarvisCppCyberSecAudit/35_doc_application_cloud_redmineCloudTaskExecutor_h/docu.output.md`



### [CRITICAL] SSRF via unvalidated URL construction

- **Location:** `RedmineCloudTaskExecutor::ExecuteCloud`, list_issues branch, ~line 195 (`url = baseUrl + "/issues.json?project_id=" + projectIdent + ...`)
- **Issue:** `projectIdent` and `status` are taken directly from attacker-controlled task params or connection params and concatenated into the URL without any validation or encoding. An attacker who controls these fields can inject arbitrary query-string fragments (e.g. `projectIdent = "foo&status_id=open&limit=1&url=http://internal-host/"`) or, if `baseUrl` itself is attacker-influenced, redirect the request to an internal network address.
- **Impact:** Full SSRF: the engine can be made to issue authenticated HTTP requests to arbitrary internal hosts/paths, potentially exfiltrating secrets or reaching metadata endpoints (AWS IMDSv1, etc.).
- **Fix:** URL-encode every user-supplied parameter with `curl_easy_escape` before appending it to a URL. Separately, validate `baseUrl` against an allowlist of known Redmine hostnames from a trusted configuration source, never from task params.

---

### [CRITICAL] Path traversal via `notes_file` and `assigned_to_id_file`

- **Location:** `RedmineCloudTaskExecutor::ExecuteCloud`, update_issue branch, ~lines 233 and 247 (`ReadFileToString(notesFile)` / `ReadFileToString(assignedToIdFile)`)
- **Issue:** Both `notesFile` and `assignedToIdFile` are read verbatim from task params JSON and passed directly to `ReadFileToString`, which opens them with `std::ifstream`. There is no path canonicalization, no check that the path stays within the workflow working directory, and no prohibition of absolute paths or `../` sequences.
- **Impact:** An attacker who can submit workflow task definitions can read any file accessible to the process: `/etc/shadow`, private keys, other workflows' secrets, environment files, etc.
- **Fix:** Resolve each file path with `std::filesystem::weakly_canonical`, then assert it is a sub-path of the workflow working directory (`workDir`). Reject absolute paths and any path that resolves outside the allowed root before opening.

---

### [HIGH] Unbounded HTTP response body accumulation (DoS)

- **Location:** `RedmineRequest`, write callback lambda, ~line 149
- **Issue:** The `writeCallback` appends every byte libcurl delivers into `responseBody` (`buf->append(...)`) with no size cap. A malicious or compromised Redmine server—or an SSRF-redirected endpoint—can stream an arbitrarily large response, causing unbounded heap growth and potential OOM of the engine process.
- **Impact:** Denial of service; process crash; potential for forcing large allocations that interact badly with memory-safety bugs elsewhere.
- **Fix:** Add a size guard inside the callback: if `buf->size() + size * nmemb` exceeds a defined maximum (e.g., 8 MB), return 0 to abort the transfer. Also set `CURLOPT_MAXFILESIZE_LARGE` as a belt-and-suspenders limit.

---

### [HIGH] API key leaked in error-response body included in `m_LastErrorMessage`

- **Location:** `RedmineCloudTaskExecutor::ExecuteCloud`, both branches, ~lines 211 and 285 (`taskState.m_LastErrorMessage += ": " + responseBody`)
- **Issue:** When Redmine returns HTTP ≥ 400, the raw response body (up to 500 bytes) is appended to `m_LastErrorMessage`. Redmine error bodies can echo back request headers or authentication context in some configurations. More broadly, `responseBody` is attacker-influenced data (from a potentially SSRF-redirected server) being inserted unfiltered into a field that is almost certainly logged or surfaced to users.
- **Impact:** Secret leakage into logs; log injection (newlines in a JSON/XML error body can forge additional log lines depending on the logger).
- **Fix:** Strip or sanitize `responseBody` before appending: remove all control characters, enforce a shorter cap (e.g., 200 bytes), and consider omitting it entirely from `m_LastErrorMessage`, instead writing it only to a debug log that is not forwarded to end-users.

---

### [HIGH] `CURLOPT_SSL_VERIFYPEER` / `CURLOPT_SSL_VERIFYHOST` not explicitly enabled

- **Location:** `RedmineRequest`, ~lines 155–170
- **Issue:** The code conditionally sets `CURLOPT_CAINFO` only when `CurlWrapper::GetCaBundlePath()` returns a non-empty string. If that function returns empty (misconfiguration, path not set), libcurl falls back to whatever its compile-time default CA store is—or, on some platforms/builds, silently disables peer verification. `CURLOPT_SSL_VERIFYPEER` and `CURLOPT_SSL_VERIFYHOST` are never explicitly set to `1`/`2`.
- **Impact:** TLS connections to Redmine (and any SSRF-redirected TLS endpoint) may succeed with forged or expired certificates, enabling MitM interception of the API key and all issue data.
- **Fix:** Unconditionally set `CURLOPT_SSL_VERIFYPEER, 1L` and `CURLOPT_SSL_VERIFYHOST, 2L`. If no CA bundle path is available, fail closed rather than proceeding without verification.

---

### [HIGH] Unvalidated `issueId` injected into URL path

- **Location:** `RedmineCloudTaskExecutor::ExecuteCloud`, update_issue branch, ~line 274 (`url = baseUrl + "/issues/" + issueId + ".json"`)
- **Issue:** `issueId` is taken from task params as either a string or integer. When it arrives as a string (via `getStringParam("issue_id")`), it undergoes no validation before being interpolated into the URL path. A value such as `"../users"` or `"1/watchers"` changes the API endpoint being targeted.
- **Impact:** Attackers can cause the engine to PUT to arbitrary Redmine API endpoints (or, combined with the SSRF issue, arbitrary URLs).
- **Fix:** After resolving `issueId`, validate that it consists entirely of decimal digits (`std::all_of` with `isdigit`). Reject any non-numeric value before URL construction.

---

### [MEDIUM] `notes` content logged unconditionally, potentially leaking sensitive data

- **Location:** `RedmineCloudTaskExecutor::ExecuteCloud`, update_issue branch, ~line 300 (`LOG_APP_INFO("[redmine] updated issue #{} ...", issueId, notes.size(), assignedToId)`)
- **Issue:** `notes.size()` is logged (not the content itself—that part is acceptable), but `assignedToId` is logged in full. If `assignedToId` originates from a file and the file contained more than just an integer (e.g., a token on the same line that survives `TrimWhitespace`), the value is emitted to the application log. More critically, the log message is a structured format string: if the logging macro expands user content into a positional format without escaping, format injection is possible depending on the logger implementation.
- **Impact:** Sensitive field values in logs; potential log-injection if the logger interpolates without sanitizing.
- **Fix:** Validate that `assignedToId` is purely numeric before logging (the numeric check already exists for JSON serialization—apply it here too, and log only the numeric value or a redacted placeholder for non-numeric cases).

---

### [MEDIUM] `notes_file` failure silently ignored for `assigned_to_id_file`

- **Location:** `RedmineCloudTaskExecutor::ExecuteCloud`, ~lines 247–250
- **Issue:** When `assigned_to_id_file` is specified and `ReadFileToString` returns an empty string (file not found, permission denied, empty file), the code silently sets `assignedToId` to an empty string and continues. Unlike the `notes_file` branch (~line 236), there is no error check. A workflow that expects to assign an issue may silently submit a PUT with no assignee, causing a logic error that is invisible to the operator.
- **Impact:** Not directly exploitable for privilege escalation, but a missing error check on file operations can mask path-traversal probing (attacker learns nothing from the silent failure) and leads to incorrect workflow behavior without alerting.
- **Fix:** Mirror the `notes_file` error handling: if `loaded.empty()` after reading `assignedToIdFile`, set `taskState.m_LastErrorMessage` and return false.

---

### [MEDIUM] `CURLOPT_FOLLOWLOCATION` enabled without redirect restriction

- **Location:** `RedmineRequest`, ~line 162 (`curl_easy_setopt(curl, CURLOPT_FOLLOWLOCATION, 1L)`)
- **Issue:** Automatic redirect following is enabled with no limit on the number of redirects (`CURLOPT_MAXREDIRS`) and no restriction on redirect protocols (`CURLOPT_REDIR_PROTOCOLS`). libcurl will follow redirects to `file://`, `gopher://`, `ftp://`, and other schemes by default in older versions, and will follow cross-origin redirects that strip the `X-Redmine-API-Key` header only starting from certain libcurl versions.
- **Impact:** Compounds the SSRF issue; a redirect from a Redmine response can reach internal file or service endpoints. The API key may be forwarded to a redirected host.
- **Fix:** Set `CURLOPT_MAXREDIRS, 3L`; set `CURLOPT_REDIR_PROTOCOLS, CURLPROTO_HTTPS` (or `CURLPROTO_HTTP | CURLPROTO_HTTPS` if HTTP is required); consider disabling redirect following entirely since Redmine's REST API does not redirect normal API calls.


---

### 36_doc_application_cloud_redmineConnector_h

<a id="36-doc-application-cloud-redmineconnector-h"></a>

*Source doc:* `../../../queue/jarvisCppCyberSecAudit/36_doc_application_cloud_redmineConnector_h/docu.output.md`



### HIGH TLS Certificate Verification Disabled by Default

- **Location:** `RedmineConnector::TestConnection`, curl setup block (~line 120–145)
- **Issue:** `CURLOPT_SSL_VERIFYPEER` and `CURLOPT_SSL_VERIFYHOST` are never set. libcurl defaults both to enabled only if a CA bundle is found; however, when `CurlWrapper::GetCaBundlePath()` returns an empty string (line ~138), no `CURLOPT_CAINFO` is set and the code silently proceeds with whatever libcurl's compile-time default CA store is — or none at all on platforms where there is no system store. There is no explicit assertion that peer verification is on. An attacker on the network path can present a forged certificate and intercept or modify traffic, including capturing the Redmine API key sent in the `X-Redmine-API-Key` header.
- **Impact:** Man-in-the-middle interception of API keys and all Redmine responses; full credential theft against deployments without a reliable system CA store (embedded Linux, Windows without a configured curl CA bundle, etc.).
- **Fix:** Explicitly set both options unconditionally before the conditional `CURLOPT_CAINFO` block:
  `curl_easy_setopt(curl, CURLOPT_SSL_VERIFYPEER, 1L);`
  `curl_easy_setopt(curl, CURLOPT_SSL_VERIFYHOST, 2L);`
  Additionally, treat an empty CA bundle path as an error rather than silently continuing, or require that `CurlWrapper::GetCaBundlePath()` always return a valid path in production builds.

---

### HIGH SSRF via Unvalidated Endpoint URL

- **Location:** `RedmineConnector::GetBaseUrl` (~line 75) and `RedmineConnector::TestConnection` (~line 110)
- **Issue:** `connection.m_Endpoint` is used verbatim to construct the request URL with no scheme validation, host allowlist, or private-network blocking. Any scheme libcurl supports (`file://`, `gopher://`, `dict://`, `ftp://`, `ldap://`, `sftp://`, etc.) is accepted. An operator-supplied or attacker-controlled endpoint value can be used to probe internal services, read local files (`file:///etc/passwd`), or reach cloud metadata endpoints (`http://169.254.169.254/`).
- **Impact:** Server-side request forgery against internal infrastructure, cloud metadata credential theft, local file read via `file://` scheme, lateral movement.
- **Fix:** Validate that the endpoint begins with `https://` (or `http://` only if explicitly allowed for local dev). Use `curl_url_get` / `CURLU` to parse and inspect the scheme and host before building the final URL. Reject private RFC-1918 / link-local ranges if the connector is reachable by untrusted configuration sources.

---

### HIGH HTTP Header Injection via API Key Value

- **Location:** `RedmineConnector::TestConnection`, header construction (~line 148)
- **Issue:** `credentials.m_Token` (the raw value from the key manager) is interpolated directly into an HTTP header string with no sanitization:
  `std::string apiKeyHeader = "X-Redmine-API-Key: " + credentials.m_Token;`
  If the key value contains a CR (`\r`) or LF (`\n`) character, libcurl versions prior to 7.81.0 (CVE-2022-27782 era) will split the string and inject arbitrary additional headers. Even on patched libcurl, the token value is not validated at all before use.
- **Impact:** HTTP request header injection; an attacker who can influence the stored API key value can inject arbitrary headers, potentially bypassing access controls or poisoning logs on the Redmine server side.
- **Fix:** Before constructing the header, validate that `credentials.m_Token` contains no `\r`, `\n`, or NUL characters. Reject or strip them with an explicit check and return an error. Additionally prefer `curl_easy_setopt(CURLOPT_XOAUTH2_BEARER, ...)` or structured header APIs where available.

---

### MEDIUM Unbounded Response Body Accumulation (DoS)

- **Location:** `RedmineConnector::TestConnection`, `writeCallback` lambda and `responseBody` string (~line 118–125)
- **Issue:** The `writeCallback` appends all received bytes into `responseBody` with no size limit. There is no `CURLOPT_MAXFILESIZE` or manual byte-count check. A malicious or misconfigured Redmine server (or an SSRF target) can stream an arbitrarily large response, causing the process to exhaust heap memory.
- **Impact:** Denial of service via memory exhaustion against the j9t engine process.
- **Fix:** Add a size guard inside the write callback — if `buf->size() + (size * nmemb)` exceeds a reasonable limit (e.g. 1 MB for a `/users/current.json` response), return 0 to abort the transfer. Also set `CURLOPT_MAXFILESIZE_LARGE` as a defense-in-depth measure.

---

### MEDIUM Unlimited Redirect Following Enables SSRF Escalation

- **Location:** `RedmineConnector::TestConnection`, curl option (~line 133)
- **Issue:** `CURLOPT_FOLLOWLOCATION` is set to `1L` with no corresponding `CURLOPT_MAXREDIRS` limit and no `CURLOPT_REDIR_PROTOCOLS` restriction. libcurl will follow redirects to any scheme it supports by default, including `file://` and `ftp://`. This compounds the SSRF issue above: even if the initial URL passes scheme validation, a server-controlled redirect can send the request to an internal target.
- **Impact:** Redirect-based SSRF bypassing scheme validation on the initial URL; potential infinite redirect loop causing thread/resource exhaustion.
- **Fix:** Set `CURLOPT_MAXREDIRS` to a small value (e.g. 3). Set `CURLOPT_REDIR_PROTOCOLS` to `CURLPROTO_HTTPS` (or `CURLPROTO_HTTP | CURLPROTO_HTTPS` if plaintext is needed). Consider disabling redirect following entirely for API health-check calls where a redirect is unexpected.

---

### LOW API Key Partially Exposed in Error Messages

- **Location:** `RedmineConnector::TestConnection`, error path (~line 156–167)
- **Issue:** When the HTTP status is ≥ 400 and the response body is short (< 500 bytes), the raw `responseBody` is appended to `errorMessage`. Depending on the Redmine server's error response format, this body could echo back portions of the request including the API key value (some servers reflect request headers in error pages). The `errorMessage` string is passed to callers who may log or surface it in UI.
- **Impact:** Inadvertent API key leakage in log files or user-facing error messages.
- **Fix:** Do not include the raw server response body in externally propagated error messages without scrubbing it. Log the body only at a debug level to an internal-only log sink, not in the `errorMessage` output parameter.


---

### 37_doc_application_cloud_s3CloudTaskExecutor_h

<a id="37-doc-application-cloud-s3cloudtaskexecutor-h"></a>

*Source doc:* `../../../queue/jarvisCppCyberSecAudit/37_doc_application_cloud_s3CloudTaskExecutor_h/docu.output.md`



### CRITICAL Path Traversal via `file_path` and `key` Parameters

- **Location:** `S3CloudTaskExecutor::ExecuteCloud`, upload branch (~line 210) and download branch (~line 245)
- **Issue:** `filePath` is read directly from user-controlled task params JSON (`getStringParam("file_path")`) and passed without any sanitization to `std::ifstream`, `std::fopen`, and `std::filesystem::create_directories`. Similarly, `key` is appended directly to the S3 endpoint URL without encoding or validation. An attacker who can submit a workflow task can set `file_path` to `../../etc/passwd`, `../../root/.ssh/authorized_keys`, or any absolute path. For uploads, arbitrary local files are read and exfiltrated to S3. For downloads, arbitrary paths are overwritten.
- **Impact:** Full read of any file readable by the process (credential files, private keys, config) via upload; arbitrary file write to any path writable by the process via download. Combined these enable remote code execution (e.g., overwrite a cron script or SSH authorized_keys).
- **Fix:** Resolve `filePath` to a canonical absolute path with `std::filesystem::weakly_canonical`, then assert it is a strict prefix of an allowed base directory (e.g., the workflow working directory). Reject any path that does not remain within the sandbox. Apply URL-percent-encoding to `key` before appending it to the URL.

---

### CRITICAL Uncontrolled File Read into Memory (DoS / Memory Exhaustion) on Upload

- **Location:** `S3CloudTaskExecutor::ExecuteCloud`, upload branch (~line 215–225)
- **Issue:** The entire file is read into a `std::string` (`fileData`) using `file.tellg()` as the allocation size, with no upper-bound check. `fileSize` is a signed `std::streamoff`; casting a negative value (e.g., if `tellg()` fails on a special file) to `size_t` produces an enormous allocation. Even without that edge case, there is no cap on file size, so a workflow task pointing at `/dev/zero` or a very large file will exhaust process memory.
- **Impact:** Process crash (denial of service); potential allocation of a near-`SIZE_MAX`-byte buffer if `tellg()` returns -1.
- **Fix:** Check that `fileSize > 0` and `fileSize <= some_max_upload_size` before allocating. Also check the return value of `file.read()` to confirm all bytes were actually read. Use a streaming upload rather than buffering the entire payload.

---

### HIGH SSRF — Unvalidated S3 Endpoint URL Construction

- **Location:** `S3CloudTaskExecutor::ExecuteCloud` (~line 195), `S3Connector::BuildEndpointUrl` (called but not shown)
- **Issue:** `endpointUrl` is built from `connection.m_Params` (user-configurable connection object) and the attacker-controlled `bucket` name. The resulting URL is passed directly to `curl_easy_setopt(curl, CURLOPT_URL, ...)` in `S3Request` and `S3Download`. If the connection object's endpoint or the bucket name can be influenced by a workflow author, they can direct the process to make signed HTTP requests to internal services (instance metadata at `169.254.169.254`, internal APIs, etc.). `CURLOPT_FOLLOWLOCATION` is enabled, making redirect-based SSRF also possible.
- **Impact:** Attacker-controlled SSRF against internal cloud metadata endpoints, VPC-internal services, or loopback; the requests carry valid AWS SigV4 signatures.
- **Fix:** Validate `endpointUrl` against an allowlist of known S3 hostnames/prefixes. Disable `CURLOPT_FOLLOWLOCATION` or set `CURLOPT_MAXREDIRS 0`. Block private/loopback IP ranges via a pre-connect callback or by resolving and checking the address before connecting.

---

### HIGH Missing TLS Certificate Verification Fallback

- **Location:** `S3Request` (~line 140) and `S3Download` (~line 168)
- **Issue:** TLS verification is only configured if `CurlWrapper::GetCaBundlePath()` returns a non-empty string. When it returns empty, no `CURLOPT_CAINFO` is set and the code never explicitly sets `CURLOPT_SSL_VERIFYPEER 1L` or `CURLOPT_SSL_VERIFYHOST 2L`. libcurl's defaults depend on compile-time configuration; on some builds (especially Windows or custom builds) the default may be to skip verification. There is no defensive assertion that peer verification is active.
- **Impact:** If the CA bundle path is absent, all HTTPS connections to S3 proceed without certificate validation, enabling man-in-the-middle interception of AWS credentials (SigV4 signed headers contain the access key ID and a HMAC of the secret key) and data.
- **Fix:** Unconditionally set `CURLOPT_SSL_VERIFYPEER 1L` and `CURLOPT_SSL_VERIFYHOST 2L` before the conditional `CURLOPT_CAINFO` block. If `caBundle` is empty, either abort or rely on the system CA store (omit `CURLOPT_CAINFO` but keep verify flags on).

---

### HIGH Unencoded `prefix` and `key` Appended to URL (Injection / Path Traversal in S3 URL)

- **Location:** `S3CloudTaskExecutor::ExecuteCloud`, list branch (~line 275) and all URL constructions (~lines 230, 260, 310)
- **Issue:** `prefix` and `key` values from user-controlled JSON are concatenated into the URL string without percent-encoding. A value containing `?`, `&`, `#`, `/`, or `%2F` sequences can manipulate the query string or path, bypassing S3 bucket/key constraints. For example, `key = "legit/../../other-bucket/secret"` or `prefix = "foo&max-keys=1"` overrides query parameters.
- **Impact:** Attacker can operate on unintended S3 keys or buckets, override query parameters (e.g., change `max-keys`), or enumerate/delete objects outside the intended scope.
- **Fix:** Apply URL percent-encoding to `key` and `prefix` before string concatenation (use `curl_easy_escape` or a proper URI-encoding function). Validate that `key` does not contain `..` components after URL-decoding.

---

### HIGH `stoi` on Unvalidated User Input — Exception / Crash

- **Location:** `S3CloudTaskExecutor::ExecuteCloud`, list branch (~line 270)
- **Issue:** `std::stoi(maxKeysStr)` is called on a value read directly from user-controlled task params JSON with no bounds checking and no exception handling. `std::stoi` throws `std::invalid_argument` for non-numeric input and `std::out_of_range` for values outside `int` range. If the surrounding executor does not catch `std::exception`, the process terminates. A negative or zero value is also passed unvalidated to the URL.
- **Impact:** Denial of service via uncaught exception; negative `max-keys` values sent to S3 may produce unexpected behavior or error responses that leak information.
- **Fix:** Wrap in try/catch, or use `std::from_chars` which does not throw. Clamp the result to `[1, 1000]` (or a configurable maximum). Reject non-numeric input explicitly.

---

### MEDIUM AWS Secret Key Potentially Leaked in Error Messages

- **Location:** `S3Download` (~line 185): `errorMessage = std::string("curl error: ") + curl_easy_strerror(res)`; `S3CloudTaskExecutor::ExecuteCloud` upload branch (~line 235): `taskState.m_LastErrorMessage += ": " + responseBody`
- **Issue:** `responseBody` from a failed S3 request is appended to `taskState.m_LastErrorMessage` (up to 500 bytes). S3 XML error responses can echo back portions of the request URL and headers. More critically, `credentials.m_AccessKeyId` is used in SigV4 headers; if `S3Request` or `S3Download` surfaces the raw HTTP response into error fields that are subsequently logged or returned to clients, the access key ID (and indirectly hints about the signing process) is disclosed. Additionally, the log line `LOG_APP_INFO("[s3] uploaded {} to s3://{}/{}", filePath, bucket, key)` logs the full local file path, which may itself be sensitive.
- **Impact:** Credential ID and internal path exposure in logs or API responses visible to users with read access to task state.
- **Fix:** Strip or sanitize S3 XML error bodies before storing in `m_LastErrorMessage`. Do not log `filePath` directly; log only the basename or a task-scoped identifier. Audit all log statements for credential-adjacent fields.

---

### MEDIUM Raw JSON Construction with Unsanitized String Values

- **Location:** `S3CloudTaskExecutor::ExecuteCloud`, all branches building `responseBody` (~lines 240, 265, 320)
- **Issue:** `bucket`, `key`, and `filePath` (all user-controlled) are interpolated into JSON strings by direct concatenation without escaping. Values containing `"`, `\`, or control characters will produce malformed or attacker-shaped JSON stored in `taskState.m_CapturedStdout` and written via `WriteResponseJson`.
- **Impact:** If downstream consumers parse this JSON, a maliciously crafted bucket or key name can inject arbitrary JSON structure, potentially overwriting other fields in the response object (e.g., `"key":"x","ok":false,"injected":true,"dummy":"`)
- **Fix:** Use a proper JSON serialization library (simdjson is already included; use its builder API, or use nlohmann/json) to construct response objects rather than string concatenation.

---

### LOW Partial File Left on Disk After Failed Download

- **Location:** `S3Download` (~line 178–195)
- **Issue:** `fp` is opened with `"wb"` (truncating/creating the file) before the HTTP request is made. If the download fails (curl error or HTTP 4xx/5xx), `fclose(fp)` is called but the partially written file is not removed. A subsequent read of that path by another task will see truncated or corrupted data.
- **Impact:** Data integrity issue; a failed download silently leaves a zero-length or partial file that a subsequent task may treat as a valid completed download.
- **Fix:** On any failure path, call `std::filesystem::remove(outputPath)` after `fclose(fp)` to clean up the incomplete file.


---

### 38_doc_application_cloud_s3Connector_h

<a id="38-doc-application-cloud-s3connector-h"></a>

*Source doc:* `../../../queue/jarvisCppCyberSecAudit/38_doc_application_cloud_s3Connector_h/docu.output.md`



### HIGH SSRF via unvalidated custom endpoint

- **Location:** `S3Connector::BuildEndpointUrl`, ~line 130–139
- **Issue:** `connection.m_Endpoint` is concatenated with the bucket name and used directly as the request URL with no scheme validation, no hostname allowlist, and no rejection of private/loopback/link-local addresses. Any user or config that can set `m_Endpoint` can point requests at `http://169.254.169.254/` (AWS IMDS), `http://127.0.0.1/`, internal services, `file://` URIs, or any other reachable host. libcurl will follow the URL as given, including schemes beyond HTTP/HTTPS unless explicitly restricted.
- **Impact:** Full SSRF: credential theft via IMDS, probing internal network topology, potentially exfiltrating SigV4-signed requests (including the secret key in the `Authorization` header) to an attacker-controlled host.
- **Fix:** Enforce an explicit allowlist of permitted schemes (`https://` only). Parse the URL before use and reject any hostname that resolves to RFC-1918, loopback, link-local (169.254.x.x), or ::1 ranges. Set `CURLOPT_PROTOCOLS` to `CURLPROTO_HTTPS` only. Consider adding `CURLOPT_RESOLVE` pinning or requiring the operator to pre-validate endpoints at configuration time.

---

### HIGH Missing TLS certificate verification

- **Location:** `S3Connector::TestConnection`, ~lines 183–191
- **Issue:** TLS peer certificate verification is not explicitly enabled. `CURLOPT_SSL_VERIFYPEER` and `CURLOPT_SSL_VERIFYHOST` are never set to `1L`/`2L`. The CA bundle is set only when `CurlWrapper::GetCaBundlePath()` returns a non-empty string; if it returns empty, no CA path is set and curl falls back to its compiled-in defaults, which may or may not include the system store. If the compiled-in default is absent or misconfigured, curl silently accepts any certificate. Critically, `CURLOPT_SSL_VERIFYPEER` defaults to `1L` in recent libcurl builds, but the code never asserts this, making it dependent on the libcurl build configuration of the deployment environment.
- **Impact:** When connecting to custom endpoints (`m_Endpoint` set), a network attacker can intercept the HTTPS session with a self-signed certificate, observe the SigV4 `Authorization` header (which carries `m_AccessKeyId` and an HMAC derived from `m_SecretKey`), and replay or forge requests.
- **Fix:** Explicitly set `curl_easy_setopt(curl, CURLOPT_SSL_VERIFYPEER, 1L)` and `curl_easy_setopt(curl, CURLOPT_SSL_VERIFYHOST, 2L)` unconditionally. Treat an empty CA bundle path as a hard error for custom endpoints rather than silently falling back.

---

### HIGH Uncontrolled response body allocation (DoS)

- **Location:** `S3Connector::TestConnection`, write callback lambda and `responseBody`, ~lines 163–175
- **Issue:** The `writeCallback` appends all received bytes into `responseBody` (a `std::string`) with no size cap. A malicious or compromised S3-compatible endpoint can stream an arbitrarily large response body, causing unbounded memory growth until OOM or process termination.
- **Impact:** A server (or SSRF pivot) under attacker control can crash or destabilize the workflow engine process by sending a multi-gigabyte response to `TestConnection`.
- **Fix:** Add a size guard inside `writeCallback`: if `buf->size() + size * nmemb` exceeds a safe ceiling (e.g., 64 KB for a list-objects response), return a value other than `size * nmemb` to signal an error and abort the transfer. Alternatively set `CURLOPT_MAXFILESIZE` to a small limit.

---

### MEDIUM HTTP header injection via signed headers

- **Location:** `S3Connector::TestConnection`, header construction loop, ~lines 193–197
- **Issue:** Headers from `signed_.m_Headers` are composed with simple string concatenation (`key + ": " + value`) and passed to `curl_slist_append` without any validation that `key` or `value` contains no CR (`\r`) or LF (`\n`) characters. If `SigV4Signer::Sign` or its inputs ever propagate attacker-controlled strings into header names or values, a CRLF sequence would split the header, injecting arbitrary HTTP headers into the upstream request.
- **Impact:** Depending on the backend, injected headers could override `Authorization`, manipulate cache behavior, or enable request smuggling against S3-compatible endpoints.
- **Fix:** Strip or reject any `\r` or `\n` characters from header keys and values before appending them. Assert that `key` matches `[A-Za-z0-9\-]+` and that `value` contains no control characters.

---

### MEDIUM Bucket name and region injected into URLs without validation

- **Location:** `S3Connector::BuildEndpointUrl`, ~lines 128–147; `S3Connector::TestConnection`, ~line 156
- **Issue:** `bucketIt->second` (bucket name) and `regionIt->second` (region) are interpolated directly into the constructed URL string with no validation against the AWS S3 naming rules or character allowlist. A bucket name containing `@`, `#`, `?`, `/`, or a region containing similar characters can alter the URL structure, bypass endpoint routing, or—combined with `CURLOPT_FOLLOWLOCATION`—redirect to unexpected hosts.
- **Impact:** URL manipulation enabling SSRF, authentication bypass (request directed to wrong service), or unexpected behavior in S3-compatible backends.
- **Fix:** Validate bucket names against the S3 spec (3–63 lowercase alphanumeric characters and hyphens, no consecutive hyphens, no IP-address format). Validate region against a strict pattern (`[a-z]{2}-[a-z]+-[0-9]`). Reject invalid inputs before URL construction.

---

### MEDIUM Secret key exposure in error messages via response body

- **Location:** `S3Connector::TestConnection`, ~lines 205–211
- **Issue:** When the HTTP response code is ≥ 400 and the response body is under 500 bytes, the raw XML/JSON response body is appended to `errorMessage`. AWS S3 error responses can include the canonicalized request, headers, or other signed material. While the secret key itself is not in the response, the pattern establishes a precedent of exposing raw server responses in user-visible error strings, and if the endpoint is attacker-controlled (SSRF scenario), the attacker controls the error content returned to the caller.
- **Impact:** Potential for injecting misleading or sensitive-looking content into operator-facing logs or UI through a crafted error response body.
- **Fix:** Parse the response as XML and extract only the `<Code>` and `<Message>` fields for inclusion in error messages. Do not embed raw response bodies.

---

### LOW Redirect following enabled without restriction

- **Location:** `S3Connector::TestConnection`, ~line 178
- **Issue:** `CURLOPT_FOLLOWLOCATION` is set to `1L` with no accompanying `CURLOPT_MAXREDIRS` limit and no `CURLOPT_REDIR_PROTOCOLS` restriction. libcurl will follow an unlimited number of redirects and, by default in older builds, will follow redirects to non-HTTP protocols (e.g., `file://`, `ftp://`).
- **Impact:** An attacker-controlled endpoint can redirect the request to internal services or local files, compounding the SSRF risk and enabling credential exfiltration via the `Authorization` header being forwarded.
- **Fix:** Set `CURLOPT_MAXREDIRS` to a small number (e.g., `3L`). Set `CURLOPT_REDIR_PROTOCOLS` to `CURLPROTO_HTTPS` only. Consider disabling redirect following entirely for signed S3 requests, since AWS S3 does not require redirects for standard operations.


---

### 39_doc_application_cloud_sigV4Signer_h

<a id="39-doc-application-cloud-sigv4signer-h"></a>

*Source doc:* `../../../queue/jarvisCppCyberSecAudit/39_doc_application_cloud_sigV4Signer_h/docu.output.md`



### MEDIUM Header Injection via Unsanitized Extra Headers

- **Location:** `SigV4Signer::Sign`, canonical headers construction loop (~line 290–300)
- **Issue:** Values from `extraHeaders` are lowercased by key but never stripped of newline characters (`\r`, `\n`). They are concatenated directly into `canonicalHeaders` as `key + ":" + value + "\n"`. A caller-supplied header value containing a newline can break the canonical request structure and inject additional signed header lines.
- **Impact:** An attacker who controls any `extraHeaders` value (e.g., a user-supplied `Content-Type`) can inject arbitrary key-value pairs into the canonical headers block, potentially forging the signed representation of a request or causing signature validation on the server side to behave unexpectedly. In practice this is limited to integrity of the signature, not request smuggling at the transport layer, but it subverts the trust model of the signing operation.
- **Fix:** Before inserting any header key or value into `headersToSign`, reject or strip all occurrences of `\r` and `\n`. For example:

    auto sanitize = [](std::string const& s) {
        return s.find_first_of("\r\n") == std::string::npos;
    };
    if (!sanitize(lowerKey) || !sanitize(value)) { /* throw or skip */ }

---

### MEDIUM Header Injection via `payloadHash` in Canonical Request and Output Headers

- **Location:** `SigV4Signer::Sign`, canonical request assembly and `result.m_Headers` population (~lines 310, 340)
- **Issue:** The `payloadHash` parameter is inserted verbatim into both the canonical headers block (`x-amz-content-sha256: <payloadHash>\n`) and the `Authorization` header string. If the caller passes a `payloadHash` containing `\n` or `\r` characters, the canonical request is corrupted and the output `Authorization` header value may contain injected line breaks, which could cause HTTP header splitting when the caller sets the headers on an HTTP request.
- **Impact:** HTTP header injection: an attacker who controls `payloadHash` (e.g., by supplying an arbitrary string instead of a true SHA-256 hex digest) can inject additional HTTP headers into the outgoing request, potentially poisoning caches, overriding security headers, or performing SSRF pivots via manipulated `Host` or redirect headers.
- **Fix:** Validate that `payloadHash` matches the expected format before use — either the literal string `"UNSIGNED-PAYLOAD"` or a 64-character lowercase hex string:

    bool validHash = (payloadHash == "UNSIGNED-PAYLOAD") ||
                     (payloadHash.size() == 64 &&
                      payloadHash.find_first_not_of("0123456789abcdef") == std::string::npos);
    if (!validHash) { throw std::invalid_argument("Invalid payloadHash"); }

---

### MEDIUM Missing Return Value Checks on OpenSSL EVP Calls

- **Location:** `SigV4Signer::Sha256Hex` (~lines 110–118)
- **Issue:** `EVP_DigestInit_ex`, `EVP_DigestUpdate`, and `EVP_DigestFinal_ex` all return `int` indicating success/failure. None of their return values are checked. If any call fails (e.g., due to FIPS policy rejecting an algorithm, or an out-of-memory condition), `hash` will contain uninitialized or zeroed bytes and `hashLen` will be 0, returning an empty or incorrect hex string silently.
- **Impact:** Silent production of an incorrect hash value, leading to signature computation over wrong data. In a security context this means the signed request may silently use a garbage hash, potentially bypassing integrity guarantees without any error surfacing to the caller.
- **Fix:** Check each return value and propagate failure:

    if (EVP_DigestInit_ex(ctx, EVP_sha256(), nullptr) != 1 ||
        EVP_DigestUpdate(ctx, data.data(), data.size()) != 1 ||
        EVP_DigestFinal_ex(ctx, hash, &hashLen) != 1) {
        EVP_MD_CTX_free(ctx);
        throw std::runtime_error("SHA-256 computation failed");
    }

Also apply the same pattern to `HMAC()` in `HmacSha256`.

---

### MEDIUM Memory Leak on EVP_MD_CTX on Exception Path

- **Location:** `SigV4Signer::Sha256Hex` (~lines 110–120)
- **Issue:** `EVP_MD_CTX_new()` is not checked for a null return. If it returns null, the subsequent calls dereference a null pointer (undefined behavior / crash). Additionally, if any intervening call were to throw, `EVP_MD_CTX_free(ctx)` at the end of the function would not be reached, leaking the context.
- **Impact:** Null-pointer dereference leading to process crash (DoS); heap leak under exception paths (minor resource exhaustion).
- **Fix:** Check for null immediately and wrap with RAII or a unique_ptr deleter:

    EVP_MD_CTX* ctx = EVP_MD_CTX_new();
    if (!ctx) throw std::runtime_error("EVP_MD_CTX_new failed");
    struct CtxGuard { EVP_MD_CTX* p; ~CtxGuard(){ EVP_MD_CTX_free(p); } } guard{ctx};

---

### LOW Credential Scope Components Not Validated for Injection Characters

- **Location:** `SigV4Signer::Sign`, credential scope and authorization header construction (~lines 320–330)
- **Issue:** `region`, `service`, and `accessKeyId` are concatenated directly into the `Authorization` header value (`"AWS4-HMAC-SHA256 Credential=" + accessKeyId + "/" + credentialScope + ..."`). None of these inputs are validated for control characters, spaces, commas, or newlines. A caller-supplied region such as `"us-east-1\r\nX-Injected: evil"` would produce a multi-line Authorization header value.
- **Impact:** HTTP header injection if the returned `Authorization` header value is written to an HTTP connection without further sanitization.
- **Fix:** Validate `region`, `service`, and `accessKeyId` against their expected character sets (alphanumeric, hyphens only for region; alphanumeric for service; alphanumeric and `+/=` for access key ID) before use, throwing `std::invalid_argument` on violation.

---

### LOW Non-Constant-Time HMAC Key Derivation Intermediate Values Held in `std::string`

- **Location:** `SigV4Signer::Sign`, signing key derivation (~lines 335–340)
- **Issue:** `kDate`, `kRegion`, `kService`, and `kSigning` are intermediate HMAC-SHA256 keys stored in `std::string`. `std::string` provides no guarantee of zeroing memory on destruction, so these derived key materials persist in heap memory after the function returns.
- **Impact:** Derived signing keys may be recoverable from a memory dump or swap file after the fact, reducing the secrecy window for the AWS secret key.
- **Fix:** Zero-out intermediate key strings before they go out of scope using `OPENSSL_cleanse` or an equivalent:

    OPENSSL_cleanse(kDate.data(), kDate.size());
    // repeat for kRegion, kService, kSigning

Consider encapsulating them in a wrapper type whose destructor calls `OPENSSL_cleanse`.


---

### 40_doc_application_cloud_slackCloudTaskExecutor_h

<a id="40-doc-application-cloud-slackcloudtaskexecutor-h"></a>

*Source doc:* `../../../queue/jarvisCppCyberSecAudit/40_doc_application_cloud_slackCloudTaskExecutor_h/docu.output.md`



### HIGH: SSRF via unvalidated API base URL
- **Location:** `ExecuteSlackPost` / `ExecuteSlackRead`, URL construction lines (~line 230, ~line 320)
- **Issue:** `SlackConnector::GetApiBaseUrl(connection)` is concatenated directly into the request URL without any validation that it resolves to an expected Slack host. If workflow definitions or cloud connection records can be influenced by an attacker (e.g., through configuration injection, a compromised workflow file, or an admin UI), they can redirect requests to an internal network endpoint.
- **Impact:** Server-side request forgery to internal metadata services (e.g., AWS IMDSv1 at 169.254.169.254), internal HTTP services, or exfiltration of the Bearer token to an attacker-controlled server.
- **Fix:** Validate that the resolved base URL has scheme `https` and host matches an allowlist (e.g., `^https://slack\.com/` or `^https://[a-z0-9-]+\.slack\.com/`). Reject or fail-safe on any other value before the URL is used.

---

### HIGH: Missing TLS peer/host verification
- **Location:** `PerformSlackRequest`, ~line 165–185
- **Issue:** `CURLOPT_SSL_VERIFYPEER` and `CURLOPT_SSL_VERIFYHOST` are never set. libcurl defaults are `1` and `2` respectively, but only when a CA bundle is actually trusted. The code conditionally sets `CURLOPT_CAINFO` only when `CurlWrapper::GetCaBundlePath()` is non-empty. When the path is empty, libcurl uses the system CA store — but if the system store is absent or empty (common in minimal containers), libcurl silently disables peer verification on some platforms/builds rather than failing. No explicit `CURLOPT_SSL_VERIFYPEER, 1L` and `CURLOPT_SSL_VERIFYHOST, 2L` are set to enforce the requirement.
- **Impact:** Bearer token and message content transmitted in cleartext or to a MitM endpoint.
- **Fix:** Unconditionally set `CURLOPT_SSL_VERIFYPEER, 1L` and `CURLOPT_SSL_VERIFYHOST, 2L`. If no CA bundle path is available, fail the request rather than proceeding.

---

### HIGH: Path traversal via `text_file` and `thread_ts_file` parameters
- **Location:** `ExecuteSlackPost`, ~lines 215–230
- **Issue:** The `text_file` and `thread_ts_file` task parameters are passed directly to `ReadFileToString`, which opens them with `std::ifstream` without any path canonicalization or confinement check. A workflow definition containing `"text_file": "../../etc/passwd"` or `"text_file": "/etc/shadow"` will read and transmit that file as a Slack message.
- **Impact:** Arbitrary file read from any path accessible to the j9t process, including credentials, private keys, `/proc/self/environ`, etc. Content is sent over the network and logged into task output.
- **Fix:** Resolve the provided path relative to a designated safe base directory (e.g., the workflow's working directory), then assert with `std::filesystem::canonical` that the result is still within that base directory. Reject paths that escape the boundary.

---

### HIGH: Unvalidated `limit` parameter enables large upstream request / DoS
- **Location:** `ExecuteSlackRead`, ~line 305
- **Issue:** The `limit` integer read from task params is passed directly into the Slack API URL as `&limit=<value>` with no bounds check. Slack's API accepts values up to 1000. A workflow param of `"limit": 1000` causes 1000 messages to be fetched, parsed, and written to disk. More critically, nothing prevents a negative value or a very large value from being injected into the URL string (e.g., `limit=-1` or, if the integer is misused as a string later, other injection).
- **Impact:** Uncontrolled memory allocation when Slack returns a large payload; potential DoS of the j9t process; also the string concatenation `std::to_string(limit)` with a negative value produces a URL like `&limit=-100` which is malformed but also demonstrates absent validation.
- **Fix:** Clamp `limit` to `[1, 200]` (or a configured maximum) before use. Reject values outside that range with a task failure.

---

### HIGH: Bearer token leaked into log on HTTP 4xx/5xx
- **Location:** `PerformSlackRequest`, ~line 195; `CheckSlackOk`, ~line 215
- **Issue:** When `httpCode >= 400`, `taskState.m_LastErrorMessage` is set to a string that includes only the HTTP code — acceptable. However `CheckSlackOk` sets `taskState.m_LastErrorMessage` to include the `error` field value returned by Slack. Slack's `invalid_auth` and `token_revoked` error responses sometimes echo partial token information. More directly: `PerformSlackRequest` constructs `authHeader = "Authorization: Bearer " + token` on the stack but the token value itself is never sanitised; if `curl_easy_strerror` or libcurl verbose mode is enabled elsewhere and writes to a shared log sink, the header (and token) can appear. Separately, the `LOG_APP_INFO` call in `ExecuteSlackPost` logs `channel` and `connection.m_Name`, which are user-controlled strings — if those strings contain log-injection characters (newlines, ANSI escape codes) they can corrupt log output.
- **Impact:** Token exposure in logs; log injection for log-forgery or log-viewer exploits.
- **Fix:** Sanitise all user-controlled strings before logging (strip or escape `\r`, `\n`, and non-printable characters). Never log the token or the full Authorization header value. Do not include Slack's raw `error` field in messages that reach persistent logs without sanitisation.

---

### MEDIUM: URL injection via `channel` and `oldest` parameters in GET URL
- **Location:** `ExecuteSlackRead`, ~lines 318–323
- **Issue:** `channel` and `oldest` are appended to the URL query string without percent-encoding. A channel value containing `&` or `=` (e.g., `"C01ABC&limit=1000&other=x"`) injects additional query parameters. `oldest` is similarly unencoded. While Slack validates channel IDs, the `#` prefix check only guards against names, not against embedded query-string metacharacters.
- **Impact:** Query-string parameter injection; could override `limit`, inject `cursor`, or manipulate other Slack API parameters beyond what the workflow definition intended.
- **Fix:** Use `curl_easy_escape` (or equivalent) to percent-encode all query-parameter values before concatenation, or use a proper URL-building helper.

---

### MEDIUM: Unbounded response body accumulation
- **Location:** `WriteBodyCallback` / `PerformSlackRequest`, ~lines 143–150
- **Issue:** `WriteBodyCallback` appends all bytes libcurl delivers into a `std::string` with no size limit. A slow or malicious endpoint (including a redirected SSRF target) can deliver an arbitrarily large body before the 30-second timeout expires.
- **Impact:** Unbounded heap growth; potential OOM crash of the j9t process (denial of service).
- **Fix:** Add a size check inside `WriteBodyCallback`: if `buf->size() + (size * nmemb)` exceeds a reasonable cap (e.g., 10 MB), return 0 to abort the transfer, and set a flag to propagate the error.

---

### MEDIUM: `CURLOPT_FOLLOWLOCATION` enabled without restricting redirect target
- **Location:** `PerformSlackRequest`, ~line 178
- **Issue:** Automatic redirect following is enabled. A server (or SSRF-redirected endpoint) can issue an HTTP 301/302 to any URL, including `http://169.254.169.254/` or other internal addresses, and libcurl will follow it. The original TLS verification and host pinning apply to the first request only; the redirect target is subject to the same (insufficient) validation.
- **Impact:** Amplifies the SSRF risk; also allows a compromised or spoofed Slack endpoint to redirect the Bearer token to an attacker-controlled host via the `Location` header.
- **Fix:** Disable `CURLOPT_FOLLOWLOCATION` for Slack API calls (Slack does not require redirect following for its Web API). If redirects are needed, set `CURLOPT_MAXREDIRS, 3L` and `CURLOPT_REDIR_PROTOCOLS, CURLPROTO_HTTPS` to restrict redirect targets to HTTPS only.

---

### LOW: `taskDefinition.m_WorkingDirectory` not validated before path join
- **Location:** `ExecuteSlackPost` / `ExecuteSlackRead`, ~lines 253–258 / ~lines 390–405
- **Issue:** `TaskPathResolver::ResolveTaskWorkingDirectoryPath` receives `taskDefinition.m_WorkingDirectory` directly. If that resolver does not itself canonicalise and confine the result, a workflow definition can specify `"working_directory": "../../sensitive_dir"` and output files will be written outside the intended working tree.
- **Impact:** Arbitrary file write to paths accessible to the j9t process (depending on resolver implementation).
- **Fix:** Verify here (or confirm that `TaskPathResolver` verifies) that the resolved path is a subdirectory of the workflow base directory before writing any files. Use `std::filesystem::canonical` after path construction and assert containment.


---

### 41_doc_application_cloud_slackConnector_h

<a id="41-doc-application-cloud-slackconnector-h"></a>

*Source doc:* `../../../queue/jarvisCppCyberSecAudit/41_doc_application_cloud_slackConnector_h/docu.output.md`



### HIGH Unvalidated URL from user-controlled input enables SSRF
- **Location:** `SlackConnector::GetApiBaseUrl` / `SlackConnector::TestConnection`, approximately lines 44–54 and 85–90
- **Issue:** `connection.m_Endpoint` is accepted verbatim and concatenated directly into the URL passed to `curl_easy_setopt(CURLOPT_URL, ...)`. No scheme validation, no hostname allowlist, and no block of private/loopback ranges is performed. `CURLOPT_FOLLOWLOCATION` is enabled, which compounds the problem by allowing redirects to internal destinations.
- **Impact:** An attacker who can supply or modify a `CloudConnection` record (e.g., through the workflow engine's configuration API or a compromised configuration file) can make the server-side curl request target arbitrary internal network endpoints (`http://169.254.169.254/`, `http://10.x.x.x/`, `file:///etc/passwd`, etc.), leaking cloud metadata or probing internal services.
- **Fix:** Enforce that the URL begins with `https://` (reject `http://`, `file://`, and all other schemes). Parse the hostname and reject RFC-1918/loopback/link-local addresses. Disable `CURLOPT_FOLLOWLOCATION` or set `CURLOPT_MAXREDIRS 0`. Consider an explicit allowlist of permitted hostnames (e.g., `slack.com` and any configured proxy).

---

### HIGH Missing TLS peer verification when CA bundle path is absent
- **Location:** `SlackConnector::TestConnection`, approximately lines 110–115
- **Issue:** `CURLOPT_CAINFO` is set only when `CurlWrapper::GetCaBundlePath()` returns a non-empty string. When the path is empty, no fallback is provided and curl defaults vary by build — on some platforms `CURLOPT_SSL_VERIFYPEER` and `CURLOPT_SSL_VERIFYHOST` default to 1 using the system bundle, but if the system bundle is absent or the curl build lacks one, verification silently degrades. Neither option is ever explicitly set to `1L` in this code, leaving the security property implicit and fragile.
- **Impact:** If TLS verification is not enforced, a network-positioned attacker can perform a machine-in-the-middle attack, intercept the Bearer token sent in the `Authorization` header, and subsequently impersonate the bot on Slack.
- **Fix:** Unconditionally add `curl_easy_setopt(curl, CURLOPT_SSL_VERIFYPEER, 1L)` and `curl_easy_setopt(curl, CURLOPT_SSL_VERIFYHOST, 2L)`. If the CA bundle path is empty, either abort with an error or rely on the compiled-in default and document that requirement explicitly.

---

### HIGH Bearer token exposed in error messages via curl error path
- **Location:** `SlackConnector::TestConnection`, approximately line 138
- **Issue:** When `curl_easy_perform` returns an error, `errorMessage` is set to a string that includes `curl_easy_strerror(res)`. While this specific path does not directly include the token, the `authHeader` string (`"Authorization: Bearer " + credentials.m_Token`) is built on the stack and lives for the duration of the function. More critically, if the caller or any logging layer records `errorMessage` together with request context, the token can appear. Additionally, error messages reflect the exact credential name (`connection.m_KeyName`) in `ResolveCredentials`, which leaks internal key manager identifiers to any consumer of `errorMessage`.
- **Impact:** Secrets leak into logs, error responses, or monitoring pipelines, enabling credential theft.
- **Fix:** Never include key names or credential identifiers in externally visible error strings. Use opaque identifiers or omit them entirely. Ensure `authHeader` and `credentials.m_Token` are zeroed before the function returns (e.g., using `explicit_bzero` or a secure string wrapper).

---

### MEDIUM Unbounded response body accumulation enables memory exhaustion / DoS
- **Location:** `SlackConnector::TestConnection`, write callback lambda and `responseBody`, approximately lines 96–103
- **Issue:** The `writeCallback` appends all data curl delivers into `responseBody` with no size cap. A malicious or misbehaving endpoint (reachable via the SSRF vector above, or a Slack API returning an abnormally large body) can cause unbounded heap allocation until the process runs out of memory.
- **Impact:** Denial of service against the workflow engine process; potential for forcing large allocations that destabilize the service.
- **Fix:** Track accumulated size inside the callback and return 0 (signaling failure to curl) once a reasonable maximum is exceeded (e.g., 1 MB for a simple JSON auth response). Set `CURLOPT_MAXFILESIZE` or `CURLOPT_MAXFILESIZE_LARGE` as an additional defense-in-depth measure.

---

### MEDIUM HTTP 200 with `"ok": false` accepted as success when JSON parse fails
- **Location:** `SlackConnector::TestConnection`, approximately lines 148–165
- **Issue:** If `parser.iterate(paddedJson).get(doc)` succeeds but the subsequent `doc["ok"].get_bool()` fails for any structural reason (e.g., unexpected JSON shape), the condition `if (doc["ok"].get_bool().get(ok) || !ok)` evaluates the `||` short-circuit and falls into the failure branch — but only if the get itself returns a non-zero error code. If simdjson returns success but `ok` stays `false` due to type mismatch, the logic is correct; however, if parsing the entire document fails silently and `ok` remains its zero-initialized `false`, the function returns `false` with a misleading error. More seriously, a response body that is syntactically valid JSON but semantically adversarial (e.g., `{"ok": true}` returned by an SSRF target) passes the authentication check unconditionally.
- **Impact:** Combined with the SSRF finding, an attacker-controlled endpoint returning `{"ok":true}` causes `TestConnection` to report success for an entirely wrong target, masking the misconfiguration.
- **Fix:** After confirming `ok == true`, also validate that the response contains expected Slack-specific fields (e.g., `team_id`, `user_id`) to confirm the response origin. This is a defense-in-depth measure; the primary fix is the SSRF URL allowlist.

---

### LOW Credential key name reflected into error messages
- **Location:** `SlackConnector::ResolveCredentials`, approximately lines 63–75
- **Issue:** `connection.m_KeyName` and `connection.m_Name` are interpolated directly into `errorMessage`. If these values originate from user input and `errorMessage` is returned to an API caller, they constitute a reflected information-disclosure path revealing internal key manager namespace structure.
- **Impact:** An authenticated but low-privileged user enumerating connection names can probe which key names exist in the key manager by observing differences in error messages.
- **Fix:** Replace key-name-specific messages with generic ones (e.g., "Credential not found") for externally visible error strings; log the specific name only at an internal/debug log level inaccessible to end users.


---

### 42_doc_application_cloud_snowflakeCloudTaskExecutor_h

<a id="42-doc-application-cloud-snowflakecloudtaskexecutor-h"></a>

*Source doc:* `../../../queue/jarvisCppCyberSecAudit/42_doc_application_cloud_snowflakeCloudTaskExecutor_h/docu.output.md`



### CRITICAL Path Traversal via `outputFile` Parameter

- **Location:** `SnowflakeCloudTaskExecutor::ExecuteCloud`, lines around `std::filesystem::path outputPath = workDir / outputFile;`
- **Issue:** `outputFile` is taken directly from attacker-controlled task params JSON without any validation or sanitization. A value such as `"../../etc/cron.d/evil"` or `"../../../home/user/.ssh/authorized_keys"` will be joined with `workDir` by `std::filesystem::operator/`, which will follow the path components literally and escape the intended working directory.
- **Impact:** Arbitrary file write to any path writable by the process. An attacker controlling task params can overwrite system files, plant cron jobs, inject SSH keys, or corrupt other workflow outputs.
- **Fix:** After constructing `outputPath`, verify it is still under `workDir`:
  ```
  auto canonical_out = std::filesystem::weakly_canonical(outputPath);
  auto canonical_work = std::filesystem::weakly_canonical(workDir);
  if (canonical_out.string().rfind(canonical_work.string(), 0) != 0)
  {
      taskState.m_LastErrorMessage = "outputFile escapes working directory";
      taskState.m_State = TaskInstanceStateKind::Failed;
      return false;
  }
  ```
  Also reject bare filenames containing path separators or null bytes before even constructing the path.

---

### CRITICAL Unbound Response Body Accumulation / DoS via `writeCallback`

- **Location:** `SnowflakeRequest`, lambda `writeCallback` and `responseBody.append(...)`, lines ~80–95
- **Issue:** The `CURLOPT_WRITEFUNCTION` callback appends every byte received from the remote server into `responseBody` without any size cap. A malicious or compromised Snowflake endpoint (or SSRF pivot) can send an arbitrarily large response, causing unbounded heap growth until the process is OOM-killed or the host is exhausted.
- **Impact:** Denial of service. The same function is called for every poll iteration, compounding the exposure.
- **Fix:** Enforce a maximum response size inside the callback and return 0 (signaling curl to abort) when the limit is exceeded:
  ```
  static constexpr size_t kMaxResponseBytes = 64 * 1024 * 1024; // 64 MB
  if (buf->size() + size * nmemb > kMaxResponseBytes)
      return 0; // causes CURLE_WRITE_ERROR
  buf->append(...);
  ```

---

### CRITICAL SSRF via Unsanitized `connection.m_Endpoint` in API URL

- **Location:** `SnowflakeCloudTaskExecutor::ExecuteCloud`, line around `std::string apiBase = SnowflakeConnector::BuildApiBaseUrl(connection.m_Endpoint);` and subsequently `submitUrl`, `pollUrl`, `cancelUrl`
- **Issue:** `connection.m_Endpoint` flows from user-supplied CloudConnection configuration directly into the URL passed to `curl_easy_setopt(CURLOPT_URL, ...)`. There is no validation that the scheme is `https://`, that the host belongs to the Snowflake domain, or that the URL does not contain credentials, redirects to localhost, or uses a non-HTTP scheme (`file://`, `dict://`, `gopher://`, etc.). `CURLOPT_FOLLOWLOCATION` is also enabled, which compounds the risk.
- **Impact:** Full SSRF: an attacker who can create or modify a CloudConnection can make the workflow engine issue authenticated HTTP requests (including the JWT Bearer token) to arbitrary internal hosts, cloud metadata endpoints (169.254.169.254), or local services.
- **Fix:** Validate that `apiBase` uses the `https` scheme and ends with `.snowflakecomputing.com` (or a configured allowlist of domains) before constructing any URL. Disable `CURLOPT_FOLLOWLOCATION` or restrict it with `CURLOPT_REDIR_PROTOCOLS`.

---

### HIGH JWT Injected into HTTP Header Without Validation for Newline Characters

- **Location:** `SnowflakeRequest`, line around `std::string authHeader = "Authorization: Bearer " + jwt;` and `curl_slist_append(headers, authHeader.c_str())`
- **Issue:** `credentials.m_Token` (the JWT) is concatenated directly into a raw HTTP header string. If the token contains CR (`\r`) or LF (`\n`) characters, the resulting header string will split into multiple headers, allowing HTTP header injection. libcurl does sanitize headers in recent versions but this is a version-dependent behavior that should not be relied upon.
- **Impact:** HTTP response splitting or header injection if the JWT value is attacker-influenced and the libcurl version in use does not strip embedded newlines.
- **Fix:** Validate that `credentials.m_Token` contains no `\r` or `\n` characters before constructing the header. Alternatively, use `curl_easy_setopt(CURLOPT_XOAUTH2_BEARER, ...)` or a purpose-built header API rather than raw string construction.

---

### HIGH JSON Injection via `warehouse`, `database`, `schema` Values

- **Location:** `SnowflakeCloudTaskExecutor::ExecuteCloud`, lines around `requestBody += ",\"warehouse\":\"" + warehouse + "\""` and equivalent lines for `database` and `schema`
- **Issue:** `warehouse`, `database`, and `schema` values sourced from task params or connection params are embedded into the JSON request body with no escaping. Only `query` receives manual JSON escaping. A value like `","timeout":0,"x":"` in any of these fields will break out of the intended JSON structure and inject arbitrary JSON keys.
- **Impact:** An attacker controlling connection params or task params can override request fields (e.g., set `timeout` to 0, inject unexpected statement parameters), potentially bypassing intended resource controls or causing unexpected Snowflake behavior.
- **Fix:** Apply the same character-by-character escaping used for `query` to all three fields, or use a proper JSON serialization library (e.g., nlohmann/json or simdjson's builder) instead of manual string concatenation.

---

### HIGH Missing TLS Certificate Verification Fallback

- **Location:** `SnowflakeRequest`, lines around `curl_easy_setopt(curl, CURLOPT_CAINFO, caBundle.c_str())` and the `if (!caBundle.empty())` guard
- **Issue:** TLS peer verification (`CURLOPT_SSL_VERIFYPEER`) and hostname verification (`CURLOPT_SSL_VERIFYHOST`) are never explicitly set. When `caBundle` is empty, neither option is set, meaning the code silently falls back to whatever the system default is. Some builds or environments default these to 0 (disabled). There is no explicit `curl_easy_setopt(curl, CURLOPT_SSL_VERIFYPEER, 1L)` call.
- **Impact:** On systems where libcurl defaults to disabled TLS verification, the JWT and all query data would be sent over an unauthenticated TLS channel, enabling MitM attacks.
- **Fix:** Always explicitly set both options:
  ```
  curl_easy_setopt(curl, CURLOPT_SSL_VERIFYPEER, 1L);
  curl_easy_setopt(curl, CURLOPT_SSL_VERIFYHOST, 2L);
  ```
  These are safe to set regardless of whether a custom CA bundle is also provided.

---

### HIGH Secrets Leakage — Raw API Response Body Logged and Stored

- **Location:** `SnowflakeCloudTaskExecutor::ExecuteCloud`, line around `WriteResponseJson(workDir, taskState, responseBody)`
- **Issue:** `responseBody` (the raw Snowflake API response, which can contain query result data including PII and business-sensitive records) is written verbatim to `response.json` on disk. Additionally, on error paths (lines ~`taskState.m_LastErrorMessage += ": " + responseBody`), up to 500 bytes of raw API response are embedded into the error message, which is typically persisted and may be logged. The Snowflake response can contain query results, error details with schema information, and token metadata.
- **Impact:** Sensitive data leakage through log files, error message stores, and disk artifacts with potentially broader access than the credential store.
- **Fix:** Remove or gate `WriteResponseJson` behind a debug/trace mode flag. Restrict what is embedded in `m_LastErrorMessage` from API responses to structured error codes only, never raw body content.

---

### HIGH Uncontrolled `statementTimeout` and `pollInterval` Enable Resource Exhaustion

- **Location:** `SnowflakeCloudTaskExecutor::ExecuteCloud`, lines around `statementTimeout = static_cast<int>(val)` and `pollInterval = static_cast<int>(std::max(val, uint64_t(1)))`
- **Issue:** `timeout` is cast from `uint64_t` to `int` without range checking; values above `INT_MAX` will produce a negative or zero timeout, disabling the timeout entirely. There is also no upper bound enforced on `statementTimeout`; a task param of `999999999` seconds will cause the polling loop to run effectively indefinitely. `pollInterval` has a minimum of 1 enforced but no maximum.
- **Impact:** A workflow that is never cancelled and has an unbounded timeout can pin a thread indefinitely, exhausting the worker thread pool.
- **Fix:** Clamp both values to sane bounds before use, e.g., `statementTimeout` to [1, 86400] and `pollInterval` to [1, 60]. Add an explicit overflow check before the `static_cast<int>` conversion.

---

### MEDIUM SQL Injection Risk — Unsanitized `query` Passed to Snowflake

- **Location:** `SnowflakeCloudTaskExecutor::ExecuteCloud`, lines around `std::string query = getStringParam("query")`
- **Issue:** The `query` field is taken verbatim from task params JSON and forwarded as a complete SQL statement to Snowflake. No parameterization or allowlist validation is performed. The code correctly JSON-escapes the query before HTTP transport, but the SQL itself is not validated. Any party with access to task definition authoring can execute arbitrary Snowflake SQL, including `DROP DATABASE`, `COPY INTO @stage`, or data exfiltration queries.
- **Impact:** Privilege escalation within Snowflake: full access to all objects accessible to the connected service account.
- **Fix:** This is an architectural issue. Access to task definition authoring must itself be access-controlled at the application layer. Additionally, consider enforcing a Snowflake role with minimal required privileges for the service account, and document that the `query` parameter is fully trusted input requiring authoring-level authorization.

---

### MEDIUM `handle` Value Not Validated Before URL Construction

- **Location:** `SnowflakeCloudTaskExecutor::ExecuteCloud`, lines around `std::string pollUrl = apiBase + "/api/v2/statements/" + handle` and `cancelUrl = apiBase + "/api/v2/statements/" + handle + "/cancel"`
- **Issue:** `handle` is extracted from the Snowflake JSON response (a `string_view` from `submitDoc["statementHandle"]`) and appended directly to a URL without validation. If the Snowflake endpoint is compromised or the response is manipulated, a handle containing URL path traversal segments (`../../admin`) or query string injections (`?foo=bar`) would alter the target URL.
- **Impact:** URL manipulation on the poll and cancel endpoints; limited in practice because the target host is fixed, but worth closing.
- **Fix:** Validate that `handle` matches the expected Snowflake statement handle format (UUID-like alphanumeric with hyphens) using a regex or character whitelist before constructing URLs, and URL-encode the value via `curl_easy_escape`.

---

### MEDIUM Synchronous Result Path Uses Stale `responseBody` from Submit Response

- **Location:** `SnowflakeCloudTaskExecutor::ExecuteCloud`, lines around `bool resultReady = (message == "Statement executed successfully.")` and later `simdjson::padded_string resultPadded(responseBody)` in the result parsing block
- **Issue:** When `resultReady` is true on the first response, `responseBody` holds the submit response and is parsed for the result set. However, if the polling loop runs and then sets `resultReady = true`, the final `responseBody` is the last poll response. The code is architecturally correct here, but `resultPadded` is constructed from `responseBody` which is a local `std::string` whose address is stable. This is not a memory safety bug currently, but the `simdjson::ondemand::document` holds internal references into `resultPadded`; the existing column/row extraction loops that use both `resultDoc` and then re-initialize `resultParser` would be safe as long as they are distinct parser instances. Verify this holds under simdjson's ondemand guarantees, as accessing a document after its backing `padded_string` goes out of scope would be undefined behavior.
- **Impact:** Potential use-after-free or undefined behavior if simdjson document lifetimes are not correctly respected. Risk is low currently but fragile.
- **Fix:** Ensure `resultPadded` and `resultDoc` have the same or longer lifetime than all their dependent `array`/`object`/`string_view` accesses. Restructure the result extraction into a scope where `resultPadded` is guaranteed live.

---

### LOW Column Names Written to JSON/CSV Output Without Bounds Check

- **Location:** `SnowflakeCloudTaskExecutor::ExecuteCloud`, JSON output block around `out << "\"" << columnNames[c] << "\": ";`
- **Issue:** Column names extracted from `resultSetMetaData.rowType[*].name` are written into JSON output with JSON escaping applied only to values, not to column names. A column name containing `"` or `\` will produce malformed JSON output. In CSV output, `CsvEscape` is correctly applied to column names.
- **Impact:** Malformed JSON output file; potential for downstream JSON parsers consuming the file to misinterpret structure.
- **Fix:** Apply the same JSON character escaping loop used for values to column names when writing JSON output.

---

### LOW Error Message Contains Snowflake Internal Error Codes and Messages

- **Location:** `SnowflakeCloudTaskExecutor::ExecuteCloud`, lines around `taskState.m_LastErrorMessage = "Snowflake query failed with code " + std::string(pollCode)` and `taskState.m_LastErrorMessage += ": " + std::string(errMsg)`
- **Issue:** Snowflake error codes and message strings from the API are propagated into `taskState.m_LastErrorMessage` without filtering. These can reveal internal schema names, object names, and query structure in error responses visible to workflow consumers.
- **Impact:** Information disclosure; schema and object name enumeration by observing error messages.
- **Fix:** Log the full error internally at debug/trace level and expose only a sanitized or code-only message in `m_LastErrorMessage`.


---

### 43_doc_application_cloud_snowflakeConnector_h

<a id="43-doc-application-cloud-snowflakeconnector-h"></a>

*Source doc:* `../../../queue/jarvisCppCyberSecAudit/43_doc_application_cloud_snowflakeConnector_h/docu.output.md`



### HIGH SSRF via unvalidated endpoint URL

- **Location:** `SnowflakeConnector::BuildApiBaseUrl` and `SnowflakeConnector::TestConnection`, approximately lines 60–75 and 115–120
- **Issue:** `BuildApiBaseUrl` explicitly allows any value that begins with `https://` or `http://` to pass through unchanged. The caller in `TestConnection` then passes the result directly to `curl_easy_setopt(curl, CURLOPT_URL, ...)`. An operator who can write a `CloudConnection` record (or an attacker who can influence `m_Endpoint`) can supply an arbitrary URL such as `http://169.254.169.254/latest/meta-data/` (EC2 IMDS), any internal service, or `file:///etc/passwd`.
- **Impact:** Full SSRF: reads from cloud metadata services, internal APIs, or local files; can be used for credential theft or internal network reconnaissance.
- **Fix:** Remove the "use as-is" fast-path entirely. Validate that the endpoint contains only characters legal in a Snowflake account locator (`[a-zA-Z0-9_\-.]`), enforce a maximum length, and always construct the URL as `"https://" + sanitized_endpoint + ".snowflakecomputing.com"`. Reject anything that does not match.

---

### HIGH JSON injection in manually assembled request body

- **Location:** `SnowflakeConnector::TestConnection`, approximately lines 135–155
- **Issue:** The warehouse, database, and schema values from `connection.m_Params` are concatenated directly into a raw JSON string with no escaping: `requestBody += ",\"warehouse\":\"" + warehouseIt->second + "\""`. A value containing `"` or `\` will break JSON structure. A value such as `COMPUTE_WH","statement":"DROP TABLE users","x":"` injects an arbitrary SQL statement into the body sent to Snowflake.
- **Impact:** SQL injection via the Snowflake REST API: an attacker who can set connection parameters can execute arbitrary SQL under the service account's privileges.
- **Fix:** Use a proper JSON serialization library (simdjson is already included for parsing; use a builder such as nlohmann/json or RapidJSON for construction) so all string values are correctly escaped. Never concatenate untrusted strings into raw JSON.

---

### HIGH Missing TLS peer verification (no `CURLOPT_SSL_VERIFYPEER` / `CURLOPT_SSL_VERIFYHOST`)

- **Location:** `SnowflakeConnector::TestConnection`, curl setup block, approximately lines 160–175
- **Issue:** `CURLOPT_SSL_VERIFYPEER` and `CURLOPT_SSL_VERIFYHOST` are never set. When `CurlWrapper::GetCaBundlePath()` returns an empty string the CA bundle is also not set. libcurl's compiled-in default may or may not enable verification depending on the build; the code relies on that implicit default rather than enforcing it explicitly. In environments where the default is off (custom builds, some embedded platforms) the JWT bearer token is sent over an unauthenticated TLS connection.
- **Impact:** Man-in-the-middle interception of the RSA JWT, which grants full Snowflake access for up to one hour; also intercept of any data returned by Snowflake.
- **Fix:** Unconditionally add `curl_easy_setopt(curl, CURLOPT_SSL_VERIFYPEER, 1L)` and `curl_easy_setopt(curl, CURLOPT_SSL_VERIFYHOST, 2L)` before `curl_easy_perform`. If no CA bundle path is available, fail closed with an error rather than proceeding.

---

### HIGH Unbounded response body accumulation (DoS / memory exhaustion)

- **Location:** `SnowflakeConnector::TestConnection`, `writeCallback` lambda and `responseBody` string, approximately lines 165–170
- **Issue:** The write callback appends every byte received into `responseBody` with no size cap. A malicious or misbehaving endpoint can stream an arbitrarily large response, exhausting process heap memory.
- **Impact:** Denial of service via memory exhaustion; potential out-of-memory crash of the workflow engine.
- **Fix:** Add a size guard inside `writeCallback`: if `buf->size() + size * nmemb` exceeds a reasonable cap (e.g., 1 MiB for a test-connection response), return a value other than `size * nmemb` to signal an error and abort the transfer. Alternatively use `CURLOPT_MAXFILESIZE` as a coarser backstop.

---

### MEDIUM `http://` scheme accepted for Snowflake API base URL

- **Location:** `SnowflakeConnector::BuildApiBaseUrl`, approximately line 63
- **Issue:** The check `base.find("http://") == 0` allows plain HTTP URLs through. The JWT bearer token in the `Authorization` header is then transmitted in cleartext.
- **Impact:** Credential exposure of the JWT (usable for ~1 hour of Snowflake access) to any network observer.
- **Fix:** If a full URL is accepted at all (which should be removed per the SSRF finding above), reject any URL whose scheme is not `https://`.

---

### MEDIUM `CURLOPT_FOLLOWLOCATION` enabled with no redirect limit

- **Location:** `SnowflakeConnector::TestConnection`, approximately line 173
- **Issue:** `CURLOPT_FOLLOWLOCATION` is set to 1 without a corresponding `CURLOPT_MAXREDIRS` limit and without `CURLOPT_REDIR_PROTOCOLS` restricting the schemes that may be followed. libcurl will follow redirects to `file://`, `ftp://`, or other internal URLs, and will forward the `Authorization: Bearer` header to the redirect target unless `CURLOPT_UNRESTRICTED_AUTH` is explicitly off.
- **Impact:** Amplifies the SSRF risk and may leak the JWT to an attacker-controlled redirect destination.
- **Fix:** Set `CURLOPT_MAXREDIRS` to a small value (e.g., 3), set `CURLOPT_REDIR_PROTOCOLS` to `CURLPROTO_HTTPS` only, and verify that `CURLOPT_UNRESTRICTED_AUTH` is 0 (the default, but make it explicit).

---

### MEDIUM JWT token written into `errorMessage` path without sanitization

- **Location:** `SnowflakeConnector::TestConnection`, approximately lines 185–195
- **Issue:** When HTTP status ≥ 400 and `responseBody.size() < 500`, the raw response body is appended to `errorMessage`. The Snowflake error response may echo back request fields including fragments of the Authorization header value or SQL text. `errorMessage` is routinely surfaced to callers, logged, and potentially returned over the API.
- **Impact:** Secret or sensitive data (JWT fragments, SQL) may appear in logs or API responses, aiding an attacker.
- **Fix:** Log or return only the HTTP status code and a Snowflake-provided error code/message after parsing it from JSON. Do not include raw response body in user-visible or log-visible error strings.


---
