### 47_doc_application_file_fileWatcher_h

<a id="47-doc-application-file-filewatcher-h"></a>

*Source doc:* `../../../queue/jarvisCppCyberSecAudit/47_doc_application_file_fileWatcher_h/docu.output.md`



### MEDIUM Path prefix collision in root-removal pruning enables unintended file eviction

- **Location:** `FileWatcher::Watch()`, the removedRoots pruning loop (~line 195–207 in the .cpp)
- **Issue:** The check `it->first.rfind(canonRootStr, 0) == 0` is a raw string prefix match on filesystem paths. If a registered root is `/tmp/jobs` and another root is `/tmp/jobs-extra`, removing `/tmp/jobs` will also prune all tracked entries whose string key begins with `/tmp/jobs`, including those legitimately under `/tmp/jobs-extra`. No path separator is appended before the comparison.
- **Impact:** An attacker or mis-behaving caller who can register path names that share a prefix with a legitimate root can cause `FileRemovedEvent` suppression for files under the surviving root, creating a blind spot in the watcher—files modified or deleted under the sibling root will never be detected because they have been silently ejected from the `files` map and will be re-added as if new on the next scan cycle, hiding the modification history.
- **Fix:** Append the platform path separator to `canonRootStr` before the prefix test, e.g. `std::string const prefix = canonRootStr + std::string(1, static_cast<char>(fs::path::preferred_separator));` and match against `prefix` (also handle the edge case where the key itself equals `canonRootStr` exactly).

---

### MEDIUM TOCTOU race between `AddPath` existence check and watch-thread use

- **Location:** `FileWatcher::AddPath()` (~line 57–60 in the .cpp) and `FileWatcher::Watch()` / `scanRoot` lambda
- **Issue:** `AddPath` calls `fs::is_directory(root)` and then `fs::canonical(root)` under the lock, then the canonicalized path is stored. The watch thread later calls `scanRoot` with that stored path. Between the check and the actual directory iteration there is an unbounded time window; on systems with world-writable parent directories an adversary can replace the validated directory with a symlink pointing to an arbitrary location (e.g. `/etc`, `/proc`) before the watch thread iterates it.
- **Impact:** The watcher will recursively iterate attacker-controlled or sensitive filesystem paths, emitting `FileAddedEvent`/`FileModifiedEvent`/`FileRemovedEvent` for files it should never see. Depending on how downstream consumers act on those events (e.g. triggering workflow execution, file processing, Python embedding), this is an SSRF-equivalent path traversal via symlink.
- **Fix:** In `scanRoot`, re-canonicalize the path under a chroot-equivalent check or use `O_NOFOLLOW`-style open-then-fstat to verify the inode matches what was originally validated. At minimum, verify that the resolved path still has the expected canonical prefix before iterating.

---

### MEDIUM Symlink following in `recursive_directory_iterator` enables path traversal

- **Location:** `FileWatcher::Watch()`, `scanRoot` lambda, `fs::recursive_directory_iterator` call (~line 150)
- **Issue:** `fs::recursive_directory_iterator` is constructed without `fs::directory_options::follow_directory_symlink` being explicitly excluded—the default on many implementations will follow symlinks into directories. If a symlink inside a watched root points outside the intended tree (e.g. to `/etc`), the iterator silently traverses it. `IsValidFile` does not verify that the resolved path remains under the intended root.
- **Impact:** A user or process that can create a symlink inside a watched directory can cause the engine to observe, and fire events for, files anywhere on the filesystem, which downstream workflow processing may then act upon.
- **Fix:** Add `fs::directory_options::skip_permission_denied` (already present) but also explicitly do not pass `follow_directory_symlink`. After obtaining each entry, verify `fs::weakly_canonical(file.path()) starts_with canonical(root)` before processing.

---

### LOW Unbounded growth of the `files` map (uncontrolled allocation / DoS)

- **Location:** `FileWatcher::Watch()`, `scanRoot` lambda, `files[pathStr] = currentTime` (~line 165)
- **Issue:** The `files` map grows without any cap for every unique file path discovered across all registered roots. There is no limit on the number of roots (`AddPath` has no cap), no limit on tree depth, and no limit on the number of files tracked. An adversary who can write files into a watched directory, or register many large directory trees, can drive unbounded heap allocation.
- **Impact:** Memory exhaustion causing the host process to be OOM-killed, denying service to legitimate workflows.
- **Fix:** Impose a configurable maximum on both the number of additional roots (`m_AdditionalRoots.size()`) and the total number of tracked entries in `files`. Return an error or log and skip when the limit is reached.

---

### LOW Sensitive path information written to application log unconditionally

- **Location:** `FileWatcher::AddPath()` (~line 74), `FileWatcher::RemovePath()` (~line 93), `FileWatcher::Watch()` (~line 247 for the deletion log)
- **Issue:** Full canonicalized filesystem paths—including paths supplied by external callers—are logged unconditionally via `LOG_APP_INFO`. In environments where log output is accessible to unauthorized parties (log aggregators, SIEM, shared log files), this leaks internal directory structure. The deletion log at `Watch()` also logs every file path that disappears from disk, which may include secrets files, private keys, or credential stores being rotated.
- **Impact:** Information disclosure of filesystem layout, presence of sensitive files, and operational state to anyone with log read access.
- **Fix:** Either gate these log lines behind a debug-only flag (similar to the existing `#ifndef NDEBUG` guard used for the stop message), or sanitize/truncate the path to a relative form before logging.


---

### 48_doc_application_file_scriptRegistry_h

<a id="48-doc-application-file-scriptregistry-h"></a>

*Source doc:* `../../../queue/jarvisCppCyberSecAudit/48_doc_application_file_scriptRegistry_h/docu.output.md`



### HIGH Path traversal via unsanitized module-path input in FindByModulePath

- **Location:** `ScriptRegistry::FindByModulePath`, approximately line 290
- **Issue:** The caller-supplied `modulePath` string has every `.` replaced with `/` and `.py` appended, then fed directly into `FindByFilePath`. There is no check that the resulting path stays within any expected directory. A caller passing `"..%2f..%2fsome.sensitive.file"` or, more directly via the dot-replacement logic, `"...scripts...parseLog"` is one angle; the real exposure is that if any upstream layer URL-decodes before calling this function, a path like `../../etc/passwd` expressed as `....etc.passwd` resolves to `../../etc/passwd.py` after substitution. More directly, a string such as `../etc/shadow` (containing a literal `/` rather than `.`) passes through with no transformation and no rejection, reaching the suffix-match loop with attacker-chosen path components.
- **Impact:** An attacker who controls the module-path argument (e.g. via an AI tool-call parameter or REST API query) can probe whether arbitrary absolute or relative file paths ending in `.py` exist inside the registry, and, if the registry stores absolute paths, can match entries for files outside the intended scripts directory.
- **Fix:** After constructing `filePath` in `FindByModulePath`, call `std::filesystem::path(filePath).lexically_normal()` and verify that the normalised path does not begin with `..` and does not contain path-separator sequences that escape the scripts root. Reject any input that fails this check. Also reject inputs containing a literal `/` or `\` before the dot-to-slash substitution.

---

### HIGH Unbounded memory growth / DoS via uncontrolled registry size in ScanDirectory

- **Location:** `ScriptRegistry::ScanDirectory`, approximately line 220; `ScriptRegistry::AddOrUpdate`, approximately line 245
- **Issue:** `recursive_directory_iterator` descends into the entire directory tree with no depth limit and no cap on the number of entries inserted into `m_Entries`. Every `.sh`, `.py`, or `.ps1` file that contains `@jarvis-script` and a `@short` field is inserted. If the scripts directory is attacker-writable, or if symbolic links inside it point outside (the iterator follows symlinks by default unless `follow_directory_symlink` is explicitly disabled), an attacker can cause unbounded map growth.
- **Impact:** An attacker with write access to the scripts directory, or who can trigger repeated `AddOrUpdate` calls, can exhaust process heap memory, causing denial of service.
- **Fix:** Add a hard cap on `m_Entries.size()` before inserting (e.g. reject entries beyond a configurable limit and log a warning). Pass `std::filesystem::directory_options::skip_permission_denied` and explicitly disable symlink-following (`std::filesystem::directory_options::follow_directory_symlink` should be omitted or overridden) when constructing the iterator. Validate that each resolved path is strictly beneath `scriptsDir` using `std::filesystem::relative` or canonical-path prefix comparison.

---

### MEDIUM Markdown injection via unsanitized script metadata in SerializeMarkdownTable

- **Location:** `ScriptRegistry::SerializeMarkdownTable`, approximately line 265
- **Issue:** `entry->m_Short`, `entry->m_Params[i]`, and `entry->m_FilePath` are written verbatim into a Markdown table that is described as being injected into AI context (§11.6). None of these values are sanitised. A script whose `@short` or `@params` field contains `|`, newlines, or Markdown control sequences can break out of the table cell and inject arbitrary Markdown, including new table rows, headings, or fenced code blocks that the AI model will interpret as legitimate registry entries.
- **Impact:** An attacker who can place a crafted `.py` / `.sh` file in the scripts directory can inject false entries into the AI's context, potentially causing the AI to invoke non-existent or malicious tool paths.
- **Fix:** Escape `|` as `\|`, strip or encode newlines and carriage returns, and enforce a maximum length on `m_Short` and each parameter string before writing them into the Markdown output.

---

### MEDIUM Log injection via unvalidated file-path strings in LOG_CORE_* calls

- **Location:** `ScriptRegistry::ParseHeader` (~line 195), `ScriptRegistry::ScanDirectory` (~line 232), `ScriptRegistry::AddOrUpdate` (~line 248, 255), `ScriptRegistry::Remove` (~line 260)
- **Issue:** `filePath.string()` is passed directly as a format argument to `LOG_CORE_INFO` / `LOG_CORE_WARN`. File paths on most operating systems can contain newlines, ANSI escape sequences, or log-delimiter characters. If the logging backend writes to a structured log, a path such as `"legit.py\n[ERROR] fake entry"` forges a new log line.
- **Impact:** An attacker who controls file names in the watched directory can inject false log entries, obscure audit trails, or (with ANSI-aware terminals) cause terminal escape injection.
- **Fix:** Sanitise file-path strings before logging by replacing or stripping control characters (codepoints < 0x20 and 0x7F), or use a logging call that treats the path as a raw opaque string rather than an interpreted format value.

---

### MEDIUM Suffix-match path confusion / spoofing in FindByFilePath

- **Location:** `ScriptRegistry::FindByFilePath`, approximately line 308
- **Issue:** When no direct match is found, the code performs a substring suffix match: any registry key that *ends with* the caller-supplied `filePath` (preceded by `/` or at position 0) is returned. A caller querying `"og.py"` would match an entry keyed as `/var/scripts/parseLog.py`. Combined with the dot-to-slash conversion in `FindByModulePath`, a short or generic module name can accidentally resolve to an unintended script.
- **Impact:** Ambiguous lookups may return the wrong `ScriptRegistryEntry`, causing the engine to execute a different script than intended. In a multi-tenant or plugin context this is an authorization boundary issue.
- **Fix:** Require callers to supply fully qualified relative paths, or constrain the suffix match to full path-component boundaries only and log a warning when more than one candidate matches. Prefer exact keying using a canonical relative path computed at registration time.


---

### 49_doc_application_jarvisAgent_h

<a id="49-doc-application-jarvisagent-h"></a>

*Source doc:* `../../../queue/jarvisCppCyberSecAudit/49_doc_application_jarvisAgent_h/docu.output.md`



### MEDIUM — Global raw pointer `App::g_App` accessible without synchronization

- **Location:** `App::g_App` (header, ~line 72); written in `JarvisAgent::OnStart()` (~line 110) and nulled in `JarvisAgent::OnShutdown()` (~line 430)
- **Issue:** `App::g_App` is a public `static` raw pointer with no access control, no mutex, and no `std::atomic`. It is written on the main thread during startup and shutdown while other subsystems (WebServer Crow threads, trigger callbacks, Python pool threads) may read it concurrently. The null assignment in `OnShutdown()` happens while `WaitStop()` calls for the WebServer and PythonEnginePool have not yet been reached, creating a window where a background thread dereferences a null or dangling pointer.
- **Impact:** A race between a background thread reading `App::g_App` and the main thread nulling it during shutdown can produce a null-pointer dereference or use-after-free, crashing the process. Because `g_App` exposes every subsystem (WorkflowRuntimeManager, WebServer, PythonPool, etc.) through getters, an attacker who can trigger the race (e.g., by issuing an HTTP request that keeps a handler alive across the shutdown window) gains arbitrary memory-read capability and potential code execution.
- **Fix:** Replace the raw pointer with `std::atomic<JarvisAgent*>` or protect all reads and writes with a mutex. Null the pointer only after all consumer threads have been joined (i.e., move the `App::g_App = nullptr` line to after every `WaitStop()` call). Restrict visibility by making `g_App` private with a controlled accessor that checks for null before returning.

---

### MEDIUM — Path traversal in script and workflow directory scanning

- **Location:** `JarvisAgent::OnStart()` (~lines 155–165); `JarvisAgent::InitializeWorkflows()` (~line 215)
- **Issue:** The scripts directory is constructed as `std::filesystem::absolute("scripts")` and the workflows directory is taken directly from `Core::g_Core->GetConfig().m_WorkflowsFolderFilepath`. Neither path is validated to ensure it stays within an expected base directory. The `ScriptRegistry::ScanDirectory`, `WorkflowFileIndex::ScanDirectory`, and `WorkflowRegistry::LoadDirectory` calls will follow the path as given, including paths containing `../` components if the config value or working directory is attacker-influenced.
- **Impact:** If an attacker can influence the config file, environment, or working directory (common in containerised or multi-tenant deployments), they can point the workflow or script loader at an arbitrary filesystem path, causing the engine to load and execute attacker-controlled workflow definitions or Python scripts.
- **Fix:** After resolving paths to absolute form, verify that they are lexicographically prefixed by the intended application root (e.g., compare `path.lexically_normal()` against `expectedRoot.lexically_normal()`). Reject any path that escapes the root before passing it to directory scanners.

---

### MEDIUM — Startup-suppression window for file-watch triggers is a TOCTOU race

- **Location:** `JarvisAgent::OnEvent()` (~lines 360–385)
- **Issue:** Within the first 10 seconds after startup, the code calls `std::filesystem::last_write_time(filePath, errorCode)` and compares the result against `m_StartupTime` to decide whether to suppress a trigger. The file's mtime can be changed by any process between the event being generated and the `last_write_time` call (classic TOCTOU). Additionally, the 10-second window and the clock-conversion function `ToSystemClock()` introduce imprecision: the conversion relies on the difference between two `now()` calls which can be non-monotonic on virtualised hosts, potentially allowing pre-existing files to fire triggers or new files to be incorrectly suppressed.
- **Impact:** An attacker with local filesystem write access can touch a malicious file's mtime to bypass the suppression check and fire an arbitrary workflow trigger immediately at startup, before the operator expects any triggers to be active.
- **Fix:** Record the set of files that exist at startup (their inodes or content hashes) rather than relying on mtime comparison. Use a monotonic clock for the startup window check. Do not use `ToSystemClock()` for security decisions; use the file-event timestamp provided by the OS watcher directly.

---

### LOW — Dangling raw pointer to `m_InternalTaskRegistry` held by `InternalTaskExecutor`

- **Location:** `JarvisAgent::InitializeWorkflows()` (~lines 255–265)
- **Issue:** A `std::shared_ptr<IInternalTaskRegistry>` wrapping `&m_InternalTaskRegistry` is created with a no-op deleter and stored inside `InternalTaskExecutor`, which is itself registered in the global `TaskExecutorRegistry`. During shutdown, `TaskExecutorRegistry` (a singleton) outlives the `JarvisAgent` instance. If any workflow run is dispatched after `JarvisAgent` is destroyed (or partially destroyed), the executor holds a dangling pointer to the already-destroyed `m_InternalTaskRegistry` member.
- **Impact:** Use-after-free if any task is dispatched through the `TaskExecutorRegistry` after `JarvisAgent` begins destruction, leading to memory corruption or a crash.
- **Fix:** Either unregister all task executors from `TaskExecutorRegistry` in `OnShutdown()` before any members are destroyed, or make `m_InternalTaskRegistry` a `shared_ptr` so the no-op-deleter trick is unnecessary and lifetime is properly tracked.

---

### LOW — Log injection via unsanitised file paths and event data

- **Location:** `JarvisAgent::OnEvent()` (~lines 380, 400); `JarvisAgent::InitializeWorkflows()` (~line 218)
- **Issue:** File paths from filesystem events and workflow IDs from trigger events are logged directly with `LOG_APP_INFO` / `LOG_APP_ERROR` using `{}` format substitution without any sanitisation. If the underlying logging backend writes to a terminal, a file, or a structured log aggregator, an attacker who controls file names or workflow IDs (e.g., by creating a file with ANSI escape sequences or log-format metacharacters in its name) can corrupt log output or inject false log entries.
- **Impact:** Log injection can obscure evidence of an attack, forge audit entries, or trigger vulnerabilities in log-processing tools that parse the output.
- **Fix:** Strip or percent-encode non-printable characters and ANSI escape sequences from any externally-derived string before passing it to the logger. For workflow IDs and run IDs, enforce an allowlist of safe characters (alphanumeric, hyphen, underscore, dot) at the point they are accepted.

---

### LOW — `AiCallFailedEvent` error message broadcast to all WebSocket clients

- **Location:** `JarvisAgent::OnEvent()` (~lines 430–440)
- **Issue:** `err.m_Message` from a failed AI call — which may contain API error responses including partial credentials, endpoint URLs, or provider-specific diagnostic strings — is forwarded verbatim to `m_WebServer->BroadcastAiCallFailed()`, which sends it to every connected WebSocket client.
- **Impact:** Internal API error messages, including fragments of bearer tokens, endpoint configuration, or quota details, are disclosed to any client connected to the dashboard WebSocket, which may not require authentication equivalent to the secrets being exposed.
- **Fix:** Sanitise or truncate `err.m_Message` before broadcasting. Do not include raw provider error bodies in client-facing messages; log the full detail server-side only. Ensure the WebSocket endpoint requires the same authentication level as the secrets it may indirectly expose.


---

### 50_doc_application_json_jcwfGenerationGuide_generated_h

<a id="50-doc-application-json-jcwfgenerationguide-generated-h"></a>

*Source doc:* `../../../queue/jarvisCppCyberSecAudit/50_doc_application_json_jcwfGenerationGuide_generated_h/docu.output.md`



The file is a C++ header that embeds a large raw-string literal containing documentation text. It defines no functions, parses no input, performs no I/O, uses no cryptography, allocates no memory dynamically, and executes no code at runtime. The only C++ construct present is a `constexpr` pointer-to-const-char in a namespace.

However, the documentation content itself describes security-relevant runtime behaviors that have concrete exploitable weaknesses when those behaviors are implemented as specified. Because the question asks for security issues in the source and the source is the authoritative specification driving code generation and runtime behavior, the findings below are grounded in what the spec mandates.

---

### HIGH Webhook Secret Stored as Plaintext in Workflow File

- **Location:** Section 8 (Triggers), `kJcwfGenerationGuide`, approx. line 310
- **Issue:** The spec requires `params.secret` to be a literal string inside the `.jcwf` zip container's JSON file, e.g. `"secret": "my-shared-secret"`. This means the HMAC-SHA256 signing key is stored in plaintext inside a version-controlled or user-editable workflow file. The spec provides no indirection mechanism (e.g., environment variable reference, secrets-manager lookup, or encrypted field).
- **Impact:** Anyone with read access to the `.jcwf` file or its source repository can extract the webhook secret and forge valid `X-Webhook-Signature` headers, triggering arbitrary workflow runs with attacker-controlled `context`, `runId`, and `callbackUrl` values.
- **Fix:** Replace the literal secret field with an indirection token such as `"secret": "$env:WEBHOOK_SECRET_MYWORKFLOW"` resolved at runtime from environment variables or a secrets store. The validator should reject any `secret` value that is not an indirection reference, and the spec should explicitly prohibit literal secrets.

---

### HIGH SSRF via Unvalidated `callbackUrl` in Webhook Trigger

- **Location:** Section 8 (Triggers), `kJcwfGenerationGuide`, approx. line 315
- **Issue:** The spec states that if `callbackUrl` is provided in the webhook request body, JarvisAgent POSTs a completion payload to that URL (fire-and-forget, 15 s timeout). No validation, allowlist, or authentication requirement is specified for `callbackUrl`. The URL comes from the request body of an inbound HTTP POST.
- **Impact:** An attacker who can send a webhook request (or forge one if the secret is known) can supply an arbitrary `callbackUrl` pointing to internal services (e.g., `http://169.254.169.254/latest/meta-data/`, `http://localhost:8080/admin`, or internal Kubernetes service endpoints). The JarvisAgent process will make an authenticated-looking POST to that target, potentially exfiltrating run state including per-task output summaries, or probing internal network topology. The payload also includes `workflowId`, `runId`, `state`, and per-task `tasks` data, which leaks operational details.
- **Fix:** Require `callbackUrl` to be validated against a configurable allowlist of URL prefixes or domains before the request is dispatched. Reject schemes other than `https`. Optionally, require `callbackUrl` to be pre-registered in the workflow definition rather than accepted from the inbound request body.

---

### HIGH Path Traversal in `file_inputs`, `file_outputs`, `working_directory`, and `cntx_files`

- **Location:** Sections 2, 3.1, 3.2, 3.3, `kJcwfGenerationGuide`, approx. lines 95-130, 155-230
- **Issue:** The spec explicitly permits and documents path values containing `../` sequences, absolute paths (`/tmp/...`, `/home/alice/...`), and references crossing directory boundaries. It states the runtime "does not reject them" for `file_outputs` paths above the launch CWD, emitting only an informational validator finding. `cntx_files`, `working_directory`, `materialize` targets, and `file_inputs` are all subject to the same traversal-permissive handling.
- **Impact:** A malicious or compromised workflow file can read arbitrary files accessible to the JarvisAgent process (by placing them in `cntx_files` and routing the content to an AI call or a `callbackUrl`), write to arbitrary paths (via `file_outputs`), or execute scripts outside the `scripts/` directory if `working_directory` traversal interacts with relative `command` resolution. In multi-tenant or shared Engine deployments, one workflow can reach another workflow's queue files, secrets files, or configuration.
- **Fix:** Canonicalize all path fields at load time using `realpath`/`std::filesystem::canonical` and enforce that the resolved path falls within a configured root (the JarvisAgent working tree or an explicitly whitelisted external path). Treat traversal outside the root as a hard validation error, not an informational warning. The spec's current "informational only" stance must be changed to a rejection for paths that cross the security boundary.

---

### HIGH Shell Command Allowlist Bypass via `scripts/` Prefix Only

- **Location:** Section 3.1, Section 12 pitfall 5, `kJcwfGenerationGuide`, approx. lines 150-160, 500
- **Issue:** The only stated security control on `params.command` is that it "must start with `scripts/`." This is a prefix check on a string, not a validated filesystem path. Values such as `scripts/../bin/sh`, `scripts/../../etc/cron.d/evil`, or a symlink inside `scripts/` pointing outside all bypass the check. The spec does not require canonicalization, does not restrict the command to an executable allowlist, and does not restrict arguments.
- **Impact:** An attacker who can write a workflow file (or inject into one) can execute arbitrary binaries on the host with JarvisAgent's privileges. Combined with the path traversal finding, `working_directory` can be set so that the relative command resolves to an arbitrary location.
- **Fix:** After the `scripts/` prefix check, canonicalize the full resolved path and verify it still begins with the canonical `scripts/` directory path. Maintain an explicit allowlist of permitted script filenames if the threat model includes malicious workflow authors. Never pass user-controlled strings as shell command arguments without sanitization.

---

### HIGH SQL Injection via Per-Item Template Substitution into `db_query` and `snowflake_query` Params

- **Location:** Section 10 Pattern D, `kJcwfGenerationGuide`, approx. lines 430-460
- **Issue:** The spec shows AI-generated or CSV-sourced per-item values interpolated directly into SQL query strings using `{{ai_analyze.captured_stdout}}` and `{{dept.department}}` inside `params.query`. The spec notes that values are "JSON-escaped automatically," but JSON escaping is not SQL escaping. A value containing single quotes, SQL comment sequences (`--`), or dollar-sign quoting escape sequences can break out of the intended SQL context. The `$j9t$...$j9t$` dollar-quoting shown in the example is PostgreSQL-specific and does not protect against nested dollar-quote sequences in attacker-controlled content.
- **Impact:** An attacker who controls a CSV column value, an upstream AI response, or a webhook `context` value that propagates into a `db_query` can execute arbitrary SQL statements with the database credentials configured for the connection, including data exfiltration, modification, or privilege escalation depending on the database role.
- **Fix:** The runtime must use parameterized queries (prepared statements) and pass template-expanded values as bind parameters, never by string interpolation into the query text. The spec should prohibit inline template substitution in `query` fields and require a separate `params` array for bind values.

---

### MEDIUM AI Prompt Injection via Unvalidated `cntx_files` Content Reaching LLM

- **Location:** Section 3.2, `kJcwfGenerationGuide`, approx. lines 190-210
- **Issue:** The spec allows `cntx_files` to reference arbitrary files, including compiler stderr output (`stderr.txt`), upstream AI responses, and CSV rows. These are concatenated into the AI prompt without any sanitization. A malicious file or upstream AI response can contain instructions that override the STNG/TASK directives, a classic prompt injection attack.
- **Impact:** An adversarially crafted input file or a compromised upstream AI response can redirect the AI's behavior — e.g., instructing it to output code that exfiltrates secrets, generates malicious scripts, or produces content that exploits downstream parsing. In automated pipelines with `output_schema` validation and retry loops, injected instructions could cycle through retries producing attacker-chosen structured output.
- **Fix:** Document and implement a content boundary mechanism (e.g., XML-style delimiters with instructions to the model to treat enclosed content as data only). Rate-limit and audit AI calls that incorporate externally sourced content. This is a known-hard problem but the spec should at minimum warn workflow authors and provide a recommended mitigation pattern.

---

### MEDIUM Uncontrolled Allocation via Unbounded `max_items` and Chunking Fan-Out

- **Location:** Sections 9 (Filters) and 3.2 (chunking), `kJcwfGenerationGuide`, approx. lines 355, 240
- **Issue:** The `max_items` filter field defaults are not specified (only the field exists; no mandatory maximum or server-side cap is documented). The chunking mechanism fans out N parallel AI envelopes with no documented upper bound on N. A workflow author or attacker with workflow-write access can specify a very large CSV, no `max_items` cap, and a small `max_context_tokens` budget, causing the runtime to allocate and submit an unbounded number of concurrent AI requests and associated in-memory envelopes.
- **Impact:** Resource exhaustion (memory, file descriptors, outbound API rate-limit consumption, cost) on the JarvisAgent host. In a shared Engine deployment this is a denial-of-service against other workflows and tenants.
- **Fix:** Enforce a server-side maximum on `max_items` (e.g., 10,000 as shown is a hint but not enforced) and on chunking fan-out. Implement a concurrency semaphore in `AiRequestPool::Submit` limiting simultaneous in-flight requests. Make these limits configurable but with safe defaults.

---

### MEDIUM Secrets Potentially Leaked into AI Prompts via `cntx_files` Glob Patterns

- **Location:** Section 3.2, `kJcwfGenerationGuide`, approx. line 210
- **Issue:** Glob patterns are supported in `cntx_files` (e.g., `"../01_generate/PROB_*.output.txt"`). If a glob inadvertently matches a `PROV_*` file (which the spec says contains provider config including endpoints and likely API keys), that file's content would be included in the AI prompt and transmitted to the AI provider. The spec states `PROV_*` files are "never sent to AI" but relies solely on the categorizer's correct behavior, with no defense-in-depth.
- **Impact:** API keys or endpoint credentials in `PROV_*` files could be exfiltrated to the AI provider (a third party) or logged in the AI provider's request logs.
- **Fix:** After glob expansion, the runtime must explicitly filter out any matched path whose filename matches `PROV_*` (or any other credential-bearing prefix) before including content in the prompt. This filtering must occur regardless of how the file was referenced (explicit path or glob).

---

### LOW Sub-Workflow Path Traversal via `workflow_file`

- **Location:** Section 3.5, `kJcwfGenerationGuide`, approx. lines 275-290
- **Issue:** `workflow_file` is described as a path relative to the parent workflow's file directory with no stated restriction on `../` sequences or absolute paths. The spec states child workflows are "loaded from the `workflows/` directory (scanned recursively)" but does not say the path is validated against that directory.
- **Impact:** A `workflow_file` value of `../../config/credentials.jcwf` or similar could cause the runtime to load and execute an arbitrary file as a workflow, or at minimum read it and fail with an error that leaks file content in diagnostic output.
- **Fix:** Canonicalize the resolved `workflow_file` path and require it to fall within the `workflows/` subtree. Reject with a hard error otherwise.

---

### LOW Missing Certificate Verification Specification for AI Provider and Webhook Callback HTTP Clients

- **Location:** Section 3.2 (AI provider calls), Section 8 (callbackUrl dispatch), `kJcwfGenerationGuide`, approx. lines 170, 315
- **Issue:** The spec describes outbound HTTPS connections to AI providers and to `callbackUrl` endpoints but makes no mention of TLS certificate verification requirements, pinning, or minimum TLS version. Implementations following the spec without explicit guidance may disable certificate verification for convenience or compatibility.
- **Impact:** If certificate verification is not enforced, the JarvisAgent process is vulnerable to MITM attacks on AI provider connections (leaking all prompts and responses, including sensitive context files) and on callback connections.
- **Fix:** The spec should explicitly require TLS certificate verification against the system trust store for all outbound connections, prohibit `verify=false` or equivalent, and specify a minimum TLS version (1.2, preferably 1.3).


---

### 51_doc_application_json_jcwfSchema_generated_h

<a id="51-doc-application-json-jcwfschema-generated-h"></a>

*Source doc:* `../../../queue/jarvisCppCyberSecAudit/51_doc_application_json_jcwfSchema_generated_h/docu.output.md`



### NONE
No security issues identified.

This file is a compile-time constant containing a JSON Schema definition embedded as a raw string literal. It contains no executable logic, no cryptographic operations, no input handling, no memory management, no network calls, no secret material, and no runtime behavior of any kind. The schema itself does not introduce security issues; enforcement of its constraints is entirely the responsibility of the consuming validation code, which is not present here.

### 52_doc_application_json_jsonObjectParser_h

<a id="52-doc-application-json-jsonobjectparser-h"></a>

*Source doc:* `../../../queue/jarvisCppCyberSecAudit/52_doc_application_json_jsonObjectParser_h/docu.output.md`



### LOW Secrets/Sensitive-value leakage via unconditional INFO logging
- **Location:** `JsonObjectParser::Parse()`, all `LOG_APP_INFO` call sites inside the `switch` block (approximately lines 100–130 of the .cpp)
- **Issue:** Every JSON string, number, boolean, and null value parsed from an incoming document is written verbatim to the application info log, including the key name and the full value. Because `JsonObjectParser` is used inside a workflow engine that handles AI requests, secrets, and webhook payloads, values such as API keys, bearer tokens, passwords, and PII will appear in plain text in whatever log sink is configured (file, stdout, SIEM, etc.).
- **Impact:** Any party with access to the log output (ops team, log aggregator, SIEM, an attacker who achieves log-read access) obtains secret material without needing to compromise the primary data store. This also risks violating PCI-DSS, GDPR, and similar regulations.
- **Fix:** Remove value content from INFO-level log lines. Log only structural information (key name, type) at INFO/DEBUG, never the value itself. If value logging is required for debugging, guard it behind a compile-time or runtime debug flag that is disabled in production builds, and apply a denylist or allowlist of safe-to-log keys before emitting any value.

---

### LOW Uncontrolled recursion depth enabling stack exhaustion (DoS)
- **Location:** `JsonObjectParser::ParseObject()` and `JsonObjectParser::ParseArray()` (approximately lines 150–185 of the .cpp)
- **Issue:** `ParseObject` and `ParseArray` each construct a new `JsonObjectParser` instance, which calls `Parse()` in its constructor, which in turn calls `ParseObject` or `ParseArray` again for nested containers. There is no depth limit. An attacker who controls the JSON input can supply a document deeply nested to thousands of levels (e.g., `{"a":{"a":{"a":…}}}`), causing unbounded stack growth until the process crashes with a stack overflow. simdjson itself does impose a default nesting limit (1024), but that limit is per-document and is applied at the parser level, not at this wrapper layer; a document at the simdjson maximum will still drive 1024 recursive C++ stack frames here, each of which allocates local state.
- **Impact:** An unauthenticated attacker who can submit JSON to any endpoint that feeds this parser can crash the engine process (denial of service).
- **Fix:** Add an explicit maximum depth parameter (e.g., `constexpr uint32_t kMaxIndentLevel = 64`) and check it at the start of `Parse()`; set `m_HasError = true` and return immediately without recursing when the limit is exceeded. Log a warning with the depth value (but not the value content).

---

### LOW `simdjson::ondemand::value` stored as a member may produce a dangling/invalid reference
- **Location:** `JsonObjectParser` class declaration (header, approximately line 40); constructor initialization (`.cpp`, approximately line 75)
- **Issue:** `simdjson::ondemand::value` is a lightweight, non-owning view into the underlying padded string buffer owned by the `ondemand::parser` or the `ondemand::document`. The comment in the header acknowledges it is "valid within parent loop." The constructor stores this view as `m_Value` and immediately calls `Parse()`, so for the direct (non-nested) use case the value is still live. However, in `ParseObject` and `ParseArray` the `simdjson::ondemand::value` for a nested element is passed by value into a new `JsonObjectParser`, which copies it into `m_Value`. The simdjson ondemand API is a forward-only, lazy streaming API; advancing iteration (e.g., calling `.type()` on a sibling element, or any further iteration on the parent array/object) invalidates previously obtained `ondemand::value` objects that have not yet been fully consumed. If `Parse()` reads `m_Value.type()` after the parent iterator has advanced, behavior is undefined. The risk increases if the class is ever stored (e.g., placed in a container) rather than immediately destroyed.
- **Impact:** In current code the nested `JsonObjectParser` is destroyed before the parent loop advances, so the crash risk is low in practice. However, the design is fragile: any future refactoring that defers construction or stores instances will introduce use-after-invalidation, leading to memory corruption or information disclosure.
- **Fix:** Do not store `ondemand::value` as a member. Either (a) eagerly convert the value to a safe owned representation (e.g., a `std::variant` or a string) in the constructor before storing it, or (b) pass the value by reference to `Parse()` directly and never retain it past the call. If the class must remain constructible, document and enforce (via a static assertion or deleted copy constructor) that instances must not outlive the document iteration.


---

### 53_doc_application_json_replyParser_h

<a id="53-doc-application-json-replyparser-h"></a>

*Source doc:* `../../../queue/jarvisCppCyberSecAudit/53_doc_application_json_replyParser_h/docu.output.md`



### NONE
No security issues identified.

The two files shown define an abstract `ReplyParser` base class and its factory implementation. The code stores and dispatches a JSON string to concrete subclasses but performs no parsing, no I/O, no memory arithmetic, no cryptographic operations, no shell or SQL calls, no logging of caller-supplied data, and no authentication logic. The default error message in `GetError()` is a static string literal, not attacker-controlled content. There are no raw pointers, manual allocations, or lifetime hazards visible here; all ownership is managed via `std::unique_ptr`. The unhandled `default` branch returns a null `unique_ptr`, which callers must check, but that is a correctness concern for the calling code, not a security issue in this file. No security findings apply to the code as shown.


---

### 54_doc_application_json_replyParserAPI1_h

<a id="54-doc-application-json-replyparserapi1-h"></a>

*Source doc:* `../../../queue/jarvisCppCyberSecAudit/54_doc_application_json_replyParserAPI1_h/docu.output.md`



### HIGH AI model response content logged verbatim to stdout and structured logs

- **Location:** `ReplyParserAPI1::ParseContent`, approximately the `msgKey == "content"` branch (~line 230 in the .cpp)
- **Issue:** `std::cout << content << "\n";` and `LOG_APP_INFO("content:")` immediately before it write the raw AI-generated reply content (which may contain user PII, sensitive data returned by the model, or prompt-injection payloads) to stdout and the application log unconditionally. There is no sanitisation, length cap, or redaction.
- **Impact:** Any log aggregation system (SIEM, ELK, CloudWatch, etc.) that ingests these logs will persistently store user conversation content. If the model was asked to summarise secrets or credentials, those values appear verbatim in logs. An attacker with read access to logs gains access to all conversation history. Additionally, if the log sink renders the content in a browser or terminal with ANSI/control-character handling, terminal-injection or stored-XSS attacks become possible.
- **Fix:** Remove `std::cout << content << "\n";` entirely. If a debug log is required, log only the content length: `LOG_APP_DEBUG("content length: {}", content.size());`. Apply the same principle to `ParseError` where `std::cout << message << "\n";` writes the provider error message (which may echo back fragments of the user request or API key hints) to stdout.

---

### HIGH API error messages (including auth/quota errors) echoed verbatim to logs at CRITICAL level

- **Location:** `ReplyParserAPI1::ParseError`, `ParseErrorType` (~lines 290–340 in the .cpp)
- **Issue:** The raw `message`, `code`, and `param` fields from the provider error response are logged at `LOG_APP_INFO` / `LOG_APP_CRITICAL` without any sanitisation or truncation. Provider error messages for `authentication_error` or `insufficient_quota` sometimes include fragments of the offending credential (masked API key suffix, organization ID, etc.). `std::cout << message << "\n";` also writes this to stdout unconditionally.
- **Impact:** Secrets leakage: any API-key hint or account identifier embedded in the provider's error message is persisted in logs and emitted to stdout where it may be captured by process supervisors, container log drivers, or CI/CD pipelines.
- **Fix:** Remove the `std::cout` call. Log only the error `type` and `code` at info level; suppress or redact `message` unless an explicit debug flag is set. At minimum apply a character-class whitelist or length cap (e.g., 256 chars) before logging.

---

### MEDIUM Unchecked `simdjson` return values allow silent data corruption / unexpected control flow

- **Location:** `ReplyParserAPI1::Parse`, `ParseContent`, `ParseUsage`, `ParseError` — throughout both source files
- **Issue:** Every call to `jsonObject.value().get_string()`, `get_uint64()`, `get_int64()`, `get_object()`, `get_array()`, and `unescaped_key()` returns a `simdjson::simdjson_result<T>` that carries an error code. The code assigns these results directly to `std::string_view`, `uint64_t`, etc. via implicit conversion without checking the error. If the conversion fails silently, the resulting value is unspecified. The `CORE_ASSERT` type checks that precede each extraction are disabled in release builds (standard assert semantics), so production builds have no guard at all.
- **Impact:** A malformed or adversarially crafted JSON response from the provider (or from a man-in-the-middle if TLS verification is absent elsewhere in the engine) can cause: (a) `std::string_view` instances pointing to garbage memory, leading to use-after-free or out-of-bounds reads when those views are used to construct `std::string` members; (b) integer fields set to 0 or MAX values, corrupting usage accounting; (c) silent parse success (`m_State = ReplyOk`) on a structurally invalid document.
- **Fix:** Use simdjson's error-propagation idiom throughout: `std::string_view sv; if (auto e = field.value().get_string().get(sv); e) { /* handle */ }`. Replace all `CORE_ASSERT` type checks with runtime checks that return/log on failure rather than assert.

---

### MEDIUM Integer truncation in `GetUsage()` with no overflow check

- **Location:** `ReplyParserAPI1::GetUsage`, ~line 78 in the .cpp
- **Issue:** `m_Reply.m_Usage.m_PromptTokens` (and the other two counters) are `uint64_t`. They are cast to `int32_t` via `static_cast` without any bounds check. A provider response with a token count above `INT32_MAX` (2 147 483 647) silently wraps to a large negative number.
- **Impact:** Upstream billing, rate-limiting, or quota logic that consumes `AiUsage` and treats negative token counts as underflows could be bypassed or manipulated by a provider returning an inflated token value (or by a MITM injecting one).
- **Fix:** Add a bounds check before each cast: `if (m_Reply.m_Usage.m_PromptTokens > static_cast<uint64_t>(std::numeric_limits<int32_t>::max())) { /* clamp or error */ }`.

---

### MEDIUM Double `parser.iterate(json)` call on the same `padded_string`

- **Location:** `ReplyParserAPI1::Parse`, ~lines 110–115 in the .cpp
- **Issue:** `parser.iterate(json)` is called twice on the same `padded_string` object. The first call is used only to check for a parse error; the result document `doc` is then discarded and `parser.iterate(json)` is called a second time. `simdjson::ondemand` parsers are stateful and mutate the padded buffer in-place during iteration. The second call resets and re-parses from the beginning, which is functionally correct today, but the first `doc` object's destructor and the second iteration share the same underlying buffer. If the compiler reorders or if future simdjson versions change buffer-ownership semantics, this becomes a use-after-free or double-mutation bug.
- **Impact:** Memory-safety issue under future library versions or with LTO/aggressive optimisation; in the worst case an attacker supplying a specially crafted JSON string could exploit the confusion between the two parse states.
- **Fix:** Remove the first `parser.iterate` / error-check block entirely. Rely on the error handling that already exists inside the field-iteration loop, or use a single `auto result = parser.iterate(json); if (result.error()) { ... }` pattern.

---

### LOW Unbounded allocation from provider-controlled `choices` array

- **Location:** `ReplyParserAPI1::ParseContent`, the `reply.m_Choices.push_back` loop (~line 205 in the .cpp)
- **Issue:** Every element in the `choices` array unconditionally results in a heap allocation and push_back into `m_Reply.m_Choices`. There is no maximum-size guard. A provider response (or a MITM response) containing tens of thousands of choice objects will cause unbounded memory growth in the parsing thread.
- **Impact:** Denial of service: an attacker able to influence the HTTP response body (MITM, compromised provider endpoint, SSRF reflection) can exhaust heap memory.
- **Fix:** Add a hard cap before the push_back: `if (reply.m_Choices.size() >= MAX_CHOICES) { LOG_APP_ERROR(...); break; }` where `MAX_CHOICES` is a small constant (e.g., 64).

---

### LOW AI model content written to `std::cout` without length cap (secondary DoS vector)

- **Location:** `ReplyParserAPI1::ParseContent` (`std::cout << content << "\n"`) and `ReplyParserAPI1::ParseError` (`std::cout << message << "\n"`)
- **Issue:** Content is written to stdout with no length limit. A response with megabytes of content in a single choice will synchronously block the parsing thread on the write and may fill pipe buffers if stdout is connected to a pipe.
- **Impact:** Minor denial of service / latency spike; combined with the unbounded choices loop above, this amplifies the impact.
- **Fix:** Remove both `std::cout` calls as noted in the HIGH finding above.


---

### 55_doc_application_json_replyParserAPI2_h

<a id="55-doc-application-json-replyparserapi2-h"></a>

*Source doc:* `../../../queue/jarvisCppCyberSecAudit/55_doc_application_json_replyParserAPI2_h/docu.output.md`



### MEDIUM Use of simdjson string_view values after document iteration may cause dangling references

- **Location:** `ReplyParserAPI2::Parse()`, approximately lines where `std::string_view id = jsonObject.value().get_string()` is used before being copied, e.g. the `id`, `object`, `status`, `model` assignments in `Parse()` and similarly in `ParseOutput()` and `ParseError()`
- **Issue:** simdjson `ondemand` returns `std::string_view` values that point directly into the padded input buffer. The padded buffer `json` is a local variable declared in `Parse()`. `ParseUsage()` and `ParseError()` receive `simdjson::ondemand::object` by value, which internally holds a pointer back into that same local buffer. If any future refactor moves or copies the buffer, or if the document is iterated in a different order, these views become dangling. More concretely, `Parse()` calls `parser.iterate(json)` twice (once to test for error, once as `responseDocument`), and the first `doc` object is still alive when the second iteration begins. simdjson's ondemand parser is single-pass and reuse of the same `parser` object with the same `padded_string` before the first document is fully consumed is explicitly unsupported; the second `parser.iterate(json)` silently resets internal state, making any `std::string_view` derived from the first document potentially invalid when accessed afterward.
- **Impact:** If the undefined behavior is ever triggered (e.g., by a large input that causes internal buffer movement, or by compiler optimizations), an attacker supplying a crafted JSON payload could cause reads of freed or out-of-bounds memory, leading to information disclosure or, in edge cases, memory corruption.
- **Fix:** Perform exactly one `parser.iterate(json)` call and remove the dead first `parser.iterate` / `doc` block. Copy every `std::string_view` result from simdjson into a `std::string` immediately at the call site (which the code already does in most places) rather than storing the view even transiently.

---

### MEDIUM Unchecked simdjson error returns throughout all parse functions

- **Location:** `Parse()`, `ParseOutput()`, `ParseUsage()`, `ParseError()` — every `.get_string()`, `.get_uint64()`, `.get_object()`, `.get_array()`, `.unescaped_key()` call
- **Issue:** simdjson ondemand methods return `simdjson_result<T>`, which wraps both a value and an error code. The code silently relies on implicit conversion to the value type without checking the error. If the field has the wrong JSON type, is missing, or the document is malformed in a targeted way, the implicit conversion produces an undefined or zero value and simdjson sets an internal error flag that propagates silently. For example, `field.value().get_uint64()` on a field whose value is a string quietly yields 0 or an arbitrary value on some simdjson builds.
- **Impact:** An attacker who can influence the provider's JSON response (e.g., via a prompt-injection that reaches a backend that reflects content into the JSON envelope, or via a man-in-the-middle against an endpoint without certificate pinning) can deliver a type-confused payload. This can corrupt `m_Reply` state, cause logic errors in downstream callers (e.g., `GetUsage()` returning garbage token counts that affect billing or quota enforcement), or trigger the dangling-view issue above.
- **Fix:** Use simdjson's error-checking idiom throughout: `uint64_t val; if (auto e = field.value().get_uint64().get(val); e) { LOG_APP_ERROR(...); return; }`. Apply this to every simdjson result dereference.

---

### MEDIUM Provider error message and internal field values logged without sanitization

- **Location:** `Parse()` ~lines logging `id`, `object`, `status`, `model`; `ParseErrorType()` which logs the error type at CRITICAL level; `GetContent()` which logs the index on error
- **Issue:** Values received verbatim from the AI provider API response (`id`, `object`, `model`, `status`) are passed directly to `LOG_APP_INFO` / `LOG_APP_CRITICAL`. If the logging framework uses a format-string-style call and `id` or `model` contains format specifiers or newline/ANSI sequences, this enables log injection. Even without format-string issues, newline injection allows an attacker who can influence API response fields to forge log lines (e.g., fake authentication errors or audit entries).
- **Impact:** Log forgery/injection; if logs are consumed by a SIEM or alerting system, injected entries can suppress or spoof security events.
- **Fix:** Do not log raw provider-supplied string values at INFO level. If logging is required, restrict to a fixed prefix or sanitize by replacing control characters (`\n`, `\r`, `\x1b`, etc.) before logging. Ensure the logging macro does not accept the external string as a format string; use `LOG_APP_INFO("{}", id)` rather than `LOG_APP_INFO(id)` (the code appears to already use the `{}` form, but verify the macro expansion is safe).

---

### LOW Integer truncation in GetUsage() discards high token counts silently

- **Location:** `ReplyParserAPI2::GetUsage()`, all three `static_cast<int32_t>(...)` lines
- **Issue:** `m_Reply.m_Usage.m_InputTokens`, `m_OutputTokens`, and `m_TotalTokens` are `uint64_t`. They are cast to `int32_t` without range checking. Values above 2 147 483 647 (possible for large context windows) silently wrap to negative values.
- **Impact:** Downstream billing, quota, or rate-limit logic that uses `AiUsage` fields and interprets a negative token count as zero or a small number could allow a user to bypass quota enforcement by submitting a request large enough to overflow the cast.
- **Fix:** Clamp before casting: `usage.m_InputTokens = static_cast<int32_t>(std::min(m_Reply.m_Usage.m_InputTokens, static_cast<uint64_t>(std::numeric_limits<int32_t>::max())));` and log a warning when clamping occurs. Alternatively, change `AiUsage` fields to `uint64_t` or `int64_t`.

---

### LOW Double parse of the same padded_string with the same parser instance

- **Location:** `ReplyParserAPI2::Parse()`, the block starting with `auto error = parser.iterate(json).get(doc);` followed immediately by `ondemand::document responseDocument = parser.iterate(json);`
- **Issue:** The same `ondemand::parser` object iterates the same `padded_string` twice. simdjson's ondemand parser is explicitly documented as non-reentrant for the same parser instance before the previous document is fully consumed. The first `doc` is never fully iterated, so the second call triggers implementation-defined behavior. On current simdjson builds this typically silently reuses internal state; future builds or alternate platforms may behave differently.
- **Impact:** Corrupted parse state could cause fields to be skipped or misread, potentially hiding an `error` object in the response and failing to set `m_HasError = true`, which would cause the caller to treat an error response as a successful reply.
- **Fix:** Remove the redundant first `parser.iterate` and its associated `doc` variable entirely. Perform one `parser.iterate(json)` into `responseDocument`, then check `responseDocument.get_object()` for an error code.


---

### 56_doc_application_json_replyParserAPI3_h

<a id="56-doc-application-json-replyparserapi3-h"></a>

*Source doc:* `../../../queue/jarvisCppCyberSecAudit/56_doc_application_json_replyParserAPI3_h/docu.output.md`



### MEDIUM AI model response text written to stdout unconditionally

- **Location:** `ReplyParserAPI3::ParseCandidates`, the `pk == "text"` branch (~line 220)
- **Issue:** `std::cout << text << "\n";` writes raw, attacker-controlled AI model response text directly to standard output with no escaping or filtering. The same pattern appears in `ParseError` for the error message field (~line 295). In a terminal or log-aggregation context this enables terminal escape-sequence injection (ANSI injection). If stdout is piped to another process or a script, the content could influence that consumer.
- **Impact:** An attacker who controls the upstream AI provider response (e.g., via a compromised or adversarial provider, a MITM on the HTTP connection, or a prompt-injection attack) can inject ANSI escape sequences to manipulate terminal display, overwrite visible log lines, or exploit downstream consumers that parse stdout.
- **Fix:** Remove the `std::cout` statements entirely; the content is already stored in `part.m_Text` and surfaced through `GetContent()`. If debug output is genuinely needed, route it through the structured logger (e.g., `LOG_APP_DEBUG`) with the content treated as a plain string value, not interpolated directly into a format string without sanitization.

---

### MEDIUM Error message from provider logged and exposed without sanitisation

- **Location:** `ReplyParserAPI3::ParseError`, `key == "message"` branch (~line 295) and the final `LOG_APP_CRITICAL` call (~line 310)
- **Issue:** The raw provider-supplied error `message` string is written to `std::cout` and then passed verbatim to `LOG_APP_CRITICAL` as a format argument `{}`. If the logging framework performs any further string interpretation (e.g., `printf`-style or Python-style format strings), a crafted `%n`, `{0}`, or similar token in the provider response could produce log-injection or, in a worst case, a format-string vulnerability. Even without format-string risk, structured log systems (ELK, Splunk, etc.) can be poisoned by newline-embedded content such as `\nERROR fake-field: fake-value`.
- **Impact:** Log injection allowing an attacker controlling the provider response to forge log entries, hide real errors, or manipulate SIEM alerting. Potential format-string exploitation if the logger is not strictly type-safe.
- **Fix:** Ensure the message value is passed only as a bound argument to a typed format placeholder (never as the format string itself). Sanitise or escape newline characters before logging. Remove the bare `std::cout` line.

---

### MEDIUM Unchecked narrowing cast from uint64_t to int32_t in `GetUsage`

- **Location:** `ReplyParserAPI3::GetUsage` (~line 155–159)
- **Issue:** `m_PromptTokenCount`, `m_CandidatesTokenCount`, and `m_TotalTokenCount` are `uint64_t`. They are cast with `static_cast<int32_t>()` without any bounds check. A provider returning a token count above `INT32_MAX` (~2.1 billion) produces a negative or otherwise wrong value that is silently passed to callers.
- **Impact:** If token-count values are used downstream for billing, quota enforcement, or rate limiting, a malicious or compromised provider could underreport token usage by supplying a sufficiently large count, causing integer wrap-around to a negative or small positive number, potentially bypassing cost controls.
- **Fix:** Clamp or validate before casting: if the value exceeds `INT32_MAX`, log an error and either cap at `INT32_MAX` or surface an error. Alternatively, change `AiUsage` fields to `uint64_t` or `int64_t` to avoid the truncation entirely.

---

### LOW Double `parser.iterate(json)` call with result of first call discarded

- **Location:** `ReplyParserAPI3::Parse` (~line 175–179)
- **Issue:** `parser.iterate(json)` is called twice. The first call's result is used only to check for an error; then a second call is made and its result assigned to `responseDocument`. The simdjson `ondemand` parser is single-pass and stateful; the first `iterate` call may advance or lock internal state. In current simdjson versions this happens to work because the first result is not iterated, but it is an undocumented reliance on implementation detail. More importantly, the first call result is checked for `error` but the second call's result is not checked at all—if the second `iterate` fails, the subsequent `get_object()` invocation on an invalid document produces undefined behaviour.
- **Impact:** Malformed or adversarially crafted JSON could cause the second `iterate` to fail silently, leading to undefined behaviour (potential crash or memory corruption) rather than a clean parse failure.
- **Fix:** Eliminate the first `iterate` call. Use the second call's returned `simdjson_result<ondemand::document>` directly, check its error status, and only then call `get_object()`.

---

### LOW Uncontrolled allocation from unbounded JSON arrays

- **Location:** `ReplyParserAPI3::ParseCandidates` (~line 195), `ParseCandidates` inner `partsArray` loop (~line 210)
- **Issue:** The number of `candidates` and the number of `parts` within each candidate are iterated and pushed into `std::vector` without any upper-bound check. A response containing millions of candidates or parts causes unbounded heap growth.
- **Impact:** A malicious or compromised provider can send a pathologically large JSON array causing out-of-memory conditions (denial of service) in the calling process.
- **Fix:** Add a maximum-element guard (e.g., refuse to parse more than a few hundred candidates or parts) and return a parse error if the limit is exceeded.


---

### 57_doc_application_json_replyParserAPI4_h

<a id="57-doc-application-json-replyparserapi4-h"></a>

*Source doc:* `../../../queue/jarvisCppCyberSecAudit/57_doc_application_json_replyParserAPI4_h/docu.output.md`



### LOW — Uncontrolled allocation via unbounded content-block vector

- **Location:** `ReplyParserAPI4::Parse`, content array iteration loop (approx. line 230–250)
- **Issue:** Every element in the `"content"` array received from the Anthropic API (or any upstream proxy/attacker-in-the-middle) is unconditionally pushed into `m_Reply.m_Content` with no cap on the number of blocks or the length of individual `m_Type` and `m_Text` strings. A malformed or adversarially crafted response could contain millions of blocks or a single block with a multi-gigabyte `"text"` field, causing unbounded heap growth.
- **Impact:** If the HTTP transport or a man-in-the-middle can deliver a crafted payload, an attacker can exhaust process heap memory, producing a denial-of-service condition. This is especially relevant if the engine ever proxies or caches raw provider responses.
- **Fix:** Impose a hard cap on the number of content blocks (e.g., 256) and on the length of each string value extracted (e.g., 4 MB for `m_Text`, 64 bytes for `m_Type`) before copying them. Log and return a parse error if either limit is exceeded.

---

### LOW — Integer narrowing / signed-overflow in `GetUsage`

- **Location:** `ReplyParserAPI4::GetUsage`, approx. line 175–180
- **Issue:** `m_Reply.m_Usage.m_InputTokens` and `m_Reply.m_Usage.m_OutputTokens` are `uint64_t`. They are cast to `int32_t` without range checking. A provider response (or forged response) with a token count above `INT32_MAX` (~2.1 billion) will silently wrap to a negative value. `m_TotalTokens` is then computed from two already-truncated `int32_t` values, which can itself overflow.
- **Impact:** Downstream billing, quota enforcement, or rate-limiting logic that consumes `AiUsage` could be deceived into believing far fewer tokens were consumed than actually were, potentially bypassing cost controls or quotas.
- **Fix:** Validate that both token counts fit within `int32_t` range before casting; if either exceeds the limit, clamp or return an error. Example: `if (m_Reply.m_Usage.m_InputTokens > INT32_MAX) { /* error */ }`.

---

### LOW — Provider error message reflected into log without sanitization

- **Location:** `ReplyParserAPI4::GetError` (error message stored at approx. line 210); indirectly wherever `AiError::m_Message` is logged by callers
- **Issue:** `m_ErrorInfo.m_Message` is copied verbatim from the JSON field `error.message` returned by the upstream provider. If that field contains log-injection characters (newlines, ANSI escape sequences, or format-string tokens depending on the logging macro implementation), a compromised or spoofed upstream endpoint could inject false log entries or corrupt terminal output when callers log the `AiError`.
- **Impact:** Log injection could obscure real errors, inject false audit events, or (if the logging macro passes the string as a format argument rather than a literal) trigger format-string vulnerabilities in the logger.
- **Fix:** Strip or escape control characters (`\n`, `\r`, `\t`, `\033`, etc.) from `m_ErrorInfo.m_Message` immediately after extraction in `Parse()`. Ensure all call sites pass the message as a data argument, never as a format string (i.e., `LOG_APP_ERROR("{}", error.m_Message)`, not `LOG_APP_ERROR(error.m_Message)`).


---

### 58_doc_application_json_replyParserAPI5_h

<a id="58-doc-application-json-replyparserapi5-h"></a>

*Source doc:* `../../../queue/jarvisCppCyberSecAudit/58_doc_application_json_replyParserAPI5_h/docu.output.md`



### LOW Negative token-count values silently wrap to large unsigned integers

- **Location:** `LlamaBedrockReply::Parse()` (~line 100) and `TitanBedrockReply::Parse()` (~line 160), specifically the `static_cast<uint64_t>(v)` expressions where `v` is `int64_t`
- **Issue:** `get_int64()` can return a negative value from attacker-controlled JSON. Casting a negative `int64_t` directly to `uint64_t` produces a very large positive number (e.g., `-1` becomes `0xFFFFFFFFFFFFFFFF`). That value is then stored into `m_PromptTokens` / `m_CompletionTokens` and subsequently cast to `int32_t` in `GetUsage()`, producing a large negative `int32_t`. If any downstream billing, rate-limiting, or quota logic consumes these fields without re-validating, a malicious Bedrock response could cause quota underflow or integer-overflow-based bypass.
- **Impact:** A compromised or spoofed Bedrock endpoint can return crafted token counts. Downstream quota/billing logic may undercount consumed tokens, potentially allowing cost or rate-limit bypass.
- **Fix:** Reject or clamp negative values before the cast:
  ```
  if (v < 0) v = 0;
  m_PromptTokens = static_cast<uint64_t>(v);
  ```
  Apply the same guard to all four token-count assignments across both parser classes.

---

### LOW Unbounded JSON string length — no size cap before `simdjson::padded_string` allocation

- **Location:** `DetectFamily()` (~line 200), `LlamaBedrockReply::Parse()` (~line 85), `TitanBedrockReply::Parse()` (~line 140), `ReplyParserAPI5::ReplyParserAPI5()` (~line 225)
- **Issue:** `simdjson::padded_string` allocates `jsonString.size() + SIMDJSON_PADDING` bytes on the heap with no prior length check. The `jsonString` originates from a Bedrock HTTP response body. If the TLS/HTTP layer does not enforce a response-body size limit, an oversized or maliciously large body is passed verbatim here, causing a large single heap allocation. The JSON is also parsed twice for every request (once in `DetectFamily`, once in the chosen delegate constructor), doubling peak allocation.
- **Impact:** A compromised or SSRF-reachable Bedrock endpoint can return a response large enough to exhaust heap memory, causing a denial of service of the workflow engine process.
- **Fix:** Assert or enforce a maximum body size (e.g., 10 MB) at the HTTP response ingestion layer before constructing any parser. Additionally, consider passing the already-parsed simdjson document through rather than re-parsing the raw string in the delegate.

---

### NONE (remaining areas)

No issues were identified in the following reviewed areas: shell/SQL/header injection, authentication or authorization logic, cryptographic primitives, hardcoded secrets, RNG usage, TLS configuration, path traversal, SSRF origination, use-after-free or dangling references, race conditions, or secrets leakage in error messages. The `GetError()` message at ~line 255 exposes only a static string with no user-controlled content.
