### 68_doc_application_web_aiJcwfService_h

<a id="68-doc-application-web-aijcwfservice-h"></a>

*Source doc:* `../../../queue/jarvisCppCyberSecAudit/68_doc_application_web_aiJcwfService_h/docu.output.md`



### [HIGH] Path traversal via attacker-controlled `scriptPath` passed to `std::ifstream`
- **Location:** `AiJcwfService::FixFailedScriptAsync`, lambda body, `std::ifstream ifs(scriptPath)`
- **Issue:** `scriptPath` arrives from an external caller (HTTP handler or WebSocket message) and is passed directly to `std::ifstream` without any validation that it is confined to the expected scripts directory. An attacker who can invoke `FixFailedScriptAsync` can supply `../../etc/passwd`, `../../keys/private.pem`, or any other path reachable by the process, causing the file's full contents to be sent to an external AI endpoint.
- **Impact:** Exfiltration of arbitrary files readable by the process, including private keys, configuration files, and secrets.
- **Fix:** Before opening the file, canonicalize the path with `fs::weakly_canonical` and assert the result starts with the expected scripts root:
  ```
  fs::path canonical = fs::weakly_canonical(scriptPath);
  fs::path root = fs::weakly_canonical(expectedScriptsRoot);
  if (!canonical.string().starts_with(root.string() + fs::path::preferred_separator))
      /* reject */;
  ```
  Also enforce that the extension is `.sh`, `.ps1`, or `.py`.

---

### [HIGH] Path traversal via AI-generated `scriptPath` values written as queue-dir subfolders
- **Location:** `AiJcwfService::RunSingleAiCall`, `fs::path const queueDir = queueBase / "_ai_jcwf_service" / subfolderName`; also `ExtractScriptPaths` → `generatedScripts[i].path`
- **Issue:** `subfolderName` is built from `seqStr` (safe) but the script paths extracted from AI-generated JCWF JSON by `ExtractScriptPaths` are accepted as-is (only guarded by `rfind("scripts/", 0) == 0`). That prefix check does not stop `scripts/../../../etc/cron.d/evil` because the check is against the raw string before any canonicalization. The path is later surfaced to the front-end and, if any caller writes the script to disk, acts as a path traversal.
- **Impact:** If downstream code writes generated scripts to the returned path (as the front-end is expected to do), an attacker who controls the AI response or the prompt can write files outside the scripts directory.
- **Fix:** After extracting each script path, call `fs::weakly_canonical(scriptsRoot / scriptPath)` and verify the result is still under `scriptsRoot`. Reject any path that traverses outside it.

---

### [HIGH] Uncontrolled thread growth — no bound on background thread count
- **Location:** `AiJcwfService::ExplainAsync`, `GenerateAsync`, `FixFailedScriptAsync` — all call `m_BackgroundThreads.emplace_back(...)` after `JoinFinishedThreads()` which is a no-op stub.
- **Issue:** `JoinFinishedThreads()` has an empty body (comment says "no active cleanup needed here"). Every call to any `*Async` method appends a new `std::thread` to `m_BackgroundThreads` and never removes finished ones. An attacker who can trigger these endpoints repeatedly will exhaust process thread limits (typically ~32 k on Linux) and then heap, crashing the server.
- **Impact:** Denial of service — process crash or resource exhaustion.
- **Fix:** Implement `JoinFinishedThreads` to actually remove joinable-and-finished threads. Check `thread.joinable()` plus a completion flag. Alternatively, cap the vector size before emplacing and return an error when the cap is reached.

---

### [HIGH] `popen("sw_vers 2>/dev/null", "r")` — shell injection vector if the PATH is attacker-influenced
- **Location:** `GetHostOsDescription()`, macOS branch, anonymous namespace
- **Issue:** `popen` launches a shell (`/bin/sh -c "sw_vers 2>/dev/null"`). While the argument string is a literal here, the result of `sw_vers` is concatenated verbatim into AI prompts that are eventually broadcast as JSON. If `sw_vers` is replaced on a compromised system, or if the output later flows into a shell command context, the risk escalates. More concretely, the output is used unsanitized inside AI prompt strings embedded in JSON built by hand (see `decomposeTask += "Host OS: " + GetHostOsDescription()`). If the OS description string contains JSON special characters (`"`, `\`, newlines), the hand-built JSON becomes malformed or injectable.
- **Impact:** Prompt injection into AI; JSON structure corruption in broadcast messages; potential command injection if the result is ever passed to another `popen`/`system` call.
- **Fix:** Pass the result of `GetHostOsDescription()` through `JsonEscape` before embedding it in hand-assembled JSON strings. Replace `popen("sw_vers …")` with direct `sysctlbyname` calls or parse `/System/Library/CoreServices/SystemVersion.plist` to avoid the shell entirely.

---

### [HIGH] AI-generated JCWF JSON is broadcast raw without sanitization
- **Location:** `GenerateAsync`, `broadcastResult` lambda, `ss << … << jcwfOrError` (the `ok=true` branch embeds raw JSON directly)
- **Issue:** When `ok=true`, `jcwfOrError` is the raw AI response embedded un-escaped into the WebSocket broadcast: `… "jcwf":` + raw string + `…`. The comment even says "jcwfOrError is raw JSON — embed directly (not string-escaped)". If the AI returns malformed JSON or JSON containing `}` sequences that close the outer envelope, the broadcast payload is structurally invalid and can confuse clients. More critically, if the AI is adversarially prompted (prompt injection), it can inject arbitrary keys into the WebSocket envelope seen by every connected client, enabling stored XSS or client-side prototype pollution depending on how the front-end processes the envelope.
- **Impact:** WebSocket message injection, potential XSS in the browser front-end, client-side JSON confusion.
- **Fix:** Parse the AI-returned JSON with simdjson before broadcasting to verify it is well-formed. Re-serialize it (compact form) to guarantee the resulting string is safe to embed. Never trust raw AI output as structurally valid JSON without parsing.

---

### [MEDIUM] `stderrContent` from a failed script is embedded verbatim into AI prompts without size cap
- **Location:** `AiJcwfService::FixFailedScriptAsync`, lambda, `task = "… --- stderr ---\n" + stderrContent + …`
- **Issue:** `stderrContent` comes from an external caller with no length validation. A runaway script or a deliberate call can supply megabytes of stderr text, which is then combined with the script content and sent as a single AI request payload. This can exceed provider token limits (causing billable failures), exhaust heap, or slow the system.
- **Impact:** Denial of service, unexpected billing costs, potential heap exhaustion.
- **Fix:** Truncate `stderrContent` to a reasonable maximum (e.g., 16 KiB) before embedding: `stderrContent.substr(0, 16384)`.

---

### [MEDIUM] `GenerateRunId` uses `time_t` (second resolution) — trivially predictable and collision-prone
- **Location:** `GenerateRunId()`, anonymous namespace
- **Issue:** The run ID is `workflowId + "_" + std::to_string(nowTimeT)`. Two requests within the same second produce the same ID, causing the second call's `RunSingleAiCall` to clean the first call's queue directory (`fs::remove` inside `for (auto const& entry : fs::directory_iterator(queueDir, ec))`). This is a TOCTOU/race condition: concurrent calls to `ExplainAsync` or `GenerateAsync` issued within the same second will silently delete each other's working files.
- **Impact:** Loss of audit/replay files; potential for one user's request to corrupt another's in-flight queue directory.
- **Fix:** Use a monotonic counter (e.g., `m_NextRequestSeq`) combined with the timestamp, or use a UUID/random component. `seqStr` is already available in each call site — use it as the folder discriminator instead of (or in addition to) the timestamp.

---

### [MEDIUM] `FindMatchingBrace` and `FindTasksObject` do naive string search — susceptible to JSON-in-string confusion
- **Location:** `FindTasksObject()`, `FindTaskBlockBounds()`, anonymous namespace
- **Issue:** `FindTasksObject` searches for the literal string `"tasks"` using `json.find("\"tasks\"")`. A JSON string value that happens to contain the text `"tasks"` (e.g., a label or doc field) will cause the function to find the wrong position and operate on the wrong section of the document. `FindMatchingBrace` handles escape sequences for `\\` but not for multi-character Unicode escapes (`\uXXXX`), so a crafted string like `"\u0022tasks\u0022"` (a quoted string encoded with Unicode escapes) would bypass the inString guard.
- **Impact:** When processing AI-generated or attacker-supplied JCWF JSON, task blocks could be misidentified, causing `PatchTasksIntoJcwf` or `MergeJcwfFragments` to corrupt the document or silently skip fixes.
- **Fix:** Replace all hand-rolled JSON traversal with simdjson (already a dependency). Use the DOM/ondemand API to locate and extract task blocks rather than byte-level string search.

---

### [MEDIUM] Script content from AI response is never virus-scanned or sandboxed before being sent to the browser for deployment
- **Location:** `GenerateAsync` and `FixFailedScriptAsync`, `broadcastResult` / final `Broadcast` calls including `"content":"` + `JsonEscape(scriptContent)`
- **Issue:** The generated script content is sent to the front-end which is expected to write it to disk and make it executable. There is no static analysis, sandboxing, or content policy applied to prevent the AI from generating a script that contains a reverse shell, data exfiltration command, or destructive `rm -rf`. The structural validator (`ValidateGeneratedScript`) only checks shebang lines, metadata markers, and function names — not content.
- **Impact:** An adversarially prompted or jailbroken AI can produce malicious scripts that the system automatically deploys to disk as executable files.
- **Fix:** At minimum, log generated script content for human review before deployment. Consider requiring explicit operator approval before scripts are written to disk. Apply a content-policy check (blocklist of known dangerous patterns: `curl | bash`, `/dev/tcp`, `nc -e`, etc.) as a defense-in-depth layer.

---

### [MEDIUM] Secrets (API keys) logged in error path via `outError = "No API key configured for key_name '" + iface.m_KeyName + "'"`
- **Location:** `AiJcwfService::TestAiInterface`, `outError = "No API key configured for key_name '" + iface.m_KeyName + "'"`, and the subsequent `LOG_APP_INFO` that logs `outError` containing the key name
- **Issue:** While `m_KeyName` is the key's name rather than the key value itself, `iface.m_Name` and `iface.m_KeyName` are both emitted to the structured log at INFO level in the success and failure paths. More critically, `outError` is returned to the HTTP caller, leaking the internal key name structure to API consumers.
- **Impact:** Information disclosure of internal key naming conventions, which can aid targeted attacks against the key management system.
- **Fix:** Return a generic error to the HTTP caller ("Authentication not configured") and log the specific key name only at DEBUG level.

---

### [LOW] `std::thread::join` called while holding `m_ThreadsMutex` in `Shutdown()`
- **Location:** `AiJcwfService::Shutdown()`, `std::lock_guard<std::mutex> lock(m_ThreadsMutex)` held across all `thread.join()` calls
- **Issue:** Background threads call `Broadcast()` which acquires `m_BroadcastMutex`, and may also attempt operations that indirectly reference `m_ThreadsMutex` (e.g., if `JoinFinishedThreads` is ever implemented). Holding `m_ThreadsMutex` while blocking on `thread.join()` means any other thread that tries to acquire `m_ThreadsMutex` (e.g., a new `ExplainAsync` call) will deadlock until all joins complete. This is a latent deadlock condition.
- **Impact:** Potential deadlock during shutdown if any code path acquires `m_ThreadsMutex` from within a background thread.
- **Fix:** Move the threads to a local vector before joining: take the lock, swap `m_BackgroundThreads` into a local, release the lock, then join the local vector.

---

### [LOW] `curl.GetBuffer()` result used after `curl` may be invalidated by next call
- **Location:** `AiJcwfService::TestAiInterface`, `auto parser = ReplyParser::Create(iface.m_InterfaceType, curl.GetBuffer())`
- **Issue:** `curl` is obtained by `CurlWrapper::GetThreadCurl()` (thread-local reference). If `ReplyParser::Create` stores a reference or pointer into the buffer returned by `curl.GetBuffer()` rather than a copy, and if anything between `Create` and the use of `parser` triggers another curl operation on the same thread-local instance, the buffer could be invalidated. This depends on `ReplyParser::Create`'s implementation which is not visible here but represents a lifetime risk.
- **Impact:** Use-after-free or reading stale data from the response parser.
- **Fix:** Copy `curl.GetBuffer()` into a local `std::string` before passing it to `ReplyParser::Create`, ensuring the lifetime is independent of the curl object.


---

### 69_doc_application_web_mcpKeyManager_h

<a id="69-doc-application-web-mcpkeymanager-h"></a>

*Source doc:* `../../../queue/jarvisCppCyberSecAudit/69_doc_application_web_mcpKeyManager_h/docu.output.md`



### CRITICAL: `Authenticate` does not check `m_Enabled` or expiry — disabled/expired keys are accepted

- **Location:** `McpKeyManager::Authenticate`, approximately lines 295–315
- **Issue:** After a successful hash match, the function never checks `rec->m_Enabled` or whether `rec->m_ExpiresAt` is in the past. It returns a valid `AuthResult` for revoked (disabled) or expired keys. `m_DaysUntilExpiry` is computed and returned, but the caller is left to enforce the check — and `SelfRenew` (line ~333) does check `m_DaysUntilExpiry < 0` and `m_Enabled`, but any other call site that invokes `Authenticate` directly and does not repeat those checks will accept dead credentials.
- **Impact:** A revoked or expired MCP key continues to authenticate successfully against every call site that relies solely on `Authenticate` returning a non-empty optional.
- **Fix:** Inside `Authenticate`, before returning, add:
  ```
  if (!rec->m_Enabled) return std::nullopt;
  if (IsPast(rec->m_ExpiresAt)) return std::nullopt;
  ```
  Authorization policy must not be distributed across call sites.

---

### CRITICAL: `CreateBootstrapAdminKey` hardcodes the admin identity as `"boss"`

- **Location:** `McpKeyManager::CreateBootstrapAdminKey`, approximately line 360
- **Issue:** The first-run admin key is created with `rec.m_User = "boss"` unconditionally. No caller-supplied identity is accepted. The comment says this is invoked from an unauthenticated `/api/settings/keys/unlock` handler. Any process that can reach that endpoint while the store is empty gets an admin key whose `m_User` is a predictable constant string — relevant anywhere user identity is used for audit, RBAC, or quota decisions.
- **Impact:** The audit trail is permanently falsified for the root admin account. If any authorization logic later checks `m_User == "boss"` to grant elevated privileges, that string is a universal bypass target.
- **Fix:** Accept a `std::string const& adminUser` parameter (validated non-empty) and use it in place of the literal `"boss"`. Ensure the calling HTTP handler passes the identity supplied during first-run setup, not a constant.

---

### HIGH: Race condition between `Authenticate` and `SelfRenew` — TOCTOU on key validity

- **Location:** `McpKeyManager::SelfRenew`, approximately lines 330–380
- **Issue:** `SelfRenew` calls `Authenticate` (which acquires and releases `m_Mutex`), then re-acquires `m_Mutex` for the write path. Between the two lock acquisitions, another thread can revoke or mutate the same key. The renewed key inherits the stale `auth->m_Record` snapshot, potentially copying role/quota fields that were concurrently changed.
- **Impact:** A key revoked by an admin can be successfully renewed in a narrow window. Concurrently lowered quotas or role downgrades do not take effect in the renewed key.
- **Fix:** Refactor so the full validity check and the new-key insertion happen inside a single `std::lock_guard` scope. Extract an internal `AuthenticateUnlocked` helper (similar to `FindByKeyIdUnlocked`) and call it from `SelfRenew` while holding the lock.

---

### HIGH: `IsPast` returns `false` on timestamp parse failure — expired/malformed dates treated as valid

- **Location:** `IsPast` anonymous-namespace helper, approximately line 215
- **Issue:** When `ParseIso8601` returns `std::nullopt` (empty string, malformed date, or overflow), `IsPast` returns `false`, meaning the record is treated as not-yet-expired. This affects both enrollment TTL enforcement in `ActivateEnrollment` and the opportunistic `PurgeExpiredEnrollments`. A stored enrollment or key with a corrupted or empty `m_ExpiresAt` will never be pruned.
- **Impact:** An enrollment record whose `expires_at` field was corrupted (e.g., by partial disk write or deliberate tampering of the encrypted blob after decryption) remains permanently activatable.
- **Fix:** Change the fail-open default to fail-closed: return `true` (treat as past/expired) when parsing fails. Add a separate validity gate in `ActivateEnrollment` that rejects any enrollment whose `m_ExpiresAt` is empty or unparseable.

---

### HIGH: `UpdateKey` accepts arbitrary free-text for `m_Role` and `m_ExpiresAt` with no validation

- **Location:** `McpKeyManager::UpdateKey`, approximately lines 410–430
- **Issue:** `fields.m_Role`, `fields.m_ExpiresAt`, and `fields.m_DefaultCleanupPolicy` are written directly to the record without any validation. An admin API caller can set `m_Role` to any string (including a novel value that a downstream RBAC check treats as elevated), set `m_ExpiresAt` to a date 10,000 years in the future, or inject a malformed timestamp that triggers the `IsPast` fail-open.
- **Impact:** Privilege escalation via role string manipulation; indefinitely long-lived keys that bypass renewal enforcement.
- **Fix:** Validate `m_Role` against an allowlist (`{"admin", "operator", "viewer"}`). Parse and range-check `m_ExpiresAt` with `ParseIso8601` before accepting it. Reject disk quota values outside a defined range.

---

### HIGH: `Load` reads the entire file into memory with no size cap — uncontrolled allocation

- **Location:** `McpKeyManager::Load`, approximately lines 245–260
- **Issue:** The file at `path` is slurped entirely into a `std::vector<uint8_t>` using `istreambuf_iterator`. There is no check on file size before or during the read. A multi-gigabyte file causes a proportional heap allocation, and the decrypted JSON is then stored in a second `std::string` of equal size before parsing.
- **Impact:** Any process or user that can write to the key-store path (or substitute it via symlink before `Load` is called) can cause an out-of-memory crash of the server process (DoS).
- **Fix:** After opening the file, check `std::filesystem::file_size(path)` and reject files larger than a reasonable bound (e.g., 1 MiB). Apply the same cap before JSON parsing.

---

### MEDIUM: `ParseFromJson` accepts crafted JSON with arbitrarily large arrays — unbounded allocation

- **Location:** `McpKeyManager::ParseFromJson`, approximately lines 470–540
- **Issue:** The `keys[]` and `enrollments[]` arrays are iterated without any count limit. An attacker who can influence the encrypted file (e.g., by corrupting it after a partial write, or if `KeyEncryption` is ever weakened) can embed millions of entries, causing unbounded `push_back` growth into `m_Keys` and `m_Enrollments`.
- **Impact:** DoS via memory exhaustion on load.
- **Fix:** Add a counter in each loop and return `false` if the count exceeds a sane maximum (e.g., 10,000 keys, 1,000 pending enrollments).

---

### MEDIUM: `disk_quota_mb` and `key_expiry_days` from JSON are not range-checked

- **Location:** `ParseFromJson`, approximately lines 492 and 530; `CreateEnrollment`, line 308
- **Issue:** `ReadInt` for `disk_quota_mb` can return any `int64_t` that fits in the file. The result is cast to `int` without overflow or range checks. A value of `INT64_MAX` truncates silently. Negative values set a negative quota. Similarly, `key_expiry_days` is used directly to compute `std::chrono::hours(24 * it->m_KeyExpiryDays)` which overflows `int` for large values.
- **Impact:** Arithmetic overflow producing negative or zero durations, leading to keys that are immediately expired or that have enormous TTLs.
- **Fix:** Clamp both values to documented ranges (e.g., quota 1–102400 MB, expiry 1–3650 days) immediately after reading from JSON and from the `EnrollmentRequest`.

---

### MEDIUM: `SelfRenew` always issues a new 90-day expiry regardless of the original key's lifetime

- **Location:** `McpKeyManager::SelfRenew`, approximately line 355
- **Issue:** `renewed.m_ExpiresAt = Iso8601FromNow(std::chrono::hours(24 * 90))` is hardcoded. An `operator`-role key originally issued with a 7-day expiry can be indefinitely renewed to 90 days. There is no enforcement of the original `m_KeyExpiryDays` policy from the enrollment record.
- **Impact:** Users can extend their own key lifetime beyond what was authorized during enrollment, defeating time-limited access grants.
- **Fix:** Store the `m_KeyExpiryDays` policy in `Record` and use it in `SelfRenew` instead of the literal `90`.

---

### MEDIUM: `Save` writes the plaintext file with no atomic rename — TOCTOU and partial-write window

- **Location:** `McpKeyManager::Save`, approximately lines 270–285
- **Issue:** `Save` opens `path` with `std::ios::trunc` and writes in place. If the process is killed or crashes mid-write, the file is left truncated. There is also a window between truncation and completion during which another reader (or a concurrent `Load` call) sees a partial file. No `fsync` is called.
- **Impact:** Corruption of the key store on crash, leading to loss of all keys or to a zero-byte file that causes `Load` to fail — effective DoS. A concurrent `Load` during the write window may read an empty or partial blob.
- **Fix:** Write to a `.tmp` sibling file, call `fsync`, then `std::filesystem::rename` over the target. `rename` is atomic on POSIX for same-filesystem moves.

---

### LOW: `ListKeys` returns `m_KeyHash` values to callers — hash exposure in API responses

- **Location:** `McpKeyManager::ListKeys`, approximately line 400; `Record` struct definition
- **Issue:** The comment in the header says "hashes are metadata only," but the full SHA-256 hash of each key is included in the returned `Record`. If the API serializes `Record` directly to JSON responses, the hash is exposed to API clients. Although SHA-256 of a 32-byte random input is not practically reversible, exposing it is unnecessary and violates the principle of minimum disclosure. It also gives an oracle for offline hash-comparison attacks if the hash algorithm is ever downgraded.
- **Impact:** Low direct risk; but combined with a weak hash or a short key, it reduces the work factor for brute-force.
- **Fix:** Provide a separate `RedactedRecord` struct (or mask `m_KeyHash` to its first 8 characters) for use in list/read API responses. Only compare the full hash internally.

---

### LOW: `Authenticate` updates `m_LastUsedAt` inside a `const`-logically-expected path without marking the side-effect

- **Location:** `McpKeyManager::Authenticate`, approximately line 312
- **Issue:** `Authenticate` takes a non-const `McpKeyManager` and mutates `rec->m_LastUsedAt` as a "best-effort" side effect. If `m_Keys` is reallocated by a concurrent insertion (from `ActivateEnrollment` or `SelfRenew`) on another thread after the pointer is obtained but before the write, this is a use-after-reallocation. The mutex is held throughout `Authenticate`, so this is safe for the current single-lock design, but the comment "best-effort" is misleading and could encourage future callers to drop the lock early.
- **Impact:** If locking is ever refactored (e.g., to a read/write lock where `Authenticate` takes a read lock), the write to `m_LastUsedAt` becomes a data race.
- **Fix:** Document explicitly that `Authenticate` requires the write lock, or move the `m_LastUsedAt` update to a separate `TouchKey` call that is explicitly documented as write-path-only.


---

### 70_doc_application_web_webServer_h

<a id="70-doc-application-web-webserver-h"></a>

*Source doc:* `../../../queue/jarvisCppCyberSecAudit/70_doc_application_web_webServer_h/docu.output.md`



### HIGH: Unauthenticated MCP heartbeat endpoint exposes timing oracle and lockout bypass

- **Location:** `RegisterCommonRoutes`, `HandleMcpHeartbeatPost`
- **Issue:** `POST /api/mcp/heartbeat` is registered with no authentication and no rate limiting. Any unauthenticated caller can set `m_McpLastHeartbeat` to the current time, making `IsMcpConnected()` return `true` indefinitely. This also means the heartbeat path is exempt from the pre-auth rate limiter applied to other unauthenticated paths.
- **Impact:** An attacker on the network can spoof MCP-connected status on the dashboard and suppress alerts that rely on heartbeat staleness detection.
- **Fix:** Require a valid MCP key (`TryMcpAuth`) on this endpoint, or at minimum apply the pre-auth rate limiter. If the sidecar uses a shared secret, verify it here.

---

### HIGH: `ServeDashboardStatic` and `ServeWorkflowEditorStatic` do not canonicalize before filesystem access

- **Location:** `ServeDashboardStatic`, `ServeWorkflowEditorStatic`
- **Issue:** `ServeDashboardStatic` strips the `/dash-assets/` prefix from the URL and passes the remainder directly to `ServeStaticFile(distRoot / relative)` without calling `lexically_normal()` or verifying the resolved path is still under `distRoot`. `ServeWorkflowEditorStatic` does the same for `/assets/` and `/editor/assets/`. Crow URL-decodes path parameters before handing them to the handler, so a request to `/dash-assets/../../../etc/passwd` becomes `relative = "../../etc/passwd"` and `distRoot / relative` resolves outside the dist directory.
- **Impact:** Path traversal: an unauthenticated attacker can read arbitrary files readable by the process (private keys, config files, `/etc/shadow` on misconfigured systems).
- **Fix:** After constructing the candidate path, call `lexically_normal()` and then assert that the result starts with `distRoot.lexically_normal()`. Reject the request with 400 if not.

---

### HIGH: `HandleLogGet` and `HandleSecurityLogGet` use hardcoded relative paths without confinement

- **Location:** `HandleLogGet`, `HandleSecurityLogGet`, `ReadLogFile`
- **Issue:** Both handlers pass literal relative strings `"log/log.txt"` and `"log/security.txt"` to `ReadLogFile`, which opens the file directly. The `ReadLogFile` function also accepts a caller-controlled `logPath` parameter. While the current call-sites use fixed strings, `ReadLogFile` is a public method on the class and is not restricted to paths under a known root. Additionally, the `offset` and `tail` query parameters are parsed with `std::atoll`/`std::atoi` without validating that `fromOffset` is non-negative before seeking, and `deltaSize` is computed as `fileSize - fromOffset` where a race-truncated file could make `fromOffset > fileSize`, causing a negative `int64_t` to be cast to `size_t` (very large allocation).
- **Impact:** If `ReadLogFile` is ever called with an attacker-influenced path (e.g., via a future refactor), path traversal results. The `fromOffset > fileSize` case is handled but only after computing a potentially large `deltaSize` from the cast.
- **Fix:** Add a guard in `ReadLogFile` asserting `fromOffset <= fileSize` before computing `deltaSize`. Restrict `ReadLogFile` to a known log directory by resolving and prefix-checking the path. Clamp `tailLines` before `std::clamp` already handles it, but also clamp `fromOffset` to `[0, fileSize]`.

---

### HIGH: OAuth callback accepts authorization code without validating it came from the expected provider; CURL performs token exchange without TLS peer verification fallback

- **Location:** `HandleOAuthCallbackGet`
- **Issue:** The CSRF state check correctly validates the state parameter, but the authorization `code` itself is not validated for length or character set before being embedded in a POST body. More critically, `curl_easy_setopt(curl, CURLOPT_SSL_VERIFYPEER, ...)` is never called; the code only conditionally sets `CURLOPT_CAINFO` when `CurlWrapper::GetCaBundlePath()` is non-empty. On systems where that returns empty (e.g., missing CA bundle), libcurl defaults to verifying peers, which is correct, but this is fragile. There is also no `CURLOPT_SSL_VERIFYHOST` set explicitly.
- **Impact:** If the CA bundle path is misconfigured or empty and libcurl is built with verification disabled by default, token exchange occurs over a connection where the token endpoint's certificate is not verified, enabling MitM token theft.
- **Fix:** Explicitly set `CURLOPT_SSL_VERIFYPEER` to `1L` and `CURLOPT_SSL_VERIFYHOST` to `2L` unconditionally. Treat an empty CA bundle path as a startup error rather than silently omitting the setting.

---

### HIGH: `HandleAiInterfacesSavePost` and `HandleConfigSettingsPut` write config by naive string replacement without JSON escaping

- **Location:** `HandleAiInterfacesSavePost`, `HandleConfigSettingsPut` (`replaceField` lambda)
- **Issue:** Both functions read the config file as raw text and splice in new values by string search-and-replace. Neither function JSON-escapes the values being written. An admin submitting an interface name or URL containing `"`, `\`, or newline characters will corrupt the JSON file. More critically, in `HandleConfigSettingsPut`, the `replaceField` lambda locates a value by key name and replaces it with the new value, but the key search is a simple `find()` with no brace/object scope awareness — a key appearing in a nested object or in a comment will be matched first.
- **Impact:** An admin can corrupt `config.json` by submitting crafted strings, causing a DoS on next restart. With specific control over the file content, arbitrary JSON injection into the config is possible.
- **Fix:** Serialize the config back to JSON using a proper serializer (parse the full document with simdjson or nlohmann/json, mutate the fields, dump back). Never splice raw user-supplied strings into a JSON file via string replacement.

---

### HIGH: WebSocket `ai-write-scripts` handler writes arbitrary file content under `scripts/` without authentication check on the message itself

- **Location:** `RegisterWebSocket`, `onmessage` handler, `ai-write-scripts` branch
- **Issue:** The WebSocket upgrade requires authentication via `onaccept`. However, once authenticated, any connected client — regardless of role — can send `{"type":"ai-write-scripts","scripts":[{"path":"scripts/evil.sh","content":"...","executable":true}]}` and the handler will write that file to disk and set it executable. There is no role check (e.g., admin-only) inside the `onmessage` handler for this message type. An operator or viewer with a WebSocket connection can write or overwrite any script under `scripts/`.
- **Impact:** An operator-role user can plant malicious scripts that are then executed by subsequent workflow runs triggered by an admin, leading to privilege escalation and arbitrary code execution.
- **Fix:** Add a role check inside the `ai-write-scripts` branch (e.g., require admin role, storing the role in the WebSocket connection's `userdata` during `onaccept`).

---

### MEDIUM: `HandleMcpHeartbeatPost` is missing body-size and content validation

- **Location:** `HandleMcpHeartbeatPost`
- **Issue:** The handler ignores the request body entirely but does not cap it. Crow will buffer the entire request body in memory before invoking the handler. A large POST to `/api/mcp/heartbeat` with no auth will allocate memory proportional to the `Content-Length`.
- **Impact:** Unauthenticated memory exhaustion / DoS.
- **Fix:** Apply the same `IsBodyTooLarge` guard used by other handlers, or configure Crow's global body size limit.

---

### MEDIUM: `HandleN8nStartPost` writes the raw request body to disk under an attacker-influenced path

- **Location:** `HandleN8nStartPost`
- **Issue:** The run directory is constructed as `workflowsDirectoryAbsolute / workflowId / taskName / "n8n" / runId`. `workflowId` is validated by `IsValidWorkflowId`. `taskName` is validated by `IsValidTaskName`. However, `runId` — when supplied by the caller via `doc["runId"]` — is used directly in the path construction without any character-set or length validation. An attacker who can reach this endpoint (admin role required) with a crafted `runId` such as `../../../` could write `request.json` outside the intended directory.
- **Impact:** Path traversal for the persisted request file; could overwrite arbitrary files writable by the process.
- **Fix:** Apply `IsValidWorkflowId` (or an equivalent alphanumeric-plus-dash-underscore check) to the caller-supplied `runId`, or generate the run ID server-side and ignore the client-supplied one.

---

### MEDIUM: `HandleWebhookPost` persists the raw request body under a caller-influenced path with the same `runId` issue

- **Location:** `HandleWebhookPost`
- **Issue:** Same as the n8n handler: `runId` from the JSON body is used directly in `workflowsDirectoryAbsolute / workflowId / "webhook" / runId / "request.json"` without validation.
- **Impact:** Path traversal for the persisted webhook request file.
- **Fix:** Validate `runId` with `IsValidWorkflowId` or generate it server-side.

---

### MEDIUM: `HandleKeysUnlockPost` logs the bootstrap event with the keys file path, leaking deployment topology, and does not rate-limit failed unlock attempts

- **Location:** `HandleKeysUnlockPost`
- **Issue:** The endpoint is public (no prior auth required, by design). It does not apply the pre-auth rate limiter or the failed-auth lockout. An attacker can brute-force the master password without any throttling. On failure, `KeyEncryption::Decrypt` is called against the encrypted file for every attempt.
- **Impact:** Offline brute-force of the master password via the live API if the decrypt function is fast. The endpoint also does not increment the auth-failure counter (`RecordAuthFailure`) on wrong passwords.
- **Fix:** Call `RecordAuthFailure(req.remote_ip_address)` on wrong-password responses. Apply `IsRateLimited(RateLimitTier::PreAuth, ip)` at the top of the handler.

---

### MEDIUM: Trusted proxy header identity is taken from the request verbatim without sanitizing newlines (HTTP header injection)

- **Location:** `Authenticate`, `HandleLoginPost`
- **Issue:** `req.get_header_value(config.m_TrustedProxyHeader)` returns the raw header value, which is then stored in `auth.m_User` and later emitted into log lines via `LOG_SECURITY_INFO`. If the gateway injects a username containing `\n` or `\r`, log injection is possible. Additionally, in `HandleLoginPost`, the gateway user value is stored directly into the session and set in the `Set-Cookie` header implicitly through downstream session ID generation — but the user field itself appears in the JSON response body, which uses crow's JSON serializer (safe). The log injection path is the immediate concern.
- **Impact:** Log forging: an attacker who can influence the trusted proxy header (e.g., misconfigured proxy) can inject fake security log entries.
- **Fix:** Strip or reject `\r`, `\n`, and other control characters from header values used in log lines and as identity tokens.

---

### MEDIUM: `HandleOAuthCallbackGet` passes `client_secret` in the POST body but `codeVerifier` is passed without percent-encoding

- **Location:** `HandleOAuthCallbackGet`
- **Issue:** The `postBody` is assembled as a raw string concatenation. `codeVerifier` (base64url, safe characters) and `authCode` (percent-encoded via `percentEncodeCb`) are handled correctly. However, `clientId` and `clientSecretForRefresh` are passed through `percentEncodeCb`, which is correct. The issue is that `codeVerifier` is appended as `"&code_verifier=" + codeVerifier` without encoding. While base64url characters are URL-safe, this is fragile. More importantly, the `authCode` comes from `req.url_params.get("code")` which Crow URL-decodes before returning; re-encoding it with `percentEncodeCb` double-processes it for URL-encoded codes that were already decoded.
- **Impact:** Token exchange may fail or succeed with incorrect values for non-standard auth codes; minor correctness/robustness issue.
- **Fix:** Use the raw query string value for `authCode` (before Crow's URL decoding) or be explicit that the value from `url_params` is already decoded and only encode it once.

---

### MEDIUM: `HandleLogAnalyzeLastRunGet` reads the entire log file into memory with no size cap

- **Location:** `HandleLogAnalyzeLastRunGet`
- **Issue:** The handler opens `log/log.txt` and reads every line into a `std::vector<std::string>` with no size limit. On a long-running system or after log rotation failure, this file could be gigabytes.
- **Impact:** An authenticated operator can trigger an OOM condition by hitting this endpoint when the log file is large.
- **Fix:** Apply a line count or byte limit (e.g., read only the last N MB of the log file, mirroring the tail logic in `ReadLogFile`).

---

### MEDIUM: `DrainPendingBroadcasts` sends to WebSocket clients while holding a stale pointer snapshot; re-validation holds and releases `m_Mutex` per-client inside the send loop (TOCTOU)

- **Location:** `DrainPendingBroadcasts`
- **Issue:** The client set is snapshotted into `clients` under `m_Mutex`, then for each client, the lock is re-acquired to check `m_Clients.find(client)` before calling `send_text`. Between the re-check and `send_text`, the connection can be destroyed by `onclose` on another thread. The per-client lock-acquire-then-release pattern does not prevent the use-after-free in `send_text` because `m_Mutex` is not held during the actual `send_text` call.
- **Impact:** Use-after-free if a WebSocket connection is closed between the liveness check and `send_text`. This is likely to manifest as a crash rather than an exploitable condition, but could be triggered by a client disconnecting at the right moment.
- **Fix:** Either hold `m_Mutex` across the `send_text` call (if Crow's `send_text` does not itself acquire a lock that would conflict), or use a weak-reference/shared-pointer scheme for connection lifetime, or catch exceptions and treat any exception as a disconnection signal.

---

### LOW: `ExtractSessionCookie` does not validate cookie value characters; a crafted `Cookie` header could inject a session token containing semicolons or other delimiters

- **Location:** `ExtractSessionCookie`
- **Issue:** The parser splits on `;` and `=` but does not validate that the extracted session value is free of unexpected characters. A `Cookie: session=abc; session=def` header would return `abc` (the first match), which is correct, but a value like `session=abc%00def` would return the raw string with a null byte.
- **Impact:** If `WebSessionManager::Validate` uses the session ID as a map key, a null byte in the key is benign in C++ `std::string`. However, if the session ID is ever passed to a C API or logged, null-byte injection could occur. Low severity given C++ string safety.
- **Fix:** Validate that the extracted session ID matches an expected character set (alphanumeric + dash/underscore) before passing to `Validate`.

---

### LOW: `HandleMcpKeysEnrollPost` does not validate `key_expiry_days` or `enrollment_ttl_minutes` upper bounds

- **Location:** `HandleMcpKeysEnrollPost`
- **Issue:** `key_expiry_days` and `enrollment_ttl_minutes` are read from the request body and stored in `EnrollmentRequest` without any range check. An admin could create a key with `key_expiry_days = INT_MAX` or `enrollment_ttl_minutes = 0`.
- **Impact:** Keys that never expire (or enrollment tokens that never expire) weaken the key rotation security model.
- **Fix:** Clamp `key_expiry_days` to a


---

### 71_doc_application_web_webSessionManager_h

<a id="71-doc-application-web-websessionmanager-h"></a>

*Source doc:* `../../../queue/jarvisCppCyberSecAudit/71_doc_application_web_webSessionManager_h/docu.output.md`



### MEDIUM Session fixation — role and user accepted verbatim from the caller without validation

- **Location:** `WebSessionManager::Create`, ~line 100 (cpp file)
- **Issue:** The `user` and `role` strings are stored directly into the session without any allowlist check. If the caller (the HTTP layer parsing a gateway header or MCP key) passes an attacker-supplied or malformed value for `role`, a session with role `"admin"` can be fabricated by any code path that invokes `Create`. There is no enforcement that `role` is one of `{"admin", "operator", "viewer"}` and no normalisation of `user`.
- **Impact:** If any upstream code path passes externally influenced data to `Create` without its own validation, an attacker can obtain an admin-role session cookie for an account that should be `viewer` or `operator`.
- **Fix:** Add an allowlist check inside `Create` before storing:
  `if (role != "admin" && role != "operator" && role != "viewer") return {};`
  and reject or sanitise `user` values containing newlines or other control characters.

---

### MEDIUM Non-constant-time session-ID comparison enables timing oracle

- **Location:** `WebSessionManager::Validate`, ~line 117 (cpp file), specifically the `m_Sessions.find(sessionId)` call
- **Issue:** `std::unordered_map::find` performs a standard `std::string` equality test (early-exit on first differing byte via the hash bucket and then `operator==`). Although the hash lookup makes pure timing attacks harder, the final per-bucket `operator==` comparison is not constant-time and can leak partial information about valid session IDs through CPU branch-prediction and cache timing side-channels.
- **Impact:** A sufficiently motivated attacker with a high-precision timing channel (local network, shared host) could distinguish valid session-ID prefixes and narrow a brute-force search.
- **Fix:** Use a constant-time comparison (`CRYPTO_memcmp` from OpenSSL, or a hand-rolled constant-time `memcmp`) when performing the equality check after the hash lookup, or switch to a keyed HMAC-validated token so the secret portion cannot be brute-forced even with timing information.

---

### MEDIUM Unbounded session store — uncontrolled memory allocation / DoS

- **Location:** `WebSessionManager::Create`, ~line 100; `WebSessionManager::PurgeExpired`, ~line 145 (cpp file)
- **Issue:** There is no cap on the number of entries in `m_Sessions`. Any caller that can reach `Create` (including unauthenticated login attempts if the HTTP layer calls `Create` before verifying credentials, or an authenticated user who repeatedly logs in) can grow the map without bound. `PurgeExpired` only removes sessions idle beyond `m_TimeoutHours`; it does not enforce a global ceiling.
- **Impact:** An attacker who can make repeated login requests can exhaust process heap memory, causing an out-of-memory crash (denial of service).
- **Fix:** Enforce a hard maximum (e.g., 10 000 entries). In `Create`, if `m_Sessions.size() >= MAX_SESSIONS` after acquiring the lock, either call `PurgeExpired` inline and retry once, or return an error. Also ensure `Create` is called only after credential verification, not before.

---

### LOW Empty session ID returned on RAND_bytes failure is not rejected by Validate but leaks a usable nil session

- **Location:** `RandomSessionId`, ~line 84 (cpp file); `WebSessionManager::Create`, ~line 100
- **Issue:** When `RAND_bytes` fails, `RandomSessionId` returns an empty string. `Create` proceeds to insert a `Session` with `m_SessionId = ""` and returns a `LoginResult` with an empty `m_SessionId`. `Validate` correctly rejects empty IDs (`if (sessionId.empty()) return std::nullopt;`), so the returned empty token cannot be used — but the zero-length session is silently inserted into `m_Sessions` under the key `""`, consuming memory and potentially being overwritten on the next failure, masking the error.
- **Impact:** Repeated RAND_bytes failures (e.g., on a system with a broken entropy source) cause silent session-creation failures returned as apparently successful `LoginResult` objects. A client receiving an empty session ID that cannot be validated may fall back to an insecure path.
- **Fix:** In `Create`, check `if (s.m_SessionId.empty()) return {};` immediately after calling `RandomSessionId()`, before inserting anything into the map. Log the error at that point so the operator is alerted.

---

### LOW No absolute maximum session lifetime — sessions live indefinitely if kept active

- **Location:** `WebSessionManager::Session` struct (header); `WebSessionManager::Validate`, ~line 117 (cpp file)
- **Issue:** Expiry is based solely on idle time (`m_LastActivity`). A session that receives any request within each `m_TimeoutHours` window is renewed indefinitely. `m_CreatedAt` is stored but never checked. There is no upper bound on how long a single session credential can remain valid.
- **Impact:** A stolen session cookie remains valid as long as the attacker continues to use it, with no forced re-authentication.
- **Fix:** Add an absolute lifetime check in `Validate` using `m_CreatedAt`, e.g., reject sessions older than 24 hours (or a configurable `m_MaxLifetimeHours`) regardless of activity, and force re-login.


---

### 72_doc_application_workflow_adhocWorkflowManager_h

<a id="72-doc-application-workflow-adhocworkflowmanager-h"></a>

*Source doc:* `../../../queue/jarvisCppCyberSecAudit/72_doc_application_workflow_adhocWorkflowManager_h/docu.output.md`



### HIGH Path Traversal via User-Controlled `m_JcwfJson` Content Written to Filesystem

- **Location:** `Stage()`, approximately line 340; `RewriteWorkflowId()`, approximately line 260
- **Issue:** The canvas JSON supplied by the caller (`req.m_JcwfJson`) is rewritten with a controlled `workflowId` and then passed directly to `m_Registry.SaveOrUpdateWorkflowFromJson(rewritten, jcwfPath, ...)`. The `jcwfPath` itself is constructed from server-controlled timestamps and counters, so the *path* is safe. However, `SaveOrUpdateWorkflowFromJson` receives the full raw JSON content, which may contain attacker-controlled strings that the registry uses to derive sub-paths, extract filenames, or create nested directories during extraction (the comment mentions a zip container). If the registry unzips or writes files based on paths embedded in the JSON/zip, a `../` sequence in the workflow JSON could escape the run folder. This code provides no sanitisation of the JSON content before handing it to the registry.
- **Impact:** An attacker with a valid MCP key could write arbitrary files to any path reachable by the process, potentially overwriting binaries, config files, or other users' run artifacts.
- **Fix:** Before calling `SaveOrUpdateWorkflowFromJson`, validate that every path token inside the parsed JSON that the registry will use to derive filenames contains no `..` components and is confined within the run folder. Additionally, after extraction, assert that every written path is a subpath of `folder` using `std::filesystem::relative` and rejecting anything whose first component is `..`.

---

### HIGH TOCTOU Race Between Quota Pre-Check and Lock Acquisition in `Stage()`

- **Location:** `Stage()`, approximately lines 315–330 and 340
- **Issue:** `WouldExceedQuota()` acquires `m_Mutex` internally and then releases it. The disk-usage map is only modified again after the subsequent `std::lock_guard lock(m_Mutex)` on line ~340. Between these two points, any number of concurrent `Stage()` calls from the same user can pass the quota check simultaneously before any of them have updated `m_DiskUsageByUser`. All of them then proceed to write files and increment usage, collectively exceeding the quota by a factor equal to the number of concurrent requests.
- **Impact:** A single MCP key holder can exhaust disk space by firing many concurrent adhoc submissions, bypassing the per-user quota entirely.
- **Fix:** Move the quota check inside the same `m_Mutex` lock that guards `m_RunIdToMeta` and `m_DiskUsageByUser`. Remove the early-exit `WouldExceedQuota` call before the lock, or re-check inside the lock before committing the new entry.

---

### HIGH Unbound JSON Input Size Allows Uncontrolled Memory / Disk Allocation

- **Location:** `Stage()`, `StageRequest::m_JcwfJson`, approximately line 95 (header) and line 310 (pre-check)
- **Issue:** There is no upper bound enforced on `req.m_JcwfJson.size()`. The quota pre-check uses `req.m_JcwfJson.size() * 2` as the estimated size, but this is only a pre-check and the multiplication can overflow if the string is near `SIZE_MAX / 2`. More practically, a caller can submit a multi-gigabyte JSON string; even if the quota check eventually fires, the server has already allocated the full string in memory and will pass it through `RewriteWorkflowId` (which calls `std::regex_replace` creating a second full copy) before the quota guard runs.
- **Impact:** An attacker with a valid MCP key can exhaust process heap and/or disk by submitting an arbitrarily large payload, causing a denial of service.
- **Fix:** Enforce a hard maximum size on `m_JcwfJson` at the REST handler level and also at the top of `Stage()` (e.g., reject anything over a few MB). Fix the multiplication overflow by checking `req.m_JcwfJson.size() > SIZE_MAX / 2` before doubling.

---

### HIGH `ReadMeta` Custom JSON Parser Does Not Unescape Values — Stored Injection / Truncation

- **Location:** `ReadMeta()`, approximately lines 295–320
- **Issue:** The `pluck` lambda finds the first `"` after the `:` and the next `"` after that. It does not handle JSON escape sequences (`\"`). A `user` or `role` value containing `\"` (which `JsonEscape` would have written as `\\\"`) will be truncated at the first escaped quote. Conversely, a legacy or hand-crafted `meta.json` whose values contain a literal `"` (impossible via `WriteMeta` but possible if the file is replaced externally) would cause `pluck` to return a substring of a different field, allowing one field's value to bleed into another — e.g., ownership attribution could be confused.
- **Impact:** If the `meta.json` file can be influenced (e.g., symlink attack, directory-listing exploit from another bug), an attacker could manipulate ownership attribution so that `GetRunInfo` returns a different user's identity, bypassing the ownership check in artifact-retrieval endpoints.
- **Fix:** Use a proper JSON parser for reading `meta.json`. The comment "fields are validated server-side so no escaping pass is needed" is incorrect reasoning — `ReadMeta` is the trust boundary for persisted data.

---

### MEDIUM `SanitizeUserSlug` Allows `@` and `.` — Permits Dot-Segment Directory Names

- **Location:** `SanitizeUserSlug()`, approximately line 215
- **Issue:** The allowed character set includes `.`, which means a user string of `..` or one whose slug after sanitisation starts with `.` (e.g., the user `..@evil.com` sanitises to `..@evil.com`, truncated to 64 bytes but still starting with `..`) will produce a slug that is a dot-segment when used as a directory component under `m_BasePath`. `std::filesystem::path / ".."` ascends out of `m_BasePath`.
- **Impact:** An attacker who can register an MCP key with a crafted username could cause run artifacts to be created (and reaped/deleted) in directories outside `_adhoc/`, potentially including the application root.
- **Fix:** After building the slug, additionally reject (or further sanitise) any slug that equals `.` or `..`, starts with `.`, or contains the sequence `..`. Or simply replace `.` with `_` in the allowed set, since slugs are only needed to be unique and filesystem-safe, not human-readable with dots.

---

### MEDIUM Reaper Reads and Deletes Files Outside the Mutex Lock — TOCTOU

- **Location:** `Reap()`, approximately lines 430–460
- **Issue:** `toDelete` is populated while holding `m_Mutex`, but the actual `ReadMeta`, `MeasureFolderBytes`, and `RemoveFolder` calls happen *after* releasing the lock. Between lock release and `RemoveFolder`, `OnRunCompleted` (or another `Stage` call) could have modified or removed the same folder, leading to double-free of the disk-usage counter, use of stale byte counts, or removal of a newly re-staged folder that happens to reuse the same path (unlikely but possible given the timestamp+counter naming).
- **Impact:** Disk-usage accounting can become negative (wrapping to a very large `size_t`), permanently blocking all further submissions for that user due to a phantom quota violation.
- **Fix:** Re-acquire the lock before updating `m_DiskUsageByUser` (already done) but also check that the entry still exists in `m_RunIdToMeta` before decrementing, and use the stored `m_DiskBytes` rather than a freshly measured value taken outside the lock.

---

### MEDIUM User Identity and Role Written Verbatim to Log in `Stage()`

- **Location:** `Stage()`, approximately line 380
- **Issue:** `LOG_APP_INFO("[adhoc] Staged runId=... user={} ...", ..., req.m_User, ...)` logs the raw MCP user string. Depending on the logging framework, a user string containing format specifiers or ANSI escape sequences could corrupt log lines or terminals. More critically, role and user values are written without any indication they are untrusted, so log-aggregation systems that parse these fields could misattribute log entries if a user string is crafted to resemble a log prefix (log injection).
- **Impact:** Log injection / log forgery; potential confusion in SIEM alert rules that key on the `user=` field.
- **Fix:** Log the already-sanitised `userSlug` instead of the raw `req.m_User`, or quote/bracket the value to make boundaries unambiguous.

---

### MEDIUM `on_completion` Disk-Usage Accounting Can Go Negative on Concurrent Completion

- **Location:** `OnRunCompleted()`, approximately lines 400–415
- **Issue:** `userTotal` is decremented twice in the `on_completion` branch: once to account for the difference between initial and final bytes (`userTotal - m_DiskBytes + finalBytes`), and then again by `finalBytes` when the folder is removed. If `m_DiskBytes` was zero or very small at staging time and `finalBytes` is large, the intermediate value assigned to `userTotal` before the second subtraction can underflow, and the second subtraction can then also underflow, leaving `m_DiskUsageByUser` at a very large `size_t` value for the user — blocking all future submissions.
- **Impact:** Denial of service against legitimate users; permanent quota lockout without admin intervention.
- **Fix:** Use saturating subtraction throughout: `userTotal = (userTotal >= amount) ? userTotal - amount : 0;` and sequence the two adjustments to avoid the double-decrement pattern.

---

### LOW `GenerateFolderName` Uses `fetch_add` on `m_Counter` Outside the Mutex

- **Location:** `GenerateFolderName()`, approximately line 250; called from `Stage()` inside the lock at approximately line 335
- **Issue:** `m_Counter` is `std::atomic<uint32_t>`, but the folder name is composed of both the timestamp (`Iso8601NowCompactUtc()`) and the counter in a single non-atomic operation. Two threads could observe the same timestamp but different counter values, or the same counter value but different timestamps (if the system clock ticks between the two calls). Because `Stage()` holds `m_Mutex` when calling `GenerateFolderName`, the combination is currently safe for the folder-name uniqueness guarantee, but the counter itself is atomic while the timestamp fetch is not protected — the guard is only incidental. If the lock were ever removed or the function called from outside the lock, name collisions become possible.
- **Impact:** Currently low risk due to the surrounding lock, but a future refactor could silently break the uniqueness guarantee, leading to two users' run data being mixed in the same folder, causing data leakage between users.
- **Fix:** Remove the `atomic` from `m_Counter` and make plain `uint32_t` access consistently guarded by `m_Mutex`, making the invariant explicit rather than accidental.


---

<a id="73-doc-application-workflow-aicallevents-h"></a>

### 74_doc_application_workflow_aiCallTaskExecutor_h

<a id="74-doc-application-workflow-aicalltaskexecutor-h"></a>

*Source doc:* `../../../queue/jarvisCppCyberSecAudit/74_doc_application_workflow_aiCallTaskExecutor_h/docu.output.md`



### CRITICAL Shell Injection via `ConvertWithMarkitdown`
- **Location:** `ConvertWithMarkitdown`, approximately line 280–320
- **Issue:** The function rejects a narrow set of shell metacharacters (`"`, `'`, `` ` ``, `$`) but the blocklist is far too short. Characters such as `;`, `|`, `&`, `(`, `)`, `\n`, `\r`, `>`, `<`, `!`, `{`, `}`, `~`, `#`, `%`, `^`, space-preceded arguments, and null bytes all pass through. The resulting command is `"markitdown \"" + rawPath + "\" 2>/dev/null"` executed via `popen`. Any caller-controlled component of `rawPath` that contains, e.g., a semicolon or pipe character achieves arbitrary shell command execution under the process's privileges.
- **Impact:** If an attacker can influence a file path that reaches this function (e.g., through a crafted `QueueFileRef.m_Path` sourced from a workflow definition or queue binding), they execute arbitrary OS commands as the engine process.
- **Fix:** Do not construct shell command strings at all. Use `posix_spawn`/`execve` (POSIX) or `CreateProcess` (Windows) with an explicit argument array so no shell is involved. If `popen` must be retained, accept only a strict allowlist of path characters (alphanumerics, `/`, `.`, `-`, `_`) and reject everything else, rather than a blocklist.

---

### CRITICAL Path Traversal in `WriteTextFile` and `MaterializeCntxFilesFromQueueBinding` / `MaterializeProbFilesFromQueueBinding`
- **Location:** `AiCallTaskExecutor::WriteTextFile` (~line 370); `MaterializeCntxFilesFromQueueBinding` (~line 440); `MaterializeProbFilesFromQueueBinding` (~line 510)
- **Issue:** `WriteTextFile` accepts an arbitrary `filePath` string and writes caller-supplied content to it after only calling `std::filesystem::create_directories` on the parent. There is no check that the resolved absolute path lies within an expected root (e.g., the task working directory). `MaterializeCntxFilesFromQueueBinding` and `MaterializeProbFilesFromQueueBinding` pass paths derived from `QueueFileRef.m_Path` (which originates from the workflow/queue binding, an external data source) directly to `WriteTextFile`. A path containing `../../` or an absolute path pointing outside the working directory writes arbitrary files anywhere the process has write permission.
- **Impact:** Arbitrary file write. An attacker who controls a workflow definition or queue binding writes files to any writable path: overwrite binaries, cron jobs, SSH authorized_keys, configuration files, etc.
- **Fix:** After computing the absolute normalized destination path, assert it has the task working directory (or a fixed allowed root) as a prefix before opening the file. Reject any path that escapes the intended root.

---

### CRITICAL Uncontrolled File Read via Path Traversal in `ReadTextFile`
- **Location:** `ReadTextFile` (~line 260); called from `MaterializeCntxFilesFromQueueBinding` (~line 445) and `MaterializeProbFilesFromQueueBinding` (~line 510)
- **Issue:** `sourcePath` is constructed from `fileRef.m_Path`, which comes from external input (queue binding / workflow definition). Although relative paths are resolved via `TaskPathResolver::ResolvePath`, absolute paths are only `lexically_normal()`-ized. There is no check that the resolved path is contained within an allowed directory before `ReadTextFile` opens and reads it. An absolute path such as `/etc/passwd` or `/proc/self/environ` passes through without restriction.
- **Impact:** An attacker who controls a `QueueFileRef.m_Path` value reads arbitrary files readable by the process and delivers their content to the AI backend or embeds it in output files.
- **Fix:** After normalization, verify the resolved `sourcePath` is a descendant of the task working directory (or a fixed allowed root). Reject and fail the task for any out-of-root source path.

---

### CRITICAL Uncontrolled Allocation / DoS via Unbounded File Read in `ReadTextFile`
- **Location:** `ReadTextFile` (~line 260)
- **Issue:** The entire file is read into memory with `textStream << inputStream.rdbuf()` without any size limit. A file path resolved from external input (e.g., `/dev/zero`, a very large file, or a named pipe that never ends) causes unbounded memory allocation, exhausting process memory.
- **Impact:** Denial of service; potential OOM crash of the engine process.
- **Fix:** After opening the file, check `std::filesystem::file_size` against a reasonable maximum (e.g., 512 MB or a configurable limit) and refuse to read files that exceed it.

---

### HIGH JSON Injection in Manually Constructed `sidecarJson`
- **Location:** `AiCallTaskExecutor::Execute`, provider sidecar construction (~line 700–740)
- **Issue:** The JSON string is built by direct string concatenation: `sidecarJson += "\"provider\":\"" + provForSidecar + "\""` and similarly for `resolvedUrl`, `resolvedApiType`, `effectiveModel`, and `temperatureOpt`. None of these values are JSON-escaped before insertion. If any value contains `"`, `\`, or control characters (sourced from config or workflow params), the resulting JSON is malformed or injectable. `temperatureOpt` comes directly from `TryExtractStringParam` of `taskDefinition.m_ParamsJson` and is inserted without validation: an attacker-controlled value like `1},{"injected":"yes` breaks out of the JSON object.
- **Impact:** Malformed or attacker-controlled sidecar JSON written to disk; replay tooling consuming this file may be exploited or confused; secondary injection into any system parsing the file.
- **Fix:** Use a proper JSON serialization library (simdjson for writing, or nlohmann/json) rather than manual string concatenation. At minimum, apply JSON string escaping to all string values before insertion.

---

### HIGH Secrets Leakage via Path Logging
- **Location:** `WriteInlineQueueFileRefs` (~line 235); `MaterializeCntxFilesFromQueueBinding` (~line 460); `MaterializeProbFilesFromQueueBinding` (~line 525); `AiCallTaskExecutor::Execute` (~line 610, 640, 680)
- **Issue:** Multiple `LOG_APP_INFO` calls emit full absolute file paths and file sizes. File paths can encode sensitive information (usernames, project names, security-sensitive filenames, internal directory structure). The `fileRef.m_Content.size()` log in `WriteInlineQueueFileRefs` leaks the size of potentially secret inline content. More critically, `TryBuildPromptFromParams` at ~line 820 logs `taskDefinition.m_ParamsJson` verbatim into the prompt fallback, which may contain API keys, credentials, or PII embedded in params.
- **Impact:** Log files accessible to operators or log aggregation infrastructure expose internal path structures, content sizes, and potentially credentials or PII stored in params JSON.
- **Fix:** Restrict path logging to basenames or relative paths in non-debug builds. Do not log raw params JSON that may contain secrets. Treat `m_Content` as sensitive data and log only sanitized metadata.

---

### HIGH `temperatureOpt` Injected Unvalidated as JSON Number
- **Location:** `AiCallTaskExecutor::Execute`, sidecar JSON construction (~line 735)
- **Issue:** `temperatureOpt` is extracted as a raw string from `taskDefinition.m_ParamsJson` via `TryExtractStringParam` and written directly into the sidecar JSON as `",\"temperature\":" + temperatureOpt.value()` without numeric validation or escaping. The value is treated as a JSON number literal but is never checked to be a valid number. Any string value (including one containing `},"malicious":true`) is written verbatim.
- **Impact:** JSON injection into the sidecar file; secondary impact on any consumer of that file.
- **Fix:** Parse the temperature value as a floating-point number and validate it is in the expected range (e.g., 0.0–2.0) before writing. Serialize it numerically, not as a raw string.

---

### HIGH TOCTOU Race in `WriteTextFile` Directory Creation and File Write
- **Location:** `AiCallTaskExecutor::WriteTextFile` (~line 390–415)
- **Issue:** The function calls `std::filesystem::exists`, then `std::filesystem::create_directories`, then opens the file for writing as three separate, non-atomic operations. Between the `exists` check and the `create_directories` call, and between `create_directories` and `std::ofstream` open, another process or thread could create, replace, or symlink the directory or file. The code also performs a second `exists` check after `create_directories` (logged for debug), compounding the TOCTOU window.
- **Impact:** In a concurrent or adversarial environment, a symlink substitution between directory creation and file open causes the engine to write file content to an attacker-controlled path. This combines with the path traversal findings above.
- **Fix:** Open the file with `O_CREAT | O_WRONLY` (using `openat` relative to a directory file descriptor opened for the task working directory) and rely on the kernel's atomic open to resolve the race. Avoid the separate directory-existence check.

---

### MEDIUM Glob Expansion Without Depth or Count Limit (DoS)
- **Location:** `ExpandCntxFileGlobs` (~line 300–360)
- **Issue:** `std::filesystem::directory_iterator` is used without recursion depth limit or match count limit. If `parentDir` contains a very large number of files, all matching entries are collected into `matches` (a `std::vector`) with no cap. A directory with millions of entries causes unbounded memory allocation and CPU consumption. The `parentDir` is derived from external input (`fileRef.m_Path`).
- **Impact:** Denial of service; an attacker who controls file paths or can create many files in a referenced directory causes the engine to stall or OOM.
- **Fix:** Impose a maximum match count (e.g., 10,000 files) and fail the task with a clear error if exceeded.

---

### MEDIUM Chunked Aggregator Callback Captures Raw Pointer `requestPool`
- **Location:** `AiCallTaskExecutor::Execute`, `ChunkAggregator` struct and `onReply` lambda (~line 940–1020)
- **Issue:** `ChunkAggregator::m_RequestPool` stores a raw non-owning pointer to `AiRequestPool`. The `onReply` lambda captures `aggregator` (a `shared_ptr`) which holds this raw pointer. If `AiRequestPool` is destroyed before all chunk callbacks fire (e.g., engine shutdown during a long-running chunked request), the callback dereferences a dangling pointer.
- **Impact:** Use-after-free; undefined behavior, potentially exploitable as a write-what-where primitive depending on heap layout at the time of the callback invocation.
- **Fix:** Store a `std::weak_ptr<AiRequestPool>` (or equivalent lifetime-safe handle) in the aggregator, and check validity before dereferencing inside the callback. Alternatively, ensure `AiRequestPool` outlives all pending callbacks via a `shared_ptr` ownership chain.

---

### MEDIUM Unbounded Memory Growth from Chunk Aggregator `m_Parts`
- **Location:** `AiCallTaskExecutor::Execute`, chunked dispatch (~line 950–980)
- **Issue:** `aggregator->m_Parts` is pre-allocated to `chunks.size()` strings and each `onReply` callback stores the full AI reply text in the corresponding slot. There is no limit on the size of an individual reply or the total memory consumed across all parts. A malicious or malfunctioning AI backend returning very large replies for each of N chunks allocates N × reply_size bytes in memory simultaneously before the reduce pass.
- **Impact:** Unbounded memory allocation; denial of service via OOM.
- **Fix:** Cap the maximum accepted reply size per chunk (e.g., reject replies over a configurable threshold) and/or cap the total number of chunks.

---

### LOW Error Messages Expose Internal File Paths
- **Location:** `ReadTextFile` (~line 262), `MaterializeCntxFilesFromQueueBinding` (~line 467), `MaterializeProbFilesFromQueueBinding` (~line 525), `AiCallTaskExecutor::WriteTextFile` (~line 405)
- **Issue:** Error strings returned via `outErrorMessage` and set on `taskState.m_LastErrorMessage` include full absolute filesystem paths (e.g., `"Failed to open file for reading: /home/user/project/..."`). These messages are likely surfaced to workflow clients or logged at warning level, leaking internal directory structure.
- **Impact:** Information disclosure; internal path structure revealed to callers who can read task state or error messages.
- **Fix:** Return only the filename component (not the full path) in user-facing error messages, or sanitize paths to be relative to the working directory before including them in errors.


---

### 77_doc_application_workflow_aiRequestPool_h

<a id="77-doc-application-workflow-airequestpool-h"></a>

*Source doc:* `../../../queue/jarvisCppCyberSecAudit/77_doc_application_workflow_aiRequestPool_h/docu.output.md`



### HIGH Path traversal via unvalidated file paths written to disk

- **Location:** `WriteTextFile`, anonymous namespace, ~line 100; called from `BuildCompletionOutputs` and the `curlCallback` lambda in `Submit`
- **Issue:** `filePath` (and `outputPath` values from `AiRequestCompletion::m_OutputValues`, `outputFilePaths`, `writtenOutputPath`) are written to the filesystem without any check that the resolved absolute path stays within an allowed base directory. `fs::absolute(...).lexically_normal()` expands the path but does not constrain it. An attacker who can control `outputFilePaths`, `outputSlotNames`, `queueFolder`, or `probName` fields in a submitted `AiInvocation` or workflow task registration can write arbitrary file content to any path the process can reach (e.g. `/etc/cron.d/backdoor`, SSH authorized_keys, startup scripts).
- **Impact:** Arbitrary file write as the process user, potentially leading to remote code execution or privilege escalation.
- **Fix:** After computing `filesystemPathAbsolute`, assert it is prefixed by a known, configured base directory (e.g. the workspace root from `Core::g_Core->GetLaunchCWDAbsolute()`). Reject and return false if the canonical path escapes that root. Apply the same check in `BuildCompletionOutputs` before calling `WriteTextFile`, and in the `curlCallback` lambda before constructing `writtenOutputPath`.

---

### HIGH Uncontrolled file read size in `OnOutputFileCreated` (uncontrolled allocation / DoS)

- **Location:** `AiRequestPool::OnOutputFileCreated`, ~line 390
- **Issue:** The file at `fullFilePath` is read entirely into memory with no size cap. `size` is obtained from `seekg/tellg` and cast directly to `std::size_t` before `resize`. A file of any size (including one placed by an adversarial external process or a misbehaving AI provider writing a multi-GB response) will cause an unchecked heap allocation.
- **Impact:** Process OOM crash (DoS); on 32-bit targets, integer truncation in the cast from `std::streamoff` to `std::size_t` could cause a heap buffer over-read on subsequent `inputStream.read`.
- **Fix:** Impose a reasonable upper bound (e.g. 256 MB) before the `resize` call and return a failure completion if the size exceeds it. Verify `size >= 0` before the cast.

---

### HIGH Schema-retry loop enables unbounded recursive `Submit` calls

- **Location:** `curlCallback` lambda inside `AiRequestPool::Submit`, schema-retry block, ~line 620
- **Issue:** When schema validation fails and `nextAttempt < maxAttempts`, the callback calls `Submit(retryEnvelope, callbackCopy)` recursively from within the curl I/O thread. `m_SchemaAttemptNumber` and `m_Retry.m_OutputSchemaMaxAttempts` come from the caller-supplied `AiInvocation`. If `m_OutputSchemaMaxAttempts` is large (or is set to `INT_MAX` by a malicious or misconfigured caller), and every attempt keeps failing validation, the recursion depth is bounded only by that field. Each recursive `Submit` call captures another copy of the lambda (which itself captures `envelopeForRetry`), growing stack and heap consumption linearly.
- **Impact:** Stack overflow or exhaustion of the curl dispatcher's thread resources; DoS against the workflow engine.
- **Fix:** Enforce a hard ceiling on `m_OutputSchemaMaxAttempts` at the point the `AiInvocation` is accepted (e.g. clamp to 5). Additionally, convert the retry logic to an iterative re-dispatch rather than a recursive lambda call.

---

### HIGH API key logged on error path via `key_name` field exposure; potential secret leakage in error messages

- **Location:** `AiRequestPool::Submit`, ~line 530; `OnRequestFailed` log at ~line 430; `curlCallback` exception handler ~line 720
- **Issue:** (a) The `curlCallback` exception handler logs `e.what()` verbatim, which for some curl or JSON parsing exceptions includes portions of the request body or URL that may contain the API key or model parameters. (b) `errorMessage` passed into `OnRequestFailed` originates from `curlResult.m_ErrorMessage` (HTTP transport errors) and from the exception `.what()` string, both of which may embed the full URL (which for some providers includes the key as a query parameter). These strings are stored in `PendingEntry::m_ErrorMessage` and propagated to `AiRequestCompletion::m_ErrorMessage`, which is surfaced to callers and potentially logged downstream.
- **Impact:** API keys or bearer tokens visible in log files, telemetry, or workflow completion records accessible to operators or attackers with log access.
- **Fix:** Sanitize error messages before storing or logging them: strip URL query strings containing `api_key` or `key` parameters. Do not log `e.what()` from parsing exceptions without scrubbing. Ensure `CurlWrapper` error messages do not embed the request URL or credentials.

---

### MEDIUM TOCTOU between `fs::exists` check and `fs::create_directories` / file open in `WriteTextFile`

- **Location:** `WriteTextFile`, anonymous namespace, ~line 110
- **Issue:** The code calls `fs::exists(parentAbsolute)` for logging, then `fs::create_directories(parent)`, then `std::ofstream(filePath)`. Between any of these steps another process or thread can create, remove, or replace the path or its components (including replacing a directory with a symlink). This is a classic TOCTOU. The `exists` check before `create_directories` is only used for logging, but the real risk is between `create_directories` and `ofstream`: a symlink planted at `parent` or `filePath` between these calls redirects the write.
- **Impact:** An attacker with local filesystem access (or a compromised subprocess) can redirect file writes to arbitrary paths by racing with a symlink swap, achieving the same impact as the path-traversal finding above.
- **Fix:** Open the file with `O_CREAT|O_WRONLY|O_NOFOLLOW` (POSIX) or use platform-equivalent flags that refuse to follow symlinks on the final path component. Validate the resolved path is within the expected directory after opening (compare the file descriptor's resolved path).

---

### MEDIUM `probName` stem used in filesystem paths without sanitization (path injection)

- **Location:** `AiRequestPool::Submit`, `curlCallback` lambda, ~line 570 and ~line 660; also `RegisterPendingWorkflowTask`, `OnOutputFileCreated`
- **Issue:** `fs::path(probName).stem()` is used to construct output file names (`.output.txt`, `.output.json`, `.transcript.json`, chunk files) directly from the caller-supplied `envelope.m_ProbName`. If `probName` contains `..` components, directory separators, or shell-special characters, `stem()` does not strip them fully on all platforms (`stem()` only removes the last extension; a name like `../../evil` yields stem `../../evil`). The resulting path is appended with `/` to `queueFolder` and written to disk.
- **Impact:** An envelope with a crafted `m_ProbName` can write output files outside the intended queue folder, intersecting with the path-traversal risk.
- **Fix:** Validate `probName` is a plain filename (no directory separators, no `..`) before use. Reject or sanitize envelopes where `fs::path(probName).filename() != fs::path(probName)`.

---

### MEDIUM `requestTimestampNs` used as part of request key with no entropy — predictable handle forgery

- **Location:** `AiRequestHandle`, header ~line 42; `AllocateRequestId`, ~line 250; `MakeKey`, ~line 270
- **Issue:** `requestTimestampNs` is set by the caller at construction time (not internally by the pool) and `requestId` is a monotonically incrementing integer starting at 1. Any caller that can observe the current timestamp and knows or can enumerate recent `requestId` values can construct a valid `AiRequestHandle` for another caller's pending request and call `TryConsumeResult`, `Forget`, or `WaitForCompletion` against it, consuming or canceling a request it did not own.
- **Impact:** One workflow task can consume or cancel the in-flight AI request of another task (within the same process), leading to incorrect workflow outputs or DoS of competing tasks.
- **Fix:** Generate `requestTimestampNs` internally within `AllocateRequestId` or `RegisterPending` (not from caller input), and add a cryptographically random nonce to the handle so it cannot be guessed. Alternatively, gate all pool operations on ownership verification (e.g. only the registering workflow run/task ID may consume).

---

### MEDIUM Unbounded `m_Completed` queue growth (uncontrolled allocation / DoS)

- **Location:** `AiRequestPool::QueueCompletionIfNeeded`, ~line 460; `TryPopCompletion`, ~line 490
- **Issue:** `m_Completed` is a `std::queue<AiRequestCompletion>` with no capacity limit. `AiRequestCompletion` holds `m_ResponseText` (the full AI response) and `m_OutputValues`. If the workflow runtime's `TryPopCompletion` consumer is slow or stopped (e.g. during a pause or crash loop), and many AI requests complete, completions accumulate unboundedly in this queue, each holding a potentially large response string in memory.
- **Impact:** Memory exhaustion DoS if the consumer falls behind under load or is intentionally delayed.
- **Fix:** Impose a maximum queue depth (e.g. 1 000 entries). When the limit is reached, either drop new completions with an error log or apply backpressure to `Submit`.

---

### LOW Integer overflow in `AllocateRequestId` wrap-around check

- **Location:** `AiRequestPool::AllocateRequestId`, ~line 255
- **Issue:** `m_NextRequestId` is `int64_t` initialized to 1 and incremented with `++`. The overflow guard checks `if (m_NextRequestId <= 0)` after the increment. For a signed 64-bit integer, overflow is undefined behavior in C++ before the check can execute. In practice `int64_t` overflow is extremely unlikely in operation, but the guard itself is unreachable via defined behavior.
- **Impact:** Negligible in practice (would require 2^63 allocations), but the guard provides false safety assurance.
- **Fix:** Check for overflow before incrementing: `if (m_NextRequestId == INT64_MAX) { m_NextRequestId = 1; } else { ++m_NextRequestId; }`.

---

### LOW `WaitForCompletion` can block the calling thread indefinitely despite timeout parameter

- **Location:** `AiRequestPool::WaitForCompletion`, ~line 510
- **Issue:** `condition_variable::wait_for` is used with `timeoutMs` milliseconds. However, if the system clock steps backward (NTP adjustment) or if spurious wakeups occur on some implementations while `m_IsCompleted` remains false, the wait loop does not re-arm.
