More critically, `wait_for` with a predicate is correct here for spurious wakeups, but the function does not check `m_ShuttingDown`, so a thread blocked in `WaitForCompletion` during `Shutdown()` will be unblocked only if the entry happens to be in the snapshot taken by `Shutdown` — if the entry was not yet registered when `Shutdown` ran, the calling thread waits until `timeoutMs` expires.
- **Impact:** Thread hangs up to `timeoutMs` during shutdown; if `timeoutMs` is large (or zero by mistake), this delays clean shutdown.
- **Fix:** Include `m_ShuttingDown.load()` in the `wait_for` predicate so blocked threads wake immediately on shutdown.


---

### 78_doc_application_workflow_aiTranscript_h

<a id="78-doc-application-workflow-aitranscript-h"></a>

*Source doc:* `../../../queue/jarvisCppCyberSecAudit/78_doc_application_workflow_aiTranscript_h/docu.output.md`



### MEDIUM Unvalidated raw JSON injected directly into transcript without sanitization

- **Location:** `AiTranscript::AppendResponse`, `AiReply::Kind::Structured` branch, approximately line 195 in the .cpp
- **Issue:** `reply.m_StructuredJson` is embedded verbatim into the output JSON string with no sanitization, escaping, or structural validation. Every other field goes through `jsonHelper.SanitizeForJson()`, but this branch does not. If the structured payload contains a prematurely closed object, extra keys, or embedded `]` characters it can corrupt the transcript array structure.
- **Impact:** A malicious or malformed provider response could inject arbitrary JSON into the transcript file, potentially causing downstream consumers that re-parse the transcript to process attacker-controlled structure (e.g., spoofed `kind`, `timestamp`, or `messages` entries in a fabricated request turn).
- **Fix:** Either (a) parse `m_StructuredJson` with a JSON library and re-serialize it to guarantee it is a single valid value before embedding, or (b) treat it as an opaque string and store it base64-encoded or as a JSON string value via `SanitizeForJson`. Do not embed it raw.

---

### MEDIUM TOCTOU / non-atomic read-modify-write on the transcript file

- **Location:** `AppendEntry`, approximately lines 130–160 in the .cpp
- **Issue:** The function reads the existing file with `ReadFile`, constructs a new string in memory, then overwrites the file with `WriteFile`. There is no file locking between the read and the write. If two threads or processes call `AppendRequest`/`AppendResponse` concurrently for the same `transcriptPath`, both will read the same stale content, one write will silently discard the other's entry, and the resulting JSON array may be structurally invalid.
- **Impact:** Under concurrent workflow execution, transcript entries are silently lost or the file is left with malformed JSON, which can hide audit evidence of AI invocations.
- **Fix:** Open the file with an exclusive advisory lock (e.g., `flock` on POSIX, `LockFileEx` on Windows) before reading and hold it through the write, or use an atomic rename (write to a temp file and rename). Alternatively, serialize all transcript writes through a single mutex-protected writer.

---

### MEDIUM Unbounded file read in `ReadFile` enables memory exhaustion

- **Location:** `ReadFile`, approximately line 105 in the .cpp
- **Issue:** `ReadFile` slurps the entire transcript file into a `std::string` via `buffer << input.rdbuf()` with no size cap. The resulting string is then copied again inside `AppendEntry`. An adversary who can grow the transcript file (e.g., by issuing many AI requests) or who can plant a large file at the expected path can cause the process to allocate an arbitrarily large amount of memory.
- **Impact:** Denial of service through memory exhaustion; on 32-bit builds, also potential allocation failure leading to undefined behavior or crash.
- **Fix:** Check `std::filesystem::file_size` before reading and reject files above a reasonable cap (e.g., 64 MB). Alternatively, seek to the end of the file, find the `]` delimiter, and only read and replace the tail rather than reading the whole file.

---

### MEDIUM Brittle hand-rolled JSON serialization risks injection if `SanitizeForJson` is incomplete

- **Location:** `AiTranscript::AppendRequest` and `AiTranscript::AppendResponse`, throughout both functions
- **Issue:** The code builds JSON by string concatenation and relies entirely on `JsonHelper::SanitizeForJson` for escaping. The implementation of `SanitizeForJson` is not visible in this file, so it is impossible to verify that it correctly handles all JSON string escape sequences (U+2028, U+2029, null bytes, multi-byte UTF-8 sequences containing `"` in continuation bytes, etc.). Any gap in that function causes JSON injection across all fields.
- **Impact:** If `SanitizeForJson` is incomplete, attacker-controlled content in model responses, message content, error messages, or model names can break out of JSON strings, inject keys, or terminate the array — corrupting all downstream consumers of the transcript.
- **Fix:** Replace hand-rolled JSON construction with a well-tested JSON library (e.g., nlohmann/json, RapidJSON) that handles serialization internally. This eliminates the entire class of injection risk.

---

### LOW Intermediate transcript content is not written atomically; partial writes leave corrupt files

- **Location:** `WriteFile`, approximately line 115 in the .cpp
- **Issue:** `WriteFile` opens the file with `std::ios::trunc` and then writes with `output << content`. If the process is killed, crashes, or the disk fills between the truncation and the completion of the write, the transcript file is left empty or partially written — permanently corrupting the audit record.
- **Impact:** Loss of transcript integrity; a crash during write loses all previously recorded entries because the file was truncated before the new content was fully written.
- **Fix:** Write to a sibling temporary file (e.g., `transcriptPath` + `.tmp`), flush and close it, then `std::filesystem::rename` (which is atomic on most filesystems) to replace the target.

---

### LOW Error message from provider written to transcript without redaction

- **Location:** `AiTranscript::AppendResponse`, `AiReply::Kind::Error` branch, approximately line 200 in the .cpp
- **Issue:** `reply.m_Error.m_Message` is serialized verbatim into the transcript. Provider error messages sometimes include request IDs, internal hostnames, partial request bodies, or (in misconfigured providers) fragments of API keys or authorization headers.
- **Impact:** Sensitive provider-side information is persisted to disk in the transcript file, potentially accessible to users or processes that should not see it.
- **Fix:** Log the full error message to a privileged internal log only. In the transcript, include only the error kind and HTTP status, or strip the message to a fixed maximum length after reviewing what providers actually return.


---

### 79_doc_application_workflow_dataflowResolver_h

<a id="79-doc-application-workflow-dataflowresolver-h"></a>

*Source doc:* `../../../queue/jarvisCppCyberSecAudit/79_doc_application_workflow_dataflowResolver_h/docu.output.md`



### MEDIUM Log injection via unvalidated task/input identifiers

- **Location:** `DataflowResolver::ResolveInputsForTask`, error-path log call around line 95; `DataflowResolver::TryResolveFromDataflowEdges`, log calls around lines 140–155
- **Issue:** `inputName`, `taskId`, `dataflowEdge.m_FromTask`, `dataflowEdge.m_ToTask`, `dataflowEdge.m_FromOutput`, and `dataflowEdge.m_ToInput` are all passed directly from workflow definition and run-context data structures into `LOG_APP_ERROR` format strings. If those strings contain newlines, ANSI escape sequences, or format specifiers and the logger does any printf-style substitution unsafely, an attacker who can supply a crafted workflow definition (e.g., via API, file upload, or deserialized JSON) can inject fake log lines, corrupt structured log records, or—if the logger is syslog-backed—forge entries in security-relevant log streams.
- **Impact:** Log forgery, audit-trail corruption, potential downstream SIEM or log-aggregation confusion; if the logging macro uses a vulnerable format-string path, further exploitation.
- **Fix:** Sanitize all externally-sourced strings before logging: replace or escape newlines (`\n`, `\r`), carriage returns, and any non-printable characters. Ensure the logging macro always treats the user-controlled data as a plain argument to a fixed format string (i.e., `LOG_APP_ERROR("... '{}'", sanitize(inputName), sanitize(taskId))`) and never allows user data to be the format string itself.

---

### MEDIUM Uncontrolled allocation / DoS via unbounded mapping injection

- **Location:** `DataflowResolver::ResolveInputsForTask`, Step 1b loop, approximately lines 105–115
- **Issue:** For every `DataflowEdge` targeting `taskId`, every key in `dataflowEdge.m_Mapping` is unconditionally inserted into `resolvedInputs.m_StringValues`. There is no cap on the number of edges, the number of mapping entries per edge, or the size of mapping keys/values. An attacker who can supply a workflow definition with a large number of dataflow edges or mapping entries (e.g., through a crafted JCWF document) can cause unbounded `std::unordered_map` growth and corresponding heap allocation.
- **Impact:** Memory exhaustion leading to denial of service of the workflow engine process.
- **Fix:** Enforce a maximum number of dataflow edges per task and a maximum number of mapping entries per edge at workflow-load/validation time (before `ResolveInputsForTask` is called). Additionally, enforce maximum string lengths on mapping keys and values. Reject workflow definitions that exceed these limits with a clear validation error.

---

### MEDIUM Secrets potentially leaked in error-log value emission

- **Location:** `DataflowResolver::ResolveInputsForTask`, template-expansion error log, approximately line 125
- **Issue:** When template expansion fails, the raw (pre-expansion) value `inputValuePair.second` is logged verbatim: `LOG_APP_ERROR("... value '{}'", inputValuePair.second)`. Resolved input values may contain secrets (API keys, tokens, passwords) sourced from `workflowRun.m_Context` or dataflow edges. Logging the raw value writes those secrets into whatever log sink is configured.
- **Impact:** Secret material (credentials, tokens) written to log files, log aggregation systems, or SIEM, where it may be accessible to operators or attackers who gain read access to logs.
- **Fix:** Do not log the value itself. Log only the input key name and task ID. If the value content is needed for debugging, gate it behind a compile-time or runtime debug/trace flag that is disabled in production, and ensure the log level for that path is not reachable in production builds.

---

### LOW Missing validation of template-expansion key scope (template injection boundary)

- **Location:** `DataflowResolver::ExpandTemplates` and the call site in `ResolveInputsForTask`, approximately lines 120–130 and 170–180
- **Issue:** `ExpandTemplates` passes the entire `resolvedInputs.m_StringValues` map as the context for template expansion. Values resolved from dataflow edges (including the Step 1b mapping injection) are themselves eligible to be both template sources and template targets in the same expansion pass. If a resolved value contains `{{inputs.someOtherKey}}` and that other key in turn contains a template reference, or if an attacker can craft values that reference keys they should not have access to within the same task's input set, cross-input value leakage or unexpected substitution can occur. The strictness of `TemplateMode::Strict` is not verified in this file.
- **Impact:** An attacker controlling one input value may be able to read the content of another input value (e.g., a secret injected via a different dataflow edge) through template expansion, if the template engine resolves `{{inputs.secret_key}}` from within a crafted value.
- **Fix:** Validate that no externally-supplied (non-literal) value contains template syntax before expansion, or expand only values that originate from literal/default fields. Ensure `TemplateMode::Strict` causes the template engine to reject references to keys not explicitly whitelisted, and confirm that behavior in the `TemplateEngine` implementation. Document the intended trust boundary clearly.


---

### 80_doc_application_workflow_filter_filterEngine_h

<a id="80-doc-application-workflow-filter-filterengine-h"></a>

*Source doc:* `../../../queue/jarvisCppCyberSecAudit/80_doc_application_workflow_filter_filterEngine_h/docu.output.md`



### HIGH Path Traversal via Unsanitized Caller-Controlled Path

- **Location:** `FilterEngine::Evaluate`, approximately lines 100–120 (csv and text_lines branches); also `FilterEngine::EvaluateQuery`, approximately line 270
- **Issue:** `filter.m_Source.m_Path` (and `source.m_IndexPath`) are concatenated with `workflowBaseDir` using `std::filesystem::path` join, but no check is performed to ensure the resulting canonical path remains inside `workflowBaseDir`. A workflow definition containing `m_Path = "../../etc/passwd"` or an absolute path like `/etc/shadow` will be accepted and opened. The absolute-path check (`is_relative()`) only skips the join; it does not reject the path.
- **Impact:** An attacker who can supply or modify a workflow definition (YAML/JSON config) can read arbitrary files accessible to the process, including secrets, credentials, and system files.
- **Fix:** After resolving the path, call `std::filesystem::canonical()` (or `weakly_canonical()` if the file may not yet exist) and assert that the result starts with `std::filesystem::canonical(workflowBaseDir)`. Reject any path that escapes the base directory. Reject absolute paths in `m_Path` entirely if user-supplied configs are untrusted.

---

### HIGH Uncontrolled Memory Allocation / DoS via Unbounded File Read

- **Location:** `FilterEngine::EvaluateCsv` (~line 195), `FilterEngine::EvaluateTextLines` (~line 240), `FilterEngine::EvaluateQuery` (~line 290)
- **Issue:** Each evaluator reads an entire file line by line and accumulates `FilterItem` objects in a `std::vector`. The `m_MaxItems` guard is checked only after a successful line parse, and it is allowed to be 0 (meaning unlimited). There is no cap on individual line length, no cap on the number of columns extracted into `item.m_Values`, no cap on the total bytes accumulated, and no size limit on the file itself. A crafted input file with many millions of lines, or a single extremely long line (e.g., 1 GB), will exhaust process memory.
- **Impact:** A local attacker (or a remote attacker who can influence the referenced file path via SSRF or symlink) can cause the process to consume all available RAM, crashing or degrading the workflow engine (DoS).
- **Fix:** Enforce a hard maximum on individual line length (e.g., 1 MB), on total items (even when `m_MaxItems` is 0, apply a compile-time or configuration-driven global cap), and on total accumulated bytes. Log and return an error when any limit is exceeded.

---

### HIGH `doc_path` Field From Untrusted Index Data Overwrites `m_SourcePath` Without Validation

- **Location:** `FilterEngine::EvaluateQuery`, approximately lines 330–334
- **Issue:** The value of the `doc_path` field is read directly from the index file and written into `item.m_SourcePath` and `item.m_Values["doc_path"]` without any sanitization. If downstream code uses `m_SourcePath` to open or stat a file (e.g., for freshness checking), an attacker who controls index file content can inject arbitrary paths, including paths outside the workflow directory.
- **Impact:** Depending on how `m_SourcePath` is consumed by the runtime, this enables path traversal, SSRF to local file URIs, or symlink attacks during freshness checks.
- **Fix:** Apply the same canonical-path containment check to `doc_path` values as recommended for `m_Source.m_Path` above. Reject values that are absolute or that escape `workflowBaseDir`.

---

### MEDIUM Secrets Potentially Leaked in Error Messages Stored in `errorMessage`

- **Location:** `FilterEngine::EvaluatePolarionQuery`, approximately lines 350–370; `FilterEngine::EvaluateCsv` (~line 165); `FilterEngine::EvaluateQuery` (~line 275)
- **Issue:** Error messages include the fully resolved filesystem path (`resolvedPath`, `indexPath`) and the connection name (`filter.m_Source.m_Connection`). The `errorMessage` string is returned to the caller with no documented trust boundary—if the caller forwards it to an HTTP response, a log shipped to an external SIEM, or a user-visible UI, it discloses internal directory structure and connection identifiers. The Polarion branch also copies `conn->m_KeyName` into `resolvedFilter.m_Source.m_KeyName`; if that structure is later serialized or logged, the key name is exposed.
- **Impact:** Information disclosure: internal paths, secret key names, and connection identifiers reach unauthorized parties.
- **Fix:** Use opaque error codes or user-safe messages in `errorMessage`. Log full detail (including paths) only to internal, access-controlled logs at DEBUG level. Never forward raw `errorMessage` content to end-users or external systems without scrubbing.

---

### MEDIUM Log Injection via Unsanitized `filter.m_Id` and `resolvedPath`

- **Location:** `FilterEngine::EvaluateCsv` (~line 215, `LOG_APP_INFO`), `FilterEngine::EvaluateTextLines` (~line 255, `LOG_APP_INFO`), `FilterEngine::EvaluateQuery` (~line 345, `LOG_APP_INFO`); also `LOG_CORE_WARN` calls in all three functions
- **Issue:** `filter.m_Id`, `resolvedPath`, and `items.size()` are interpolated directly into log messages via what appears to be a fmt-style logger. `filter.m_Id` is caller-supplied from workflow config and is never sanitized. If the log sink renders ANSI escape sequences or the log is parsed by a SIEM with structured-log injection vulnerabilities, a crafted `m_Id` containing newlines or escape sequences can forge log entries.
- **Impact:** Log injection; an attacker who controls workflow definitions can forge audit-log entries, hide malicious activity, or exploit SIEM parsers.
- **Fix:** Sanitize `filter.m_Id` before logging: strip or escape newline characters (`\n`, `\r`), null bytes, and ANSI escape sequences. Apply the same to any user-controlled string written to logs.

---

### MEDIUM TOCTOU Between Path Resolution and File Open

- **Location:** `FilterEngine::Evaluate` (~lines 100–110), `FilterEngine::EvaluateQuery` (~line 270)
- **Issue:** The code constructs `resolvedPath` using `std::filesystem::path` string operations, then passes it to `std::ifstream`. Between path resolution and `ifstream` open, a symlink can be created or swapped (classic TOCTOU). There is no use of `O_NOFOLLOW` or equivalent. On Linux, an attacker with write access to any directory component can redirect the open to an arbitrary file.
- **Impact:** Combined with relaxed filesystem permissions, this allows reading files outside the intended directory.
- **Fix:** Where supported, open files using a file descriptor obtained with `O_NOFOLLOW | O_CLOEXEC` via POSIX `open(2)`, and verify the resulting canonical path after opening. At minimum, resolve via `std::filesystem::canonical()` immediately before the open and re-verify after.

---

### LOW Denial of Service via Malformed Range String Producing SIZE_MAX Loop Bound

- **Location:** `FilterEngine::ParseRange`, approximately lines 380–430; consumed in `FilterEngine::EvaluateCsv` (~line 168)
- **Issue:** A range string of the form `"5-"` correctly sets `outEnd = SIZE_MAX`. While the reading loop breaks on `EOF`, an extremely large file paired with `SIZE_MAX` as the upper bound means the loop reads every byte before stopping. This is by design, but combined with the unbounded allocation issue above, a range like `"1-"` on a huge file amplifies the DoS impact. Additionally, there is no check that `rangeStart` is less than `SIZE_MAX`.
- **Impact:** Amplifies the unbounded allocation DoS when the file is large.
- **Fix:** Cap `outEnd` at a configurable maximum row count (e.g., 10,000,000) rather than `SIZE_MAX`. Validate that `outStart < outEnd` even when `outEnd` is the sentinel value.

---

### LOW Missing Column Count Validation Allows Arbitrary Keys in `m_Values`

- **Location:** `FilterEngine::EvaluateCsv`, approximately lines 200–210
- **Issue:** Every column from every CSV row is inserted into `item.m_Values` using either a positional key (`col_N`) or the header name. There is no cap on the number of columns, and header names are taken verbatim from the first line without sanitization. A CSV file with thousands of columns or header names containing template injection sequences (e.g., `{{...}}`) will insert those keys into `m_Values`, which are later injected into task instances.
- **Impact:** Memory exhaustion per item (DoS); if downstream task rendering performs template substitution on `m_Values` keys or values without escaping, a crafted header name is a template injection vector.
- **Fix:** Enforce a maximum column count (e.g., 512). Validate header names against an allowlist pattern (alphanumeric plus underscore). Strip or reject header names that contain template delimiters.


---

### 81_doc_application_workflow_filter_filterManifest_h

<a id="81-doc-application-workflow-filter-filtermanifest-h"></a>

*Source doc:* `../../../queue/jarvisCppCyberSecAudit/81_doc_application_workflow_filter_filterManifest_h/docu.output.md`



### HIGH Path Traversal via filterId in ManifestPath
- **Location:** `FilterManifestManager::ManifestPath`, and callers `WriteManifest` / `ReadManifest`
- **Issue:** `filterId` (and `workflowBaseDir`) are concatenated directly into a filesystem path with no validation. A caller-controlled `filterId` containing `../` sequences (e.g., `../../etc/cron.d/evil`) causes the constructed path to escape the intended workflow directory. `WriteManifest` will then create directories and write attacker-controlled JSON content to that arbitrary location. `ReadManifest` will read from it.
- **Impact:** Arbitrary file write (with JSON-structured content) to any path writable by the process. Combined with predictable target paths this can achieve code execution (e.g., writing cron jobs, SSH authorized_keys, or overwriting application config files). Arbitrary file read for sensitive files.
- **Fix:** After constructing the path in `ManifestPath`, verify that `std::filesystem::weakly_canonical(result)` starts with `std::filesystem::weakly_canonical(workflowBaseDir)`. Also validate `filterId` against an allowlist (alphanumeric plus hyphen/underscore, no path separators or dots) before use.

---

### HIGH Uncontrolled Memory Allocation / DoS in ReadManifest
- **Location:** `FilterManifestManager::ReadManifest`, item-reading loop (~line 195)
- **Issue:** The manifest file is read entirely into memory (`oss << file.rdbuf()`) and then every element of the `items` JSON array is pushed into `manifestOut.m_Items` without any size cap. There is no check against `m_ItemCount` or any upper bound. A crafted manifest with tens of millions of items will exhaust heap memory.
- **Impact:** An attacker who can write or replace a manifest file (possible via the path traversal above, or by compromising the filesystem) can cause the process to exhaust memory and crash (DoS).
- **Fix:** Before the item loop, read `m_ItemCount` from the JSON and refuse to proceed if it exceeds a reasonable limit (e.g., 1,000,000). Also add a file-size check before reading the entire file into memory.

---

### MEDIUM Weak Fallback Hash (std::hash) Accepted as Security-Relevant Digest
- **Location:** `FilterManifestManager::ComputeQueryHash`, `#else` branch (~line 270)
- **Issue:** When OpenSSL is unavailable, `std::hash<std::string>` is used as the query hash stored in `m_QueryHash`. `std::hash` is not collision-resistant, is implementation-defined, and on most platforms is trivially reversible or predictable. The hash is used in `CompareManifests` to decide whether to treat all cached items as stale — an attacker who can influence filter definitions could craft a collision to suppress cache invalidation.
- **Impact:** An attacker who can control or predict filter definition contents can construct a modified filter definition whose `std::hash` matches the original, causing stale or malicious cached data to be used without re-evaluation.
- **Fix:** Remove the `std::hash` fallback entirely and make the build fail if OpenSSL is absent. Alternatively, bundle a minimal SHA-256 implementation (e.g., a single-header library) so the secure path is always taken.

---

### MEDIUM JSON Injection via Incomplete JsonEscape (Control Characters)
- **Location:** `FilterManifestManager::JsonEscape` (~line 310) and `WriteManifest` where it is called
- **Issue:** `JsonEscape` handles `"`, `\`, `\n`, `\r`, `\t` but does not escape other control characters (U+0000–U+001F, U+007F). Per RFC 8259 §7, all characters in that range must be escaped as `\uXXXX` inside JSON strings. If any `FilterItem` field (key, source_path, etc.) contains a NUL byte or other raw control character, the written file will be invalid JSON that may be parsed differently by different consumers, and injected content could confuse the simdjson parser on re-read or a downstream tool.
- **Impact:** Malformed JSON written to disk; potential for downstream mis-parsing if control characters appear in attacker-influenced field values (e.g., `m_Key` sourced from a file index).
- **Fix:** In `JsonEscape`, add a default-case check: if `static_cast<unsigned char>(c) < 0x20`, emit `\u00XX` using `std::ostringstream` with `std::hex`.

---

### MEDIUM TOCTOU Race Between exists() Check and open() in ReadManifest
- **Location:** `FilterManifestManager::ReadManifest` (~line 155–165)
- **Issue:** The code calls `std::filesystem::exists(path)` and then separately opens the file with `std::ifstream`. Between these two operations the file can be replaced (e.g., with a symlink pointing to a sensitive file) by another process or a concurrent workflow run.
- **Impact:** On a multi-tenant or multi-process system an attacker with local filesystem access can replace the manifest file between the existence check and the open to cause the engine to read a file it otherwise would not, leaking its contents into `manifestOut` fields which may be logged or returned to callers.
- **Fix:** Remove the `exists()` check. Open the file directly; if `!file.is_open()` after the open, treat that as "file not found" (inspect `errno`/error state). This collapses the two operations into one and eliminates the race.

---

### LOW Secrets / Sensitive Paths Logged in WriteManifest
- **Location:** `FilterManifestManager::WriteManifest`, `LOG_APP_INFO` call (~line 185)
- **Issue:** The full filesystem path to the manifest file is written to the application log. If `workflowBaseDir` or `filterId` contains sensitive directory names, API key fragments, or internal infrastructure topology, this information is exposed in log files that may be forwarded to external SIEM systems or accessible to lower-privileged users.
- **Impact:** Information disclosure of internal path structure, potentially aiding further attacks.
- **Fix:** Log only the `filterId` and item count; omit the full path, or log it only at DEBUG level behind a verbose-logging flag.


---

### 82_doc_application_workflow_filter_polarionClient_h

<a id="82-doc-application-workflow-filter-polarionclient-h"></a>

*Source doc:* `../../../queue/jarvisCppCyberSecAudit/82_doc_application_workflow_filter_polarionClient_h/docu.output.md`



### [HIGH] SSRF — Unvalidated URL passed directly to curl

- **Location:** `HttpGet`, `HttpRequest`, `HttpDownloadFile`, `HttpUploadFile` (all four curl-performing functions); called from `BuildPageUrl` which concatenates `source.m_BaseUrl` directly.
- **Issue:** `source.m_BaseUrl`, `source.m_ProjectId`, `source.m_Query`, and every other string that feeds into the constructed URL are read from filter configuration with no validation of scheme, host, port, or path prefix. The URL is passed verbatim to `CURLOPT_URL`. An attacker who controls filter configuration (e.g., via a crafted workflow definition loaded from disk or a remote source) can direct requests to internal services (`http://169.254.169.254/`, `file:///etc/passwd`, `gopher://`, `dict://`, etc.). `CURLOPT_FOLLOWLOCATION` is enabled in all four functions, compounding this by allowing redirects to arbitrary internal targets.
- **Impact:** Full SSRF: exfiltration of cloud metadata credentials, probing of internal RFC-1918 addresses, file-system reads via `file://` scheme, potential request smuggling via other curl-supported protocols.
- **Fix:** (1) Validate `m_BaseUrl` against an allowlist of trusted scheme+host combinations before any HTTP call. (2) Explicitly restrict curl protocols: `curl_easy_setopt(curl, CURLOPT_PROTOCOLS, CURLPROTO_HTTPS)` (or `CURLPROTO_HTTPS | CURLPROTO_HTTP` if HTTP is required). (3) Set `CURLOPT_REDIR_PROTOCOLS` to the same restricted set. (4) Disable `CURLOPT_FOLLOWLOCATION` or enforce a maximum redirect count of 0 for API calls.

---

### [HIGH] Path Traversal — Unvalidated `outputPath` and `filter.m_Id` written to filesystem

- **Location:** `HttpDownloadFile` (~line where `std::fopen(outputPath.c_str(), "wb")` is called); `WriteItemFile` (~line constructing `filePath`); `FetchAll` (~line constructing `outDir`).
- **Issue:** `outputPath` passed to `HttpDownloadFile` (and propagated from `DownloadAttachment`'s caller) is written directly to `fopen` with no canonicalization or containment check. `filter.m_Id` is concatenated into directory and file paths in `WriteItemFile` and `FetchAll` without validation; a value like `../../etc/cron.d/evil` would resolve outside the intended `workflowBaseDir`.
- **Impact:** Arbitrary file overwrite anywhere the process has write permission, enabling configuration corruption, cron-job injection, or overwriting executables.
- **Fix:** After constructing each path, call `std::filesystem::weakly_canonical()` and assert the result starts with the expected base directory. Validate `filter.m_Id` against a strict allowlist (e.g., `[A-Za-z0-9_-]+`) before use in path construction. For `outputPath`, require callers to pass a pre-validated path or perform the same canonical-prefix check.

---

### [HIGH] Bearer Token Leaked in Error Messages

- **Location:** `HttpGet` (~line building `errorMessage` on HTTP ≥ 400, returning `responseBody`); `HttpRequest` (same pattern); `FetchAll` (~`LOG_APP_WARN` call logging `errorMessage`).
- **Issue:** When the server returns HTTP 4xx/5xx, the raw `responseBody` (up to 500 bytes) is appended to `errorMessage`. Some Polarion error responses echo back request metadata including Authorization headers or token fragments. Additionally, `errorMessage` is then logged via `LOG_APP_WARN` in `FetchAll`, so any such content reaches the log sink. Separately, the `bearerToken` itself is concatenated into `authHeader` as a plain `std::string`, which will persist in memory until the string destructor runs; there is no zeroing.
- **Impact:** Bearer tokens (PATs) may appear in log files accessible to other users or log-aggregation systems. Logs are often retained longer than session lifetimes, extending the exposure window.
- **Fix:** Strip or truncate `responseBody` to non-sensitive status information before appending to `errorMessage`. Log only HTTP status codes, not body content, at warn level. Zero sensitive strings (e.g., `authHeader`) explicitly after `curl_easy_perform` using `OPENSSL_cleanse` or `explicit_bzero`.

---

### [HIGH] TLS Peer Verification Conditionally Absent

- **Location:** `HttpGet`, `HttpRequest`, `HttpDownloadFile`, `HttpUploadFile` (all curl setup blocks).
- **Issue:** `CURLOPT_SSL_VERIFYPEER` and `CURLOPT_SSL_VERIFYHOST` are never explicitly set. The CA bundle is only applied when `CurlWrapper::GetCaBundlePath()` returns a non-empty string: `if (!caBundle.empty()) { curl_easy_setopt(curl, CURLOPT_CAINFO, ...) }`. If `GetCaBundlePath()` returns an empty string (e.g., on a minimal container image where no system CA bundle exists), curl falls back to its compiled-in default, which may itself be absent or stale. There is no explicit enforcement that peer verification is on and failing-safe. Without `CURLOPT_SSL_VERIFYPEER=1` being set unconditionally, a missing CA bundle silently downgrades to unverified TLS on some libcurl builds.
- **Impact:** Man-in-the-middle interception of Bearer tokens and all API payloads in environments without a discoverable CA bundle.
- **Fix:** Unconditionally set `curl_easy_setopt(curl, CURLOPT_SSL_VERIFYPEER, 1L)` and `curl_easy_setopt(curl, CURLOPT_SSL_VERIFYHOST, 2L)` in every curl setup block. If `caBundle` is empty, fail the request rather than proceeding without a verified trust store.

---

### [MEDIUM] Unbounded Memory Growth in curl Write Callbacks

- **Location:** `writeCallback` lambda in `HttpGet` (~line `buf->append(...)`), `HttpRequest`, and `HttpUploadFile`.
- **Issue:** The write callback appends all bytes received from the server into a `std::string` with no size cap. A malicious or malfunctioning Polarion server can stream an arbitrarily large response, causing unbounded heap growth until the process is OOM-killed or the allocation throws `std::bad_alloc`.
- **Impact:** Denial of service against the workflow engine process; potential for exhausting system memory shared with other tenants.
- **Fix:** Add a size guard inside the callback: if `buf->size() + totalSize > MAX_RESPONSE_BYTES`, return 0 (which causes curl to abort with `CURLE_WRITE_ERROR`). Choose `MAX_RESPONSE_BYTES` appropriate to expected Polarion page sizes (e.g., 32 MB).

---

### [MEDIUM] Unvalidated `fields` Values Injected into URL Without Encoding

- **Location:** `BuildPageUrl` (~lines building `fieldsCsv`).
- **Issue:** `source.m_Fields` entries are concatenated with commas into `fieldsCsv` and then passed through `UrlEncode`, which only encodes the *entire* CSV string. However, individual field names are not validated before concatenation. A field name containing a `&` or `=` followed by a URL parameter (e.g., `foo&page[number]=1`) will be encoded as a unit, but if `UrlEncode` is bypassed or if the field names reach other consumers (e.g., log output, downstream parsers) unencoded, injection is possible. More concretely, field names containing characters meaningful within a CSV field separator context (`,`) can alter which Polarion fields are requested, potentially exposing additional sensitive data.
- **Impact:** Parameter manipulation leading to disclosure of unrequested work-item fields, or altering query semantics.
- **Fix:** Validate each entry in `m_Fields` against a strict allowlist (alphanumeric plus `_`) before use. Apply `UrlEncode` to each individual field name, not the assembled CSV.

---

### [MEDIUM] Incomplete JSON Escaping in `WriteItemFile` — Control Character Injection

- **Location:** `WriteItemFile` (~lines in the `for (char c : value)` loop).
- **Issue:** The manual JSON serializer handles `"`, `\`, `\n`, `\r`, `\t` but does not escape other ASCII control characters (U+0000–U+001F excluding those listed). JSON RFC 8259 §7 requires all control characters to be escaped. A Polarion work-item field value containing, for example, a NUL byte or form-feed character will be written verbatim into the JSON file, producing invalid JSON that may be mis-parsed by downstream consumers, potentially causing injection when the file is later processed by another tool.
- **Impact:** Malformed JSON files that downstream parsers may interpret unexpectedly; potential for log injection or content injection in tools that consume these files.
- **Fix:** Add a `default` branch that checks `static_cast<unsigned char>(c) < 0x20` and emits `\uXXXX` escapes for those code points. Replace the hand-rolled serializer with a well-tested JSON library (already available: simdjson writes, or nlohmann/json).

---

### [LOW] `UrlEncode` Silently Falls Back to Unencoded Value on `curl_free` Failure

- **Location:** `UrlEncode` (~line `std::string result = escaped ? escaped : value`).
- **Issue:** If `curl_easy_escape` succeeds in allocating the handle but returns a null pointer for `escaped` (documented as possible on allocation failure), the function returns the original unencoded `value`. This means URL parameters are silently inserted raw into request URLs, potentially breaking or altering query semantics.
- **Impact:** Malformed URLs sent to Polarion; in edge cases, unencoded special characters in `m_Query` could reach the server in ways that alter query logic.
- **Fix:** Treat a null `escaped` return as an error: log a warning and return an empty string (or throw), rather than falling back to the raw value. The caller should then propagate the error rather than sending a malformed URL.

---

### [LOW] `filter.m_Id` and Field Keys Written Unescaped as JSON Keys in `WriteItemFile`

- **Location:** `WriteItemFile` (~line `file << " \"" << key << "\": \""`).
- **Issue:** `key` (sourced from `item.m_Values` map keys, which are Polarion field names extracted from server JSON) is written directly into the JSON output without escaping. If a key contains `"` or `\`, the output JSON is structurally invalid. Keys are also extracted from the simdjson `unescaped_key()` return in `ParseJsonApiPage`, meaning Unicode and backslash sequences in field names flow through unescaped into the key position.
- **Impact:** Malformed JSON files; potential for a crafted server response to inject content into downstream JSON parsing.
- **Fix:** Apply the same escaping logic to keys as to values in `WriteItemFile`, or use a proper JSON serialization library.


---

### 83_doc_application_workflow_filter_queryParser_h

<a id="83-doc-application-workflow-filter-queryparser-h"></a>

*Source doc:* `../../../queue/jarvisCppCyberSecAudit/83_doc_application_workflow_filter_queryParser_h/docu.output.md`



### MEDIUM Unbounded token-vector growth enables memory exhaustion (DoS)
- **Location:** `QueryParser::Tokenize`, quoted-string branch (~line 220) and word-token branch (~line 250)
- **Issue:** There is no limit on the number of tokens accumulated in `tokens`, no limit on the length of any individual token's `m_Text`, and no limit on the total length of a quoted string. A caller supplying a multi-megabyte query string causes the tokenizer to allocate an arbitrarily large `std::vector<Token>` and arbitrarily large `std::string` values inside each token before the parser ever runs.
- **Impact:** Any code path that calls `QueryParser::Parse` with attacker-controlled input (e.g., a search query from an HTTP request) can exhaust process heap memory, causing a denial of service.
- **Fix:** Enforce a maximum query-string byte length at the top of `Parse` (e.g., 4 KB) and a maximum token count inside `Tokenize` (e.g., 512 tokens), returning an error immediately when either limit is exceeded.

---

### MEDIUM Deeply nested queries cause unbounded recursion (stack exhaustion / DoS)
- **Location:** `ParseOrExpr` → `ParseAndExpr` → `ParseUnaryExpr` → `ParseOrExpr` (grouped expression branch, ~line 370)
- **Issue:** Parenthesised sub-expressions recurse through `ParseOrExpr` without any depth counter. A query consisting entirely of nested parentheses, e.g. `(((((…)))))`, drives the call stack as deep as there are tokens. There is no recursion-depth guard.
- **Impact:** An attacker supplying a deeply nested query string can overflow the C++ call stack, crashing the process.
- **Fix:** Pass a `size_t depth` parameter through all recursive parse functions and return an error (e.g., "query nesting too deep") when depth exceeds a safe threshold (e.g., 64).

---

### MEDIUM Error messages reflect raw user input without sanitisation (log/response injection)
- **Location:** `ParseFieldExpr` (~lines 410–430), `ParseRangeExpr` (~lines 460–490), `Parse` (~line 305), and multiple other sites that write `state.Current().m_Text` or `field` into `errorMessage`
- **Issue:** Token text drawn directly from the untrusted query string is interpolated into `errorMessage` strings without any escaping or length truncation. If `errorMessage` is subsequently written to a log file or returned in an HTTP response body, it enables log injection (newline characters in token text inject fake log lines) and, depending on the response context, header injection or reflected content injection.
- **Impact:** An attacker can inject arbitrary content into log files or HTTP responses by embedding control characters or CRLF sequences in the query string.
- **Fix:** Before interpolating any token text into an error message, strip or escape non-printable characters and newlines, and truncate to a reasonable length (e.g., 64 characters).

---

### LOW Inconsistent range-bracket matching allows mismatched open/close delimiters
- **Location:** `ParseFieldExpr` (~lines 430–440) and `ParseRangeExpr` (~lines 450–495)
- **Issue:** The opening delimiter (`[` vs `{`) is recorded via the `loInclusive` parameter, but `ParseRangeExpr` independently accepts either `]` or `}` as the closing delimiter regardless of which opening bracket was used. A query such as `field:[a TO b}` is accepted and produces a node where `m_RangeLoInclusive=true` and `m_RangeHiInclusive=false`, which may not match the intended grammar semantics. While this is primarily a correctness issue, in a security-relevant filtering context (e.g., access-control queries) a user could craft ranges whose boundary inclusion behaviour differs from what an administrator intended, potentially bypassing range-based access restrictions.
- **Impact:** In contexts where range queries gate access to data, an attacker could exploit the permissive bracket matching to craft queries that include or exclude values contrary to policy intent.
- **Fix:** Pass the opening bracket type into `ParseRangeExpr` and enforce that the closing bracket matches the expected type (`[` must close with `]`, `{` must close with `}`), returning an error otherwise.

---

### LOW Numeric comparison silently uses floating-point equality for non-numeric strings that parse as doubles
- **Location:** `QueryParser::CompareValues` (~line 510)
- **Issue:** `std::stod` accepts strings like `"1e308"`, `"inf"`, `"nan"`, and very long numeric strings. NaN comparisons with `<` and `>` both return false, so `CompareValues` returns 0 (equal) for any pair where either operand is NaN. A document value or range bound containing `"nan"` will therefore match every inclusive range boundary check, producing incorrect (and potentially security-relevant) filter results.
- **Impact:** In a security-relevant document-filtering context, a field value of `"nan"` satisfies every numeric range predicate, potentially bypassing range-based access-control filters.
- **Fix:** After calling `std::stod`, explicitly check `std::isnan(da) || std::isnan(db)` and fall through to lexicographic comparison in that case. Also consider rejecting `"inf"` / `"-inf"` unless they are intentionally supported range sentinels.


---

### 84_doc_application_workflow_internalTaskExecutor_h

<a id="84-doc-application-workflow-internaltaskexecutor-h"></a>

*Source doc:* `../../../queue/jarvisCppCyberSecAudit/84_doc_application_workflow_internalTaskExecutor_h/docu.output.md`



### HIGH Path Traversal via Unvalidated Task-Scoped Paths

- **Location:** `InternalTaskExecutor::Execute`, calls to `TaskPathResolver::ResolveTaskScopedPath` (lines ~230–250 in the .cpp)
- **Issue:** `inputPathString` and `outputPathString` values come directly from `taskDefinition.m_FileInputs` and `taskDefinition.m_FileOutputs`, which originate from workflow definition files or external input. They are passed to `ResolveTaskScopedPath` without any validation that the resolved canonical path stays within `taskWorkingDirectoryPath`. A value such as `../../etc/passwd` or an absolute path could resolve outside the intended sandbox.
- **Impact:** An attacker who controls a workflow definition can read arbitrary files on the host (via input path traversal) or write arbitrary files (via output path traversal), potentially overwriting binaries, config files, or credentials.
- **Fix:** After resolving each path with `ResolveTaskScopedPath`, call `std::filesystem::weakly_canonical` on both the resolved path and `taskWorkingDirectoryPath`, then assert that the canonical resolved path begins with the canonical working directory path. Reject any path that does not. Example:
  ```
  auto canonical_base = std::filesystem::weakly_canonical(taskWorkingDirectoryPath);
  auto canonical_resolved = std::filesystem::weakly_canonical(resolved);
  auto [b, e] = std::mismatch(canonical_base.begin(), canonical_base.end(), canonical_resolved.begin());
  if (b != canonical_base.end()) { /* reject */ }
  ```

---

### HIGH Unrestricted Action Dispatch / Authorization Bypass

- **Location:** `InternalTaskExecutor::Execute`, ~line 270; `TryGetActionFromParamsJson`, ~line 80
- **Issue:** The `action` string extracted from the JSON `params` field is used directly to look up and instantiate a registered task via `registry->CreateTask(action)`. There is no allowlist check, no caller-identity check, and no validation beyond "is it a non-empty string." Any workflow definition that can set `params.action` to any registered action name gets that action executed unconditionally.
- **Impact:** If a lower-privileged workflow or an externally supplied workflow definition is processed by the same executor as privileged internal tasks, an attacker can invoke privileged actions (e.g., file deletion, secret access, network calls) simply by naming them in the `action` field.
- **Fix:** Maintain an explicit allowlist of actions permitted per workflow trust level, validated before `CreateTask` is called. If trust levels are not yet modeled, at minimum document and enforce that `paramsJson` must only come from trusted, validated workflow definitions, and add an assertion or registry-level ACL.

---

### MEDIUM Sensitive File Paths Logged at INFO Level

- **Location:** `InternalTaskExecutor::Execute`, multiple `LOG_APP_INFO` calls (~lines 195–265); `EnsureOutputParentDirectories`, ~lines 155–175
- **Issue:** Absolute resolved input and output file paths, workflow IDs, run IDs, task IDs, working directory paths, and workflow file paths are all emitted at `LOG_APP_INFO`. Log files are often shipped to aggregation systems, accessible to ops staff, or inadvertently exposed. Paths can reveal internal directory structure, user names, secret store locations, or data file names.
- **Impact:** Information disclosure; an attacker or insider with log access learns the full filesystem layout of the workflow engine, aiding further exploitation (path traversal, privilege escalation targeting known paths).
- **Fix:** Demote detailed absolute-path logging to `LOG_APP_DEBUG` (only enabled in non-production builds). In production, log only relative paths or opaque task/run identifiers. Remove or gate the `[paths debug]` and `[folder creation debug]` log calls behind a compile-time or runtime debug flag.

---

### MEDIUM Error Messages Reflect Internal File System Paths to Callers

- **Location:** `ValidateExistingInputs`, ~line 130; `InternalTaskExecutor::Execute`, ~lines 240, 255, 285, 295
- **Issue:** `taskState.m_LastErrorMessage` is set to strings that include absolute filesystem paths (e.g., `"Missing required input file: " + inputPath.string()`, `"Failed to create internal task working directory: " + taskWorkingDirectoryPath.string()`). If `taskState` is serialized and returned to API callers or stored in externally visible workflow run records, these paths are disclosed.
- **Impact:** Filesystem layout disclosure, which reduces the cost of targeted path traversal or local privilege-escalation attempts.
- **Fix:** In error messages stored in `taskState.m_LastErrorMessage`, use relative paths or opaque identifiers only. Log the full path internally (at DEBUG level), but do not surface it to the caller. Example: `"Missing required input file: " + inputPath.filename().string()` or a task-relative path only.

---

### LOW Uncontrolled Directory Creation (DoS via Disk Exhaustion)

- **Location:** `EnsureOutputParentDirectories`, ~line 145; `InternalTaskExecutor::Execute`, ~line 210 (`create_directories` for working directory)
- **Issue:** `std::filesystem::create_directories` is called for every output path's parent and for the task working directory, based entirely on attacker-controllable path strings from the workflow definition. There is no limit on depth, no quota check, and no restriction on the number of directories created. Path traversal is not blocked (see first finding), so deeply nested paths or paths across different volumes could be created.
- **Impact:** A malicious workflow definition can cause unbounded directory tree creation, leading to disk exhaustion (DoS) on the host.
- **Fix:** After path traversal validation (fix for finding #1), additionally enforce a maximum path depth relative to the working directory and a maximum number of output paths per task. Reject inputs that exceed these limits before any filesystem operation.


---

### 85_doc_application_workflow_jcwfContainer_h

<a id="85-doc-application-workflow-jcwfcontainer-h"></a>

*Source doc:* `../../../queue/jarvisCppCyberSecAudit/85_doc_application_workflow_jcwfContainer_h/docu.output.md`



### [CRITICAL] Path Traversal via Zip Entry Filenames in Extract()

- **Location:** `JcwfContainer::Extract`, the loop starting around line 115, specifically `std::filesystem::path const destPath = targetDir / filename;`
- **Issue:** The filename read from each zip entry (`filename[1024]`) is used directly to construct the destination path with `targetDir / filename`. There is no check that the resolved `destPath` is actually under `targetDir`. A malicious `.jcwf` file can contain entries with names like `../../etc/cron.d/evil` or absolute paths (e.g. `/etc/passwd`), causing extraction to write arbitrary files anywhere the process has write permission. This is the classic "zip slip" vulnerability.
- **Impact:** Arbitrary file write as the process user. On a server, this can lead to remote code execution (overwrite init scripts, cron jobs, application configs, Python `.pyc` files loaded by the embedded interpreter, etc.).
- **Fix:** After constructing `destPath`, canonicalize both `targetDir` and `destPath` and assert the latter starts with the former before creating directories or extracting:

    std::filesystem::path const canonical_target = std::filesystem::weakly_canonical(targetDir);
    std::filesystem::path const canonical_dest   = std::filesystem::weakly_canonical(destPath);
    if (canonical_dest.string().rfind(canonical_target.string(), 0) != 0)
    {
        mz_zip_reader_end(&zip);
        errorMessage = "Zip entry escapes target directory: " + std::string(filename);
        return false;
    }

---

### [HIGH] Fixed-Size Buffer for Zip Entry Filenames (Potential Truncation Leading to Path Confusion)

- **Location:** `JcwfContainer::Extract` ~line 113 and `JcwfContainer::ListEntries` ~line 228, `char filename[1024]`
- **Issue:** `mz_zip_reader_get_filename` writes into a 1024-byte stack buffer. The ZIP specification allows entry names up to 65,535 bytes. If a zip entry name is longer than 1023 bytes, it will be silently truncated. The truncated name is then used to construct a destination path. This can bypass path traversal checks (if added) by crafting a name that is benign when truncated but malicious at full length, or cause extraction to a path the author did not intend.
- **Impact:** Path confusion, potential bypass of any filename validation, extraction to unintended locations.
- **Fix:** Use `mz_zip_reader_get_filename` with a `nullptr`/`0` argument first to obtain the required size, allocate a `std::string` of that size, then call again. Alternatively use `mz_zip_reader_stat` which populates `mz_zip_archive_file_stat::m_filename` with a known-length field, and validate the length before use.

---

### [HIGH] ReadFile() Accepts Caller-Supplied internalPath Without Validation

- **Location:** `JcwfContainer::ReadFile`, ~line 193, `mz_zip_reader_extract_file_to_heap(&zip, internalPath.c_str(), &size, 0)`
- **Issue:** `internalPath` is passed directly from the caller into `mz_zip_reader_extract_file_to_heap` without any sanitization. If the underlying miniz implementation resolves relative components inside the archive name (e.g. `../../global.json`), or if the caller passes a path derived from untrusted input (e.g. a webhook or API request specifying which sub-workflow file to load), an attacker may be able to reach unintended entries. More concretely, no check prevents `internalPath` from containing `..` sequences or null bytes.
- **Impact:** Unauthorized read of arbitrary zip entries; if combined with other logic, SSRF-equivalent information disclosure within the workflow engine.
- **Fix:** Validate that `internalPath` contains no `..` components and no leading slash before passing it to miniz. A simple check:

    if (internalPath.find("..") != std::string::npos || (!internalPath.empty() && internalPath[0] == '/'))
    {
        errorMessage = "Invalid internal path";
        return false;
    }

---

### [MEDIUM] TOCTOU: IsExtractedStale() Result Used as a Security Gate

- **Location:** `JcwfContainer::IsExtractedStale`, ~line 253
- **Issue:** The function compares filesystem modification times of the zip and the extracted directory. Callers that use this to decide whether to re-extract (i.e., "if stale, extract fresh") are susceptible to a TOCTOU race: the zip file can be swapped between the staleness check and the subsequent `Extract()` call. Additionally, mtime comparison alone is not a reliable integrity check—an attacker who can set file timestamps can make a tampered zip appear fresh.
- **Impact:** A local or concurrent attacker can substitute a malicious `.jcwf` after the staleness check passes but before extraction, causing extraction of attacker-controlled content.
- **Fix:** Do not rely on mtime for integrity. After extraction, verify a cryptographic digest (SHA-256) of the zip against a stored trusted hash. If staleness checking is needed for caching only (non-security), document clearly that it must never be used as a security gate.

---

### [MEDIUM] Uncontrolled Memory Allocation in ReadFile() via Heap Extract

- **Location:** `JcwfContainer::ReadFile`, ~line 197, `mz_zip_reader_extract_file_to_heap`
- **Issue:** miniz reads the compressed entry's declared uncompressed size from the zip central directory and allocates that many bytes on the heap before decompressing. A malicious zip can declare an uncompressed size of, say, 4 GB for a 1 KB compressed entry (a "zip bomb"). There is no size cap before allocation occurs. The result is assigned to a `std::string` without any limit check.
- **Impact:** Process memory exhaustion / OOM crash; denial of service against the workflow engine.
- **Fix:** Use `mz_zip_reader_stat` to retrieve `m_uncomp_size` before calling the heap extractor. Reject entries above a reasonable maximum (e.g. 64 MB for JSON workflow files):

    mz_zip_archive_file_stat stat{};
    if (mz_zip_reader_file_stat(&zip, index, &stat) && stat.m_uncomp_size > MAX_ALLOWED_SIZE)
    {
        errorMessage = "Entry too large";
        return false;
    }

The same concern applies to `Extract()` for every extracted file.

---

### [MEDIUM] Pack() Follows Symlinks and Can Read Files Outside sourceDir

- **Location:** `JcwfContainer::Pack`, ~line 158, `std::filesystem::recursive_directory_iterator`
- **Issue:** `recursive_directory_iterator` follows symbolic links by default. If `sourceDir` contains a symlink pointing outside the directory (e.g. to `/etc/shadow`), `mz_zip_writer_add_file` will read and archive the target file's contents. An attacker who can place a symlink in a directory that gets packed gains an arbitrary file read.
- **Impact:** Sensitive files outside `sourceDir` silently included in the packed workflow archive, potentially exfiltrated when the archive is distributed or uploaded.
- **Fix:** Pass `std::filesystem::directory_options::skip_permission_denied` and additionally check each entry with `std::filesystem::is_symlink`; skip or error on symlinks. Alternatively use `follow_directory_symlink` only if explicitly required, and validate the real path of each file is under `sourceDir` before adding.

---

### [LOW] Zip Entry Filenames Logged Without Sanitization

- **Location:** `JcwfContainer::Extract`, ~line 143, `LOG_APP_INFO("[JcwfContainer] extracted ...")` and error message at ~line 138, `"Failed to extract '" + std::string(filename) + "'"`
- **Issue:** `filename` comes directly from the zip archive (attacker-controlled) and is interpolated into both log output and the `errorMessage` string returned to callers. Depending on the log sink and any downstream display of `errorMessage`, this can enable log injection (newline/ANSI escape injection into log files or terminals) or, if `errorMessage` is ever rendered in a UI, reflected content injection.
- **Impact:** Log forging; potential downstream injection if errorMessage is displayed in a web UI or passed to another interpreter.
- **Fix:** Sanitize or escape `filename` before logging: strip or replace control characters (`\r`, `\n`, ESC) and non-printable bytes.


---

### 86_doc_application_workflow_pythonTaskExecutor_h

<a id="86-doc-application-workflow-pythontaskexecutor-h"></a>

*Source doc:* `../../../queue/jarvisCppCyberSecAudit/86_doc_application_workflow_pythonTaskExecutor_h/docu.output.md`



### HIGH Path Traversal via Unvalidated File Inputs

- **Location:** `ValidateFileInputsExist`, `PythonTaskExecutor::Execute` (approximately lines 50–70 and 175–185)
- **Issue:** `taskDefinition.m_FileInputs` values are passed directly to `TaskPathResolver::ResolvePath` and then used to construct `fs::path` objects and `contextValues["_file_input_N"]` entries without any check that the resolved path stays within `taskWorkingDirectoryPath` or `workflowBaseDirectoryPath`. An attacker-controlled workflow definition (or any path that reaches `m_FileInputs`) can supply `../../etc/passwd` or an absolute path like `/etc/shadow`, which `lexically_normal` will not reject. The resolved absolute path is then handed to the embedded Python engine as a callable argument.
- **Impact:** Arbitrary file read (and potentially write if Python code opens the injected path). An attacker who can author or modify a workflow definition can exfiltrate files outside the workflow sandbox.
- **Fix:** After resolving to an absolute path, assert that the canonical path starts with the canonical `taskWorkingDirectoryPath` (or an explicitly approved base directory). Reject and fail the task if the check fails. Use `fs::weakly_canonical` on both paths before the prefix comparison.

---

### HIGH Path Traversal via Unvalidated File Outputs

- **Location:** `PythonTaskExecutor::Execute` (approximately lines 210–240)
- **Issue:** `taskDefinition.m_FileOutputs` values follow the same pattern: they are resolved with `TaskPathResolver::ResolveTaskScopedPath` and the resulting absolute path string is inserted into `callArguments` (keyed only by a heuristic `outputName.find("Path")` check) without verifying the resolved path is inside the task sandbox. An absolute or `..`-relative output path is accepted silently.
- **Impact:** A crafted workflow can direct Python code to overwrite arbitrary files the process has write access to, including other workflow outputs, configuration files, or executable binaries.
- **Fix:** Same sandbox-prefix check as for file inputs, applied after `ResolveTaskScopedPath`. Fail the task on violation.

---

### HIGH Unvalidated Python Module/Function Name Injected Into Interpreter

- **Location:** `PythonTaskExecutor::Execute` (approximately line 195, call to `pythonEnginePool->ExecuteWorkflowTask`)
- **Issue:** `taskDefinition.m_ParamsJson` (module and function name) is passed to `ExecuteWorkflowTask` without any sanitization visible in this file. The code explicitly logs a warning when `m_ParamsJson` is empty but still proceeds to call `ExecuteWorkflowTask`. If the downstream Python engine constructs an import or `eval`/`exec` call from this value, an attacker-controlled workflow can inject arbitrary Python module paths (including relative imports resolving to attacker-controlled `.py` files on disk).
- **Impact:** Remote code execution within the Python interpreter's privilege level.
- **Fix:** Validate `m_ParamsJson` against a strict allowlist of known module/function identifiers before passing to `ExecuteWorkflowTask`. At minimum, reject empty values with a hard failure rather than a warning-and-continue.

---

### MEDIUM Secrets and Sensitive Context Values Exposed via Debug Logging

- **Location:** `PythonTaskExecutor::Execute` (approximately lines 100–130 and 250–270)
- **Issue:** `workflowRun.m_Context` values are copied wholesale into `contextValues` and the full `callArguments` map (which includes `taskState.m_InputValues`) is iterated and logged by path-detection heuristics. If any context key or input value contains API keys, tokens, or other secrets, they will appear in the application log. The log lines are marked `[paths debug] debug` suggesting they were intended as temporary diagnostics.
- **Impact:** Secrets stored in workflow context or task inputs leak into log files. Anyone with log access (operators, SIEM pipelines, log aggregators) can harvest credentials.
- **Fix:** Remove or gate the verbose `[paths debug] debug` log statements behind a compile-time or runtime debug flag that is off in production builds. Never log `contextValues` or `callArguments` values, only keys when strictly necessary.

---

### MEDIUM Unbounded Allocation from Workflow Context and Python Outputs

- **Location:** `PythonTaskExecutor::Execute` (approximately lines 155–165 and 290–300)
- **Issue:** `contextValues` is pre-reserved to `workflowRun.m_Context.size() + 4` and then populated without any size cap. `pythonOutputs` returned by `ExecuteWorkflowTask` is merged into `taskState.m_OutputValues` with no bound on the number of keys or the size of individual values. Both maps accept attacker-controlled data from the workflow definition or Python execution results.
- **Impact:** A malicious workflow with a very large context or a Python function that returns enormous output values can exhaust process memory, causing a denial of service.
- **Fix:** Enforce a maximum number of context entries and a maximum byte length per value before inserting into `contextValues`. Apply the same limits to `pythonOutputs` before merging into `taskState.m_OutputValues`.

---

### MEDIUM TOCTOU on Task Working Directory Creation

- **Location:** `PythonTaskExecutor::Execute` (approximately lines 135–155)
- **Issue:** `fs::exists` is called (storing the result in `existedBefore`), then `fs::create_directories` is called, then `fs::exists` is called again. The existence checks and directory creation are not atomic. On a shared filesystem an attacker (or another process) could replace the target directory with a symlink between the first exists check and `create_directories`, redirecting subsequent file writes to an arbitrary location.
- **Impact:** File writes (stdout.txt, stderr.txt, Python outputs) land in an attacker-chosen directory, enabling overwrite of sensitive files or privilege escalation depending on process permissions.
- **Fix:** Remove the pre/post existence checks used only for the `created` diagnostic log. Open and write output files with `O_NOFOLLOW` (or the Windows equivalent) to avoid following symlinks, and verify the final path is still inside the expected sandbox after creation.

---

### MEDIUM Python stdout/stderr Written to Attacker-Influenced Path Without Size Limit

- **Location:** `PythonTaskExecutor::Execute` (approximately lines 300–325)
- **Issue:** The full `capturedStdout` and `capturedStderr` strings are written to `taskWorkingDirectoryPath / "stdout.txt"` and `"stderr.txt"` without any size cap. Only the in-memory tooltip copy is truncated to 1024 bytes. If the Python task produces unbounded output, this writes an arbitrarily large file.
- **Impact:** Disk exhaustion denial of service. On a shared host this can starve other services or fill the root filesystem.
- **Fix:** Apply a maximum file size cap (e.g., configurable, defaulting to a few MB) to the data written to stdout.txt and stderr.txt. Log a warning if truncation occurs.

---

### LOW Heuristic Output Argument Injection Based on Key Name Substring Match

- **Location:** `PythonTaskExecutor::Execute` (approximately lines 215–230)
- **Issue:** The condition `outputName.find("Path") == std::string::npos` is used to decide whether to inject a file output path into `callArguments`. This is a substring heuristic, not a declared binding. An output slot named `"BypassPath"` or `"SomethingPathological"` will silently receive the output file path, and a legitimate path slot named `"outputpath"` (lowercase) will be missed. No validation of the injected path against the sandbox occurs before insertion.
- **Impact:** Logic error leading to unintended path injection into Python function arguments; combined with the path traversal issues above, increases attack surface.
- **Fix:** Use an explicit, schema-declared mapping between output slot names and their associated file paths rather than name-substring heuristics.


---

### 87_doc_application_workflow_scriptCatalog_h

<a id="87-doc-application-workflow-scriptcatalog-h"></a>

*Source doc:* `../../../queue/jarvisCppCyberSecAudit/87_doc_application_workflow_scriptCatalog_h/docu.output.md`



### HIGH Path Traversal via Unsanitized Relative Path Construction

- **Location:** `ScriptCatalog::ParseFile`, approximately lines 140–155 (`.cpp`)
- **Issue:** `entry.m_Path` is computed by calling `std::filesystem::relative(absolutePath, rootDir.parent_path())` and stored verbatim as a `std::string`. No check is performed that the result stays inside the intended `scripts/` subtree. A symlink inside the scanned directory, or an OS-level race between the `recursive_directory_iterator` walk and path resolution, can yield a relative path such as `../../etc/passwd` or an absolute path if `relative()` fails to produce a contained result. Downstream callers described in the comment ("same string the scripts/ security boundary check expects") trust this stored value — if that boundary check is done elsewhere and uses this pre-computed string rather than re-canonicalizing, the catalog itself becomes the bypass vector.
- **Impact:** An attacker who can place a symlink in the `scripts/` directory (or influence its contents) can inject catalog entries whose `m_Path` points outside the scripts root. Any consumer that shells out using `m_Path` directly, serves the file, or executes it based on catalog membership will operate on an unintended file.
- **Fix:** After computing `rel`, verify it does not begin with `..` and that `std::filesystem::weakly_canonical(rootDir / rel)` is still a prefix-descendant of `weakly_canonical(rootDir)`. Reject and skip entries that fail this check. Also resolve symlinks on `absolutePath` before storing or processing it (`std::filesystem::canonical`).

---

### HIGH Symlink Following During Directory Scan Enables Path Traversal / Arbitrary File Read

- **Location:** `ScriptCatalog::Refresh`, `std::filesystem::recursive_directory_iterator` call, approximately line 200 (`.cpp`)
- **Issue:** `recursive_directory_iterator` is invoked without `std::filesystem::directory_options::skip_permission_denied` or any symlink-follow restriction. By default it follows symlinks. An attacker who can write a symlink into `scripts/` pointing to an arbitrary file (e.g., `/etc/shadow`, a private key file) with a `.sh`, `.py`, or `.ps1` extension will cause `ParseFile` to open and partially read that file via `std::ifstream is(absolutePath)`.
- **Impact:** Up to `kMaxMetadataLines` (60) lines of any readable file on the filesystem are parsed. While metadata extraction is limited, the file is opened and iterated; if any parsed field (e.g., `m_Short`, `m_Description`) ends up logged or returned via the API, content leaks externally.
- **Fix:** Use `std::filesystem::directory_options::follow_directory_symlink` only intentionally; for security-sensitive scans, either disallow symlinks entirely (check `is_symlink()` on the directory entry before processing) or resolve to canonical paths and verify containment before opening.

---

### MEDIUM Uncontrolled Allocation / DoS via Unbounded `m_Entries` Growth

- **Location:** `ScriptCatalog::Refresh`, loop body, approximately line 200–215 (`.cpp`)
- **Issue:** There is no upper bound on the number of entries added to `m_Entries`. An attacker or misconfiguration that plants a large number of `.sh`/`.py`/`.ps1` files (or symlinks to directories containing many such files) in the scanned tree will cause unbounded heap allocation. Each `Entry` holds several `std::string` fields populated from file content, compounding memory use.
- **Impact:** Memory exhaustion leading to denial of service of the workflow engine process.
- **Fix:** Enforce a hard cap (e.g., 10 000 entries) inside the loop; log a warning and break when exceeded.

---

### MEDIUM Metadata Field Content Injected into Structured Log Without Sanitization

- **Location:** `ScriptCatalog::Refresh`, `LOG_APP_INFO` call, approximately line 220 (`.cpp`)
- **Issue:** `m_RootDirectory.string()` is interpolated directly into a structured log message. If the path contains format-string metacharacters relevant to the logging framework (e.g., `{}` for fmtlib-style loggers), the behavior depends on how `LOG_APP_INFO` is implemented. More concretely, if any parsed metadata field were ever added to this or similar log lines, attacker-controlled script metadata would flow into logs unescaped.
- **Impact:** Log injection or, if the logger uses printf-style formatting and the macro expands unsafely, format-string exploitation. In the current call the direct risk is limited to the path value, but the pattern establishes a dangerous precedent for future log statements that include metadata fields.
- **Fix:** Ensure all externally-sourced strings passed to log macros are treated as data arguments, never as format strings. Audit `LOG_APP_INFO` and sibling macros to confirm they use a compile-time format string with typed arguments (as fmtlib does), and never pass runtime strings as the format argument.

---

### LOW TOCTOU Between `IsExecutable` Stat and File Open in `ParseFile`

- **Location:** `ScriptCatalog::ParseFile`, `IsExecutable` call then `std::ifstream is(absolutePath)`, approximately lines 125–165 (`.cpp`)
- **Issue:** `IsExecutable` calls `std::filesystem::status` and then `ParseFile` separately opens the file. Between the two operations the file can be replaced (classic TOCTOU). The `m_Executable` flag stored in the entry will reflect the state of the file at stat time, not at open time.
- **Impact:** If downstream execution decisions rely on `m_Executable` being accurate, an attacker with filesystem write access in the scripts directory could swap in a different file after the stat but before execution is gated on the catalog flag. In practice the impact depends heavily on how `m_Executable` is consumed.
- **Fix:** Open the file first (obtain a file descriptor), then `fstat` the open descriptor to set `m_Executable`, eliminating the race window.


---

### 88_doc_application_workflow_shellTaskExecutor_h

<a id="88-doc-application-workflow-shelltaskexecutor-h"></a>

*Source doc:* `../../../queue/jarvisCppCyberSecAudit/88_doc_application_workflow_shellTaskExecutor_h/docu.output.md`



### CRITICAL — Shell injection via `JoinArgumentsForSystem` / `popen` command construction

- **Location:** `JoinArgumentsForSystem` (anonymous namespace, ~line 230) and `ExecuteCommandWithCapturedOutput` (~line 280), called from `ShellTaskExecutor::Execute`
- **Issue:** `JoinArgumentsForSystem` only quotes arguments that contain whitespace. Arguments without whitespace (e.g., `foo;rm -rf /`, `$(evil)`, `` `evil` ``) are passed to the shell unquoted. The resulting string is then fed directly to `popen()` / `_popen()` which invokes `/bin/sh -c`. Even if `IsSafeArgument` blocks a subset of dangerous characters, the argument validation runs *before* template expansion records the final value — but more critically, the command path (`commandPath`) itself is placed as `argumentList[0]` and unconditionally joined without shell-quoting, regardless of content. Any argument that passes `IsSafeArgument` but contains characters like `$`, `(`, `)`, `!`, `{`, `}`, `~`, `\`, `%`, `#`, `@` can still trigger shell interpretation.
- **Impact:** If any template-expanded argument contains unfiltered user-controlled data and passes the incomplete blocklist in `IsSafeArgument`, an attacker can execute arbitrary shell commands in the context of the process.
- **Fix:** Never build a shell command string for `popen`. On POSIX, use `fork`+`execvp` directly (as is already done in `ExecuteCommandWithWatchdog`; extend that path to all cases). Apply `QuoteForPosixShell` to **every** argument, not only those containing whitespace. On Windows, use `CreateProcess` rather than `_popen`. Remove `JoinArgumentsForSystem` entirely or restrict it to non-shell exec paths.

---

### CRITICAL — Hardcoded network port exposed to child processes

- **Location:** `ExecuteCommandWithWatchdog`, child process block, ~line 490
- **Issue:** `setenv("JARVIS_PORT", "8080", 1)` unconditionally exports a fixed port number into every child process. Any script or binary the workflow invokes can read `JARVIS_PORT` and make unauthenticated requests to whatever service listens there (presumably the internal heartbeat/REST API). The port is not configurable and not secret, making the internal API trivially discoverable by any spawned process.
- **Impact:** A malicious or compromised workflow script can contact the internal API on the hardcoded port and perform actions (heartbeat manipulation, task state mutation, or further exploitation of the internal API surface) without any credential.
- **Fix:** Read the port from a runtime configuration value, not a compile-time constant. If the internal API requires authentication, pass a per-task single-use token rather than only the port, and revoke it after the task completes.

---

### HIGH — `IsSafeArgument` blocklist is incomplete and applied post-expansion

- **Location:** `ShellTaskExecutor::IsSafeArgument` (~line 640) and its call site in `Execute` (~line 810)
- **Issue:** The function blocks `;`, `&`, `|`, `>`, `<`, `'`, `"`, and `` ` `` but permits `$`, `(`, `)`, `\`, `!`, newline-via-`\n` escape sequences embedded in multi-byte sequences, and null bytes that may terminate C-string processing early. It is applied to the post-expansion value, meaning the template engine may produce a value containing permitted characters that still form a shell injection when concatenated (e.g., `$(cat /etc/passwd)`). The function returns `true` for that string.
- **Impact:** Bypass of the intended safety gate; shell injection as described in finding 1.
- **Fix:** Adopt an allowlist (`[A-Za-z0-9._/=:@,-]` only) instead of a denylist. Better still, eliminate shell interpolation entirely by using `execvp`.

---

### HIGH — TOCTOU race on stderr temp file path

- **Location:** `ExecuteCommandWithCapturedOutput`, `stderrTmpPath` construction and use, ~line 295–340
- **Issue:** `stderrTmpPath` is fixed as `workingDirectoryPath / ".ja_stderr_tmp"`. Multiple concurrent shell tasks sharing the same working directory will collide on this file. There is no locking between the `OpenPipe` call and the subsequent `std::filesystem::exists` + `std::ifstream` read + `std::filesystem::remove` sequence. A second task could truncate or replace the file between the pipe closing and the read.
- **Impact:** stderr output from one task is silently replaced by another's; a local attacker (or malicious script) can pre-create a symlink at `.ja_stderr_tmp` to redirect writes to an arbitrary path (symlink attack / arbitrary file write).
- **Fix:** Use `mkstemp` (POSIX) or `GetTempFileName` (Windows) to generate a unique, unpredictable temp file name per invocation. Alternatively, use the fork/exec/pipe path (`ExecuteCommandWithWatchdog`) which does not require a temp file for stderr.

---

### HIGH — `ValidateScriptPath` bypass via absolute path or platform path separators

- **Location:** `ShellTaskExecutor::ValidateScriptPath` (~line 610) and its call site (~line 750)
- **Issue:** The check `path.rfind("scripts/", 0) == 0` uses a forward-slash literal. On Windows, a workflow definition may supply `scripts\run.ps1` (backslash), which fails the prefix check; the code then falls through to PowerShell `.ps1` substitution, which calls `std::filesystem::exists` on a path resolved from the launch CWD *before* the validation result is confirmed. Separately, `lexically_normal()` is applied to the *relative* string only; if a workflow supplies `scripts/../../etc/passwd` and the lexically normal form happens to retain a `scripts/` prefix on some path (it won't here, but edge cases exist with drive-relative Windows paths), the guard fails to protect.
- **Impact:** An attacker controlling workflow definitions can reference scripts outside the intended `scripts/` directory.
- **Fix:** After resolving to an absolute path, verify that `std::filesystem::canonical(commandPath)` is lexicographically prefixed by `std::filesystem::canonical(allowedScriptsDir)`. Do this check *after* absolute path resolution, not on the raw relative string.

---

### HIGH — Unbounded memory allocation from captured command output

- **Location:** `ExecuteCommandWithCapturedOutput` (`capturedStdout`/`capturedStderr` accumulation, ~line 310–340) and `ExecuteCommandWithWatchdog` (same, ~line 440–520)
- **Issue:** Both functions append all child process output into `std::string` accumulators (`stdoutOut`, `stderrOut`) with no size cap. A runaway or malicious script can produce gigabytes of output, causing unbounded heap growth until OOM.
- **Impact:** Denial of service; process crash; potential swap exhaustion affecting the host.
- **Fix:** Enforce a hard cap (e.g., 256 MB) on the combined accumulated size; after exceeding it, stop appending to the in-memory buffer (continue draining the pipe to prevent the child from blocking, but discard excess bytes). Log a warning and note truncation in `m_LastErrorMessage`.

---

### HIGH — Command path and expanded arguments logged at INFO level

- **Location:** `ShellTaskExecutor::Execute`, ~lines 840 and 870 (`LOG_APP_INFO("[shell] Command: {}", fullCommand)` and the `[paths debug]` log at completion)
- **Issue:** `fullCommand` contains the fully expanded argument list, which may include secrets injected via `{{env.SECRET_KEY}}` or other sensitive template variables. These are logged at INFO level unconditionally.
- **Impact:** Secrets appear in log files and any log aggregation system, violating the principle of secret non-exposure.
- **Fix:** Redact or omit the full command string from INFO logs. If the command must be logged for debugging, gate it behind a DEBUG/TRACE log level that is off by default.

---

### MEDIUM — `setpgid(0, 0)` before `dup2` — signal delivery window

- **Location:** `ExecuteCommandWithWatchdog`, child block, ~line 455
- **Issue:** `setpgid(0, 0)` is called in the child before `dup2` wires up the pipes.
